# Chapter One: The Map at Halcrest

[narrator] *Book One of the Kōkan · Chapter One: The Map at Halcrest*

### Before

[narrator, slowly] There was a war fought above the world, and the world paid for it.

The Archons fought it, the fourteen crowned principles, and the Titans fought it, the fourteen laws that bear the weight of things and want nothing. For forty years the spiritual world was its field, while the houses of men fought the same war below in their own coin, and in the last four years, which were the worst of the forty, the spiritual world did not survive the fighting in the shape it had been given. Whole provinces of it folded like wet paper. Rivers of it ran backward into the places they had come from and did not come out again. The scholars had been calling it the Great Spirit War since before most of the living were born, because scholars must call a thing something, and the people who had lived under it called it nothing at all, because they had buried too many to want a name in the house.

Three houses kept what was left.

The Yukari kept the spiritual world, or what remained of it: a torn country of thresholds and drowned shrines that had to be walked, mended and walked again, stitch by stitch, by people who went in whole and did not always come out so. The Zettari kept the physical world and the mandates of law, the measure and the boundary stone and the sentence handed down, so that whatever the war had done to the world above, the world below would still hold its shape when a man set his foot on it. And the Moto kept fate, which is the oldest of the three charges and the least forgiving, because a house that reads what must happen is always tempted to arrange it.

All three were counting what was left. All three had lost thousands, and some had lost more than others. Among the Moto there had been a line that once led them, older than the others, and twenty years into the war its own king put it into the dust. He burned its seat and cut its branches one by one, kin and cousin and child, until his was the last name of that line anyone would say aloud, and then he rode on through the twenty years after under it, and never once sat the red chair that was his, which had stood empty since the war began. The Zettari recorded the line gone. The Yukari recorded it gone. The other Moto lines, who had watched it go and not lifted a hand, recorded it gone with the most care of all, in a fine hand, and closed the register on it, and were satisfied. When the king himself fell at Shimane, at the war's end, they opened the register only long enough to write that too.

It was still the age of the Grand World then, before the great split of the continents, when a man could walk from the salt basins of the north to the warm marches of the south without once setting foot in a boat. That is the world this begins in.

[pause] A month after the war ended, in the chain-cities of Nalūn, a mercenary of sixteen sat with a map and could not believe it.

---

### I

The room above the salt-tea house at Halcrest was rented by the week and smelled of it. Lamp oil, wet wool, the butter-and-brine fug of the tea boiling below, and under all of it the mineral warmth that came up through the floor from the city cut into the dead sea's bed. The walls were salt, the pale Halcrest kind with its bedding planes showing like the grain of a board, and the lamp on the table was cut from salt too, so its light came through the stone the color of weak honey. It was an oil lamp. Draw cost what a sergeant's pay could not cover, and the tea-house paid for none above the kitchen.

Outside the one small window the upper town lay under its fifth month of snow, and it was beautiful in the way only a city cut from a dead sea could be. The terraces stepped up the basin wall in pale tiers, roofed in salt slab that the frost had furred white, and the bridge-chains strung from terrace to terrace carried worked lamps along their length like beads on a cord. Up on the factor quarter the lamps burned behind panes of clear salt-glass, rose and amber and a clean cold white, and the snow falling past them went pink and gold for the space of a breath before it dropped into the dark. Along the shaft-heads the brass gauge-housings stood capped in snow, their needles trembling under slate and Guild seal, and below the noise of the street ran the low draw-hum of the brine pumps, felt in the window glass more than heard. Somewhere out past the roofs, the chain at Bridgemarch was taking up slack, and its slow iron clank came through the glass so regularly that nobody in the room heard it any more.

Muken had the map pinned at the corners with whatever was to hand. A whetstone held the north-east. His sister's tin of salve held the south. His own left boot, which he had taken off an hour ago to look at a blister and then forgotten, held the west, and the east was held down by the flat of his scrap-iron sword, a length of black and pitted steel that three different smiths had looked at and declined to put a price on.

He was sixteen, and already built like the work had built him, the size of a grown man and then some: long in the arm, heavy through the shoulder and the back of the neck, with the forearms of someone who had swung a pick before he ever swung a blade. His skin was a deep brown, roughened at the knuckles and cracked white in the creases the way every hand raised in the Cuttings went cracked, as if the salt had got in and stayed. His hair was already going white. It had started at thirteen, at the temples, and by now there was nearly as much white in it as black, and he wore it tied back with a strip of leather because he could not be bothered to cut it and would not pay someone else to. His eyes were slate gray and set deep under a heavy brow. In a certain light, if he was tired or angry, there was a fleck of amber low in the left one, like a spark caught in a fire grate, and he did not know it was there, because he did not own a mirror and would not have looked if he had.

His coat was a dead sergeant's coat, cut down to fit him, and a sergeant's again now, because the Lanternless had made him one at sixteen, the youngest the company had ever had: brown wool gone the color of tea, the facings picked off and the holes where they had been darned shut by a hand that was not his. A fighting harness sat over it, worn shiny at the right hip where the scabbard rode. There was a pale seam of scar tissue through his left eyebrow and another across the back of his right hand. He had a broken front tooth, the left one, chipped to a corner. He smiled more than people expected him to, and when he did the tooth made him look about twelve, which was nearer the truth than most of his men liked to think about.

He was not smiling now.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, disbelieving]Nah." He pressed one cracked finger to the map, dragged it a long way, and lifted it as if the paper had burned him. "Nah, this can't be right, sis. You're telling me the monsters migrated *that* much?"

[narrator] Yukazuri, at the washstand with her sleeves pushed up, smiled at her brother in the scrap of mirror nailed above it.

"[young girl, Northern English accent, sharp, amused]Yeah. That's what it looked like."

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, incredulous]That's four hundred miles."

"[young girl, Northern English accent, sharp, matter-of-fact]Four hundred and thirty, by the scribe's scale. I measured it twice."

"[Northern English accent, low and rough]In two seasons."

"[young girl, Northern English accent, sharp, calmly]In two seasons," she agreed, and dried her hands.

[narrator] She was thirteen. She came across the room the way she did everything, without hurry and without one wasted step, a small girl made smaller by her brother's bulk filling half the room, and nobody who had watched her work over the bed of a cart ever called her a child twice. Her hair was silver-white and had been since she was a girl, a white so clean it looked deliberate, and she kept it braided down her back and pinned up off her neck when she was working. Her face was narrow and grave, deep brown like his, with a straight nose and a mouth that rested in a line most people took for disapproval until they saw it move. Her eyes were a deep blue that went gray in poor light, and in the lamplight off the salt they were nearly the color of his. Along the outside of her left forearm, from the wrist bone almost to the elbow, ran a long scar, flat and very straight and only just gone white, the kind a blade leaves when someone raises an arm to it at the right time and not a moment later. She had been twelve, last winter. She did not talk about it, and she never covered it.

She wore a field coat in undyed gray, cut short for kneeling in, with deep pockets she had sewn herself. One of them clinked with the small brass and glass of her trade: needles, gut, a flask of spirit, the salve tin's twin. A short blade rode at her left hip, its grip wrapped in gray cloth she rewound every morning, and nobody in the company had ever seen her draw it for anything but cutting a bandage.

She bent over the map beside him. When she came close, his breathing slowed. It always did, near her. He had stopped noticing years ago, the way he had stopped hearing the chain.

"[young girl, Northern English accent, sharp, dry]Look at the dates first," she said. "Then argue with me."

---

### II

[narrator] The map was a copy of a copy, and it was beautiful. Someone in a Zettari survey office had drawn the original, Yukazuri said, the kind of thing the Zettari had gone on doing all through the war's last years, measuring the world over again to be sure it was still there. A factor house in Ironlink had bought a copy because factor houses bought everything that might move the price of salt, and a scribe in that factor house had made a second copy on his own time and sold it on the quiet to a small white-haired girl who had sewn his brother's hand back together the winter before.

It showed the whole width of the Grand World's northern quarter in brown ink and red, from the salt basins on the left edge to a blank of boreal forest on the right where the surveyors had given up and written *uncharted, heavy timber*. Over the land, in a finer hand and a paler red, someone had drawn the sightings: little marks like hooked crosses, each with a date beside it in the Zettari reckoning, crowded thick in the south and the middle and thinning to the north-east in a long ragged tongue.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough]Right," Muken said. "Dates."

"[young girl, Northern English accent, sharp, explaining]These, along the Thresh." Her finger touched a dense knot of marks in the south. "Three years ago. The big herds. The stilts, the greywalkers, the things that hunt in the dry beds that nobody's named yet because nobody's come back to name them. That's where everyone was getting killed when we first took a contract."

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, wry]I remember. I was there for some of it."

"[young girl, Northern English accent, sharp]You were there for most of it. I was there for after." She moved her finger up. "Two years ago, here. The Marrow crossings. Fewer marks. They're bigger, though, look at the notation, the scribe's put a little crown on the ones the surveyors called *great*." Up again. "Last spring. Here." Up again, a long way, out past every road on the map and into the first fringe of the timber. "And this winter. Here."

[narrator] Muken leaned over the last cluster. They were drawn tight together, almost on top of one another, right at the edge where the brown ink gave out.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, quietly]What's out there?"

"[young girl, Northern English accent, sharp, flat]Nothing," Yukazuri said. "That's the point. No towns. No convoys. No cuttings. Nothing for them to eat, if they were following food. Nothing to kill, if they were following us."

[narrator] He was quiet for a moment. Downstairs, someone dropped a pot and swore by their token, and the tea-house keeper swore back.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough]So what are they following?"

"[young girl, Northern English accent, sharp, quietly]I don't know."

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, pressing]You've got a guess."

"[young girl, Northern English accent, sharp, thoughtful]I've got a guess." She straightened, and her hand went to the gray wrap on her blade's grip and rested there. "The surgeon at the Marrow camp told me something last year, when I was boiling his needles for him. He'd been up in the timber with a Yukari walker, in the war's bad years. He said the spirit side of the world isn't torn evenly. Some places it came apart like cloth. Some places it came apart like ice. He said the Yukari go where it's worst first, and the worst is south, where the fighting was heaviest, because that's where people live and that's where it bleeds into them."

"[Northern English accent, low and rough]And?"

"[young girl, Northern English accent, sharp, serious, low]And the things on this map aren't animals, Muken. Not really. They're what fell through when the spirit side broke. They came out where it broke worst. And now they're walking somewhere else." She tapped the far edge of the paper, the blank and the timber. "My guess is they're going where it's thinnest. Where it's quiet. Somewhere they can get back."

[narrator] He studied her. She had a way of saying the strangest things in the voice she used for telling a man to hold still while she pulled a splinter of rib out of his lung, and he had learned, slowly, over a lot of bad nights, that this was the voice she used when she was most sure.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, teasing]You got all that from boiling needles."

"[young girl, Northern English accent, sharp, dry]I got all that from listening while I boiled needles. You should try it."

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, wry]I'd burn myself."

"[young girl, Northern English accent, sharp, deadpan]You would," she said. "You'd burn yourself and then tell everyone it was a fight."

[narrator] [laughs] He laughed, a short bark, the broken tooth showing, and dragged a hand down his face. His palm rasped over two days of stubble. "[Northern English accent, low and rough, chuckling]Sky above. Four hundred and thirty miles."

"[young girl, Northern English accent, sharp, firm]Thirty," she said. "Don't round it. That's how factors cheat you."

---

### III

[narrator] They had been raised in the Cuttings, under Halcrest, three galleries down where the warmth was steady and the air tasted of powdered halite and lamp soot. The draw came down the shafts in sealed conduit for the brine pumps and the lift-cages and went past the galleries without stopping. The cutting families burned fish-oil in tin cups, and the cages hummed by in the dark with their worked lamps lit and their gates shut, carrying factor men down to the stamping floor and up again. Where a cup's flame caught a seam of clean halite the wall threw it back in pink and violet and a faint drowned green, and the gallery children used to lie on their backs and count the colors, because it was the one thing in the Cuttings nobody charged for. Their mother cut salt for a factor house called Orrel until her lungs went, and then she sorted bars at the stamping floor, under the draw-presses that struck the house seal into every bar with a sound like a door slammed, until her eyes went, and then she did nothing at all for nearly a year in a room the size of a cart, with the salt-throat coughing out of her in long dry fits that sounded like someone sawing a board, and her children's hands on her whenever a contract let them come down. [softly] She died a month ago, on the morning the news of Shimane came down the galleries, while the whole of the Cuttings stood out in the passages passing it from mouth to mouth that the war was over. Her name was Kasane. She had no other name the clerks would take.

[narrator] That was the whole of it, as far as anyone at Orrel was concerned. A cutting-class woman with no house name, no papers and no token, entered in the drainage book under a number because there was nothing else to enter her under. She had come down into the Cuttings sixteen years ago, in the middle of the war, a young woman alone with a boy at the breast, from somewhere she never once named, and the Orrel house had taken the pair of them in the way the factor houses took in everything that arrived without a claim on it. The boy was too young to remember anywhere else, and grew up believing he had been born in the gallery, because nobody told him otherwise. Three years later there was Yukazuri. Whose either of them was, their mother never said. Muken had stopped asking before he was ten. Yukazuri, who could read most people the way the scribes read a bill, had never been able to read it out of her, and had gone on trying to the end.

"[young girl, Northern English accent, sharp, quietly]Do you ever think about where she came from?" Yukazuri asked. She had sat down on the edge of the cot with the map across her knees, and she was not looking at him. "When you look at maps. All the places it might have been."

[narrator] Muken pulled his boot out from under the corner of the map and began, with a great deal of attention, to put it back on.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, gruff]No."

"[young girl, Northern English accent, sharp, knowing]Liar."

[narrator] He worked his heel down into the boot. The blister bit and he hissed through his teeth. "[Northern English accent, low and rough, grudging]Sometimes."

"[young girl, Northern English accent, sharp, wistful]She'd have hated this room."

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, warm]She'd have loved this room. It's got a window."

[narrator] That got a sound out of Yukazuri that was nearly a laugh. She ran her thumb along the map's edge. "[young girl, Northern English accent, sharp, softly]She used to say the salt was patient. Do you remember? She'd put her hand on the wall at night and say, *[gentle, light Japanese accent, softly]it's patient, it'll keep us*. [young girl, Northern English accent, sharp] I thought she meant it'd keep us alive."

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, quietly]What did she mean?"

"[young girl, Northern English accent, sharp, barely steady]I think she meant it'd keep us. The way it keeps the dead." Yukazuri's voice did not change at all, and that was how he knew it cost her. "She knew she wasn't going to get out. She was talking to the wall about us."

[narrator] [long pause] Muken said nothing for a while. He finished lacing the boot and sat with his forearms on his knees and his big cracked hands hanging between them, and he looked at the floor. He had been two seasons short of buying her number out of the drainage book when she died. The salt for it was still in a bag under the boards of this room, counted twice, with nothing left to buy. When he was a boy, the house steward at Orrel had had a habit of asking the cutting children the merchant's question, *What's your oldest?*, as a joke, because the answer was always nothing. A merchant showed his token before his name. A child of the Cuttings had neither, and the steward thought that was very funny, and laughed every time.

Muken had been thirteen the last time the steward asked him. He had answered with his fists. It was the first contract he ever honored, as he told it later, and the only one he never got paid for.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, quietly]I got us out," he said to the floor.

"[young girl, Northern English accent, sharp, gently]You got us out," Yukazuri agreed. "I was there."

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, proud, low]Bought your number out of the drainage book with my first season's pay. Every salt bar of it."

"[young girl, Northern English accent, sharp, softly]I know. I counted it with you. Twice."

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, wry]Don't round it."

"[young girl, Northern English accent, sharp, dry, amused]I wouldn't dare."

[narrator] He looked up then, and she was watching him with that grave face that most people mistook for disapproval, and he saw the corner of her mouth was turned up. [sighs] He let out a breath. It went long and slow out of him and he felt his shoulders come down from round his ears, and he did not wonder why. He never did.

"[young girl, Northern English accent, sharp, quietly]Do you remember the mark?" she asked.

[narrator] He knew which mark. "[Northern English accent, low and rough, softly]Course I do."

[narrator] Their mother had drawn it for them once, when Yukazuri was very small and Muken was not much bigger, with the end of a burned stick on the salt of the wall. It was not a letter. It was not any letter in the Concord hand or the scribe's hand or the trader's ciphers or anything else they had ever seen since, and they had seen a great deal of writing by now on a great many contracts. It looked a little like a knot and a little like a closed eye, and a little like neither. Kasane had said, *[gentle, light Japanese accent, whispers]that's ours. That's our name. Don't show it to the clerks.* [narrator] And then she had rubbed it out with the heel of her hand and never drawn it again.

Yukazuri had drawn it in secret ever since, on frosted windows and in the dirt at camp and once, when she was eleven, in spilled lamp oil on the stamping floor, where it had burned blue for a moment before the oil went out. Muken had tried, a few times, to draw it himself. His hand would not make it. Every time, the line went somewhere wrong and the shape came apart, and in the end he stopped trying and let his sister keep it for both of them.

"[young girl, Northern English accent, sharp, carefully]I showed it to a Zettari once," Yukazuri said.

[narrator] His head came up sharply. "[Northern English accent, low and rough, sharp, alarmed]You what?"

"[young girl, Northern English accent, sharp, calm]A surveyor. At the Marrow, the same season as the surgeon. I drew it in the frost on his window while he was asleep, and in the morning I asked him what it was."

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, warning]Sis."

"[young girl, Northern English accent, sharp, thoughtful]He looked at it a good while," she said, as if he had not spoken. "And then he said it wasn't anything. He said it was a child's scribble. He said it very fast, and then he wiped the window with his sleeve, and he wouldn't look at me after that." She folded the edge of the map down flat under her palm. "His breath went short when he said it. Here." She touched the hollow of her own throat. "Right up high. They do that when they're frightened and they're trying to be polite about it."

[narrator] The chain at Bridgemarch clanked. The lamp hissed in its salt bowl.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, low]What's a Zettari frightened of a scribble for," Muken said.

"[young girl, Northern English accent, sharp, quietly]I don't know." She met his eyes. "I thought you'd want to know he was."

---

### IV

[narrator] [knocking] Somebody knocked at the door, two raps and a pause and a third, which was the company knock, and then the door opened before either of them answered it, which was the company manners.

[pause] It was not anyone from the company.

The man who stepped in out of the stair had the look of the factor houses all over him. Middle years, middling height, a long gray traveling coat with the snow still on its shoulders, cut close and lined with something dark and soft at the collar. Thin sandy hair combed flat from a high parting. A clean-shaven face that had been handsome once and had settled into the expression of a man who was always, quietly, doing sums. Ink on the second finger of the right hand, the scribe's stain, though he did not have the scribe's stoop. Good boots, oiled, salt rime on the toes. And on his belt, where a fighting man wore a knife, a bronze seal-token the size of a large coin, hanging from a short chain. It hummed. In the small room you could hear it, a faint steady note just under the lamp's hiss, and feel it a little behind the eyes.

"[Northern English accent, smooth and polite]Muken of the Lanternless," the man said, and smiled. "And the Lanternless's surgeon, I take it. Forgive me. Your captain said I'd find you above the tea-house, and the keeper said you'd not mind."

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, pleasant]The keeper's a liar," Muken said pleasantly. He had not stood up. His sword was still lying across the east of the map, and his hand had come to rest on the table about a forearm's length from its grip. "Who are you?"

"[Northern English accent, smooth and polite]Hollis Draycott. I act for the Vessant house." He let a short silence fall where the greeting ought to go, and when neither of them filled it, his eyes went, quickly and quite openly, to Muken's belt, and then to Yukazuri's, and found no token on either, and came back up. It was not rudeness, precisely. It was how a man of the factor houses read a room, the way a sailor looked at the sky. "[awkward]Ah. Well. I'll keep it short, then."

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, flat]Tokenless," Muken said. "You can say it. I've heard it."

"[Northern English accent, smooth and polite, protesting]I wasn't going to say anything of the kind."

"[young girl, Northern English accent, sharp, cool]No," Yukazuri said. "You weren't." She had not moved from the cot. She was watching his breathing.

[narrator] Draycott glanced at her, a little longer this time, at the white hair and the gray coat and the long flat scar down her forearm. Then he unbuttoned his coat, took a folded paper out of an inner pocket, and held it up between two fingers without offering it.

"[Northern English accent, smooth and polite, businesslike]The Vessant house runs a supply contract into the eastern timber," he said. "Iron stock and lamp oil out, furs and resin back. A small post on a river, past the last of the roads. Very profitable. Very old contract, as it happens." He touched the humming token at his belt, briefly, with the inky finger. "This one. Our oldest. It's been sound for thirty-one years and the house would very much like it to stay sound for thirty-one more. But the last two convoys have had trouble on the road."

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, flat]Trouble," Muken said.

"[Northern English accent, smooth and polite, evasive]Animals."

[narrator] Muken looked down at the map, at the thick red knot of hooked crosses on the edge of the timber. Then he looked back up at Draycott with the slate-gray eyes and said nothing at all.

[narrator] Draycott followed his look and saw what was on the table, and for the first time something in his face shifted. It was very small. He had good control. "[Northern English accent, smooth and polite, guarded]I see you've done some reading."

"[Northern English accent, low and rough]My sister has."

"[Northern English accent, smooth and polite, brisk]Then you'll understand why we're paying what we're paying. Escort of a single convoy, eleven wagons, from the Halcrest gate to the river post and back. Twenty fighting men, your captain's choice, plus the surgeon. Forty bars a man, stamped Vessant, full weight. Eighty for you as sergeant. Half at the gate."

[narrator] It was a great deal of salt. Muken kept it off his face. Yukazuri saw his right thumb move once against the edge of the table, which was where it showed on him.

"[young girl, Northern English accent, sharp, level]What happened to the last escort?" Yukazuri asked.

[narrator] Draycott turned to her. "[Northern English accent, smooth and polite, too smooth]Light losses. Three men. It was a rough road."

[narrator] He touched his token again as he said it. His thumb went to the bronze and ran once around the rim of it, the way another man might touch a lucky coin, and his breath came in high and short, right up under the collarbone, and stopped there a moment before it went out.

Yukazuri looked at her brother.

Muken had seen her look at men like that a thousand times over the bed of a cart and the floor of a tent, when she was deciding whether a man was going to live, and he knew the look the way he knew the chain. He sat back. The chair creaked under him.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, quiet, hard]How many came back?" he said.

"[Northern English accent, smooth and polite, stiff]I've told you."

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, cold]You told her how many died. I'm asking how many came back."

[narrator] The token hummed. Downstairs, the tea was boiling over, and someone was shouting about it.

"[Northern English accent, smooth and polite, pause, reluctant]Four," Draycott said.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, flat]Of how many."

"[Northern English accent, smooth and polite, quietly]Of twenty-six."

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, cold]So that's twenty-two dead."

"[Northern English accent, smooth and polite, prim]Or missing. The road was very confused. One must be accurate." He put the paper on the table, on the corner of the map, beside the whetstone. He did it neatly and without hurry, and he did not look at Yukazuri again. "[candid]I'll be frank, since your surgeon seems determined that I should be. The house has had difficulty finding a company. The Grey Lamps turned us down. So did Fennick's. Your captain, to his credit, said he'd hear me out if his sergeant would go, and that his sergeant was the only man in the company who'd go anywhere for money."

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, wry]My captain's a liar too," Muken said. "I'll go anywhere for enough money."

"[Northern English accent, smooth and polite]Name it."

[narrator] Muken looked at the map. He looked at the long ragged tongue of red marks stretching out of the south, out of everything that had happened in the war's last years, out past the roads and the towns and the cuttings and up into the white space at the edge where the surveyors had given up. He thought about four hundred and thirty miles in two seasons. He thought about things that were not really animals, walking home through the snow.

[softly] He thought about a room the size of a cart.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, steady]Double it," he said. "Full at the gate, nothing on return. Half again for every man who doesn't come back, paid to whoever he names. And she doesn't go past the river post."

"[young girl, Northern English accent, sharp, firm]She does," said Yukazuri.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, firm]She doesn't."

"[young girl, Northern English accent, sharp, cool]She does, or you'll be paying the half-again on yourself." Her voice had not risen. "You can dislike me when we get back."

[narrator] Muken opened his mouth and shut it again. Draycott looked from one of them to the other with the faint interest of a man watching two other men haggle over a horse he did not intend to buy.

"[Northern English accent, smooth and polite, slowly]Double," Draycott said at last, slowly. "Full at the gate. The death-price I can do. The house will want it sealed."

"[Northern English accent, low and rough]Seal it, then."

"[Northern English accent, smooth and polite, not unkindly]With what? You've no token for it to bind to." He did not say it unkindly, and it was the truest thing he had said since he walked in. "A Nalūn seal binds to a house name, and there's none in the book for you. It's got nothing to sit under. The Wellspring doesn't see you. You understand that? You could break it and walk away and the salt wouldn't so much as notice."

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, proud, steady]I've never broken a contract in my life."

"[Northern English accent, smooth and polite, surprised]I believe you," Draycott said, and seemed surprised to find he did. "But the house won't care what I believe."

[narrator] Muken stood up then. The room got smaller. He was half a head taller than the factor's agent and a great deal broader, and the lamp threw his shadow up the salt wall behind him until it bent over the ceiling. He did not put his hand near the sword. He held out his right hand instead, the one with the scar across the back of it, open and cracked white at every knuckle.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, steady]Then it's on that," he said. "Same as it's always been."

[narrator] Draycott considered the hand. Then, because he was a man of the factor houses and a factor knew a good price when he was shown one, he took it.

---

### V

[narrator] When he had gone, his snow melting into dark spots on the floorboards by the door and the hum of his token fading down the stair after him, Muken stood looking at the paper on the table and did not pick it up.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, quietly]Twenty-two," he said.

"[young girl, Northern English accent, sharp, softly]I know."

"[Northern English accent, low and rough]You knew before he said it."

"[young girl, Northern English accent, sharp]I knew he was lying. I didn't know by how much." Yukazuri got up off the cot and came and stood beside him, and his breathing slowed, and neither of them remarked on it. "You could still say no. Captain would back you."

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, wry]Captain would sell me to Vessant by the pound if they asked nicely."

"[young girl, Northern English accent, sharp, dry, teasing]Captain would sell you by the ounce," she said. "He'd get more for you that way."

[narrator] [chuckles] He laughed again, quieter, and put his arm round her shoulders, and she let him, which she did not let anyone else do. They stood like that over the map. The lamp burned down in its salt bowl and the light through the stone went from honey to amber, and outside the window the snow came on thicker over the roofs of Halcrest, rose and gold where it fell past the factor lamps and white again below them, and down in the dark the chain at Bridgemarch kept the city hung together, link by link, the way it always had.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, thoughtful]They're going somewhere," Muken said at last. "The things on the map."

"[young girl, Northern English accent, sharp]Yes."

"[Northern English accent, low and rough]Then that's where the work's going to be. Everyone's going to want an escort out there. Every house with a contract past the roads."

"[young girl, Northern English accent, sharp, quietly]That's where the dying's going to be," Yukazuri said.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, tired]Same thing, sis. It's always been the same thing."

[narrator] She did not answer that. She reached past him, across the table, to the small window, and breathed on the cold glass until it fogged white. Then, with the tip of one finger, without looking at her hand, she drew their mother's mark in the mist. The knot that was a little like an eye. The name the clerks were never meant to see.

Muken watched her do it. He had watched her do it a hundred times. This time, for no reason he could have told anyone, a sharp heat started behind his left eye, as if a cinder had blown into it, and for a single breath the mark on the glass did not look like a scribble at all. [hushed] It looked like a seam. It looked like the place where something had been joined to something else, a long time ago, and was under strain, and he could see exactly where it would give.

[narrator] He blinked, and it was a scribble on a fogged window again, already beginning to run.

"[young girl, Northern English accent, sharp, concerned]You all right?" Yukazuri said.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, dismissive]Fine." He rubbed the eye with the heel of his hand. "Smoke."

"[young girl, Northern English accent, sharp]There's no smoke."

"[Northern English accent, low and rough]Then it's the salt."

[narrator] She looked at him the way she looked at men in the backs of carts, for a moment longer than he liked. Then she let it go, and turned back to the window, and watched the mark run down the glass until it was gone.

[long pause] [ominous, slowly] Far out to the north-east, past the last of the roads, in the white space at the edge of the Zettari map where the surveyors had written *uncharted, heavy timber*, something very large lifted its head out of the snow and turned it, slowly, toward the south.

---

# Chapter Two: Big Bertha

[narrator] *Book One of the Kōkan · Chapter Two: Big Bertha*

As sergeant, Muken knew the cargo was his to get where it was going. Eleven wagons of Vessant iron stock and lamp oil, one covered carriage, twenty fighting men of the Lanternless and the company surgeon, and every salt bar of it his responsibility from the Halcrest gate to the river post in the timber. He had known that when he shook Draycott's hand. He had known there would be fighting.

[pause] He had not known what kind.

They came out of the trees on the sixth morning, where the road narrowed between two shoulders of granite and the snow lay in the ruts packed hard and glassy. The sun was barely clear of the timber. It came low and copper through the spruce and laid long blue shadows across the road, and every needle on the near trees carried its own rind of frost, so that the whole wood glittered as if it had been dusted with ground salt. Six days out from Halcrest there was no draw-hum anywhere, no gauge, no lamp but the wagons' oil; only the creak of the axles and the horses blowing. There were three of them. At first, through the spruce, he took them for goblins, and for a moment, before he saw them properly, he was nearly relieved. Goblins he knew. Goblins you could frighten, or pay, or kill.

[low] These were wrong.

They had the shape of goblins the way a burned house has the shape of a house. Something had been done to what was inside them. He could feel it from thirty paces, a sick pressure behind the breastbone, the way the air felt near a seal that was going bad in the Cuttings: a sourness, a wrongness of weight, as if the essence in them had gone off like milk and kept them moving anyway. Their skin was black. It was black all over, wet-black, the color of a lamp's soot when it is fresh and still greasy, and it moved over the muscle beneath like oil over water. Their eyes were small and set too far forward, and they were red. The whites were shot through with crimson, every vessel burst, as if they had been weeping blood for days.

They were covered in blood. Most of it was old and had dried brown and flaked at the joints. Some of it was not. The middle one had something gray and soft hanging from the corner of its mouth, a long string of it, and as Muken watched it slid loose and dropped into the snow, and he knew what it was, because he had seen the inside of a man's head before. Brain. It had been eating somebody's brain on the way here and it had not finished.

The biggest was half again the height of a man. Its shoulders were humped like a bear's and its arms hung to its knees, and its face had come out of the corruption as something that should never have been put together: the broad heavy muzzle and lower jaw of a bear, full of yellow teeth, and set into the front of it, where the nose should be, a hooked black beak like a hawk's, as long as a man's hand and sharp as a gaff. The second was nearer a man's size, long and narrow, with fingers that ended in claws as long as table knives, and from the claws of its right hand hung a pale, wet, rope-like sheet of something that Muken's eye skated off twice before his mind would name it. Gut. The lining of somebody's belly, still hooked on where it had been pulled out.

The third was small. It came no higher than his hip. It was the fastest of the three, and it was the one he liked least.

*[Northern English accent, low and rough, inner voice]A fucking demon*, he thought. *That's a demon. That's what they look like.*

[narrator] [whistles] He put two fingers in his mouth and whistled, three short blasts, and four of his men peeled off the column at a run toward the head of it, where the lead wagons were already slewing to a stop and somebody was shouting about the horses. The rest of the line knew the signal and drew in around the wagons. Behind him he heard the carriage door bang open.

He knew without turning that it would be his sister. She would be leaning out of the door with one hand on the frame and her white hair coming out of its pins, and her face would be doing nothing at all, because that was what it did when she was frightened for him.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, curt]Stay in," he said, without looking round.

"[young girl, Northern English accent, sharp, flat]I'm in."

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, firm]Stay further in."

[narrator] The carriage door did not close.

Three men had stayed back with him. Oskar Pell, who was fifty and had a spear and a bad hip and no intention of going anywhere near the front, had already planted himself at the carriage wheel, and Muken left him there. The other two were the Starks.

Rob Stark was the elder by four years, a big rawboned northerner of twenty-six with a beard the color of wet rust and a face like a closed door, who had come south out of the Stark holdings with his brother two winters back for reasons neither of them had ever told anyone. He wore a mail shirt that had belonged to someone shorter and fought with a boar-spear, a heavy ash haft with an iron crossbar below the head, and a hand axe at his belt. Mark Stark was twenty-two and looked younger, with the same rust in his hair and none in his chin yet, freckled, quick, with a round shield on his left arm and a short sword in his right. He talked when he was frightened. He was talking now, under his breath, a steady stream of it, and Muken could not make out the words and did not try.

The three things on the road had stopped. They stood in a ragged line across the ruts, perhaps twenty paces off, and they were watching. The big one's beak opened and closed with a small wet click.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, flat, steady]Rob," Muken said. His voice came out flat and steady, and he was glad of it. "I'll take the two big ones. You and Mark handle the cub."

[narrator] The Stark did not complain. The Starks never did. Rob only shifted his grip on the spear, and spat to one side, and said, "[Yorkshire accent, cheerful, hoarse, grim]Aye," and his brother's muttering stopped.

[narrator] Muken looked at the two big ones. He reached over his shoulder and drew the scrap-iron sword, the black pitted blade sliding out of the scabbard with a dry rasp, and brought it down and round into both hands and fell into a stance, left foot forward, weight sitting low in the hips, the point at the height of his own throat. He felt the cold of the hilt through the wrappings.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, cold, taunting]You two ugly motherfuckers," he told them, "[menacing]are fighting me."

---

[narrator] At that time in his life, Muken's essence was nowhere near what it would become. Neither was his aether. He had no school, no teacher and no name for anything he did. What he had was a body that had cut salt from the age of six and swung a sword, of one kind or another, since he was eight, and a trick he had found on his own the way a child finds that it can whistle: when the danger came close enough, he could feel something warm move in him, down out of his chest into his arms and back and legs, and for a little while everything in him was heavier, harder, faster, a little less likely to break. He did not know it was called reinforcement. He did not know that what he could do was the very least of it, a candle's worth against a bonfire. He knew it had kept him alive in other people's wars, and he thought it would be enough to kill these.

[pause] He thought.

The big one came first. He named it in his head as it came, the way he named everything that tried to kill him, because it helped. *[Northern English accent, low and rough, inner voice]Big Bertha.* [narrator] It lurched forward out of the line with a heavy rolling gait, the hump of its shoulders rising and falling, and swung its right arm up and over in a long arc like a mantis bringing down its foreleg, the hooked claws at the end of it falling toward him faster than anything that size had a right to.

His body moved before he decided to. That was how it always went. The warmth flooded down into his legs and his back and he went sideways and round, turning on the ball of his left foot, spinning clear under the falling arm so close that the wind of it lifted his hair. The claws went into the frozen ground where he had been standing and the road broke under them like a dropped plate, chunks of ice and gravel bursting up into the air.

And the other one was already there.

They had done this before. They had done this a great many times. Even as Bertha's blow fell the narrow one had come in low under it from the other side, timing its rush to meet him at the end of his turn, and its right hand came straight for his belly, claws first, the gut-lining still swinging from them, a wet pale flag. It meant to open him the way it had opened the last one. He saw it coming the way you see a cart coming downhill with no driver on it, and there was no time to bring the sword round.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, through gritted teeth]Nah," Muken said through his teeth. "It won't be that easy."

[narrator] He pushed hard off his left foot. Big Bertha's arm was still driving down into the road beside him, all that weight still moving, and something in him, something he had no name for yet, read the line of it the way he would read a fault in a salt face before he set the pick: the shoulder, the elbow, the wrist, the planted claws, the whole arm locked straight and loaded like a beam under a roof. There. *That* was the place. He used it. He threw his weight against Bertha's locked forearm as if it were a post set in the ground and swung his right leg out off it, a wild flailing kick with everything his reinforced hips could put behind it, and the heel of his boot came down on the narrow one's wrist from above and drove the claws down past his belly and into the frozen ground, smashing the hand flat against the road.

He felt the small bones in it go under his heel like a fistful of kindling.

[shrieking] The creature shrieked. The sound was high and wet and very nearly a word. Its pinned arm held it bent over for half a breath with its neck stretched out in front of him, long and black and bare, and Muken brought the sword up and round in both hands, over his head and down, and put all of it into the neck.

---

[narrator] The blade went in a hand's depth and stopped.

He felt it stop. It went through the black skin and through the thick ropes of muscle at the side of the neck, and blood came out round the steel, very dark, almost purple, and smoking faintly in the cold, and then the edge hit the bones of the spine and lodged between two of them with a jolt that went up through his wrists and elbows and into his shoulders like a hammer on an anvil. It did not go through. Scrap iron and a candle's worth of essence did not take a head off in one stroke, whatever the songs said.

The creature did not die. It thrashed. It ripped its broken hand up out of the ground and swung it at him backhanded, and the shattered fingers caught him across the left side of the face and tore his cheek open from the hinge of the jaw to the corner of the eye. He felt the skin part. He felt his own blood go hot down his neck and into his collar. The sword was still stuck in its spine and it was trying to wrench it out of his hands by turning its whole body, and behind him, he knew without looking, Bertha was pulling its claws out of the road.

He put his boot on the narrow one's shoulder and shoved and pulled at the same time, and the blade came free with a sound like a spade coming out of wet clay. Black blood sprayed across the snow in a long fan. The creature staggered, its head lolling to the right where the neck was half cut through, and still it would not go down. It turned toward him, head hanging, crimson eyes rolling, and opened its mouth.

He cut again. Lower, flatter, into the wound he had already made. This time the spine was already parted and the edge went on through the meat and gristle on the far side and the head came off and fell into the snow and rolled once, face up, and the jaws kept working. The body stood for a moment longer with black blood pumping up out of the stump in thick pulses, and then the knees went and it folded down onto them and pitched forward onto its chest.

There was no time to watch it. Bertha's arm was coming round again.

He heard it more than saw it: the hiss of the claws through the air at the height of his head. He threw himself flat. The claws went over him and took the top off a snowbank and sprayed it white across the road. He rolled. The warmth was still in his legs but it was thinner now, a thread of it, and when he came up onto one knee his thighs were shaking as if he had climbed four galleries of the Cuttings with a full load, and there was a coppery taste in his mouth that was not only the blood from his cheek.

*[Northern English accent, low and rough, inner voice, strained]Not much left,* he thought. *Make it count.*

[narrator] Bertha came on. It came on the way it had come at the start, with that heavy rolling lurch, and this time Muken watched how it moved instead of where its claws were, and he saw it. Every step it took, it came down on the right leg. The left it set down lightly and lifted fast, the way a horse favors a bad hoof. Something in that left leg was wrong: an old break, or a wound from whoever had fought it before him, or just the way the corruption had put it together. Whatever it was, every ounce of that great humped weight was going through the right knee. He could see it now, the knee itself, the joint where all the loading met, standing out to his eye as clearly as a crack in a wall.

The beak opened, the bear-jaw under it opened, and the whole head came down at him like a falling bell.

He went in under it. He went in low and to its right, so close he could smell it, rot and wet fur and something like hot pennies, and he swung the sword in a flat hard arc at knee height with both hands and every scrap of warmth he had left poured into his arms. The edge took Bertha across the front of the right knee, just below the cap, where the thick tendon ran down over the joint, and went in deep and grated on bone.

The knee buckled. The weight on it was too much and the tendon was gone, and the leg simply folded the wrong way, bending forward at the joint with a crack like a green branch, and all of Bertha came down after it. It fell forward onto its hands. Its head came down level with his.

He did not try for the neck this time. He had learned that much. He brought the point up from below, under the hinge of the bear-jaw, where the throat met the skull and the bone was thin, and drove it upward with both hands and his shoulder behind it and his legs straightening under him. The point went in under the jaw and up through the roof of the mouth and on into whatever Bertha had for a brain, and he felt it go through, a soft give and then a harder one, and then nothing.

The hawk's beak opened very wide and stayed open. Black blood ran out of it over his hands. The red eyes rolled up white, or what had been white, and the whole great weight of it sagged down onto the sword, and he could not hold it. He let go of the hilt and stepped back, and Bertha slid down onto its side in the snow with the blade still standing up out of its throat like a stake in a field, and its legs kicked twice, and it was still.

Muken stood there with his empty hands at his sides and his breath going in and out of him in great white plumes. His cheek was hanging open and his own blood had run down inside his coat to his belt. His hands would not stop shaking. The warmth had gone out of him entirely, all at once, the way a lamp goes when the oil is gone, and what it left behind was a cold so deep that his teeth started to chatter.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, panting, hoarse]Two," he said aloud, to nobody. His voice cracked on it. "That's two, you ugly bastards."

[narrator] [screams] Then he heard Mark Stark scream.

---

[narrator] The cub had gone for the brothers the moment Bertha moved, and it had been fighting them the whole time behind him, and they had been losing.

It was small. That was the problem. It was small and it was quicker than anything Muken had ever seen move on two legs, and it fought like a stoat in a henhouse, low and fast and everywhere. Rob had met its first rush the right way, the boar-spear leveled and the butt planted in the snow, so that the thing would run itself onto the head. It had not run onto the head. It had gone under the crossbar and up the haft, climbing the spear itself with its claws, hand over hand, as fast as a squirrel up a trunk, and Rob had had to throw the whole spear sideways to shake it off before it reached his hands. It hit the snow and was up again before the spear did.

Mark had come in then, with the shield up and the short sword low, the way they had both been taught by someone a long time ago in a northern yard, and the cub had gone straight at the shield. It took the rim of the round shield in its jaws and bit down, and the wood split under its teeth with a crack, and its head came on through the split, and its jaws closed on Mark's left forearm through the leather of his sleeve.

[screams] That was when he screamed.

[narrator] Muken was already running. His legs were wrong under him, numb and heavy and shaking, and he had no sword, and he was too far away. He saw it all happen in front of him with that terrible clearness that comes in a fight, where everything is slow and there is nothing you can do about any of it.

Mark went down on his back in the snow with the cub on top of him, still clamped to his arm, shaking its head from side to side the way a dog shakes a rat, and with every shake Mark's arm went with it and he screamed again, high and cracked, like a boy. Blood was coming out from between its teeth and running down Mark's sleeve. Its back claws were raking at his belly. The mail shirt was saving him, just; the claws skidded on the rings and snagged and tore and skidded again, and links were flying loose into the snow like spilled coins.

"[Yorkshire accent, cheerful, hoarse, roaring]*Mark!*" Rob roared.

[narrator] He did not use the spear. There was no room; he would have gone through his brother. He dropped it and drew the hand axe from his belt and came down on the cub from above, both knees on its back, his whole weight on it, and grabbed it by the back of the neck with his left hand. The black skin slid under his fingers like greased leather and he could not hold it. He brought the axe down anyway. The blade hit the cub between the shoulders and bit in and stuck, and the thing let go of Mark's arm and twisted round under Rob's knees with its whole body, impossibly, like something with no bones in it, and sank its teeth into Rob's thigh just above the knee.

[groans] Rob did not scream. He made a sound like a man lifting something too heavy, a long grinding groan through clenched teeth, and he wrenched the axe free and hit it again. And again. The axe came down on its back and its shoulders and the side of its head, a butcher's rhythm, and black blood flew up in spatters across Rob's face and beard, and still the thing would not let go of his leg.

[narrator] Mark was on his knees beside them. His left arm hung at his side, the sleeve torn open and the forearm under it torn open too, so that Muken could see the white of something inside it. His face was gray. He had kept hold of the short sword. He had kept hold of it the whole time. He got his right hand round it and shuffled forward on his knees and put the point against the cub's side, just behind where its foreleg joined the body, and leaned on it with all his weight.

It went in. The short sword went into the cub up to the hilt, and the cub stiffened all over and let go of Rob's leg and opened its mouth, and Mark held it there, leaning on it, [sobbing] sobbing, his face a hand's width from the thing's red eyes, and the cub's claws scrabbled at his chest and his face and his good arm, and he held it there.

"[Yorkshire accent, cheerful, hoarse, hoarse, desperate]Hold it," Rob said hoarsely. "*Hold it.* Hold it, Mark, hold it, don't you let go."

[narrator] He took the axe in both hands and raised it high over his head, and brought it down on the cub's neck where it lay pinned between the sword and the snow, and the blade went through and buried itself in the frozen ground beneath. He stayed there on his knees with both hands still on the haft and his head bowed over it, breathing.

[softly] The head lay in the snow. Its jaws opened and closed, opened and closed, slower each time, and then stopped open.

[narrator] Muken reached them at last and went down on his knees beside them because his legs would no longer hold him up. For a while none of them said anything. The three bodies smoked on the road in the cold. The snow around them was trampled down to the frozen gravel, black where the creatures had bled and a red so bright where the men had that it hurt to look at in the low sun, and here and there in it lay the scattered rings of Mark's mail, catching the copper light like coins somebody had thrown away.

[crying] Mark was crying. He was doing it quietly now, with his face turned away from his brother, holding his ruined arm against his chest with his good hand. Rob was bleeding steadily through his breeches from the bite above the knee, a dark wet stain spreading down to his boot, and he did not seem to have noticed it yet.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, distant, gentle]You're all right," Muken said. His own voice sounded very far away. "You're both all right. You did it."

"[Yorkshire accent, young, quick, crying, shaking]That was the *small* one," Mark said. His voice shook so badly the words came out in pieces. "That was the small one, Sarge. That was the *cub.*"

"[Yorkshire accent, cheerful, hoarse, grim]Aye," said Rob, and spat black into the snow, and wiped his mouth with the back of his hand, and looked at the carcass of Big Bertha lying on its side with Muken's sword standing up out of its throat. He took his time over it. Then he looked at Muken, at the open cheek and the blood down his coat and the hands that would not stop shaking, and something in that closed northern face shifted, very slightly, and settled again in a different place.

[narrator] Whatever it was, he kept it behind his teeth.

[narrator] Behind them, quick light footsteps were coming across the snow from the carriage, and Muken knew whose they were without turning round. He heard the clink of glass and brass in a deep coat pocket. He heard his sister's voice, low and level, already giving orders, the voice she used for telling men to hold still. Her breath stood white in front of her and her hair had come entirely out of its pins, silver in the copper light, and her face was doing nothing at all.

"[young girl, Northern English accent, sharp, low, level, commanding]Mark first," Yukazuri said. "Rob, lie down. Muken, sit before you fall."

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, weak]I'm sitting."

"[young girl, Northern English accent, sharp, dry]Sit lower."

[narrator] He would have laughed, if his face had let him.

[long pause] [horn blowing] Then, from the head of the column, far up the road past the lead wagons and round the shoulder of the granite where the four men he had sent forward had gone, came the sound of a horn.

It was the column's horn, the Lanternless call, one long note and two short. He knew it the way he knew the chain at Bridgemarch. It meant the cargo had stopped.

[horn blowing] It blew again, one long and two short. And then, before it had finished, a third time, ragged, and much too soon, as if whoever was blowing it had no breath left to blow it properly with.

[slowly] Muken looked up the road. Then he looked down at his empty hands, and at his sword standing up out of the dead thing in the snow, and began, very slowly, to get to his feet.

---

# Interlude · The Verdict of Six

[narrator] *The Moto Seat · After Gimbzo*

[narrator, slowly] There were seven chairs in the Hall of Verdicts, and six of them were filled.

The hall was the oldest room in the Moto seat, and the seat was the oldest thing in the western shield, and there were scholars in the Holy Sea who argued that the hall was older than the Moto themselves and that the house had only moved into it, the way a hermit crab moves into a shell. It was round. Its walls were a dark gray stone so close-grained that it took a polish like iron, and they rose without a single window to a dome that was lost in shadow, and from somewhere up in that shadow, on a chain too long to see the end of, hung a great ring of black iron set with worked lamps in iron cages, each with a Guild seal stamped in brass on its collar, that burned a steady gold without smoke or smell. They drew from the great main in the rock under the hold, and the draw came up through the walls with it, a low hum felt in the teeth and the breastbone more than heard, so that the whole round room seemed to be holding one long note. The floor was the same stone as the walls, and the Guild had laid heated lines under it, so that even in the dead of the northern winter it was warm to kneel on. Cut into it, in a circle that ran just inside the line of the chairs, was a band of script no living scholar could read, deep and clean, as fresh as the day it was carved. Prisoners were made to kneel inside that circle. The house said that the script listened.

The seven chairs stood round the outside of it, facing in.

They were not thrones. The Moto had always been very careful about thrones. Each was a plain high-backed seat of black wood on a low stone dais, and each was dressed in the colors of its line, so that a man brought in to kneel at the center of the hall could turn his head and know, without a word spoken, exactly who was going to decide whether he lived.

Bright hard silver for the Shirogane. Copper and raw red-bronze for the Akagane. Unrelieved black and ash for the Kurenai, with the crimson kept to a single hidden thread. Near-white for the Byakuya, pale as a church. Gold and banner-heraldry for the Tenrai, the banner behind the chair worked so thick with gold thread that it threw the lamplight back like a shield. Indigo and mist-gray for the Amagiri, deep as a lake at dusk.

And red enamel over near-black steel, with a lion's head in a boss at the crest, for the Kōkan: a red so deep and live under the gold lamps that it looked wet.

[pause] The Kōkan chair was empty. The seventh chair had not been sat in since the war began. Gimbzo had ruled from a saddle and a tent and left it to the dust, so that every lord at the ring would look at it and know he could walk in any day and take it. Nobody had moved it in all that time, and nobody had ever had it taken out, and there was a fine gray skin of dust on its arms that the servants who swept the hall had been told, forty years ago, never to touch. The lion on its crest looked down at the center of the floor with blind enamel eyes. Under it, on the night of the verdict, Torvald Stark knelt in chains.

---

[narrator] He had been made to kneel, and he had not made it easy.

It had taken four of the Tenrai house-guard to put him down, and one of them was still in the corner with a cloth pressed to his face where Torvald's forehead had met it. They had put chains on his wrists and his ankles and a third round his waist that ran down to a ring set in the stone, inside the circle of script, and the chains were worked steel with Shirogane silver in the links, and he could feel the silver drinking at him through his skin like a leech. They had beaten him before they brought him in. They had beaten him the night before as well, and the night before that. His left eye was swollen shut. Two of his teeth were gone and a third was loose, and the blood from his mouth had run down into his beard and dried there and run again, so that the great rust-red beard of the Stark chief hung clotted and black on his chest.

He was a big man, even kneeling. Broad through the back and the shoulders the way the northern men were broad, from a lifetime of cutting wood and hauling it, with a neck like a bull's and forearms covered in old white scars where hounds and wolves and men had all at one time or another tried to take them off. His shirt had been torn half off him. Under it his skin was a map of bruises gone every color from yellow to black, and on his left side, low, the ribs moved wrong when he breathed. He was fifty years old. He had been leading the north against the Tenrai for three of them.

He lifted his head and looked up at the Moto.

Every child in the north was taught, before it was taught anything else, never to meet a Moto's eye. The Moto read through the eyes. A man who met them had opened his door. Torvald had been taught it as a boy at his grandmother's knee, and he had taught it to his own sons, and he lifted his one good eye now and looked straight into the faces of the six most powerful people in the Inner World, one after another, slowly, the way a man looks over horses at a fair.

Kagenori Tenrai sat in the gold chair. He was a lean man of middle years, narrow-faced and motionless as a heron, with gray at his temples and a mouth like a closed purse, and the Moto blood showed on him plainly: the faint metallic sheen at the brow and the temple, catching the lamplight like old brass, and the ring-lines in the iris that sharpened when he looked at something he meant to keep. He wore gold-trimmed black, and behind his chair hung the Tenrai banner, and he was the one who had called the council. Everyone in the hall knew he was the one who meant to keep it.

Shizuha Shirogane sat in silver at his right. She was older than him, and very upright, a small gray-haired woman with a face like a hatchet and silver at her collar and her wrists and in the pins in her hair, and she had not looked at the prisoner once since he was brought in. Tetsugen Akagane, in copper and red-bronze that glowed like a banked forge under the lamps, was the biggest of the six: thick-necked, heavy-handed, bald as a stone, with a jaw that worked continually as though he were chewing on something he did not like the taste of. Sumie Kurenai sat in black. She was perhaps forty, pale, thin as a rope, with her hair cut short at the jaw and ink-dark eyes that did not seem to blink, and her hands lay folded in her lap, and there was nothing of crimson about her anywhere that Torvald could see. Hayato Amagiri, in indigo and gray, was the youngest of them, a slight sharp man with restless fingers who sat a little forward in his chair and kept glancing, without seeming to know he did it, at the hall's single door.

And [softly] Kiyome Byakuya, in white.

She was the only one of them who looked as though she had not slept. She was perhaps thirty, tall and pale and very straight, in a robe so near to white that under the lamps it seemed to give off a faint light of its own, with a veil of fine glass beads pinned back from her face and a halo-ring of pale metal at the crown of her head. The sheen at her temples was silver, faint as frost. She was sitting forward in her chair, both hands on its arms, and she was the only one of the six who was looking at Torvald as if he were a man and not a problem.

Behind him, kneeling in a row along the edge of the circle, chained the same way, were the others.

---

[narrator] There were five of them.

Two were his own. Hakon and Sten, riders of his household, cousins to each other, taken with him at the ford when the Tenrai came over the hill. Hakon had lost an ear. Sten could no longer hold his head up and had to be propped against his cousin's shoulder, and his breathing was the bad wet kind that Torvald had heard before in men who did not live to see the next morning.

Beside them knelt a man in the remains of a gray surcoat with a wolf's head worked on the breast in darker thread. Aldwin Greymane. Younger brother of the Lord of the Ridgewall, and the man the Greymanes had sent north with forty riders a year ago to stand with the Starks when the Tenrai first began closing the passes. Thirty-eight of the forty were dead. He was forty-four, iron-haired, with the long gray Greymane face and a soldier's stillness, and he had been beaten as badly as Torvald and showed it less. He knelt with his back perfectly straight and his chained hands resting on his thighs, and he was looking at the empty Kōkan chair.

Next to him knelt Osbert Lambert, and Osbert did not look like a man who should have been in chains at all.

He was the tariff-master of the Hallenfeld river corridor. Fifty-odd, round-shouldered, soft in the belly, with thinning sandy hair and small square spectacles that were miraculously still on his face, one lens cracked across. He had ink on the second finger of his right hand that no amount of beating had got off. He was not a fighting man and he had never pretended to be one. What he had done was keep books. For eleven years he had sat at the Hallenfeld junction with his tally board and read the carts in against their manifests, and when the Tenrai began moving grain off the eastern routes and calling it the requisition of the realm, he had read those carts in too, and written down what was in them, and where they went, and who signed for them, and how much of the grain that left the corridor marked for the starving provinces arrived anywhere at all. Then he had made a fair copy of it and sent it north.

He had a son. The son was seven, and was at home in the corridor with his mother, and Osbert had not seen either of them in four months. He was thinking about the boy now; Torvald could see it on him, the way a man's face goes inward when he is trying to remember something exactly.

[pause] And last, at the end of the row, a Moto.

A common soldier of the Akagane house-guard, in copper-buckled harness with the buckles cut off. Young, perhaps twenty-five, square-faced, with the faint sheen of the blood at his wrists that every Moto foot-soldier had, and a look on his face of a man who still could not quite believe where he was kneeling. His name, Torvald had learned in the cells, was Ren. He had been ordered, three weeks before, to put the granaries at Tōmi to the torch, so that the village would have nothing left to give the Starks. He had refused. He had stood in front of the granary door with his spear held crossways and said that his grandmother lived in Tōmi.

Tetsugen Akagane had not looked at him once, either.

---

"[light Japanese accent, calm, warm, measured]We shouldn't be so hasty," Kiyome Byakuya said, "about this decision. We still haven't fully agreed."

[narrator] Her voice was clear and very calm, and it went round the hall and came back from the dome, and it was the first thing anyone had said since the prisoners were brought in.

"[light Japanese accent, calm, warm, firm]We have the testimony of the Tenrai captains," she went on, "and we have the Stark's own riders, and we have a confession that was got out of a man after four nights in a cell. We do not have the council's consent. Not all of it." She looked along the line of chairs. "There are seven lines and one blood. We have always said so. The verdict of this hall is the verdict of the house, or it's the verdict of a man in a gold chair, and I would like to know, before we shed a drop of anyone's, which of those this is going to be."

[narrator] Kagenori Tenrai turned his head and looked at her.

He did it slowly. The ring-lines in his eyes sharpened. When he spoke his voice was perfectly pleasant, and very soft, and it carried to every corner of the hall without his raising it at all.

"[light Japanese accent, smooth, soft, pleasant]Do you think," he said, "that we can sit here delegating, Lady Byakuya, while they seek to poison our people with lies?"

[narrator] He did not wait for her to answer.

"[light Japanese accent, smooth, reasonable]The Stark has been riding the northern provinces for three years with a story," he said. "That the house is starving its own. That the grain the house takes in requisition goes into the house's own granaries and never comes out. That the house could feed every child between the Thresh and the shield if it chose to and has simply decided not to." He spread his hands, very slightly. "It is a good story. It is the kind of story that makes a man pick up a scythe and walk toward a wall. There are eleven thousand people in the north who have picked up scythes because of it. Every day we spend discussing whether it is lawful to stop the man telling it, another hundred of them join him."

"[light Japanese accent, calm, warm, quietly]Some of the story is true," Kiyome said.

[narrator] [long pause] The hall went quiet around the hum.

Kagenori did not look away from her. "[light Japanese accent, smooth, soft]All the best lies are," he said.

[narrator] [grunts] Across the circle, Tetsugen Akagane grunted, a heavy sound low in the chest, and shifted his great weight in the copper chair.

"[light Japanese accent, flat, iron, deep, grudging]I agree," he said.

[narrator] They all looked at him. He did not seem to enjoy it.

"[light Japanese accent, flat, iron, deep, blunt]I don't like you, Kagenori," he said. "I want that said, here, where the script can hear it. I've never liked you, I don't like what you've done with the roads, and I don't like how quick you are to put the word *house* in front of whatever it is you happen to want." He rubbed his jaw with one enormous copper-ringed hand. "But you're right about this. He's right," he said, to Kiyome. "We can't let them think they've got the upper hand. Not now, with Gimbzo dead and every line in the house looking at the others to see who flinches first. We let the Stark walk out of here, or we let him sit in a cell for a year while we argue about it, and every man with a scythe in the north will know we were afraid to finish it."

[narrator] He did not look at the soldier at the end of the row as he said it. He very carefully did not look.

---

[laughs] Torvald laughed.

[narrator] It hurt. It hurt badly, the broken ribs grinding on his left side, and the laugh came out wet [coughs] and turned into a cough halfway through, and he bent over his chained hands and spat a mouthful of blood onto the band of script in the floor, very deliberately, right in the middle of a word.

[narrator] Several of the house-guard took a step forward. Kagenori lifted one finger and they stopped.

Torvald raised his head again. Blood was running from the corner of his mouth into his beard.

"[Yorkshire accent, deep, hoarse, mocking]You," he said, and looked at Kiyome with his one good eye. His voice was hoarse and thick and very northern, the vowels flattened out like a hammered nail. "You're the only one in here with any sense, lass, and you're still too shallow to see it." He jerked his chin at the five other chairs. "These fucking idiots made up their minds two weeks ago. Before they ever caught me. Before the ford. They had the post set in the yard before I ever saw your walls." He grinned at her, a ruin of a grin, with the gaps in it. "You're not arguing for my life. You're arguing for the right to say you argued."

[narrator] Kiyome did not answer him. But her hands had tightened on the arms of the white chair.

At the end of the line of seats, Hayato Amagiri leaned forward, his restless fingers going still for once, and looked down at the Stark with something on his narrow face that was very nearly regret.

"[light Japanese accent, quick, wry, quietly, regretful]And to think," he said quietly, "that I once counted you among the few men in this capital with any honor."

[narrator] Torvald turned his head to him.

"[Yorkshire accent, deep, hoarse, scornful]Honor," he said.

[narrator] [chuckles] He laughed again, softer this time, and it did not turn into a cough.

"[Yorkshire accent, deep, hoarse, bitter, amused]You think my life's some precious thing to me?" he said. "You think I came south to bargain for it?" He shook his head slowly, and the chains rattled on his wrists. "I'm fifty years old, Amagiri. I've buried a wife and two sons and every brother I had but the one, and he'll bury me, and then he'll come for you. I've buried more of my own riders in three years than you've ever had in your house-guard. My life's an old coat. I'd have thrown it in a ditch a long time ago if there'd been anybody else to wear it."

[narrator] He looked round the circle of chairs.

"[Yorkshire accent, deep, hoarse, fierce]And you think me dying here will change the truth?" he said. "You think you put me on that post in your yard, and the grain comes back out of your granaries by itself? The children in Tōmi stop being hungry because the man who told them why they were hungry is dead?" He spat again, onto the script. "The truth doesn't care what you do to me. It was true before I said it. It'll be true when I'm in the ground. You can kill the man who carries the lamp. The thing he was carrying it toward is still standing there, plain as day, for anybody else who walks by."

[narrator] Kagenori said nothing. His face had not changed at all.

[narrator] Torvald leaned forward against the chain at his waist, as far as it would let him.

"[Yorkshire accent, deep, hoarse, low, savoring]And here's another truth for you, while I've breath to give it," he said. "It's only a matter of time. Only a matter of time before the world finds out." He let it hang there. "[pause]That there's two left. Two children of the Kōkan line, still breathing, somewhere you haven't looked."

---

[narrator] [long pause] The hall changed.

Torvald felt it. Every man and woman kneeling in the circle felt it. The lamps in the great iron ring overhead held steady, and yet their light seemed to draw in, all at once, a little closer to their cages, as if something enormous had taken a breath, and the draw-hum in the walls dropped half a tone. The warm floor stayed warm. The script cut into it under his knees grew cold, the way iron is cold at thirty below, so that it burned through the cloth at his knees and into the bone. The silver in his chains bit harder.

Six heads had turned toward him. Six pairs of eyes were looking at him, and in every one of them the ring-lines had sharpened, and in some of them the iris had begun to break up into fine patterns like frost on a pane, shards and lines and turning rings of light, and Torvald understood that six of the oldest bloodlines in the world were reading him at once. He felt it like hands. Like six sets of fingers going through him, through his face and his chest and the back of his skull, turning him over, looking for the lie.

[narrator] He let them look. He grinned at them while they did it.

[pause] Sumie Kurenai had gone white.

She was the only one of them who had moved. She had not stood up, or spoken, or so much as lifted a hand from her lap. But she had gone white to the lips, and the ink-dark eyes in her thin face had gone wide, and she was not looking at Torvald any longer. She was looking at the empty Kōkan chair.
