Torvald saw it. Aldwin Greymane, kneeling three places down, saw it too. [sharp inhale] Torvald heard the Greymane's breath go in, very softly, through his nose.

"[light Japanese accent, smooth, cold]Where," Kagenori said.

[narrator] It was the first time his voice had risen. It was not much. It was enough.

"[light Japanese accent, smooth, rising]Where are they?"

"[Yorkshire accent, deep, hoarse, grinning]Go and look," Torvald said.

"[light Japanese accent, smooth, shouts]*Where.*"

"[Yorkshire accent, deep, hoarse, amused, grunting]I don't know." Torvald shrugged, as far as the chains would let him, and the broken ribs ground together and he grunted and kept grinning. "That's the beauty of it. I don't know, and you can read me inside out all night and you'll find I don't know. I heard it the way you hear anything in the north. From a man who heard it from a woman who heard it from somebody who was there." He tilted his head. "But I know it's true. You know it's true. Look at her." He nodded at Sumie Kurenai. "*She* knows it's true. She's the one whose line was meant to make sure it couldn't be."

[narrator] Every head in the hall turned to the black chair.

Sumie Kurenai did not speak. She drew a breath, slowly, and her hands in her lap closed into fists, and the color did not come back into her face.

"[elderly woman, light Japanese accent, cold, precise]Kurenai," said Shizuha Shirogane.

[narrator] It was the first word the old woman had spoken. Her voice was dry and thin and sharp as a needle.

"[elderly woman, light Japanese accent, cold, precise, accusing]You told this council," she said, "that the register was closed. You told us the line was ended. You told us there was nothing left of them but a chair." She did not look at the chair. "You signed for it."

"[light Japanese accent, low, dark, low, careful]I signed for what I was shown," Sumie Kurenai said. Her voice was low. "The register was closed. Every name that could be read was read out of it and every name that was read was ended. That is the truth. I would swear it before the script." She looked up, and her ink-dark eyes found the Shirogane's. "But there were marks in that register, Shizuha, that could not be read, by me or by anyone. Names written in a hand the instrument would not take." [swallows] She swallowed. "We closed the book on what we could see. What we couldn't see, we called nothing. Because it gave us no reading. Because a thing that gives no reading is a thing that isn't there."

[narrator] Torvald had spent half his life at fords and toll-gates listening to men explain themselves to him, and he knew the shape of what she had just done. Every word of it was true. She had laid them down one after another, carefully, the way a man lays flat stones over the mouth of a well, so that you could walk across them all day and never once hear the water underneath.

The six chairs held their silence, silver and copper and gold, black and white and indigo, round the red one.

[creaking] Up in the dark of the dome, very faintly, something in the great iron ring creaked on its chain.

---

[narrator] [laughs uncontrollably] Torvald began to laugh again, and this time he could not stop it.

It went on a long time. It went on until the tears ran out of his good eye and down into his beard with the blood, and the broken ribs ground, and he had to bend almost double over his chains to get his breath. The house-guard shifted. Kagenori did not lift his finger this time. He sat in his gold chair and waited, with his mouth closed like a purse, until it was done.

"[Yorkshire accent, deep, hoarse, wheezing, laughing]Oh," Torvald said at last, wheezing. "Oh, Sky above. Look at you. Look at all of you." He wiped his mouth on his bound wrist. "The keepers of fate. The house that reads the world. And you couldn't read two names in your own book."

[narrator] He straightened up on his knees.

"[Yorkshire accent, deep, hoarse, quiet, steady]And you think," he said, and his voice had gone very quiet, and very steady, and there was no laughter left in it at all, "that by killing me you'll stop my family going to war."

[narrator] He looked at Kagenori.

"[Yorkshire accent, deep, hoarse, steady, defiant]My sons are dead," he said. "But I've a brother living, and he's had the wolf off me since before ever I rode for Kessen, sealed and witnessed in his own hall at Hollenmark, so you can take my head tonight and the north won't miss a step for it. He's older than me, and slower to start, and I've never once in my life seen him stop. I've nephews. I've a brother's widow at the Ironwood March who'll put a spear through you herself if she ever gets close enough. I've eleven thousand people with scythes, and every one of them is going to hear what was done in this room tonight, because one of your own guards is going to tell it in a tavern within a month. They always do." He nodded at Aldwin Greymane, beside him. "The Greymanes have already lost thirty-eight men for me. You think the Ridgewall's going to sit quiet when the Lord's brother doesn't come home?"

[narrator] He leaned forward again, against the chain.

"[Yorkshire accent, deep, hoarse, fierce, low]Here's the thing about it," he said. "Here's the thing none of you have ever understood about the north. You think I'd want to live with my honor clean. I don't. I'd trade my honor for a few more years of war. I'd trade it in a heartbeat. A few more years of war, and death, and burning, and every one of you sitting up at night in your chairs listening for a scythe at the door." He grinned again. "That's what I came south for. Not to live. To make it cost."

[narrator] The lamps burned in the great iron ring. The script lay cold in the floor.

"[Yorkshire accent, deep, hoarse, almost gently]It was good," Torvald Stark said, almost gently, "to see Gimbzo finally meet his end." He let his good eye travel slowly round the six chairs, and come to rest, at last, on the seventh. On the red enamel. On the lion's head in the boss, looking down at him with its blind eyes.

"[Yorkshire accent, deep, hoarse, slowly]Long live the king," he said.

---

[narrator] [long pause] There was a silence after that which nobody in the hall would ever forget.

It was Kiyome Byakuya who broke it. She did not speak. She simply stood up, all at once, in a rustle of white silk and a faint clicking of the glass beads at her temple, and stood there in front of her chair with her hands at her sides.

"[light Japanese accent, calm, warm, trembling, resolute]I won't sign it," she said.

"[elderly woman, light Japanese accent, cold, precise, cold]Sit down, Kiyome," said Shizuha Shirogane.

"[light Japanese accent, calm, warm, shaking, passionate]I won't sign it. Not tonight, with what's just been said in this room." Her voice shook, very slightly, for the first time. "We've just been told that the Kōkan line is not ended. That this council was told it was ended on a reading that couldn't read. And the first thing we mean to do, the very first thing, is kill the only man in the Inner World who's willing to tell us so to our faces."

"[light Japanese accent, smooth, softly]He told us nothing," Kagenori said softly. "He told us a rumor he heard from a man who heard it from a woman."

"[light Japanese accent, calm, warm, sharp]He told us enough to turn Sumie white."

[narrator] Kagenori let the hum of the hall fill the space where his answer should have been. Then he turned his head, without hurry, and looked at the other four chairs.

"[light Japanese accent, smooth, formal]Shirogane," he said.

[narrator] The old woman did not hesitate. "[elderly woman, light Japanese accent, cold, precise, immediately]Yes."

"[light Japanese accent, smooth]Akagane."

[narrator] Tetsugen's jaw worked. He looked down at his own copper-ringed hands. At the end of the row of prisoners the young soldier Ren had lifted his head and was looking at him, straight at him, for the first time since they had brought him in. Tetsugen did not look back.

"[light Japanese accent, flat, iron, deep, heavy]Yes," he said heavily.

"[light Japanese accent, smooth]Amagiri."

[narrator] Hayato Amagiri's fingers had begun moving again on the arm of his chair. He glanced, one more time, at the hall's single door. Then he looked at Torvald, kneeling in the circle with blood in his beard and the grin still on his face, and something passed across the young lord's narrow features that might have been shame.

"[light Japanese accent, quick, wry, ashamed]Yes," he said. "The seven lines forgive us. Yes."

"[light Japanese accent, smooth]Kurenai."

[narrator] Sumie Kurenai did not answer at once. She was still looking at the empty chair. When she turned back at last, her face had closed up again, smooth and pale as paper, and only her eyes were wrong in it.

"[light Japanese accent, low, dark, cold, controlled]It is my line's office," she said. "It will be done by my line's hand." She rose. "Yes."

"[light Japanese accent, smooth, final]Five," said Kagenori Tenrai. He did not look at Kiyome. "The house has spoken."

---

[narrator] Sumie Kurenai came down from the black chair and across the stone and into the circle of script, and the script did not grow cold for her. She walked the way the Kurenai walked, without any sound at all. At the edge of the circle a guard held out to her a blade on a cloth of black silk, and she took it without looking: a long, plain, single-edged sword with a black grip and no guard at all, and when it came out of its scabbard the lamplight ran down the edge and found, for the first time, the crimson. A single fine line of it, inlaid down the length of the spine of the blade, red as a vein.

[quietly] Torvald watched her come.

"[Yorkshire accent, deep, hoarse, calm]Kurenai," he said. "You'll do it yourself, then."

"[light Japanese accent, low, dark, flat]It is my office."

"[Yorkshire accent, deep, hoarse, gruff, satisfied]Good." He nodded. "Better you than him." He jerked his chin at the gold chair. "I'd not give him the satisfaction."

[narrator] He shifted on his knees, and straightened his back as well as the ribs would let him, and lowered his head. His great clotted beard fell forward onto his chest. The back of his neck was broad and brown and scarred, and there was a little gray in the hair at the nape.

Behind him, along the edge of the circle, Hakon Stark had begun to say something in the old northern tongue, low and fast, over and over. Sten could not lift his head to see. Aldwin Greymane had not moved at all. He knelt straight-backed with his hands on his thighs and his long gray face turned toward Torvald, and his eyes were open, and he did not look away. Next to him Osbert Lambert had shut his. His lips were moving too. Not in a prayer. Torvald knew it, because he had heard it in the cells three nights running, through the wall: the man counted. Under his breath, steadily, numbers. Carts. Sacks. Grain by the bushel and by the stone. It was what he did when he could not bear a thing. He was counting the requisitions out of the Hallenfeld corridor for the year, one cart at a time, from the first day of spring.

[slowly] Sumie Kurenai set her feet.

"[light Japanese accent, low, dark, formal, resonant]Torvald Stark," she said, in the formal voice, the voice of her line's office, that went up into the dome and came back down out of it doubled. "For sedition against the seven lines and the one blood. For raising the commons of the north in arms. For the deaths of four hundred and twelve men of the Tenrai house-guard and the burning of the Tenrai granaries at Kessen ford. The house has spoken, and the house's word is the house's hand."

"[Yorkshire accent, deep, hoarse, muttering]Sky take your house," said Torvald Stark, into his beard.

[narrator] [pause] She cut.

It was a clean cut. The Kurenai had been doing it for longer than there had been a Stark in the north, and they had made an art of it the way other lines made arts of reading or of war. The long blade came down in a single flat arc, and there was a sound like a heavy cleaver going through a joint on a butcher's block, and the edge passed through the back of Torvald's neck between the bones of the spine and on out through the throat, and stopped a finger's width above the stone. His head came off and struck the floor and rolled, once, and came to rest face up on the band of script, with the one good eye still open and the grin still on the ruined mouth. The body stayed kneeling for a heartbeat longer. Then the blood came out of it in a great pulsing rope, bright under the gold lamps as the enamel on the empty chair, and the knees went, and it fell forward onto its chest across the circle, and the chains rang on the stone.

[softly] The blood ran out across the script. It filled the cut letters one by one, the way water fills a channel, and ran on round the circle in a slow red ring.

[screams] Hakon Stark screamed.

[narrator, quietly] Nobody else made a sound. Aldwin Greymane had not looked away. Osbert Lambert had opened his eyes, and was looking at the head on the floor, and his lips had stopped moving, and he had lost his count.

[narrator] Sumie Kurenai lowered the blade. There was a single drop of blood on the crimson line down its spine, and it ran, very slowly, down the inlay, and did not stain it, and fell off the point onto the floor.

She did not look at the head. She was looking past it, at the seventh chair, the way she had looked at it when the Stark first said *two*, and her face had gone smooth again over whatever was under it. She had been twenty-three the night of the Kōkan cellars, and new to her line's office, and she had stood with the register open in her hands while the names were brought to her and ended one by one. She had known since that night, as Shigure Tenrai knew, and the man in the gold chair, and a handful of Tenrai captains, and nobody else at the ring was meant to, that one had not been brought to her. A woman of the white line had walked up out of the cellars on her own feet with a newborn held inside her coat, and nobody had stopped her, and Sumie Kurenai had written nothing down and signed for a closed book. Sixteen years she had kept it closed on the reasoning she had just given the council, that a thing which gave no reading was a thing that was not there. One child, nameless, gone out into the cold like a thousand others. The Stark had said two.

[narrator] Up on the dais, Kiyome Byakuya was still standing in front of her white chair. She had not sat down. She had watched the whole of it. Her hands were shaking at her sides, and the glass beads at her temple were shaking with them, a very faint clicking, like rain on a window.

[narrator] Kagenori Tenrai rose from his gold chair.

"[light Japanese accent, smooth, cold, precise]The Greymane goes back to the Ridgewall," he said, "with his brother's ransom set at the weight of the man in salt. The Lambert goes to the cells until his ledger is found and burned. The Stark riders go on the post in the yard at dawn." He did not look at the end of the row. "The Akagane soldier is Lord Tetsugen's to dispose of. Lord Tetsugen will dispose of him."

[narrator] Tetsugen Akagane said nothing. [weeping quietly] At the end of the row, young Ren had begun, very quietly, to weep.

[narrator] Kagenori turned to go. Then he paused, and looked back past the prisoners and the body to the lion on the crest of the seventh chair.

"[light Japanese accent, smooth, soft, intense]And find them," he said. "Whatever it costs. Find out who heard it from whom, and who heard it from them, back to the woman, back to whoever was there. Kurenai. Amagiri. Every road, every register, every salt pan and mine and bonded house from here to the end of the world." His voice had gone very soft again. "Two children. Find them before anyone else does."

[narrator] He walked out. After a moment, one by one, the others followed him.

[softly] Kiyome Byakuya went last. At the edge of the dais she stopped, and turned, and came back down the steps, and walked across the stone to the edge of the circle, where the blood had not yet reached. She knelt there in her white robe. Very carefully, so that nobody watching could have said what she was doing, she reached out and closed Torvald Stark's one good eye.

Then she stood, and went out, and did not look back at the chair.

[narrator] Nobody noticed, when the doors had shut and the servants had turned the worked lamps down to a red ember-glow in their cages, that Osbert Lambert had begun to count again.

He was not counting carts this time. His lips were moving slowly, over two words, the same two, again and again, the way a man goes over a figure he means to carry home and set down in a ledger somewhere it cannot be burned.

*[clipped Received Pronunciation, whispers]Two children.*

---

# Chapter Three: The Tokenless

[narrator] *Book One of the Kōkan · Chapter Three: The Tokenless*

[narrator, tired] They lost five men at the head of the column before the horn stopped blowing, and three more on the Marrow crossing two days after that, when the ice under the lead wagon gave without a sound and took a team, two riders and the young driver everyone called Pell's Nephew down into the black water together. They never found any of them. On the ninth day the road came out of the timber at last onto a long bare shoulder of hill above a river. The sky over it was a hard clean blue, the first open sky in days. The river ran black-green between shelves of ice that the sun turned to blue glass at their edges, and smoke went straight up from the far bank in a single thread, and at the bottom of the hill, behind a stockade of split logs gone gray with weather, stood the Vessant post. There was no draw out here and never had been. The lamps in its windows were oil, and the yellow of them was the warmest color for a hundred miles.

[narrator] Eleven wagons had left the Halcrest gate. Nine came in. Muken rode beside the lead team with his cheek stitched shut in a long black seam from the jaw to the corner of his eye, and his sister's stitches pulling every time he talked, and he did not talk much.

The factor at the post counted the wagons in against his manifest twice. Then he counted the men, which took less time. Then he paid, in stamped Vessant bars, full weight, on a trestle by the gate, and every bar was there to the last, and the death-price for the eight who had not come back was there too, in a separate stack, sealed in oiled cloth and labeled in a neat clerk's hand with the names Muken gave him. He did not haggle. He did not ask a single question about the road. He looked at Muken's face, at the seam in it, and then he looked away and wrote something down in his book that Muken could not read upside down.

[pause] That was the first thing that was wrong.

[narrator] The second was the road itself. Muken had walked it again in his head every night since the granite shoulder, lying awake under the wagon with his face throbbing, and he could not make it come out right. Draycott had given him a route on the morning they left the gate, drawn on a slip of paper in the agent's own hand, and he had followed it, because that was what the paper was for. It had taken them through every narrow place in the timber. Every ford with no bridge. Every pass where the sightings on Yukazuri's map lay thickest. There had been a shorter road, two days shorter, along the river valley to the south, with a Vessant way-station on it and a garrison at the halfway mark. He had found it on the map on the fourth night and asked the drivers, and the drivers had looked at each other and said the house had told them it was closed.

It was not closed. They had passed the end of it on the sixth morning. There was fresh dung on it.

Somebody had wanted this convoy to take the worst road in the north. Somebody had paid twice the going rate for an escort to take it, and paid the death-price without blinking, and put Muken's name on the contract in particular, when there were three bigger companies in Halcrest that would have taken the work for half.

He did not know who. He did not know why. He knew what it felt like, though. It felt like the first time the Orrel steward had made him carry a full load up four galleries and stood at the top with his watch in his hand.

[slowly] It felt like a test.

---

[narrator] Rob Stark found him at the water trough by the stockade gate, with his coat off and his sleeves pushed up, washing the last of the road off his forearms.

The big northerner was limping. The bite above his knee had gone bad on the fifth day, the way bites did, hot and swollen and stinking under the bandage, and Yukazuri had opened it again on a wagon tailboard and cleaned it out with spirit while Rob bit down on a strap and did not make a sound. He walked now with a stick cut from a spruce sapling. Mark was riding in the last wagon with his arm splinted to the shoulder and a fever that had broken only that morning. Neither of them had complained. The Starks never did.

"[Yorkshire accent, cheerful, hoarse, quiet]Sarge," Rob said.

[narrator] Muken looked up. Water was running off his elbows into the snow.

"[Yorkshire accent, cheerful, hoarse, guarded]There's a man wants to see you," Rob said. "Inside. In the factor's hall." He shifted his weight off the bad leg. "Just you."

[narrator] Muken straightened slowly. He reached for his coat and shook it out, and as he did it he glanced, without turning his head, along the stockade to where the company's wagons were drawn up in a row, and his sister was sitting on the tailboard of the last one beside Mark Stark, with her kit open on her knees, changing the dressing on the boy's arm. She was not looking his way. He saw her hands stop moving for a moment, then start again.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, wary]What man," Muken said.

[narrator] Rob met his eyes. His face did what Stark faces did, which was close up like a door. "[Yorkshire accent, cheerful, hoarse, closed]He'll tell you that himself."

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, suspicious]He one of yours?"

"[Yorkshire accent, cheerful, hoarse, proud, quiet]He's all of ours," said Rob Stark.

[narrator] He turned and limped off toward the factor's hall without waiting to see whether Muken followed. After a moment, Muken put his coat on, settled his sword-belt, and did.

---

[narrator] The factor's hall was the biggest building in the post: a long, low log house with a turf roof and a smoke-hole, and one end of it partitioned off with a wall of planed boards and a door with a real iron lock. Rob led him through the outer room, where a half-dozen Vessant clerks and teamsters were eating at a long table and fell quiet as they passed, and opened the door in the partition, and stood aside.

Muken went in. Behind him, he heard the door close. He heard the key turn in the lock from the outside.

It was a small room and very warm. There was a stone fireplace at the far end with a fire of whole split logs going in it, and the heat came off it in a wall, and the only light was the fire's, gold on the planed boards and red in the iron of the lock and deep amber in the wolfhound's coat. It smelled of woodsmoke and old leather and wet dog. A wolfhound the size of a pony lay asleep on the hearthstone with its legs twitching. Beside it, in a great high-backed chair that had certainly not been made for a factor's hall, a chair of dark oak and iron and old wolf-pelt that looked as if it had been hauled down from some hall in the north on the back of a cart for this one meeting, sat a man.

He was big. He was the kind of big that Rob Stark was going to be in twenty years, if Rob Stark lived that long: broad as a doorway, with a belly gone heavy under the mail and the arms gone heavier, and a great beard that had been rust-red once and was now salt-and-rust, braided at the chin with a ring of black iron. His hair was cut short, almost to the scalp, and the scalp was scarred white in three places. His left hand rested on the arm of the chair, and two fingers were missing from it, the small and the ring, cut away clean at the knuckle a long time ago. His face was broad and weathered and red with the fire, and his eyes in it were pale, northern blue, and very much awake.

He wore no crown. He sat in the chair the way a mountain sits on a plain.

[narrator] Muken knew who it was before the man opened his mouth. He did not know how he knew. Everyone north of the salt basins knew the name, the way you knew the name of a winter that had killed your grandfather's cattle. He had just never imagined he would be in a room with it.

*[Northern English accent, low and rough, inner voice, hushed]Ulf Stark.* [narrator] Lord of Hollenmark. Master of the eastern March. One of the Seven Warlords of the north, and by most counts the worst of them to have against you.

"[Yorkshire accent, gruff and warm, amused]Ah," said Ulf Stark. "So there he is."

[narrator] His voice was deep and rough and amused, and the vowels in it were flattened out like hammered nails, the way the Starks talked. He looked Muken up and down, slowly, from his boots to the seam in his face, and back again.

"[Yorkshire accent, gruff and warm, appraising]Muken," he said. "The Tokenless."

[narrator] Muken bowed his head. It was not a good bow. He had never been taught one, and it came out the way the Lanternless bowed to a paymaster: short, stiff, a little sullen at the neck. But he held it, and he meant it, which was more than he could have said for most of the bows he had given in his life.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, stiff, formal]You called for me, Lord Stark?"

[laughs heartily] Ulf laughed.

[narrator] It was a big laugh, from the belly, and the wolfhound on the hearth woke at the sound of it and lifted its head and looked round and lay down again.

"[Yorkshire accent, gruff and warm, laughing]*Lord Stark*," Ulf said. "Listen to him. I heard you weren't half so formal with the last man I sent you." He was grinning. "I heard you told my representative in Halcrest that the keeper of a tea-house was a liar, and your captain was a liar, and you'd go anywhere for enough money, and then you doubled his price and made him shake your hand on it because you hadn't a token for him to seal to." He wiped his eye with the back of his maimed hand. "Draycott's not been the same since. He writes me letters about you. Long ones."

[narrator] Muken kept his face shut and his hands at his sides.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, flat]Draycott," he said. "Draycott's Vessant."

"[Yorkshire accent, gruff and warm]Draycott's Vessant," Ulf agreed. "And Vessant's oldest contract is a supply line into the eastern timber that runs, every mile of it, through Stark country. Thirty-one years that contract's been sound. You know why?" He did not wait. "Because every one of those thirty-one years, the Vessant house has paid Hollenmark a tithe for the road. And because every one of those years, when the house of Stark has wanted a thing done quiet in the south, the house of Vessant has done it. That's what a contract is, lad. It's not the paper. It's the thing both sides know that nobody wrote down."

[narrator] [fire crackling] The fire cracked. A log settled.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, slowly]The road," Muken said slowly. "You picked the road."

[narrator] Ulf's grin widened.

"[Yorkshire accent, gruff and warm, grinning]I picked the road."

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, cold, controlled]Three of my men are at the bottom of the Marrow. Five more are on the granite shoulder under a cairn. Rob Stark's going to walk with a stick till the spring." Muken did not raise his voice, and found, a little to his own surprise, that the room carried it anyway. "You picked the worst road in the north for us on purpose."

"[Yorkshire accent, gruff and warm, unapologetic]I did." Ulf did not look away. "And you brought nine wagons out of eleven down it, and every man but eight, and both my nephews, alive. Through the thickest country on that pretty Zettari map your sister bought off a scribe in Ironlink." He let that land. "Yes, I know about the map. I know about the room over the tea-house. I know you bought her number out of the Orrel drainage book with your first season's pay and counted it twice. I know a great many things about you, Muken. I've been knowing them for some time."

[narrator] He leaned back in the great chair and looked at Muken over his steepled fingers, the whole and the maimed.

"[Yorkshire accent, gruff and warm, calling out]Now," he said. "Rob."

[narrator] Muken heard the key in the lock behind him turn again, and the door open a hand's width.

"[Yorkshire accent, gruff and warm, commanding]Go and stand outside, lad," Ulf said, past him. "Outside the hall, not outside the door. If anybody comes within ten paces of this wall, I want to hear about it before they do."

"[Yorkshire accent, cheerful, hoarse]Aye," said Rob, behind him.

[narrator] The door closed. The key turned. Muken heard the stick and the uneven step go away through the outer room, and the outer door bang, and then nothing but the fire.

---

"[Yorkshire accent, gruff and warm]Sit," said Ulf Stark.

[narrator] There was a stool by the hearth. Muken looked at it, and then at the man in the chair, and then, after a moment, he sat. The wolfhound lifted its head and looked at him with yellow eyes and sniffed once, delicately, at his knee, and put its head down again on its paws.

"[Yorkshire accent, gruff and warm, wry]She likes you," Ulf said. "She doesn't like anyone. Bit a Tenrai tax-collector's hand off at the wrist last winter. Well. Most of it."

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, blunt]You didn't bring me in here to talk about your dog."

"[Yorkshire accent, gruff and warm, tired, serious]No." Ulf's grin went away. Without it his face looked older, and very tired, and harder than any face Muken had ever seen. "No, I didn't."

[narrator] He was quiet for a moment, looking into the fire.

"[Yorkshire accent, gruff and warm, serious]You've proven yourself," he said. "I'll give you that plainly, because you've earned it plainly, and because I'm too old to dance. Any company in Halcrest could have taken that convoy. Most of them would have lost it. A few would have brought it through and lost two men in three doing it, and called it a good season. You lost eight, and you kept your column together the whole way down, and you killed two of the big ones yourself with a scrap-iron sword and a cut-down reinforcement a first-year apprentice would be ashamed of." He looked at Muken sideways. "Oh, yes. Rob told me. He told me about the knee. He's never in his life told me about anyone."

[narrator] Muken said nothing.

"[Yorkshire accent, gruff and warm]So I'll tell you why you're here," Ulf said. "I want to recruit you."

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, disbelieving]Recruit me."

"[Yorkshire accent, gruff and warm, grave]Into my service. Into the north's service." He turned the iron ring on his beard-braid with his thumb. "There's a threat coming down on us, Muken. A bigger one than anything you've seen on that road. Bigger than anything the north saw in forty years of the war, and the war's only a month done."

[narrator] Muken studied him across the hearth.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, flat]Lord Stark," he said. "I'm a nobody."

[narrator] He said it flatly, without any bitterness in it, the way he would have said the sky was gray. It was simply the truth, and he had been carrying it his whole life, and it was as familiar to him as the weight of his own sword.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, flat, matter-of-fact]I'm a commoner. Less than. I'm tokenless. In the chain-cities a tokenless man can't sign for a sack of meal. I was born in a cutting gallery under Halcrest and I've been selling my arm to whoever pays since I was fourteen." He spread his hands, the cracked white knuckles, the scar across the back of the right one. "I've got a sister, a sword three smiths wouldn't price, and a reputation for not dying. What in the name of the Sky can a man like me do for one of the Seven?"

[narrator] Ulf shook his head slowly.

"[Yorkshire accent, gruff and warm, earnest]No common man," he said, "brings a column down that road. Let alone a boy of sixteen with his little sister in the carriage. No common man looks at the map on the fourth night and sees the river road and asks the drivers why it's closed. He leaned forward in the great chair, and the oak creaked under him. "You don't know what you are, lad. You've never had anyone to tell you. You've got the making of a general in you. A commander of men. I've seen two in my life with what you've got, and one of them's dead and the other's sitting in this chair."

[narrator] The fire burned. [sighs] The wolfhound sighed in its sleep.

"[Yorkshire accent, gruff and warm, low, rough]I want you in my army," Ulf said. "I want you to join the fight. To protect the north. The people in it. The ones who can't fill their own bowl." His voice had gone low and rough. "And to make sure that whatever the south thinks it's going to take control of up here, it doesn't."

[narrator] Muken sat on the stool and did not answer.

"[Yorkshire accent, gruff and warm, grim]You'll be wondering," Ulf went on, "why all this. The locked door. Rob in the yard. The dog." [dry laugh] He made a short sound that was nearly a laugh and was not. "It's because if anyone outside this room ever learned what I've just said to you, lad, the Moto lines would have you dead within the month. You and your sister both. They'd not even trouble to make it look like an accident." He paused. "[pause, heavy]This very thing I'm talking to you about has already cost me my brother."

[narrator] Muken's head came up.

"[Yorkshire accent, gruff and warm, heavy]Torvald," Ulf said. "You'll have heard."

[narrator] He had. Everyone had. It had come down the road ahead of the convoy, the way news did in the north, faster than horses: in a tavern at the Marrow crossing, from a teamster who had it from a courier who had it from a man who had been in the Moto seat on the night. The Stark chief, taken at Kessen ford. Brought before the council of six in the Hall of Verdicts. Beheaded on the floor of it, by the Kurenai's own hand, with his blood running round the old script in a ring.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, slowly, quiet]They say he laughed at them," Muken said slowly. "Right at the end. They say he said something that turned them all white, and then he laughed."

"[Yorkshire accent, gruff and warm, grief-stricken, steady]He'd have laughed," Ulf said. "He always did, when it was going badly." He looked into the fire. "They gave the Greymane back to the Ridgewall for his weight in salt. They put the Hallenfeld tariff-man in the cells for his ledger. They hanged my brother's riders on the post in the yard at dawn, both of them, my own cousins' boys, and then they sent me my brother's head in a cask of brine with a note in Kagenori Tenrai's own hand." He did not move. "It said: *[bitter]The north is reminded.*"

[narrator] The log in the fire broke in two with a soft crack and fell in on itself in a burst of sparks.

[quietly] Muken sat down further on the stool. He had not meant to. His legs had simply stopped holding him up quite so straight.

He had known it was bad. Everybody in the north knew it was bad. But there was knowing a thing the way you knew there was weather in the next valley, and there was sitting in a hot room with a man whose brother's head had come to him in a cask, and they were not the same.

---

"[Yorkshire accent, gruff and warm, flat, steady]Here's what's coming," Ulf said.

[narrator] He had pulled himself back from wherever he had gone, and his voice was steady again, and flat, the voice of a man reading out the strength of an enemy.

"[Yorkshire accent, gruff and warm, grim]They're going to starve us," he said. "The Moto. That's the plan, and it's a good plan, I'll give them that. They don't need to march an army up here. They don't need to win a single battle. All they need is to stop the north getting the things it needs to live through a winter. Salt. Iron. Lamp oil. Medicine. Seed grain for the spring. Every one of those comes up out of the south on the roads, and the Moto have been closing the roads one at a time since the war went bad, four years back, and faster since Shimane. Two winters more of it and there'll be ten thousand dead in the north from hunger and the cold, and nobody'll have to swing a sword to do it." He let a breath out through his nose. "Only the Seven can stop it. The seven great families of the north. That's what we're for. That's what we've always been for."

"[Northern English accent, low and rough]Seven," Muken said.

"[Yorkshire accent, gruff and warm, odd, guarded]Seven." Ulf looked at him oddly for a moment, and something moved behind the pale northern eyes and was gone. "We've always said seven. "[Yorkshire accent, gruff and warm, low]Two months from now," he said, "Kagenori Tenrai Moto means to lay martial law on the Kessen road."

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, quiet, realising]That's the last road," he said.

"[Yorkshire accent, gruff and warm, grim]That's the last road." Ulf nodded. "Everything the north still gets from the east comes up it. The Mahuo's salt. The Yukari's medicine. The Sum-gol iron. His mouth thinned. "Kagenori's not stupid. He's choking two birds with one hand."

"[Northern English accent, low and rough]And you mean to stop him."

"[Yorkshire accent, gruff and warm, resolute]I mean to stop him." Ulf's maimed hand closed on the arm of the chair. "Me, and Wystan Greymane."

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, surprised]Greymane." Muken's mind went to the ridge, the long wall in the west, the iron-haired men with the wolf on their breasts. "The Lord of the Ridgewall?"

"[Yorkshire accent, gruff and warm, dry]The same. His brother Aldwin came home to him in a salt-cart with a ransom tag on his collar. Wystan's not a man who forgets that sort of thing."

[narrator] Muken was quiet. He was thinking. He found that he could not stop himself: the way he could not stop himself looking at a road and seeing where it narrowed, the way he could not stop himself reading a big creature's gait for the leg it favored. The north was a map in his head now, the way Yukazuri's had been, laid out in brown ink and red.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, thoughtful]Two houses," he said. "Out of seven." He looked up. "What about the rest of them? Ashmore. Thornwall. The Keld. Hallenfeld."

[cackles] Ulf cackled.

[narrator] It was an ugly sound, high and cracked, and it made the dog lift its head.

"[Yorkshire accent, gruff and warm, scornful]Ashmore," he said, "has the grain. All of it. Every sack of it between the Greenhollow and the treeline, and they've worked out that a man who owns the grain in a famine doesn't need to fight anybody. They'll sell to the Moto. They'll sell to us. They'll sell to whoever's standing at the gate with silver when the snow comes, and they'll call it neutrality." He spat into the fire. "Thornwall's no better. Thornwall's got the iron corridor and the Ore Council on their doorstep, and they're so busy reading the Ore Council's moods that they've forgotten there's a north behind them. They'll not come to terms with us. They're too frightened of what they'll lose."

"[Northern English accent, low and rough]And the Keld?"

"[Yorkshire accent, gruff and warm, thoughtful]The Keld can be brought round." Ulf's face changed, a little. "Maybe. They're out past the treeline on the tundra, and they don't care much what happens on the roads, because they've never needed roads. Sitting with them. Eating their food. Sleeping in their shelters. They don't swear to a letter. They swear to a man."

"[Northern English accent, low and rough]Hallenfeld?"

"[Yorkshire accent, gruff and warm, dismissive]Hallenfeld are clerks and couriers. They'll carry anybody's letters." Ulf shrugged. "Their tariff-master's in a Moto cell for keeping honest books. [narrator] He spread his hands.

"[Yorkshire accent, gruff and warm, tired]That's all we've got."

---

[narrator] Muken sat on the stool and watched the fire eat into the split logs, white ash and orange seams opening in the bark.

There was a question in him. It had been in him since Rob found him at the trough, and it had grown, the whole time he had been in this room, until there was no room left in him for anything else.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, quiet]Why me?" he said.

[narrator] Ulf did not answer.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, pressing]I mean it," Muken said. "Why me? You're one of the Seven. You've got your own riders, your own household, your own blood. He gestured at the room, the lock, the dog, the chair hauled down out of the north. "Why pick the worst road in the world and send me down it, just to see if I'd come out the other end?"

[narrator] Ulf Stark weighed him. [chuckles] Then he chuckled. It was a quieter sound than the others, and warmer, and a little sad.

"[Yorkshire accent, gruff and warm, warm, a little sad]Very perceptive," he said. "Aren't you."

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, sharp]Don't do that."

"[Yorkshire accent, gruff and warm, innocent]Do what?"

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, irritated]Don't call me perceptive and then not answer."

[narrator] Ulf's mouth twitched in the braided beard.

"[Yorkshire accent, gruff and warm, gentle, firm]I'm afraid," he said, "I can't tell you that. Not all of it, and not yet." He held up his maimed hand before Muken could speak. "It's not that I don't want to. It's to protect you. There are things that, once a man knows them, he can't stop knowing. And there are people who can read a man for what he knows, lad, better than you'd ever believe. People who'd only have to look at you once, the way you're looking at me now, to see it on you. If you don't carry it, they can't find it."

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, stubborn]That's not an answer."

"[Yorkshire accent, gruff and warm, rueful]No," Ulf agreed. "It's not. But it's the one I've got."

[narrator] He leaned forward in the great chair. The firelight moved across his face, and the old white scars on his scalp, and the black iron in his beard.

"[Yorkshire accent, gruff and warm, low]I'll give you this much," he said, "because you've earned something, and because I think you'd go mad without it. You know what the Moto are doing is wrong. You don't need me to tell you that. You've known it since you were a boy." He paused. "[pause]You were raised by a man I knew very well."

[narrator] Muken went still.

"[Yorkshire accent, gruff and warm, quietly]Yusif Stark," Ulf said.

[narrator] [long pause] The name went into the room and stayed there.

"[Yorkshire accent, gruff and warm, proud, warm]One of the greatest warlords this family ever put in the field," Ulf said. "My uncle. My father's younger brother." Something moved at the corner of his mouth. "Don't look at me like that. He was born the winter I was given my first sword, twelve years after me. My grandfather's last mistake, and the only one he ever got right. He rode with Gimbzo in the war, in the long middle of it, when the north still thought the Moto might be the ones to set things right. He was the best of them. The best of us." He looked down at his hands. "And then one day he looked at where Gimbzo's road was going, and he saw the end of it, and he stepped off. Just like that. Turned his horse round and rode away from the whole of it. From his rank and his name and his place in the Seven. Everyone in this family thought he'd gone mad." He was quiet a moment. "He hadn't. He'd gone to raise you. You and your sister both."

[narrator] Muken could not speak.

[softly] He remembered. He remembered a big quiet man with a gray beard, who came down into the Cuttings once a season with salt-meat and wool and never stayed the night. Who had taught a boy of eight to hold a stick like a sword in the back of a worked-out gallery, and a girl of five to hold a needle. Who had never told them his name except the short one, *Yusif*, and had never once in all those years explained why a man like him would climb down four galleries under Halcrest to visit a cutting woman and her two uncounted children. Who had told Muken, over and over, until he could have said it in his sleep: *[Yorkshire accent, quiet, deep, gentle]You protect the weak. You defend the man next door. You keep the north quiet so the children can sleep in it. Everything else is just weather.*

[narrator] Who had stopped coming, the winter Muken was thirteen. Who had never come again.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, hoarse, shaken]Why," Muken said. His voice came out hoarse. "Why would a man like that..."

[narrator] He stopped. He did not know how to finish it.

"[Yorkshire accent, gruff and warm, gently]That," said Ulf Stark gently, "is the part I can't tell you." He held Muken's eyes. "But I'll tell you this. Yusif Stark did not throw away everything he was for a nobody. He knew exactly what he was doing and who he was doing it for. That makes you special, lad. In ways you don't even begin to understand."

---

[narrator] Muken sat on the stool by the fire with his elbows on his knees and his head down, and looked at the floor between his boots, where the firelight came and went over the boards.

He thought about the road. About Big Bertha going down onto her broken knee in the snow. About Mark Stark sobbing with a short sword in his hand, holding the cub still. About the ice going out from under the lead wagon without a sound.

He thought about the steward at Orrel asking a child of the Cuttings *what's your oldest?* and laughing, every time, because the answer was always nothing.

He thought about ten thousand dead in the north from the cold, and nobody having to swing a sword to do it.

And under all of it, the way it was always under everything, he thought about a big quiet man in the back of a worked-out gallery, holding his small hands round the end of a stick. *[Yorkshire accent, quiet, deep, whispers]You protect the weak.*

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, quiet, slow]If it's really as bad as you say," he said at last.

[narrator] He did not look up.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, resigned, warm]Then you've not left me much choice, have you." [sighs] He let out a long breath through his nose. "My sister would never let me live it down if I walked away. Never. I'd hear about it every day till I died." Something that was almost a smile touched the stitched side of his face, and pulled at the stitches, and went away. "And I don't think I could. In good conscience. Not after the road." He lifted his head and met the pale northern eyes. "Not after what you've told me about him."

[narrator] Ulf Stark held his eyes, and did not smile.

Then he reached inside his coat and took something out, and held it out across the hearth, on the flat of his maimed palm.

[slowly] It was a seal-token. A small bronze disc on a short chain, the size of a large coin, stamped on one face with the wolf of Hollenmark and on the other with the Nalūn chain-mark. Muken had seen ten thousand of them in his life, on ten thousand merchants' belts. He had never once been handed one.

"[Yorkshire accent, gruff and warm, formal, warm]A contract," said Ulf Stark. "Between the house of Stark and you. Sealed under the Chain Light in Ironlink, a month ago, by my factor there. Your service to the north, and the north's protection to you and yours, for as long as you live." He held it out further. "Take it. You've an oldest now, Muken. Show it to the next man who asks."

[narrator] Muken took it.

He held it in his cracked white palm, and looked at it, and waited.

[pause] Every token he had ever seen hummed. That was the whole of what they were. A sound contract hummed in the bronze, a faint steady note you could hear in a quiet room and feel a little behind the eyes, for as long as the contract was sound. Draycott's had hummed. The tea-house keeper's had hummed. Every merchant on every belt in Halcrest had walked about humming like a hive.

[long pause] The token in Muken's palm lay perfectly still, and cold, and silent.

[narrator] He looked up.

Ulf Stark was watching the token. He was not watching Muken's face at all. He was watching the little bronze disc, in the firelight, in the cracked palm, with an expression on his own broad weathered face that Muken could not read, and had never seen on anyone. It was the face of a man who has been told something he did not want to believe, and has just watched it turn out to be true.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, puzzled]It's dead," Muken said. He turned it over. "Your factor's cheated you. It's dead."

"[Yorkshire accent, gruff and warm, quietly]No," said Ulf Stark quietly. "It's not."

[narrator] He sat back slowly in the great chair, and let his maimed hand fall onto the arm of it, and looked at Muken over the sleeping dog, and the fire, and the dead bronze, for a long time.

"[Yorkshire accent, gruff and warm, low]Keep it anyway," he said at last. "Keep it on your belt where people can see it. And Muken." His voice had gone very low. "[whispers]Don't ever let anybody see you hold it in your hand."

---

# Chapter Four: Three Days

[narrator] *Book One of the Kōkan · Chapter Four: Three Days*

Muken closed his hand round the dead token and put it inside his coat, against his chest, where nobody would see him hold it. It was cold there. It stayed cold.

He sat on the stool by the fire for a while longer without speaking. The wolfhound had gone back to sleep with its nose on his boot. Across the hearth Ulf Stark was watching him the way a man watches weather coming in over a ridge, patiently, without hope of changing it, only wanting to see what it would do.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, hesitant]Lord Stark," Muken said at last.

"[Yorkshire accent, gruff and warm]Ulf. In here, it's Ulf."

[narrator] Muken did not take him up on it. He was not sure he ever would.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, careful]You keep saying *your army*," he said. "*Your men.*" He turned the thought over, carefully, the way he would have turned over a stone at a ford before he put his weight on it. "[uncertain]Are you sure they'll want to follow somebody like me? Somebody my age?" He looked up. "[earnest]I'm sixteen. I've got men in the Lanternless who were swinging a sword in the war before my mother ever came down into the Cuttings. Half of them still call me *lad* when they think I can't hear. And they've had a year of me. Your riders don't know me from a hole in the road."

[narrator] Ulf smiled. It was a tired smile, and there was nothing amused in it at all.

"[Yorkshire accent, gruff and warm, tired]You've not seen the resistance," he said. "Have you."

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, quiet]No."

"[Yorkshire accent, gruff and warm, grim]No." Ulf leaned back in the great chair. "I'll tell you what it is, then, so you don't walk into it with your eyes shut. Most of the men carrying spears for the north this winter are your age, lad. Sixteen. Seventeen. Some of them younger, and lying about it, and I let them lie because I've no one to send home in their place." He turned the iron ring on his beard-braid with his thumb. "[bitter]Their fathers are dead. At Kessen. In the Moto cells. On the posts in the Tenrai yards. Their uncles went with Gimbzo in the long middle of the war, when the north still thought he meant to set things right, and most of those never came back either. There's a whole generation of men missing out of the north, Muken. Missing like a tooth. And the boys have come up into the gap."

[narrator] He was quiet for a moment.

"[Yorkshire accent, gruff and warm, steady]So it's not the spearmen you need to worry about," he said. "They'll follow you because you're the first man they've seen in three years who looks like them and still wins. It's the others. The ones at the top." He spread his maimed hand on the arm of the chair. "[wry]Me. Wystan Greymane. Old Harald at the Keld fires, if he can be got to come at all. The Seven, what's left of them. Do you know how old we are?"

[narrator] Muken shook his head.

"[Yorkshire accent, gruff and warm, heavy]I'm fifty-seven," Ulf said. "Wystan's sixty-one. Harald was too old to sit a horse when my father was a boy. Every man on the council of the north is past fifty-five, lad, and every one of us is a man who fought the war from its first winter to its fortieth, and buried his sons in it, and has nothing left in him now but the stubbornness to not lie down." [sighs] He let a long breath out through his nose. "[tired]We can hold the north for a few years more. That's all. We can't win it. Not on our own. We're too old, and too tired, and the boys who'd die for us can see it on our faces every time we ride past."

[narrator] He leaned forward.

"[Yorkshire accent, gruff and warm, intent]If they won't follow you," he said, "then they were never any use to our cause in the first place, and I'd rather know it now than on the field. But they will. I've watched Rob look at you. I've never once in his life seen Rob look at anybody like that." He held Muken's eyes. "[passionate]We need someone young. Young enough to still be alive when this is over. Young enough to be the one the tales are told about, and young enough to be the one who tells them. The north doesn't rise for an old man's grudge, Muken. It never has. It rises for a story. And it's been a long time since anybody gave it one worth getting up for."

[narrator] The fire cracked.

Muken did not argue. He could not have, honestly, if he had wanted to. He had seen the men Ulf meant without knowing he was seeing them: the gray heads in every Stark hall the convoy had passed, the stooped backs at the fords, the old riders with no sons beside them. He had seen the boys too. Riding point on the Vessant road. Holding the gate at the Marrow crossing with spears longer than they were. Sixteen. Seventeen. Some younger, and lying about it.

He had not put the two together until now.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, quiet]I'll think on it," he said.

"[Yorkshire accent, gruff and warm, firm]You'll do more than that." Ulf sat back. "You've given me your word already, in this room, and I'll hold you to it. But think. Take three days. Sleep on it, if you can sleep. When you've stopped asking yourself whether you can, come and find me, and we'll talk about how." He nodded at the door. "[gentler]Go on. Rob'll let you out. And Muken."

[narrator] Muken stopped with his hand on the bolt.

"[Yorkshire accent, gruff and warm, softly]Your sister," said Ulf Stark. "Tell her. She'll know anyway, by the look of her. Better she hears it from you than reads it off you."

---

[narrator] He did not sleep much, those three days.

The Vessant post was a small place and an idle one once the convoy was paid off, and the Lanternless did what free companies did when they were paid and idle, which was drink. They drank the post's beer and then the post's spirit, and then whatever the teamsters had brought up from the south in the bottom of their kit, and they sang and fought and slept it off in the straw of the long barn, and on the second night one of them put a knife in another over a game of bones and Yukazuri sewed the man up on the trestle in the factor's outer room while the factor's clerks watched with their mouths open. Nobody asked where their sergeant was. Sergeants were allowed to be somewhere else.

He was on the stockade.

There was a walkway round the inside of the wall at the height of a man's shoulder, plank laid on log brackets, where a sentry could stand and see out over the sharpened tops of the stakes, and Muken stood on it, those three days, more than he stood anywhere else. He stood at the north corner, mostly. From there he could see the river, black and fast between its shelves of ice, and the timber going away on the far bank in long blue-gray ranks toward the edge of the world, and the sky over it, which in the third week of the Thin Weeks was white all day and went the color of a bruise at evening. In the mornings the frost on the stake-points glittered like ground glass, and the hoar on the far timber caught the first sun and burned pink along the tops of the ranks for the length of a breath before it went white.

He kept the token in his coat. He took it out, sometimes, when he was sure nobody could see, and held it in his palm and waited for it to hum. It never did.

[Northern English accent, mocking] *What's your oldest?* [narrator] the steward had asked him, every time, at Orrel, when he was a child. And laughed. And every time, the answer had been: nothing. No contract. No history. No credit. No name the clerks would take. He had spent his whole life since believing that was the thing that was wrong with him. That he was nothing, born of nothing, the son of a cutting woman and of a father nobody had ever named to him, and that everything he had done since, every season's pay, every contract honored, every road walked to the end, was the slow work of making something out of that nothing by hand.

And now a Warlord of the north had handed him a contract, sealed under the Chain Light, and it had lain dead in his palm. And the Warlord had not looked surprised. He had looked as if something he had been told for years had finally turned out to be true.

He thought about Yusif.

He had not let himself think about Yusif in a long time. It had hurt too much, after the visits stopped, and then it had stopped hurting, which was worse, and he had put the big quiet gray-bearded man away somewhere at the back of himself, in a worked-out gallery where he did not go. Now he went. He sat down in it, in his head, on the cold floor, and let it come back.

The salt-meat wrapped in oiled cloth. The new wool. The way Yusif had always had to stoop under the gallery roof, and never once complained of it. The stick, a length of ash the right weight for a small boy's hands, and the big rough hands closed round his own on the end of it. [Yorkshire accent, deep and patient] *Again. Again. You're thinking. Stop thinking. Let your feet do it. There. That's it. Again.* [narrator] The way Yusif had looked at their mother, the few times he stayed long enough to look: carefully, with his head a little bowed, the way you might look at something in a shrine. The way he had looked at Muken, sometimes, when he thought Muken was not watching. As if he were waiting for something.

[Yorkshire accent, deep and patient, slowly] *You protect the weak. You defend the man next door. You keep the north quiet so the children can sleep in it. Everything else is just weather.*

[narrator] A man who had been one of the Seven. One of the greatest warlords the house of Stark had ever put in the field. Who had turned his horse round and ridden away from all of it, his rank and his name and his place, and gone down four galleries under Halcrest once a season for years to teach a cutting woman's boy to hold a stick.

[pause] Nobody did that for a nobody.

Muken stood on the stockade with his hands on the cold points of the stakes and looked north, toward the timber, toward the end of the world, toward whatever was out there that Ulf would not name.

[Northern English accent, low and rough, inner voice] *Is this it?* he thought. *Is this what he was waiting for?* [narrator] He did not know what the question meant, exactly, or who he was asking it of. [Northern English accent, low and rough, inner voice, barely audible] *Is this what I'm for?*

[narrator] The river did not answer. The timber did not. The sky went the color of a bruise, violet and rose low along the treeline, and then went dark, and later the aurora came up over the timber in long slow curtains of green hemmed with violet, bright enough that the snow on the walkway took the color round his boots. It did not answer either.

---

[narrator] On the third morning his sister found him there.

She came up the ladder to the walkway without a sound, the way she did everything, and he did not hear her until she was beside him. She had her gray field coat on, buttoned to the throat against the cold, and her white hair was braided and pinned up off her neck, the way she wore it when she was working, though there was nobody left at the post who needed working on. Her small grave face was pink with cold at the nose and the cheekbones. She stood beside him at the stakes without a word and looked where he was looking, at the river and the timber and the white sky over it, until the cold had got into both their hands.

He felt his breathing slow down. It always did, when she was near. He had never once in his life wondered why.

She was thirteen. She came up to the middle of his chest. When she had been small, five or six, too small to work the stamping floor, she had used to sit on his feet in the gallery while he worked the salt face, holding onto his knees with both arms, and he had walked her up and down that way until she shrieked. He looked down at her now, at the white braid and the gray coat and the long new scar down her forearm where the sleeve had ridden up, and felt something in his chest that he did not have a word for.

He reached out and put his hand on the top of her head, the whole big cracked palm of it, and ruffled her hair, the way he had done since she was born. It came loose from one of its pins. She did not slap his hand away. That was how he knew she was worried.

"[young girl, Northern English accent, sharp, worried]What's wrong, big brother?" Yukazuri said.

[narrator] He took his hand back. "[Northern English accent, low and rough, gruff]Nothing's wrong."

"[young girl, Northern English accent, sharp, dry]You've slept with your boots on for three nights."

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, defensive]I always sleep with my boots on."

"[young girl, Northern English accent, sharp, knowing]You've slept with your boots *laced* for three nights." She did not look at him. She was still watching the river. "[clinical]You've eaten one meal in two days and you gave half of it to Mark Stark's dog, which isn't even Mark's, it's the factor's. Your breath's gone short and high when you think nobody's listening, right up under the collarbone, here." She touched the hollow of her own throat with two fingers without taking her eyes off the river. "[softer]And you've had your hand inside your coat on your chest every time I've looked at you since you came out of that room. Like there's something there you're frightened you'll drop." [pause] She paused. "[quietly]Or frightened somebody will see."

[narrator] Muken was silent.

"[young girl, Northern English accent, sharp, firm]So I'll ask you again," said Yukazuri. "[fierce]And this time you'll tell me, or I'll take that coat off you myself and look. What's wrong?"

---

[narrator] He told her.

He kept his voice low, and he kept his back to the inside of the stockade and his eyes on the gate, and he told her all of it, the way Ulf had told it to him. The road that was chosen. Draycott, who was Ulf's man. Torvald's head in a cask of brine, and the note in Kagenori Tenrai's hand. The Kessen road, and the martial law that was coming down on it in two months like a door. Stark and Greymane, and maybe the Keld, against six Moto lines. The army. The offer. The word he had already given.

When he got to Yusif, she turned her head and looked at him for the first time.

"[young girl, Northern English accent, sharp, startled, quiet]Yusif," she said.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, low]Yusif Stark. Ulf's uncle. One of the Seven." He swallowed. "[hesitant]He rode with Gimbzo. In the war. And then he stopped. And then he came to us."

[narrator, long pause] Yukazuri was a while finding anything to say.

"[young girl, Northern English accent, sharp, even, remembering]He taught me to hold a needle," she said at last. Her voice was very even. "I was five. He brought it down wrapped in a scrap of leather with a reel of gut, and he sat me on a sack of salt and showed me how to put it through a fold of wool and draw it tight so it wouldn't pull." She looked down at her own hands on the stakes, at the small brown fingers that had closed a hundred men. "[softly]He said it was the most useful thing in the world. He said more men would live because of a needle than would ever die because of a sword." She was quiet. "[quietly]I thought he was just being kind."

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, quiet]So did I."

"[young girl, Northern English accent, sharp, slowly]He wasn't." She looked up at him. "Was he?"

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, low]No," Muken said. "I don't think he was."

[narrator] The wind came over the stockade from the north, off the river, and it was cold enough to make his eyes water. Yukazuri did not seem to feel it. She was looking at his chest, at the place where his hand had been, and her face had changed.

"[young girl, Northern English accent, sharp, quietly]Show me," she said.

[narrator] He hesitated. Then he looked over his shoulder, at the empty yard, the closed doors of the barn and the factor's hall, the smoke going up straight from the chimneys in the still morning. Nobody. He reached inside his coat and took out the token and held it out to her on his palm.

She did not take it. She looked at it first, the way she looked at a wound before she touched it. Then she bent her head, close, closer, until her ear was nearly touching his hand, the way she bent over a man's chest in the back of a cart to listen to what was going on inside it, and closed her eyes.

He waited.

She straightened up. Her eyes, when she opened them, had gone very dark, nearly black, the deep blue swallowed up by the gray of the morning.

"[young girl, Northern English accent, sharp, careful, thoughtful]It's sealed," she said. "It's a good seal. Old wall. Bright light. I can feel it in there, the shape of it, like a coin in a pocket." She frowned. "[puzzled]It's not broken. It's not dead. It's just..." She searched for the word. "It's just not talking to you. As if it's looking straight at you and can't see anyone there."

[narrator] He closed his hand over it.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, cold]Ulf knew it would do that," he said. "Before he gave it to me. He wasn't watching me. He was watching it."

"[young girl, Northern English accent, sharp, intent]I know. I've been listening to that thing for three days." Yukazuri did not take her eyes off his closed fist. "Every token in this post hums, Muken. The factor's. The clerks'. Every teamster with a supply contract on his belt. I can hear them all the way across the yard at night, like bees in a wall. And yours is the only one that's quiet." She drew a breath. "[hesitant]I thought at first it was broken. And then I thought it was Mother. Something in her, that came down to us."

[narrator] He went still.

"[young girl, Northern English accent, sharp, softly]It isn't Mother," Yukazuri said softly. "Mother had a token once, she told me, and it hummed fine. She told me near the end, when the fever had her and she wasn't always sure which of us she was talking to. A little bronze thing on a cord, from before the Cuttings, and it sang on her like a kettle on the hob, she said, and she dropped it down a dead shaft the week she came to Orrel, because a thing that hums can be heard." She looked up at him. "[certain, low]It's us. I've been standing in that yard three days listening to every token in this post, and I've worked out what they're listening for. A token wants a name to sit under, Muken. A house. That's what the steward was laughing at with his *oldest*, and what Draycott meant about the book. It goes looking for our name, and we haven't got one it can find."

[narrator] The mark. Their mother's mark, in burned stick on the salt wall of the gallery. [gentle, light Japanese accent, whispers] *That's ours. That's our name. Don't show it to the clerks.* [narrator] The Zettari surveyor at the Marrow, wiping it off his window with his sleeve, with his breath gone short and high under his collarbone.

Neither of them said it.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, barely audible]What are we, Yuka?" Muken said. It came out of him very quietly, almost before he knew he had said it.

[narrator] His sister looked up at him, with the white braid coming loose where he had ruffled it, and her small grave face pink with cold, and her eyes very dark.

"[young girl, Northern English accent, sharp, softly]I don't know," she said. "Somebody's, with the name scraped off." She put her hand on top of his closed fist, over the token, and held it there. "[steady]But I don't think Yusif came down four galleries every season until I was ten to teach a needle to nothing. And I don't think a Warlord of the north hauls his own chair down out of Hollenmark on the back of a cart to talk to nothing either." She paused. "[low, intense]We're something, Muken. Somebody knows what. And whoever they are, they're frightened enough of it to make sure we never found out."

---

[narrator] They stood together on the stockade, her hand over his, and the river went on below them, black and fast between the ice, throwing the low sun back in hard white sparks wherever it broke over a stone.

"[young girl, Northern English accent, sharp, knowing]You've already decided," Yukazuri said after a while.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, gruff]I haven't."

"[young girl, Northern English accent, sharp, dry]You decided in the room. I could hear it on you when you came out." She took her hand away and tucked it back into the sleeve of her coat. "[pointed]You've spent three days up here trying to find a way to make it not your fault. In case it goes wrong."

[narrator] He laughed. He could not help it. It came out short and rough and pulled at the stitches in his cheek, and he put his hand up to them and laughed again anyway.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, laughs]Sky above," he said. "Is there anything you don't know?"

"[young girl, Northern English accent, sharp, dry]Lots of things. Not that." She looked up at him. "[firm]And I'm coming."

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, warning]Yuka."

"[young girl, Northern English accent, sharp, stubborn]I'm coming. Don't start."

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, exasperated]You're *thirteen.*"

"[young girl, Northern English accent, sharp, very evenly]I was twelve at the Veyrath ditch," said Yukazuri, very evenly, "when I went down it on my knees through two hundred men the lords had left lying there, with my needle and my gut and the salve tin, and closed every one of them I could reach, and sat with the ones I couldn't until they went. You were up the bank holding the line. You didn't see." She did not raise her voice. She never did. "[steady, fierce]I've been the company's surgeon for a year. I've sewn your face shut twice. There are men alive in this post right now who wouldn't be if I'd been somewhere sensible. And if you go and lead an army of sixteen-year-olds against six Moto lines, Muken, then somebody's going to have to be there afterward for the ones who don't come back from it. That's my work. That's what Yusif gave me the needle for." She held his eyes. "[firm, dry]So I'm coming. And if you try to leave me behind at some fort with a nice warm fire, I'll walk after you in the snow, and you'll have to explain to Lord Stark why your surgeon's lost three toes."

[narrator] He looked down at her, and found he had no argument left that she had not already sewn shut.

Then he put his hand on her head again, gently this time, and left it there, and did not ruffle her hair. He just left it there, the weight of it, the way he remembered Yusif doing, once or twice, to both of them, in the gallery, when he was going away and did not want to say so.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, softly]Mother would've hated this," he said.

"[young girl, Northern English accent, sharp, warm, wry]Mother would've packed us food for the road," said Yukazuri, "and told you to keep your boots laced."

[narrator] Down in the yard, the barn door creaked. A tall shape came out of it with a stick under one arm, limping, and stood at the foot of the ladder, and looked up at them, with his breath going up white in front of his face. Rob Stark. He did not call out. He just stood there, waiting, the way the Starks waited, as though he had all the time in the world and knew exactly what the answer was going to be.

Muken took his hand from his sister's head.

He looked out once more over the stakes, at the river and the timber and the white sky going away north toward whatever it was that Ulf would not name, and the long road down to Kessen, and the two months left before the door came down on it. Then he looked down at Rob Stark at the foot of the ladder.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, steady]Rob," he said.

"[Yorkshire accent, cheerful, hoarse]Sarge."

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, steady]Go and tell your uncle," Muken said, "[slowly, resolute]that the Tokenless is coming."

---

# Chapter Five: Kharven's Foot

[narrator] *Book One of the Kōkan · Chapter Five: Kharven's Foot*

The night before the assault, the two old men sat up late in the command tent with a jug between them and the lamp turned low, and talked about the boy.

The tent was Greymane, and it smelled of it: oiled canvas and wet wolfskin and the iron-and-tallow stink of armor that had been greased against the cold and worn for a week. A brazier of charcoal stood in the middle of it, ticking as it cooled. A map was pinned to a trestle under the lamp, weighted at the corners with a dagger, a helm, a horn cup and a stone, and on it in charcoal someone had drawn the long road climbing out of the low country, and the ridge, and the squat square shape at the top of the ridge where the road went through a gate. Beside the shape, in Wystan Greymane's cramped hand, three words: *Kharven's Foot. Two hundred.*

Wystan sat with his long legs stretched out toward the brazier and his boots off, flexing his toes in their wool. He was sixty-one, and long, and gray all through: gray hair cropped short, gray beard cut close to the jaw, gray eyes set deep in a long gray Greymane face that had been carved, it seemed, for weathering and nothing else. He had the stillness that the Ridgewall men had, the stillness of men who spent their lives standing on a wall looking west for something that might come, and he had a way of not moving at all and then moving very fast. On the breast of the surcoat that hung over the back of his chair, worked in darker thread, was the wolf's head of his house. On the table at his elbow, folded small, was a scrap of parchment on a cord, with a number on it. It had been round his brother Aldwin's neck when the Tenrai sent him home in the salt cart. Wystan had not let it out of his reach since.

"[soft Scottish accent, dry]So," he said. "The boy."

[narrator] Ulf grunted. He was sitting on a camp chair that creaked every time he breathed, with a cup in his maimed hand, looking at the map.

"[soft Scottish accent, dry, curious]What do you make of him?" Wystan said. "Truly. Now that you've had him under your eye for a week." He turned the cup slowly on his knee. "[lower]Is he one of them? One of the Kōkan?"

[narrator] Ulf did not answer at once. He looked at the charcoal shape of the fort for a long while. When he spoke, his voice was low, and very certain.

"[Yorkshire accent, gruff and warm, low, certain]There's no doubt of it," he said. "None at all. He's Gimbzo's son."

[narrator] The brazier ticked.

"[Yorkshire accent, gruff and warm, intent]You've seen it yourself," Ulf said. "Don't tell me you haven't. The way he stands. The way he goes quiet before he moves, as if he's already moved and he's only waiting for his body to catch up. The way a room changes when he walks into it, and he hasn't the first idea it's changing." He set the cup down. "[remembering]I rode beside Gimbzo for two summers when I was young and stupid enough to think he'd save us. I know what that man felt like at ten paces. I felt it again in the factor's hall at the river post, from a sixteen-year-old sitting on a stool by my fire with his sister's stitches in his face." He shook his head slowly. "[quietly]And he doesn't know. He doesn't know any of it."

"[soft Scottish accent, dry, quiet]Yusif knew."

"[Yorkshire accent, gruff and warm, tight]Yusif knew." Ulf's mouth went tight in the braided beard. "[grim]Yusif was there the night of the cellars in Halcrest. That's the whole of why he stepped off. You know that. He watched Gimbzo go down into those cellars with a lamp after his own line, every Kōkan he'd left breathing, his own blood, for no reason any man in the north ever got out of him. And Yusif turned his horse round the next morning and never rode with him again." He was quiet for a moment. "[softer]And there was Kasane. The Byakuya girl who took the Yukari name, Gimbzo's own sworn guard, the one who stood at his shoulder in every hall he ever walked into. The way the north tells it, he took her. The way Yusif told it, she walked out of that cellar on her own feet with the boy under her coat, and never once said his name after." He turned the cup a quarter-turn on his knee and did not drink from it. "[heavy]Then Yusif spent the rest of his life going down into the salt every season to make sure that boy, and the girl who came after him, grew up knowing which end of a sword goes into the other man. Right up to the winter they caught up with him, and he gave them nothing. And never once told either of the children why."

[narrator, sighs] Wystan let out a long breath through his nose.

"[soft Scottish accent, dry, quietly]And now we've got him," he said.

"[Yorkshire accent, gruff and warm, troubled]And now we've got him." Ulf looked down at his hands, the whole and the maimed. "[low, uneasy]I don't like it, Wystan. I'll say it to you, since I can't say it to anyone else. It sits wrong with me. Every time I look at the lad I feel like a man selling a horse with a cracked hoof and a blanket over it." He rubbed his face. "[bitter]He's a Moto. He's the last of the Kōkan and Gimbzo's own blood, and he's about to ride into a Moto fort tomorrow at the head of our vanguard to kill Moto for us, and he doesn't know what he is. It feels like theft. It feels like we're stealing something off him."

[narrator] Wystan was silent for a while.

"[soft Scottish accent, dry, gently]Don't take this wrong, Ulf," he said at last. "[wry]But you're soft on him."

"[Yorkshire accent, gruff and warm, protesting]I'm not..."

"[soft Scottish accent, dry, firm]You are. It's the uncle in you. Yusif's blood." Wystan held up a long gray hand before Ulf could argue. "[measured]And I'll tell you why you're wrong, and then you can hit me if you like, but you'll not tell him." He leaned forward. "[intent]Think it through. You tell that boy tomorrow, or the day after, or any day this year, that he's Gimbzo's son and the last of the Kōkan and a Moto by blood, and what happens? He stops knowing whose he is.
