He stands in front of a Tenrai gate with his sword up and he looks at the men on the other side and he thinks, *those are mine.* He thinks, *that's my blood up on that wall.* And he hesitates. And one hesitation in a breach is the whole of a man's life."

[narrator] Ulf said nothing.

"[soft Scottish accent, dry, certain]He'll learn it," Wystan said. "He'll learn it on his own. He's halfway there already, I'd wager, him and that sister of his. And when he does learn it, he'll have learned it the hard way, the way every man who ever led anything worth following learned what he was. With his own hands." His voice went lower. "[low]If we give him all the answers, Ulf, we take the war away from him. We take the pain of it. And it's the pain that makes them. You know that. You've buried enough of them who were made too soft."

[narrator] The brazier ticked. Outside, somewhere in the lines, a horse stamped and blew.

"[soft Scottish accent, dry, amused]Besides," Wystan said, and a dry, crooked smile came onto his long gray face for the first time. "Who'd have thought it? At our age? That we'd live to see the rise of another great warlord of the north. Another one of the old kind." [chuckles] He chuckled, low in his chest. "[warm]I thought I'd be in the ground long before I saw that again."

[narrator] Ulf turned that over in the brazier light. Then, slowly, he picked his cup up again.

"[Yorkshire accent, gruff and warm, quiet]You think he'll do it, then," he said. "Tomorrow. You think he'll take the Foot."

[narrator] Wystan nodded.

"[soft Scottish accent, dry, certain]No doubt," he said. "No doubt at all."

[narrator] He reached for the jug and filled both cups, and they drank without speaking. On the trestle the lamp burned low over the map, over the charcoal road and the charcoal ridge and the squat charcoal square at the top of it with its two hundred, and over a second scrap of parchment lying folded by Ulf's elbow, sealed in gray wax. It had gone south that morning in a copy, by crow, through the Mahuo post at Sum-gol, and from there by another crow east into the Yukari valleys. It said, in Ulf Stark's square northern hand: *The Foot will be in our keeping by the new moon. The Kessen road will stay open. Tell the Lord Naiser the north has not forgotten who stood with it.*

---

[narrator] Kharven's Foot stood at the top of the long ridge where the Kessen road came up out of the low country and went through the mountains toward the north.

It had been there longer than anyone could say. The old northern clans had built the first of it, a ring of dry stone round a well, back when the land was still grass and the riders still rode; then somebody had built over that, and somebody over them, and eleven years back, with the war already thirty years old and the north going badly in it, the Tenrai had come and rebuilt all of it in their own square gray stone, with a gatehouse and two towers and a curtain wall eight men high, and put a garrison in it, and a toll-post at the gate, and a Tenrai banner on the tallest tower, gold heraldry on black, that you could see in clear weather from three days down the road. Whoever held the Foot held the road. Whoever held the road held the north by the throat. In two months, when Kagenori Tenrai Moto laid his martial law on the Kessen road, the Foot was where the door would be.

So the north had come to take the door off its hinges first.

They had come up through the dark, six hundred of them, Stark and Greymane and a scatter of lesser houses and a great many men who belonged to no house at all, and they stood now in the dead hour before dawn in the snow under the ridge, below the line of sight of the walls, in their ranks. Their breath went up white in the cold and hung over them in a cloud. Above the ridge the aurora was still up, a slow green river with violet at its hem, and it lit the snow under their boots a faint drowned green and threw the line of the wall hard and black against the sky. The ranks stood silent. Somewhere far above them, on the wall, a Tenrai sentry was singing to himself to stay awake, a thin small sound in the enormous dark, and every man in the six hundred could hear it.

The vanguard stood at the front.

There were a hundred and twenty of them, and Muken had walked down their lines twice in the dark already, and both times he had felt his stomach go cold. Ulf had not lied to him. They were boys. Not all of them; there were older men among them too, here and there, gray-bearded veterans with no sons left, who had asked for the vanguard because the vanguard was where you went when you had nothing left to keep yourself alive for. But most of them were boys. Sixteen. Seventeen. A few of them younger, and lying about it, with spears they had to hold two-handed and helmets stuffed with rags to keep them on, and the soft blurred faces of children who had not yet decided what shape they were going to be.

Like him. They looked like him.

He stood at the front of them in a mail shirt Ulf had given him that was too long in the arms and too short in the body, with the scrap-iron sword on his back and the stitched seam on his cheek gone tight and itching in the cold, and the dead token inside his coat against his chest, and looked up at the black line of the ridge, and the black line of the wall on top of it, and tried not to think about anything at all.

Ulf and Wystan came down the line toward him through the snow.

The Lord of Hollenmark was in his war-mail, with a great axe across his shoulder and his breath going up white through his braided beard, and men straightened as he passed them without knowing they did it. Wystan walked beside him with the long Greymane stride, in a gray surcoat and a gray cloak, bareheaded, with his sword at his hip and a hunting horn at his belt. They stopped in front of the vanguard, and turned, and looked at it.

Ulf did not raise his voice. In the silence under the ridge it carried to every man in the hundred and twenty and some way past them.

"[Yorkshire accent, gruff and warm, steady, carrying]This is ours," he said. "This battle. This morning. This is ours, lads, and for no one else. Not for the Seven. Not for me. For the people. For your mothers in the long houses with nothing in the pot and the snow at the door. For your sisters. For your little brothers who'll be stood where you're stood in three years if we don't do this now." He let it sit. "[heavy]For every one of you who's buried his father this winter. This is for them."

[narrator] The breath of a hundred and twenty boys hung in the air over them.

"[Yorkshire accent, gruff and warm, wry]Some of you will have noticed," Ulf said, "that I've put a stranger at your front."

[narrator] Nobody laughed. A few of them looked at Muken. Most of them already were.

"[Yorkshire accent, gruff and warm, earnest]I'll tell you about him," Ulf said, "because you've a right to know who you're following up that hill. He's got no house. He's got no family name. He's tokenless. He's never sworn to me nor to Lord Greymane nor to any lord in the north, and there's no contract on him anywhere in the world that would hold him to this ground for one breath longer than he chose to stand on it." He looked down the line. "[steady]He owes nobody anything. He could have taken his pay at the river post and gone south with his sister and never thought about any of you again."

[narrator] He put his maimed hand on Muken's shoulder.

"[Yorkshire accent, gruff and warm, proud]And he's here," Ulf said. "He came. Nobody made him. He's here because he wants to fight for you. Because he thinks the people of the north are worth keeping safe, and he's no more reason to think so than any of you has. That's why the house of Stark and the house of Greymane have named him your lieutenant, as of this morning. And that's why I want you to trust him." [pause] He paused. "[warm]Rely on him. He'll lead you into more battles than this one, lads. A great many more. Some of you'll be old men telling your grandchildren you were stood in the snow under the Foot the morning he first drew his sword for the north."

[narrator] He took his hand away.

Wystan Greymane stepped forward. He looked down at Muken, from his great gray height, with something in his deep-set gray eyes that Muken could not read, and put his long hand on Muken's other shoulder, and squeezed it once.

"[soft Scottish accent, dry, quietly]You're up, lad," he said quietly.

[narrator] Then he stepped back, and Muken was alone at the front of them.

---

[narrator] He did not know what to say.

He stood in the snow and looked at them, the hundred and twenty, and they looked back at him, and the silence went on, and he could feel it stretching and going thin like ice over a river, and he could not find a single word. He had never made a speech in his life. Sergeants did not make speeches. Sergeants said *move* and *hold* and *on me* and *get your shield up, you stupid bastard,* and that was the whole of it. He looked at the soft faces of the boys under their rag-stuffed helmets, and the hard gray faces of the old men among them, and he thought: [Northern English accent, low and rough, inner voice, anxious] *They're waiting for me. They're waiting for me to tell them why.*

[narrator] And he did not know why. Not in words.

Then, behind the vanguard, past the end of its ranks, where the surgeons had set up their line in the lee of a fallen pine with their kits open in the snow and their braziers lit for the irons, he saw a small figure in a gray coat with a white braid pinned up off her neck, the brazier coals behind her throwing orange up the side of her face. She stood without moving. She was not looking at Ulf or Wystan or the ridge or the wall. She was looking at him.

She did not nod. She did not smile. She did not do anything at all. She only stood there and looked at him, steadily, the way she looked at a man on a cart when she had decided he was going to live.

He felt his breathing slow down.

And then it came, all at once, the way his body moved in a fight before he decided to, and he opened his mouth and let it.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, rough, loud]We're going to free ourselves," Muken said.

[narrator] His voice came out rough and too loud and cracked on the last word. He did not care.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, fierce]That's what this is. That's all it is. We're going up that hill to take back what's ours, from the people who think it's theirs." He pointed up at the black line of the wall with his whole arm. "From the ones up there. From the ones who sent them. From every Moto lord who's ever sat in a warm hall in the south and looked at a map of the north and decided what's going to happen to us." He took a breath. "[angry]They don't get to do that any more. They don't get to decide our lives. They don't get to arrange us. Like stones on a board. Like sacks in a cart. Like *numbers* in a drainage book."

[narrator] The hundred and twenty were very quiet.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, bitter]They'll tell you it's for unity," Muken said. "They'll tell you it's for order. They'll say the roads have to be closed and the grain has to be taken and the salt has to be counted, because it's for the good of everybody, and they understand what the north needs better than the north does." He felt his face go hot under the stitches. "[heated]They don't understand anything. They've never once lived up here. They've never had to break the ice on a bucket to wash. They've never sat up all night by a sick child with nothing to put in its mouth. They've never buried their father in ground so frozen they had to light a fire on it for a day to dig. They don't know the first thing about what it takes to stay alive in this frozen wasteland, and they think they've the right to tell *us* how to do it."

[narrator] He was not shouting any more. They were leaning toward him, the whole front rank of them, the way men lean toward a fire.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, quieter, earnest]So this is for the north," he said. "For the people in it. That's all. That's all I've got to give you, I've nothing else." He looked down the line, at the boys and the old men, at the Stark wolves and the Greymane wolves on the breasts of some of them and the bare cloth on the breasts of most of them. "[rising]I don't care if you're Stark. I don't care if you're Greymane. I don't care if you're some house nobody's ever heard of, from some valley nobody's ever been to. I don't care if you're nothing at all. If you're tokenless. Like me." He swallowed. "[steady, warm]We're neighbors. That's what we are. Every one of us. We're the man next door. And you don't let the man next door go hungry, and you don't let anybody burn his house, and you don't let him walk up a hill on his own."

[narrator] He reached up over his shoulder, and drew the scrap-iron sword.

It came out of the scabbard with its dry rasp in the silence, black and pitted and ugly in the blue dark before the dawn, with the aurora's green running faintly along the flat of it, and he raised it up over his head, as high as his arm would go, toward the sky.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, passionate]So we fight for each other," Muken said. "Every one of us. For every one of us. And we rage together." He held the sword up. "[shouts]Because our fury is one."

[narrator, long pause] For a moment nothing happened at all.

Then a boy in the front rank, a thin red-haired boy of perhaps fifteen with a spear twice his height, lifted the spear up over his head in both hands, the way Muken had lifted the sword. And the boy next to him did. And the old gray-bearded veteran behind them did, without a word, his axe going up into the dark. And then it went down the line like fire through dry grass, spears and axes and swords and bare fists going up, a hundred and twenty of them, all at once, and from somewhere in the middle of them a sound came up, too close under the wall for cheering: a low, hard, rising growl, from a hundred and twenty throats, like wolves.

Wystan Greymane turned his head and looked at Ulf Stark.

He was smiling. It was not a large smile. It was the dry crooked smile of an old man who has been right about something and is enjoying it more than he will ever admit.

[soft Scottish accent, dry, inner voice] *For someone so young,* [narrator] the smile said. [soft Scottish accent, dry, inner voice, amused] *Who taught him to talk like that?*

[narrator] Ulf did not smile back. He was looking at Muken. His pale northern eyes were wet, and it was not the cold.

Up on the wall, the Tenrai sentry had stopped singing.

[softly] Thirty of the hundred and twenty would not come down off the ridge again. None of them knew that yet. Most of them would not have cared.

---

[horn blowing] Wystan blew the horn.

[narrator] They went up the ridge in the first light, at a run, with the aurora fading out over their heads and the snow going from blue to rose under their boots.

The Foot woke above them. Muken heard it wake: a shout on the wall, and then another, and then a bell, iron and frantic, hammering out across the dawn, and the scrape and clatter of men running on stone. Then the first arrows came down out of the half-light, and he heard the sound they made, the long thin hiss in the air and then the flat wet thump, and a boy two places to his left made a small surprised sound, and sat down in the snow with a shaft through his throat, and did not get up.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, shouts]*Shields!*" Muken roared. "*Shields up, get your shields up, keep moving, don't stop, don't stop!*"

[narrator] They kept moving. That was the only rule. You did not stop under a wall. A man who stopped under a wall was a man who had decided to die there. The snow on the ridge was knee-deep and crusted, and every step broke through it and caught, and the slope was steeper than it had looked in the dark, and the arrows came down and came down, and men fell, and the men behind them went round them and kept going.

The plan was the gate. It had always been the gate. Wystan's men had the ram, a great iron-capped pine trunk slung on chains from a timber frame, and they were dragging it up the road behind the vanguard, and the vanguard's work was to get to the foot of the gate-towers and hold the ground there long enough for the ram to come up. Ladders on the curtain either side, to split the defenders. Then the gate, and the yard, and the keep. Simple. Every plan was simple until the first arrow.

Halfway up the ridge, Muken looked at the wall, and saw it.

He did not know how he saw it. He would never know, not for years. One moment the curtain wall was a long blank face of square gray Tenrai stone gone rose along its top in the dawn, eight men high, featureless, with heads and bows moving along the top of it. The next, there was a line in it. A fault. A long jagged seam running down through the stone from the battlements to the footing, thirty paces left of the gatehouse, where the Tenrai had built their new gray wall over the old dry-stone ring of the clans, and the frost had got in between the two, year after year after year, and pushed. Nobody had seen it. Nobody could have. It was something you *felt,* the way you feel the one stair in a staircase that is going to give under your weight: a place where the whole of the wall's load was going down through stone that could not carry it, and had not been able to for years, and was only waiting.

The heat came up behind his left eye, sharp, like a cinder.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, shouts]*Left!*" he roared. "*Ladders left! On me! Thirty paces left of the gate, on me, ON ME!*"

[narrator] They came with him. He would remember that, later, more than anything else about the morning. He turned away from the gate and ran for a blank piece of wall where the plan had said nothing at all, and he did not look back, and they came with him. Forty of them, fifty, with the two ladders. The thin red-haired boy with the spear. The old gray-bearded veteran with the axe.

The ladders went up against the seam. The Tenrai on the wall saw where they were going and ran along the battlements to meet them, and the stones and the boiling pitch came down, and the first ladder went over backward with six men on it, and Muken heard them hit the slope. The second ladder held. He went up it. He did not remember deciding to. The warmth came down out of his chest into his legs and arms and back, as it always did, and he went up the rungs three at a time with the scrap-iron sword in one hand, and at the top of the ladder a Tenrai spearman leaned out over the battlement to thrust down at his face, and Muken caught the haft below the head with his free hand and pulled, and the man came over the wall headfirst past him and was gone.

He came over the battlement onto the wall-walk.

And the wall moved under him.

It was a small movement. A shiver, a settling, like an old horse shifting its weight. But he felt it through his boots, and the men on the walk felt it, Tenrai and northern both, and every one of them stopped, for one breath, and looked down. And then the seam he had seen from halfway down the ridge opened along its whole length with a sound like a tree splitting in a hard frost, and thirty feet of the Tenrai curtain wall leaned slowly outward over the slope, and kept leaning, and went down.

He was not on it. He had come over the wall six paces to the right of the seam, by luck or by something that was not luck, and he stood on the wall-walk and watched the whole section beside him go over in a long roar of gray stone and snow and dust and screaming men, down the ridge, and when the dust began to thin there was a gap in the curtain of Kharven's Foot that a cart could have driven through.

The hundred and twenty came through it.

---

After that, for what felt like half the morning, it was the yard.

He would remember very little of the yard afterward, and he would be glad of it. It was the kind of fighting that has no shape. The Tenrai garrison came down out of the towers and the barracks and the keep to plug the breach, and the northerners came up through it to widen it, and they met in the snow of the courtyard between the walls, snow that was red within minutes, a bright raw red that steamed, and it became a crush, two hundred men and more in a space built for forty, pressed so close that half of them could not lift their arms. Men died there without knowing who killed them. Men were killed by their own side. Men drowned in the trampled slush with their faces pushed into it and a dozen pairs of boots on their backs.

He fought. He did not remember most of it. He remembered the warmth in his body, and the scrap-iron sword going in and coming out, and the shock of it up his wrists every time it met bone. He remembered that the warmth was thinner than it had been on the Vessant road, and going faster, and that his arms were growing heavy. He remembered the red-haired boy with the spear, beside him through most of it, and then not beside him, and when he looked round for him, the boy was lying on his back in the snow by the well with his spear still in his hands and his eyes open and a Tenrai sword through his belly, and there was nothing to do about it and no time to do it. He remembered the old gray-bearded veteran with the axe going into a knot of Tenrai spearmen by the barracks door, roaring, alone, and the knot closing over him, and not opening again.

He remembered, somewhere in the middle of it, hearing the ram hit the gate. And then hit it again. And then a great splintering crash, and Wystan's horn, and the roar of the Greymanes coming in through the gatehouse behind the Tenrai, and the crush in the yard breaking all at once, the way ice breaks on a river in spring.

The Tenrai fell back toward the keep.

There was a broad stone stair up to the keep door, and they went up it in a body, the last of the garrison, fifty or sixty of them, and turned at the top of it with their shields locked, and held. And the northerners came across the yard after them, and stopped at the foot of the stair, and the whole of the fighting stopped for a moment, the way a wave stops at the top of a beach before it goes back. In the sudden quiet you could hear men breathing, and men crying, and somewhere a horse screaming over and over in the burning stables.

Muken stood at the foot of the stair, with blood running off the end of the scrap-iron sword into the snow, and his chest heaving, and the stitches in his cheek torn open again and bleeding down his neck into the collar of the borrowed mail. The warmth was nearly gone out of him. His legs were shaking. He looked up at the shield wall at the top of the stair and thought, quite clearly and calmly: [Northern English accent, low and rough, inner voice, tired] *I don't have another one of these in me.*

[narrator] The shield wall opened.

---

It opened, deliberately, in the middle, two shields swinging back like a door, and a single figure came out through the gap and down three steps of the stair, and stopped.

And every man at the foot of it, northern and Tenrai alike, went still.

Muken felt it before he understood what it was. It came down the stair ahead of the figure like the heat ahead of an opened kiln, and it went into him through his chest and his face and the backs of his eyes, and the warmth that was left in his body shrank down from it the way a snail shrinks from salt. It was pressure. Something enormous pushing against the air of the yard from the inside, the way water pushes on a dam, and every man in the yard felt his knees want to bend under it. Behind him, he heard a boy of the vanguard begin to vomit. He heard somebody else drop a spear.

The figure on the stair was a woman.

She was perhaps forty. She was tall, and lean as a blade, and she wore the war-dress of the Tenrai: black lamellar laced with gold, a gold-trimmed surcoat over it, and on the breast of it, where another house would wear a boss, the banner of her line, the Tenrai heraldry, worked in gold thread. She carried no shield. She held a long, slightly curved sword loosely at her side in one hand, with the point resting on the stone of the stair, and there was no blood on it at all. Her hair was black, threaded with gray, bound up high on her head with a gold cord. Her face was long and narrow and pale and very calm, and at her brow and her temples and the hollow of her throat, the skin caught the light of the burning stables with a faint metallic sheen, like old brass.

Her eyes were the worst of it. There were rings in them. Fine bright lines, turning slowly in the dark of the iris, like the rings that spread on a pond after a stone has gone in, round and round, never stopping.

She looked down the stair at the northern army in the yard below her, at the dead in the snow and the burning stables and the broken gate and the gap in her wall, and nothing at all moved in her face.

Then she looked at Muken.

And something did.

He saw it. He would have sworn afterward that he saw it, though there was nothing to see, and nobody else in the yard ever said they had. The turning rings in her eyes stopped. All at once, as if a hand had closed on them. And her face, her long calm narrow face, went perfectly white, white as the snow, white as the face of the Zettari surveyor at the Marrow when Yukazuri showed him the mark on the frosted glass.

"[light Japanese accent, hard, whispers]You," she said.

[narrator] It was barely a whisper. It carried across the whole of the silent yard.

Muken did not answer. He did not know what he could have said.

The Moto commander came down the last steps of the stair, slowly, one at a time, and stopped on the bottom one, so that her eyes were level with his, an arm's length and a sword's length away. The pressure coming off her was so great now that he could hardly breathe in it. His eyes were watering. The heat behind his left eye had come up, sharp, like a cinder, like a coal, so bright that for a moment the whole yard went red at the edges.

She was looking into his face. Into his eyes. She looked at him the way a woman looks at a grave she has been told was empty, and has just seen move.

"[light Japanese accent, hard, shaken]Where did you get that face?" she said.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, flat]I was born with it."

"[light Japanese accent, hard, softly]No." Her voice was very soft. "[trembling]No. I knew that face. I stood behind that face for twenty years. I watched it in the war." The sword-point lifted a hand's width off the stone. "[bitter]I watched it burn the Akagane granaries at Hiroe with the children still inside. I watched it on the night of the Kōkan cellars." Her lips had gone thin and white. "[rising, furious]And I watched it on the field at Shimane when the Zettari boy put it in the mud. *I was there.* He's dead. He's *dead.*"

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, bewildered]I don't know what you're talking about," said Muken.

[narrator] He did not. He did not understand one word of it. But something in him did. Something underneath the cold and the exhaustion and the blood running down his neck, something that had always been there, at the back, in the worked-out gallery where he did not go: it heard her, and it understood, and it went still and cold and heavy, like a stone settling to the bottom of a well.

The Moto commander looked at him for one breath longer. Then the rings in her eyes began to turn again, faster now, much faster, bright as struck steel.

"[light Japanese accent, hard, formal]My name," [narrator] she said, and her voice had changed, and was the voice of her line, flat and formal and enormous, so that it filled the whole of the yard and came back off the walls, "[light Japanese accent, hard, booming, formal]is Shigure Tenrai Moto. Captain of the Foot, by the hand of Kagenori Tenrai. Daughter of the Gold Banner. Blade of the seven lines and the one blood."

[narrator] She brought the long curved sword up from the stair into both hands.

"[light Japanese accent, hard, cold, menacing]And whatever you are, boy," said Shigure Tenrai Moto, "I am going to find out what's inside you."

[narrator] Behind Muken, across the yard, at the end of the surgeons' line by the broken gate, a small figure in a gray coat had stopped with a roll of bandage in her hands, and was looking at him over the heads of the army.

He did not see her. He saw only the woman on the stair.

He lifted the scrap-iron sword.

The rings in her eyes flared gold.

She came down off the last step.

---

# Chapter Six: The Captain of the Foot

[narrator] *Book One of the Kōkan · Chapter Six: The Captain of the Foot*

She came down off the last step, and the first cut was already in the air before her foot touched the snow.

Muken never saw it start. He saw the end of it: a long bright line of curved steel coming across at the height of his neck, flat and level, faster than anything had ever come at him in his life. His body moved before he did. The scrap-iron sword came up of its own accord, the flat of it, both hands, and the curved blade struck it a hand's width from the hilt [sword ringing] with a sound like a hammer on a bell.

The shock went through his wrists and his elbows and his shoulders and into the roots of his teeth. His arms went numb to the shoulder. His feet went backward in the snow without his asking them to, one step, two, sliding, and he would have gone down on his back if the dead man behind him had not been there for his heel to catch on.

He kept his feet. He did not know how.

Shigure Tenrai Moto had not moved after the cut. She stood where she had landed, at the foot of the stair, with the long curved sword held out to the side at the end of its swing and her eyes on his face. She had not even shifted her weight. The morning was coming up hard and blue behind the keep, and it caught on her: black lamellar laced in gold, the Tenrai heraldry worked in gold thread on the banner at her breast, a gold cord at the hilt of the sword. She was the only bright thing on the stair that was not on fire. She had cut at him the way a woman reaches out to snuff a candle, and the candle had flinched, and she was looking at it with mild interest.

"[light Japanese accent, hard, mildly curious]Hm," she said.

[narrator] Behind Muken, across the yard, somebody of the vanguard started forward with a spear. Shigure did not look at him. She lifted her free hand a little from her side, palm down, the gesture of a woman settling a dog, and the pressure that was coming off her, the great slow weight against the inside of the air, *pushed*. The spearman's knees went. He went down on them in the slush with a sound like a man who has been punched in the stomach, and the spear fell out of his hands, and he knelt there with his mouth open and could not get up.

"[light Japanese accent, hard, cold]No," said Shigure Tenrai Moto. "Not you." She did not raise her voice. "[quietly]Him."

[narrator] She looked back at Muken.

"[light Japanese accent, hard, cold]Again," she said, and came.

---

[narrator] The second exchange was three cuts long, and he lived through it because of the stair.

She came in low and from the left, a rising cut toward his hip, and he got the scrap-iron down across it, and the shock jarred him again to the shoulder. She was already turning off it before the steel had finished ringing, the curved blade circling up over her head and coming down from the right toward his collarbone, and he got under that one too, barely, the edge sliding off his blade and down past his ear so close that he felt the wind of it in the torn stitches of his cheek. The third came straight down from above, a cut to split his skull, and there was nothing he could do about it at all. He was too slow. He knew it the way you know you've missed a step in the dark. His sword was in the wrong place and his weight was in the wrong place and the steel was coming down.

She cut short, by a finger's width. The curved edge came down and stopped in the air above his face, and turned, and went back up, and she stepped back onto the bottom stair with her blade at her shoulder and her eyes on him, and he stood there in the snow with his sword in the wrong place and his mouth open and his heart going like a hammer.

He did not understand it. He did not understand it for a full breath. Then he looked at her eyes.

The rings in them had stopped turning.

Every time. He saw it, now that he was looking, as he had seen the gait of the big creature on the Vessant road and the seam in the wall. The fine bright rings in the dark of her eyes turned and turned while she moved, slowly, like ripples on a pond, gold on black, and then, the instant before each cut, they stopped. They stopped dead and went bright, as a marksman's eyes fix when he takes aim. And on the last cut, the one that should have split his skull, they had stopped and gone bright, and then they had flickered, and gone out, and come back on, like a lamp in a draught.

And she had pulled the cut.

She was frowning. It was the first expression he had seen on her long pale face that was not calm. She was looking at him the way a scribe looks at a column of figures that will not add up.

"[light Japanese accent, hard, puzzled]You don't *read*," she said.

[narrator] He had no idea what she meant. He had no breath to ask.

"[light Japanese accent, hard, softly, thoughtful]Everything reads," said Shigure Tenrai Moto. She said it softly, almost to herself. "Everything that lives has its next moment in front of it, and every next moment has a line, and every line can be seen if you know how to look. A man's sword arm. A horse's foot. An arrow. A stone on a slope. I look at a man, and I see where he will be when my blade gets there, and I put my blade there." She tilted her head. "[unsettled]I have been doing it since I was nine years old. I have never once, in all that time, looked at anything that lives and seen nothing."

[narrator] She lifted the curved sword off her shoulder.

"[light Japanese accent, hard, low]I look at you," she said, "and there's nothing there. It's like reading a page with no ink on it."

[narrator] Her lips thinned.

"[light Japanese accent, hard, bitter]Gimbzo read. Gimbzo read like a forest fire. I could see him coming three moves out." The rings in her eyes began to turn again, faster. "[demanding]So what are you?"

---

[narrator] He did not answer, because he did not know, and because at that moment the heat came up behind his left eye.

It came up hard. This time it came as a brand. It came up out of somewhere at the back of his skull, out of the worked-out gallery where he never went, and it went into the eye like a nail, and for one breath he could not see out of it at all for the pain. He staggered. He felt something wet run down out of the eye onto his cheek, into the torn stitches, and did not know if it was tears or blood.

And then he could see again. And the world had changed.

It was still the yard. The snow, trampled black and red, and beyond the trampling a field of it glittering untouched under the walls. The burning stables, orange and roaring, throwing sparks up into a sky gone the hard clean blue of a northern morning. The dead. The Tenrai on the stair with their shields locked, and the northerners at the foot of it, and Wystan's gray men coming across from the broken gate. The woman in front of him in black and gold, with her long curved sword. But it was all of it laid open now, the way a frozen river is laid open when the ice is clear, so that you can see the current moving under it. He could see where everything was carrying its weight. The stair. The walls. The shields. The bodies of the living and the dead. Every one of them was a set of loads going down through bones and stones and timber into the ground, and every load went through some place that was carrying more than it could hold. He could see those places. They stood out to his left eye like cracks of light in a dark wall, a fine hot gold, the color of a forge seen through a chink.

He could see them in her.

The Tenrai captain stood on the bottom step of the keep stair with her weight on her left foot and her right foot a little forward, and her whole tall lean body balanced on that left foot like a blade balanced on a finger, beautifully, perfectly. And under the left foot, through the boot and the snow, he could see the step. The bottom step of the keep stair. Old stone, older than the Tenrai, older than the dry-stone ring of the clans, worn hollow in the middle by a thousand years of feet, with the frost got into a hairline crack along its face from the left end to the middle. The same crack. The same frost. The same thing that had taken thirty feet of curtain wall off the ridge an hour ago, smaller and quieter and only waiting.

The rings in Shigure Tenrai Moto's eyes had stopped turning.

She was staring at his left eye.

"[light Japanese accent, hard, whispers, horrified]*Shingan,*" she whispered.

[narrator] Her face had gone white again, paper-white, to the lips.

"[light Japanese accent, hard, shaken]Seven lines and one blood," she said. "Oh, no. Not *that.* Not the eye. The eye went into the dust with the rest of them." She was staring at it as if it might go out if she looked away. "[rushed]Any Moto brat can see where a wall wants to fall. That's Shingan. The gold in it isn't." Her voice cracked on it. "[voice cracking]I haven't seen gold in an eye since the cellars. I stood beside Gimbzo twenty years and never once read the red line in his eyes, in a fight or out of one, even the night he went down into the cellars after it. That eye is *Kōkan.* He thought he'd burned the last of it."

[narrator] He did not know what any of the words meant. He did not care. He was looking at the step.

---

[narrator] She came at him. She came fast, and hard, and furious, and frightened, and that was her mistake, though neither of them knew it yet.

The first cut came high, and he did not block it. He went down under it. He dropped onto one knee in the slush so suddenly that the curved blade went over his head and took a lock of his white hair with it, and from his knee, with both hands, with every last thread of warmth that was left in his body poured down into his arms and his back and his hips in one burning rush, he brought the scrap-iron sword round in a flat, low arc, aimed at the step and nowhere near her.

The pommel hit the old stone at the left end of the crack, where the frost was thickest.

He felt it give. He felt it through the iron and through his wrists and into his chest, the way he had felt the wall move under his boots: a shiver, a settling, a long low *crack* like river ice in the spring. The face of the bottom step split off along its whole length and slid forward into the snow in a single slab. And Shigure Tenrai Moto, balanced on it on her left foot like a blade on a finger, beautifully, perfectly, had nothing under her.

She went down.

She did not fall far. She was too good to fall far. She twisted in the air as the stone went out from under her and came down on her right knee and her left hand in the snow, with the curved sword still up in her right, and her face turned up toward him, and the rings in her eyes turning so fast now that they blurred into a single bright golden disc.

He was already up. He was already moving. He did not decide to. His body moved before he did, the way it always had, and the scrap-iron sword came round and down in both hands, and she got her curved blade up across it.

Steel on steel. The shock went through both of them.

But she was on one knee in the snow, with her weight wrong and her footing gone, and he was standing over her, and the cut had his whole body behind it falling forward. Her blade held. Her arm did not. The scrap-iron sword drove her own curved edge back and down into her, and her own edge bit into the top of her left shoulder where the black lamellar left the collarbone bare beneath the gorget, and went in, and stuck against the bone. Muken felt it go in through two swords. Black-gold lacing parted. Blood came up between the plates, very bright, steaming in the cold.

[hisses] She made a sound, a long hiss through her teeth, like water on a hot stone.

And then she did a thing he had not seen anyone do, and would not see again for many winters.

She let go of her sword.

She let it go, the long curved beautiful thing, and it fell out from between them into the snow, and with her free hands she took hold of the scrap-iron blade where it lay on her shoulder, both hands, bare fingers round the black pitted edge, and *pulled* it, down and to her right, past her, out of the cut, into the snow. Her palms opened on the edge. He saw her blood run down the iron. She did not seem to feel it. And as the blade went past her and down, his whole weight went with it, forward and down, off balance, and her knee came up into his belly under the mail, and all the breath went out of him at once, and he folded over it and went down in the snow on his side.

He lay there. He could not breathe. He could not get his arms under him. The warmth was gone out of him entirely, all of it, all at once, and there was nothing left in his body but cold and a great grinding emptiness, as if someone had scooped him out with a spoon.

Above him, Shigure Tenrai Moto got slowly to her feet.

She was bleeding badly. The cut at the top of her shoulder was deep, to the bone, and the blood was coming out of it through the black plates in a steady pulse and running down the gold of the banner on her breast, and her left arm hung down at her side and did not move when she moved. Her palms were laid open across both of them where she had taken the blade in her hands. She stood over him in the snow, swaying, with the turning rings in her eyes beginning to slow and dim, and looked down at him while the blood ran off her fingers and pocked the snow red.

Her own sword lay in the snow beside his head. She did not pick it up.

"[light Japanese accent, hard, quiet, shaking]You read nothing," she said. Her voice was very quiet, and shaking, and very sure. "And you have his face. And you have *the eye.*" She breathed. "[urgent]Do you understand what you are? Do you have any idea? Do they know? The old men who brought you here, the Stark, the Greymane, do they know what they've got?"

[narrator] He could not answer. He could not get the air into him to answer.

"[light Japanese accent, hard, softly]No," said Shigure. "No, you don't." Something moved in her face that might almost have been pity. "[almost pitying]Whoever's had the raising of you hasn't told you a thing."

[narrator] From across the yard, Wystan Greymane's horn sounded, very close. There were boots on the stone, a great many of them, coming fast. Men shouting in northern voices. The Tenrai shield wall on the stair above them was breaking up; Muken heard it go, the clatter of dropped shields and the scrape of retreat.

"[light Japanese accent, panicked, shouts]Captain!" someone on the stair was shouting. "[shouts]*Captain!* The postern! The postern's open, captain, we can still..."

[narrator] Shigure Tenrai Moto looked down at Muken for one breath more.

She could have killed him. He knew it. She knew he knew it. Her sword was a hand's width from her foot, and she had one good arm, and he could not lift his own. One stroke. And the face that had come down out of Gimbzo, and the eye that had come up out of the dust, would have gone back into the snow of Kharven's Foot where nobody would ever see them again.

She looked at him, at his face more than at the eye, and the rings in her eyes slowed and stopped, and something passed across her long white face that he would not understand for years.

[grunts] Then she bent, with a grunt of pain, and picked up her sword left-handed by the blade, careless of her open palm, and straightened.

"[light Japanese accent, hard, cold, strained]Kagenori will want you," she said. "Alive, if he can have you. Dead, if he can't. And so will every Moto in the seven lines, the moment they hear what I've seen." She took a step back. "[menacing]I'll be the one who tells them. Remember that, boy. When they come for you. Remember who told them."

[narrator] She turned. Two of her men came down the broken stair for her, and she put her good arm round the neck of one of them, and the three of them went up the steps and in through the keep door, fast, without looking back, and the door slammed behind them, and a bar came down across it on the inside with a sound like a coffin lid.

A moment later, from somewhere round the far side of the keep, he heard the sound of horses on stone, going away fast down a road toward the south.

Then nothing. Only boots, and northern shouting, and the burning stables, and the long low cold sound of the wind coming over the broken wall and lifting the snow off the rubble in glittering skeins.

---

[narrator] He lay on his side in the snow and watched the world come back.

It came back slowly. The cold first. Then the pain, in his belly where her knee had gone in, and in his left eye, which was weeping steadily now, something warm and thin down his cheek, and in his chest under the borrowed mail where he had not felt it until now: a long burning line from his left armpit down across his ribs. One of the cuts in the second exchange. One of the ones he had thought he blocked. The mail had turned the edge, mostly. Not all of it. He could feel the wet of it soaking into his shirt.

Somebody was kneeling beside him. Many people. Boots, and voices, and a hand on his shoulder, very big, with two fingers missing from it.

"[Yorkshire accent, gruff and warm, urgent, hoarse]*Muken.* Muken, lad, look at me. Look at me." Ulf Stark. His breath was going white over Muken's face, fast and hoarse. "[astonished]Sky above. Sky above, lad, you put a Tenrai captain on her knees. With that. With *that.*" He was looking at the scrap-iron sword in the snow. "[excited]Wystan. Wystan, come and see this. Come and see what he did to the step."

[narrator] Another face. Long and gray. Wystan Greymane was not looking at the step. He was looking at Muken's left eye, very closely, from a hand's width away, and his face had set like ground taking the frost, in a way that Muken would remember long afterward.

"[soft Scottish accent, dry, quietly]Ulf," said Wystan Greymane quietly. "[grave]Look at his eye."

[narrator] Ulf looked.

Neither man spoke. Behind them the stable roof fell in, and a column of sparks went up gold into the blue.

Muken could not see his own eye. He would not see it for days, until he found a scrap of polished steel in the fort's armory and looked into it, and saw what they had seen: a crack in the slate-gray of the left iris, low, fine as a hair, running out from the amber fleck that had always been there toward the edge, bright as a seam of gold in a stone. It would fade, over the next week, until he could barely find it. It would never go entirely.

"[soft Scottish accent, dry, brisk]The keep," said Wystan, and stood up, and his voice was ordinary again, an old soldier's voice giving orders. "[commanding]She's gone out the postern with what's left of them. Ten, maybe twelve. Let her go. We've no horses to catch her with and no men to spare." He looked round the yard, at the dead. "Get the gate up. Get the stables out before it takes the barracks with it. Get a count."

"[Yorkshire accent, gruff and warm, low]Wystan."

"[soft Scottish accent, dry, firm]Later," said Wystan Greymane. He did not look at Muken's eye again. "[quietly]Later, Ulf."

---

[narrator] The count came in at noon.

The Tenrai garrison of Kharven's Foot had been two hundred. Sixty-one of them were dead in the yard and on the walls and in the rubble of the breach. Eighty more had thrown down their arms in the barracks when the gate went and were sitting now in the snow of the yard with their hands on their heads, under the spears of gray-bearded Greymane men. The rest had gone out through the postern with their captain, or over the north wall on ropes, or nobody knew where.

The north had come up the ridge six hundred strong. Fifty-two of them would not go down it again.

[softly] Thirty of those fifty-two had been in the vanguard.

They laid them in a row along the inside of the curtain wall, by the breach, where the light came in, with their faces to the sky. The noon sun came through the gap in a long white bar, and the frost on their mail and in their hair caught it and glittered, so that the row of them shone as if someone had dressed them for a feast. Thirty of them. Most of them were boys. The thin red-haired one with the spear was third from the end. Somebody had closed his eyes and crossed his hands on the spear-shaft, and somebody else had taken the rags out of his helmet and folded them and put them under his head, as if that would make the ground softer.

Muken walked down the row.

He walked it slowly, because he could not walk it any other way, with his left arm strapped across his chest and his ribs bound under his shirt and a pad of wool tied over his left eye. He stopped at every one. He did not know most of their names. He made himself learn them. He asked the men who were standing nearest, at each one, *What was he called,* and they told him, and he said the name aloud, once, and went on to the next.

At the end of the row, someone was waiting for him.

She was sitting on a fallen block of the breach with her kit open on her knees and her gray coat buttoned to the throat, and her hands were red to the wrists, and there was blood dried brown on her face and in the white of her braid. She had been working since the gate went down. She looked about a hundred years old. She looked about nine.

She looked up at him, at the strapped arm and the bound ribs and the pad over his eye, and her small grave face did not change at all.

"[young girl, Northern English accent, sharp, tired, firm]Sit," said Yukazuri.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, weary]I'm all right."

"[young girl, Northern English accent, sharp, clipped]You're not. You've opened your face again and your ribs are bleeding through the binding and you're walking like Rob Stark." She pointed at the block of stone beside her. "[firm]*Sit.*"

[narrator] He sat.

She reached up without a word and untied the pad from over his left eye, gently, and looked into the eye, close and unhurried, as she looked into wounds. He felt her breath on his face. He felt his own breathing slow down, the way it always did, and the grinding emptiness in his chest ease, a little, for the first time since the stair.

She did not say anything about the crack in his iris. She looked at it, and he saw her see it, and he saw her decide not to say anything, and he loved her for it more than he had ever loved anyone.

Then she tied the pad back on, and took a curved needle out of the kit on her knees, and threaded it, and turned his face toward the light with two red fingers under his jaw.

"[young girl, Northern English accent, sharp, gentle, dry]Hold still," said Yukazuri. "[wry]You can dislike me when it closes."

---

# Chapter Seven: Crows

[narrator] *Book One of the Kōkan · Chapter Seven: Crows*

The word went out of Kharven's Foot the way word always went in the north: faster than horses.

It went out in a teamster's mouth to the Marrow crossing, and in a courier's satchel to Hollenmark, and in a frightened Tenrai deserter's to the first village down the southern road, where he drank himself senseless in the common room and told everyone who would listen about the wall that fell down on its own and the boy with the gold in his eye. It went out by crow. Ulf Stark's crows went west to the Ridgewall and north to the Keld fires, and two of them went south and east, one to the Mahuo post at Sum-gol and one past it, over the low country and the river and the long climb of cedar forest, to a valley where the trees stood so close and so tall that the light came down between them in thin bars of green-gold.

And it went out on a horse, south down the Kessen road, with a woman on it in black-and-gold Tenrai lamellar with her left arm strapped to her chest and her palms bound in bloody linen, who did not stop to sleep for four days.

By the end of the first week, everyone who mattered in the Inner World knew that Kharven's Foot had fallen.

By the end of the second, a few of them knew who had taken it.

---


---

### II · The Hall of Verdicts

The Hall of Verdicts stood on the crown of the rock Kharven-Seat was built on, and the rock hummed. The draw came up through it from the great main under the hold, a low note felt in the teeth, and fed the worked lamps that hung in iron cages from the ring overhead, each with its Guild seal in brass on the collar, burning a clean smokeless gold. It fed the lines under the floor too, so that the dark stone was warm underfoot while frost stood thick on the great doors outside. Round the circle of script on the floor stood the seven chairs, each in its line's colors: Tenrai gold, Shirogane silver, Kurenai black and ash, Akagane copper and red-bronze, Amagiri indigo and mist-gray, Byakuya near-white hung with glass beads, and the Kōkan red.

Kagenori Tenrai Moto did not shout. He had not shouted in thirty years. He sat in the gold chair, under the Tenrai banner, its heraldry worked in gold thread on black and lit gold from below, with his narrow face perfectly still and his mouth closed like a purse, and the whole of his rage came off him in a slow, steady pressure that made the lamps in the great iron ring overhead burn low and blue in their cages.

"[light Japanese accent, smooth, cold, softly]Commoners," he said.

[narrator] He said it softly. It went round the hall and came back down out of the dome.

"[light Japanese accent, smooth, contemptuous]We lost," said Kagenori Tenrai, "to commoners."

[narrator] Three of the chairs were filled. Shizuha Shirogane sat in silver at his right, upright and iron-haired and hatchet-faced, lamplight running down her silk sleeves like water down a blade, and had not spoken since the hall was opened. Sumie Kurenai sat in black and ash across from him, pale as tallow, the crimson lining of her sleeves showing only when she moved. The Akagane chair, the Amagiri chair and the Byakuya chair were empty, their copper and indigo and beaded white catching the light. So was the seventh, the red one, under its dust, with its blind enamel lion, its lacquer still the deep red of a coal under ash wherever the dust had slid. Nobody looked at it. Everybody knew exactly where it was.

In the center of the circle of script, where prisoners knelt, there was a stool, and on the stool sat Shigure Tenrai Moto.

She had ridden four days to get here and had not changed her armor. The black lamellar was stiff with dried blood down the whole left side, where the cut at the top of her shoulder had bled and bled and been bound and bled again. Her left arm was strapped across her chest. Her palms were wrapped in linen gone brown. Her face was gray with exhaustion and loss of blood, and the rings in her eyes turned very slowly, dimly, like the last ripples on a pond long after the stone has gone in. She had told her story once, from the beginning to the end, in a flat level voice, without being asked a single question. Now she sat and waited.

"[light Japanese accent, smooth, cold]They took our fort," Kagenori said. "The Foot. The gate on the Kessen road. Two hundred of our house-guard on a ridge that has never fallen to anybody, on walls the old clans laid in our grandfathers' grandfathers' time and our own house raised again in good square stone. And they took it in a morning. Stark and Greymane, with their old men and their boys." He did not move. "[venomous]Led by a tokenless. A *tokenless*. Do you understand what that does? Do you understand how that makes us look? Every village between the Thresh and the shield is going to hear it before the month's out. The great Moto, the seven lines and the one blood, the house that reads the world. Beaten off its own wall by a boy who can't sign for a sack of meal."

[narrator] He let it lie.

"[light Japanese accent, smooth, quiet, pointed]And the boy is sixteen," he said. "Sixteen years old." His eyes moved, slowly, toward Sumie Kurenai. "[insinuating]Isn't that a coincidence."

[narrator] Sumie did not look away.

"[light Japanese accent, low, dark, quiet]It isn't a coincidence," she said.

[narrator] Her voice was quiet. She had not slept, either, by the look of her. She sat in the black chair with her thin hands closed in her lap and her ink-dark eyes on the woman on the stool.

"[light Japanese accent, low, dark, intent]Think about it, Kagenori," Sumie said. "Think about it properly, for once, instead of how it looks. Sixteen years. You know what was carried out of Halcrest sixteen years ago as well as I do. You and I put our names to the same silence over it." She did not look at Shizuha Shirogane, and Shizuha, in the silver chair, did not look away from her. "[grave]If the Stark was right, at the end, kneeling there with his blood on the script, then it lived. And it grew. And it has come back up the Kessen road." She looked at the stool. "[urgent]And then think about who he fought. Shigure is the best blade the Tenrai have put in the field in a generation. She was Gimbzo's right hand for twenty years. There aren't ten people in the Inner World who could put her on her knees in a straight fight, and every one of them is a name we know." She drew a breath. "[certain]Nobody beats Shigure that easily. Nobody nameless. It's the Kōkan child. It has to be."

"[light Japanese accent, hard, tired, flat]He didn't beat me *easily*," said Shigure Tenrai Moto.

[narrator] They all looked at her.

"[light Japanese accent, hard, flat]He beat me," she said. "I'll not dress it. He put me on my knees and he put my own blade into my shoulder. But not easily. Not by being stronger. He isn't. He's barely woken. He's running on a reinforcement a first-year Tenrai cadet would be beaten for." Her voice was flat and level, worn thin by four days in the saddle. "[haunted]He beat me because I couldn't read him. I looked at him, and there was nothing. There was no line on him, no thread, no next moment at all. In twenty years of fighting I have never once looked at anything that lives and seen nothing at all." She turned her dim turning eyes on Sumie. "[pointed]Your register couldn't read them. Neither could I."

[narrator] Sumie Kurenai had gone white to the lips.

"[light Japanese accent, hard, quiet]And then the eye opened," said Shigure.

[narrator] The lamps in the great iron ring overhead dipped, all at once, and the draw-hum in the rock under them faltered half a note and came back.

"[light Japanese accent, hard, low]I saw it," Shigure said. "The left eye. In the middle of the fight. Gold came up through the iris, like a crack running through slate." She was quiet for a moment. "[slowly]I've seen that eye before, Kagenori. Once. In the cellars under Halcrest, sixteen years ago, the night Gimbzo went down with a lamp after the last of his own line. Gold, coming up through the gray in the eyes of the ones he cornered there, right to the end." She held his gaze. "[pointed]You know what else went up out of that cellar alive. You had it from me the next morning." Her voice did not change. "[flat, certain]The boy has that eye. And he has Gimbzo's face. I'd know that face if I were dead. I stood behind it for twenty years."

[narrator, long pause] The hall held its breath. Somewhere under the floor a heated line ticked as it cooled.

Shizuha Shirogane, in the silver chair, turned her head very slowly and looked for the first time at the empty red chair with its blind lion.

"[light Japanese accent, smooth, cold]Then we have to be rid of them," Kagenori said. "Both of them. Quickly."

[narrator] His voice had not risen. But the pressure coming off him had, and the blue lamps flattened in their cages, and Shigure on her stool closed her eyes for a moment against it.

"[light Japanese accent, smooth, intense]If he's what you say he is," Kagenori said, "if he really is Gimbzo's get, with the Kōkan eye in his head, then he's going to *grow.* Every fight he walks into is going to wake more of it. Every one. He woke the eye on the stair of the Foot. What does he wake in the next one? And the one after that?" He leaned forward, very slightly, in the gold chair. "[low, menacing]He isn't a boy. He's a Kōkan who doesn't know he's a Kōkan yet. And the day he finds out, he'll walk into this hall and sit in that chair, and every one of us will have to kneel to him on our own script."

[narrator] He sat back.

"[light Japanese accent, smooth, very softly, bitter]Damn the Starks," he said, very softly. "Damn the Greymanes. We should have ended both those houses when the war was young, root and branch, and every cousin who could sit a horse. I said so at the time. Gimbzo wouldn't hear it. He wanted them for his front line." His mouth thinned. "[contemptuous]And now look."

[narrator] He was quiet for a moment, looking at nothing. Then something moved in his narrow face, and his eyes narrowed with it.

"[light Japanese accent, smooth, realizing]The Ridgewall," he said.

[narrator] Shizuha Shirogane turned her head back toward him.

"[light Japanese accent, smooth, rising]It all makes sense now," Kagenori said. "Why the Ridgewall's been left to fall. Three years, and the Greymanes have pulled their men back off the western wall a few at a time, and we've all said it was because they had no men left to hold it with." His fingers tapped once on the arm of the gold chair. "[cold]They had men. They had men enough to put three hundred on the Foot. They've been letting the wall go on purpose. Letting Undaar-Keth come through the passes in the west and raid down into our own lands, so that we'd have to send the house-guard west to meet them. So that when the north rose, we'd be fighting on two fronts. Three." He looked at Shizuha. "They've been opening the back door for three years and we've been thanking them for closing it."

"[light Japanese accent, elderly woman, cold, precise]It's what I would have done," said Shizuha Shirogane.

[narrator] It was the first thing she had said. Kagenori weighed her across the circle of script, and then, very slightly, inclined his head.

"[light Japanese accent, smooth, calculating]Then we do what they won't expect," he said. "We don't fight the north. Not yet. We buy it."

[narrator] He turned to the Tenrai steward standing at the edge of the hall.

"[light Japanese accent, smooth, commanding]Send to Ashmore," he said. "To Thornwall. To Hallenfeld. And to the Keld fires, if any crow of ours can find them. Bring their lords here. Under the house's protection, with the house's gifts, and every courtesy the seven lines can show." His voice was very soft. "[softly]Ashmore wants to sell its grain to whoever pays. We'll pay. Thornwall wants the Ore Council off its back. We'll take it off. Hallenfeld wants its tariff-master back out of our cells. They can have him." He paused. "[wry]And the Keld want to be left alone. We'll promise to leave them alone. For ever. In writing."

"[light Japanese accent, low, dark, quietly]And then?" said Sumie Kurenai quietly.

"[light Japanese accent, smooth, cold]And then the north has two houses. Stark and Greymane. Against everyone." Kagenori looked at the empty chairs. "And then we call the whole council. All seven lines. All of them." His eyes went to the Akagane chair, the Amagiri chair, the Byakuya chair, standing empty in their copper and their indigo and their near-white. "[hard]Every one of them. In this hall. Whether they wish to come or not."

[narrator] He stood.

"[light Japanese accent, smooth, quiet]Shigure," he said.

[narrator] She opened her eyes.

"[light Japanese accent, smooth, cold]You let him live," said Kagenori Tenrai Moto.

[narrator] It was not a question. Shigure did not pretend it was.

"[light Japanese accent, hard, flat]I did," she said.

"[light Japanese accent, smooth, dangerous]Why?"

[narrator] She looked up at him from the stool, gray-faced, with her ruined hands in her lap and her strapped arm across the gold banner on her breast.

"[light Japanese accent, hard, tired, steady]Because you'd have wanted to see him first," she said. "Before you killed him. You'd have wanted to know for certain." Her dim eyes held his. "[quietly]And because I did."

[narrator, long pause] Kagenori looked at her until the lamps burned lower.

Then he walked out of the hall, and did not look back, and the blue lamps in the great iron ring slowly, slowly, came up to their ordinary gold again behind him, and the gold ran back across the seven chairs one color at a time.

---

### III · The White House

The Byakuya kept their seat in the old city below the Moto hold, in a house of pale stone with a garden of white gravel and glass, where glass beads hung in the white-barked trees and threw small cold rainbows across the gravel. Kiyome Byakuya had not left it in eleven days.

She had not been to the Hall of Verdicts since the night of Torvald Stark. She had not answered the summons that came the morning after Shigure rode in, or the one that came the morning after that. She sat in the long pale room at the back of her house that her grandmother had used for prayer, with the shutters open on the garden and the glass beads of the veil laid on the floor beside her, and did not eat very much, and waited.

When Tetsugen Akagane and Hayato Amagiri came to her, they came at night, by the garden gate, without servants, in plain cloaks over their house colors, copper showing at Tetsugen's throat and Amagiri indigo at Hayato's cuff, the way men come to a house when they do not want it known that they have come.

She received them in the prayer room. There were three cushions on the floor and a single worked lamp under a shade of milk-glass, turned down to a pearl glow, and a pot of tea, poured hot from the house line, that nobody touched. The floor was warm under the mats. Somewhere in the walls the draw ran in its sealed conduit with a hum so low it was felt in the floor more than heard. Tetsugen lowered his great bulk onto his cushion with a grunt, like a bull lying down, and sat with his huge copper-ringed hands on his knees. Hayato did not sit at all at first. He stood by the open shutters with his restless fingers moving at his sides and looked out into the dark garden, and only sat when Kiyome looked at him, and then on the very edge of the cushion, as if he expected to have to leave in a hurry.

"[light Japanese accent, calm, warm, tired]You heard," Kiyome said.

"[light Japanese accent, flat, iron, deep]The whole city's heard." Tetsugen's voice was a low rumble. "Shigure rode into the outer court four days off the Kessen road with her arm hanging and her hands cut to the bone, and told it to the gate guard before she told it to Kagenori. Half the Tenrai house-guard will have it in every tavern from here to the river by morning."

"[light Japanese accent, quick, wry, hushed]A Kōkan," Hayato said quietly. "A living Kōkan. With the eye."

[narrator] Kiyome closed her eyes.

They did not know. Neither of them knew, not really. They were of the Akagane and the Amagiri, and to them the Kōkan were a king in the saddle and a chair nobody sat in, and then, at the war's end, a body in the mud at Shimane. But the Byakuya had been something else to the Kōkan, once, a very long time ago, before the Recension and the long diminishment and the closing of the book. The near-white line had stood behind the red one. Its veils and its glass and its halos had been the Kōkan's shield, in the oldest days, the ones nobody wrote down any more, and the oaths of that protection had been spoken over every Byakuya child for a thousand years, through all the long thinning of the red line, because nobody had ever thought to stop. Kiyome had said them herself, at nine years old, kneeling on this floor, in this room, with her grandmother's hand on her head. [light Japanese accent, calm, warm, slowly, reverent] *Purity before power. Peace before the blade. And the red line before the white, until the end of the red line.* [narrator] Her sister had said them too, and had meant them further than any Byakuya in a hundred years, and had stood at the last Kōkan king's shoulder as his sworn guard until the night of the cellars took her out of the world.

She had never once in her life imagined that the end of the red line might not have come with him at Shimane.

"[light Japanese accent, calm, warm, firm]I'll not go to the council," she said.

[narrator] Hayato's head came round.

"[light Japanese accent, quick, wry, alarmed]Kiyome."

"[light Japanese accent, calm, warm, steady]I'll not go. Not to this one, nor to any of them." She opened her eyes. "You know what he's going to do in there. You know what he's going to ask for. He's going to bring the northern lords to the hall with gifts in their hands and buy them out from under the Starks one by one. And then he's going to call the whole house together and stand up in front of the seven chairs and ask us to vote to hunt down a sixteen-year-old boy, and his sister, and kill them both, and close the book again." Her hands had tightened on her knees. "[bitter]I sat in that hall and watched him take a man's head on the script for telling us the truth. I'll not sit there and watch him do it again."

[narrator] Tetsugen was silent. Hayato looked at the floor.

"[light Japanese accent, flat, iron, deep]Then where?" Tetsugen said at last. "If not the hall. Where do you mean to go?"

[narrator] Kiyome looked at him.

"[light Japanese accent, calm, warm, quiet]East," she said. "To the Yukari."

[narrator, sighs] Hayato let out a breath. "[light Japanese accent, quick, wry, incredulous]Kiyome. You can't be serious."

"[light Japanese accent, calm, warm, calm]I'm entirely serious."

"[light Japanese accent, quick, wry, rushed]The Zettari are there." His fingers had begun moving again. "The Sovereign of Kushara has been sitting at Naiser Yukari's table for a week, by every report. Making peace. Making marriages. Three Moto lords riding into the Yukari enclave while the Zettari are still in it?" He shook his head. "[wry]They'll think it's an embassy. Or an assassination. Or a defection. And they'll be right about one of them."

"[light Japanese accent, calm, warm, measured]The Zettari may still be there," Kiyome agreed. "And they may not. Either way, Hayato, it's the only door left in the Inner World that isn't Kagenori's." She leaned forward. "[earnest]The Yukari keep the spirit side. They've always known more about the Kōkan than they've ever said. And if a Kōkan child has come back into the world, they'll know it before anyone. They may know it already."

[narrator] Tetsugen rubbed his bald head slowly with one enormous hand.

"[light Japanese accent, flat, iron, deep, skeptical]And what do we say to them," he said, "when we get there? Three Moto lords. Our house just lost its war with the Zettari and took the head of a Stark on the floor of its own hall. What do we tell Naiser Yukari, who's just beaten the Seikai into the dirt, that's going to make him trust anything that comes out of a Moto mouth?"

[narrator, pause] Kiyome let the lamp burn a while before she answered.

"[light Japanese accent, calm, warm, quietly]The truth," she said. "That we were wrong."

[narrator] Neither of them answered.

"[light Japanese accent, calm, warm, steady]You know what Kagenori's doing isn't right," she said. "Both of you. You've known it since before Torvald. You voted for his head anyway." She looked from one to the other, steadily, without anger. "[heavy]And you knew what Gimbzo was doing was wrong. All of us did. The granaries at Hiroe. The cellars. The burnings. Every one of us sat in those chairs and knew, and voted, and went home and slept." Her voice did not rise. "[sorrowful]And we didn't stop it. Not one of us. We told ourselves it was the house's will and the house's hand. And now there's a boy in the north with Gimbzo's face and a Kōkan's eye, and the only reason he's alive at all is that nobody in the seven lines ever had the courage to stop the man who made him."

[narrator] The lamp burned between them. In the garden, the glass hanging in the white trees chimed very faintly in a breath of wind.

"[light Japanese accent, calm, warm, resolute]I'm going east," Kiyome Byakuya said. "Before Kagenori's council sits. I'd rather you came with me. I'd rather have the Akagane and the Amagiri beside me at that gate than go alone. But I'll go alone if I have to."

[narrator] Tetsugen Akagane looked at her, and went on looking, his heavy face red-bronze in the pearl light.

Then he looked down at his great copper-ringed hands on his knees, and opened them, and looked at the palms, as if there were something written on them.

"[light Japanese accent, flat, iron, deep, low]I didn't hang him," he said.

[narrator] Kiyome frowned. "[light Japanese accent, calm, warm, puzzled]Who?"

"[light Japanese accent, flat, iron, deep, heavy]Ren. My soldier. The one who wouldn't burn the granaries at Tōmi." Tetsugen did not look up. "[low]Kagenori told me to dispose of him. I disposed of him. I put him on a horse in the dark with a purse and the name of a man at the Marrow crossing and told him if I ever saw his face again I'd kill him myself." His jaw worked. "[gruff]I've not told anyone that. I'm telling you."

[narrator] He lifted his head.

"[light Japanese accent, flat, iron, deep, heavily]I'll come," he said heavily. "Sky help me. I'll come."

[narrator] Kiyome turned to Hayato.

The young lord of the Amagiri sat on the edge of his cushion with his restless fingers gone still at last, looking at the open shutters, at the dark garden and the glass chiming in the white trees, and beyond them the black shape of the Moto hold on its rock, with the aurora standing over it in slow folds of green and violet, and the city's lamps strung gold down the rock along the mains, and at the top the lamps of the Hall of Verdicts, still burning.

"[light Japanese accent, quick, wry, very quiet]I've been mapping the roads out for a year," he said. His voice was very quiet. "Every one of them. Every pass, every ford, every way out of the western shield that a man could ride by night without being seen from the hold. I told myself it was the Amagiri's office. Route-reading. Escape." [laughs softly] He laughed, softly, without any humor in it at all. "[rueful]I think I was just getting ready to run."

[narrator] He looked at Kiyome.

"[light Japanese accent, quick, wry]Well," said Hayato Amagiri. "[wry, resolved]Now I know which way."

---

# Interlude · The White Knot

[narrator] *The Cedar Enclave · The Moto Arrive*

They came in a day and a half.

The crows had said three days. The crows had been counting by the western road, the way any sane rider would come, down out of the shield and across the low country and up the long valley through the villages. The Moto did not come by the western road. They came out of the forest on the north side of the enclave, by a path through the cedars that Naevra had walked a hundred times as a girl and had never once seen a horse on, a deer track over a shoulder of the hill that ended in a gully nobody used. Twenty riders in plain gray cloaks, with no banners, picking their way down the gully in single file in the blue hour before dawn. The wards had felt them coming an hour before the sentries saw them. By the time the house woke, they were already standing at the north gate with their horses' heads down and steaming, waiting to be let in.

[soft Korean accent, measured, inner voice] *Hayato's roads,* [narrator] Naevra thought, when the runner brought her the word. [soft Korean accent, measured, inner voice] *He found a way through the hills that we didn't know we had.*

[narrator] She was not sure whether that frightened her or not. She decided, on the way to the gate, that it ought to.

---

The north gate was the smallest of the enclave's four: two posts of dark cedar and a crossbeam carved with the crow, and under the crow the old words of the house cut small into the grain, *a crow perches, a crow watches, a crow remembers*, and a pair of plain wooden doors, and beyond them a stretch of trampled snow where the gully came out of the trees, blue in the dawn and glittering where the first light reached it. The house-guard had opened the doors and stood back from them, as Naiser had ordered, and Naevra walked out through them alone, in dove-gray, barefoot on the cold boards of the threshold, with her white hair in its braid and nothing in her hands.

The wards rose round her as she stepped through. She felt them reach out across the snow and touch the riders, and weigh them, and settle. Caution, very deep. Exhaustion. Grief, in some of them, old and worn smooth. And under all of it, in the one at the front, something so still and so clean that the wards went over it like water over glass and found nowhere at all to catch.

The one at the front had already dismounted. She stood a little ahead of the others, in the trampled snow, with her horse's rein looped over one arm.

She was tall. As tall as Naevra, nearly, and as pale, so that standing face to face on either side of the threshold they might have been the same white figure and its reflection in dark water. Her traveling clothes were near-white, layered, plain, the white of fresh linen and not of silk, stained gray at the hem from the road. The glass veil of her line was pinned back from her face and the beads of it hung at her temples, and they clicked very faintly when she moved her head. At the crown of her head the halo-ring of pale metal caught the first rose of the dawn, and the glass beads took it too and threw it back in small colored sparks, rose and lavender and a cold clear green. The sheen of the Moto blood lay faint as frost along her brow and at her temples, and her eyes were gray, light gray, the gray of rain on a window, with fine silver rings in them that lay motionless as the face of a deep well.

At her left hip, in a white scabbard bound with white cord, she wore a katana.

Behind her, still in the saddle, a huge bald man in a gray cloak with copper showing at the cuffs. And beside him, already off his horse and standing very close to it, a slight sharp man with restless fingers who kept glancing back up the gully the way they had come.

Naevra looked at the woman in white across the threshold.

"[soft Korean accent, measured, softly]Kiyome," she said.

[narrator] The silver rings in Kiyome Byakuya's eyes moved, once, very slightly, and were still again.

"[light Japanese accent, calm, warm, quiet]Naevra."

[narrator] They stood there on either side of the threshold, in the cold, and neither of them moved.

"[soft Korean accent, measured, level]We haven't met," said Naevra, "since we were children."

[narrator] She heard her own voice say it and was surprised by how level it sounded. She was not level. There was a summer in her, behind her breastbone, that she had not thought about in twenty years, and it had come up all at once at the sight of that face, whole and bright and unbearable, the way a smell will bring a whole room back.
