A white house in the old city below the Moto hold. A garden of white gravel with glass hung in the trees, chiming in every wind. A long pale prayer room with the shutters open. Two girls, one dark-browed Yukari and one white Byakuya, lying on their stomachs on the cool floor of that prayer room with their chins in their hands, swearing to be friends for ever, and the glass in the trees going on chiming over them all that long white summer. They had been nine, the last summer before Hiroe, when the houses still swapped their children for a season as if the war were weather and would pass.

"[light Japanese accent, calm, warm, quiet]No," said Kiyome. "We haven't."

[narrator] She looked down at the threshold between them, at Naevra's bare feet on the cedar boards.

"[light Japanese accent, calm, warm, sorrowful]Hiroe came between us," she said. "Hiroe and everything after it. Between our houses. Between everyone." She raised her eyes again. "[regretful]I'm sorry, Naevra. I wasn't much of a friend to you, after. I never wrote. When your envoys came to my gate, I gave them tea and sent them home alive, and I told myself that was a kind of letter. It wasn't." Her voice did not change. "[rueful]And now here we are again, after all of it, and it's not a summer and we're not nine. It's a political crisis on your doorstep with twenty riders behind it." [pause] She paused. "[careful]My presence here must trouble you. And your people. I know what it means for a Moto to come to this gate so soon after the war. I know what the Seikai did. I know what we did."

[narrator] Naevra shook her head.

"[soft Korean accent, measured, gently]You don't trouble me," she said.

[narrator] It was true. She felt it, saying it. Whatever else was in her, it was not that.

"[soft Korean accent, measured, careful]I know how delicate this is," she said. "I know exactly how delicate. I've been reading the threads of it for three days." She drew a breath. "[urgent]But I have to ask you something. Before you come in. Before you sit at my brother's table, and before the Zettari hear it from anyone but us. I have to know, Kiyome, and I need you to tell me true, the way you'd have told me on that floor when we were nine."

[narrator] Kiyome waited.

"[soft Korean accent, measured, quiet, intent]Is Muken really Gimbzo's son?" Naevra said. "Gimbzo's son, and your elder sister's?"

[narrator] The glass beads at Kiyome's temples clicked, once, very softly, as she nodded.

"[light Japanese accent, calm, warm, softly]He is," she said.

[narrator] Behind her, the big man in the saddle shifted his weight, and his horse blew.

"[light Japanese accent, calm, warm, steady]He is," Kiyome said again. "Gimbzo's son, and my sister's. My sister Kasane." Her voice was very quiet now. "[very quiet]And they had a daughter too. Younger. Her name is Yukazuri."

[narrator] The wind came down the gully out of the cedars and moved the plain gray cloaks of the riders and set the glass beads at Kiyome's temples chiming.

"[light Japanese accent, calm, warm, remembering]Kasane was the elder of us by twelve years," Kiyome said. "Kasane Byakuya. She heard the Telling in this valley when she was fourteen and took your house's name, and she answered to it till she died. And she was the one who was sworn. The red line before the white. When the time came for a Byakuya to stand at the Kōkan king's side in the old way, as his sworn guard, it was Kasane they sent." She looked away, past Naevra, into the enclave, at the dark roofs and the cedars. "[heavy]And the king she was sent to stand beside was Gimbzo. And when Gimbzo turned on his own line, and put the Kōkan into the ground one by one for telling him no, Kasane was the one standing closest to him. She couldn't stop it. Nobody could." She was silent for a moment. "[softly, sorrowful]She loved him. I was a girl, and even I could see it. She loved him the whole way down, through all of it, and on the night of the cellars she walked up out of them on her own feet with his son inside her coat."

[narrator] Naevra did not speak.

"[light Japanese accent, calm, warm, quiet]From what I know," Kiyome said, "she went into the salt. Halcrest, the Orrel cuttings, under no name the clerks would take, and she cut salt there sixteen years and raised the boy in the galleries. Gimbzo found her once more, when the boy was three. The girl is his." Her voice caught, very slightly, and she waited until it was steady again. "[voice catching]She died of the salt-throat not two months ago, on the morning the news of Shimane came down the galleries." She drew a breath. "[pained]I looked. I want you to know that. I looked for her for years and never found her, and when the council closed the book on the Kōkan I let myself believe she had gone into it with them, and the boy too. It was a Stark who kept her. Yusif Stark. He'd ridden with Gimbzo in the war, and he'd been a big brother to Kasane since she was a girl at the king's side, and after the cellars he turned his horse round and never rode with Gimbzo again. He went down into the salt once a season for years. He shielded her, and he half raised her children, and he never once gave them his name, because his name would have led the hunters straight to them." She looked down at her hands. "[softly]He's three years dead. The crows didn't say how."

[narrator] She looked back at Naevra.

"[light Japanese accent, calm, warm, steady]That's all I know," she said. "That's everything. Some of it I've had from the Starks themselves, in the last ten days, by crows that shouldn't have been able to find me. Some of it I've had from Shigure Tenrai, in front of the whole council, without her knowing what she was telling me." Her gray eyes held Naevra's. "[fierce, emotional]My sister's son took the Foot. My sister's son has the eye. And he doesn't know any of it: who his father was, who his mother was, or that he has an aunt sitting in a white chair in the Moto hold who'd have given anything in the world to know he was alive."

---

[narrator] Behind her, Hayato Amagiri took a step forward. His restless fingers had gone still. His narrow face had the look of a man who has been holding something in all night and can hold it no longer.

"[light Japanese accent, quick, wry, urgent]Kiyome," he said. "Before we go in..."

[narrator] Kiyome turned her head and looked at him.

It was a very small look. It did not last long. Naevra, standing in the threshold, felt it go past her like a cold draught from an opened door, and saw Hayato stop where he stood as if he had walked into a wall.

He did not say anything else. But Naevra saw his face, and read it as clearly as she would have read a page. [light Japanese accent, quick, wry, inner voice, anxious] *We don't have the strength for this,* [narrator] it said. [light Japanese accent, quick, wry, inner voice] *We haven't got another war in us, none of us, Amagiri or Akagane or Byakuya. If this goes wrong in there, if the Zettari take it badly, if Kagenori hears where we are before we're home, there's nothing left to fight it with.*

[narrator] And Kiyome's face, turning back, said just as clearly that she knew. That she had never once thought otherwise. That she had not come here to fight anything.

She had come to keep a promise. To make sure that whatever happened in the world now, her sister's son would have friends in it. The Yukari. The Zettari, if they would have him. A door in the east that the Tenrai could not close. Unity between this house and the white one, and through it, perhaps, one day, between this house and all seven. Alliances for a boy who did not know he needed them.

[soft Korean accent, measured, inner voice] *The red line before the white,* [narrator] Naevra thought. [soft Korean accent, measured, inner voice, slowly] *Until the end of the red line.*

[narrator] She stepped back out of the threshold and turned, so that her shoulder was to the open gate.

"[soft Korean accent, measured, formal]Then come," she said. "This way. My brother is waiting for you in the long hall." [pause] She paused. "[carefully]And the Sovereign of Kushara with him. And the Crown Prince."

[narrator] Hayato's face went a shade paler. The big bald man in the saddle [sighs] let out a long breath through his nose and began, heavily, to dismount.

"[light Japanese accent, calm, warm]Guide us, then," said Kiyome Byakuya.

[narrator] Naevra hesitated, on the cedar boards. It hardly wanted saying. They knew. Every Moto in the Inner World knew. But it was the custom, and the custom was the house, and the house was hers to keep.

"[soft Korean accent, measured, formal, slowly]We ask," she said, "that no weapon be drawn on our sacred ground."

[narrator] Kiyome's hand went to the hilt of the katana at her hip.

She was the only one of the twenty who was armed. Naevra had felt it as they came through the wards: the rest of them had left their steel with the horses, and the big man had nothing at his belt but a knife for meat, and Hayato had nothing at all. Only Kiyome, at the front, with her white blade at her side.

She did not draw it. She took the white cord that bound the scabbard, and, with three quick turns of her long pale fingers, knotted it round the guard of the hilt and the mouth of the scabbard together, tight, so that the sword could not leave its sheath without the cord being cut. Then she let her hand fall away.

"[light Japanese accent, calm, warm, gentle]The white knot," she said. "My grandmother taught it to me. A Byakuya ties it when she goes into a house where she means to keep the peace, whatever comes." She looked at Naevra. "[warm, sincere]You have no reason to worry about this, Naevra. None. I give you my word on it."

[narrator] Naevra looked at the knot for a moment. Then she nodded, and turned, and led them in.

---

[narrator] Naiser was standing when they came into the long hall.

He had not meant to stand. He had meant to receive them seated, behind the low table, the way he had received the Zettari. But when the screens at the north end of the hall slid back and his sister came through them with three Moto lords behind her, in their road-stained gray, and the cold of the north gate coming in with them, he found that he was on his feet, and could not quite remember getting there. The hall's worked lamps were still lit against the dawn, honey-gold in their cedar frames, and the light of them ran warm along the red-brown beams and lay on the road-stained cloaks of the three as they came.

The Sovereign of Kushara and the Crown Prince were at the table. Naiser had seated them on the east side, in the places of honor. He did not look at them now. He looked at the three coming toward him down the long room, and the hall's own threads lay open to him as they always did in that room, and he read them.

Kiyome Byakuya first, white and straight and composed, the lamplight sparking in the glass at her temples, her silver rings lying quiet in her gray eyes and her katana bound in a white knot at her hip. He had seen her once, years ago, across a field under a flag of truce. She had not changed. She had only grown more like herself.

Behind her, Tetsugen Akagane.

Naiser had met him too, once, in the war: a great bald bull of a man in copper, roaring at the head of a column on the Sennen road while a Zettari border town burned behind him. He had grown. Not in size; he could hardly have grown in size. In something else. He walked heavily down the long room with his huge copper-ringed hands loose at his sides and his eyes on the floor, and the threads Naiser read in him now belonged to a man who had opened his hands and looked at the palms and found something written there he could not unread. [soft Korean accent, measured, inner voice] *The Fox,* [narrator] Naiser thought, [soft Korean accent, measured, inner voice, wry] *who could make a whole province happy, and had nothing left for his own table.* [narrator] The Fox had come a long way to sit at this one.

And Hayato Amagiri, slight and sharp and very pale, at the back.

Naiser had known Hayato as a boy. Before Hiroe, at one of the great gatherings of houses that nobody held any more, a thin quick child with ink on his fingers who had spent the whole of a three-day feast drawing maps of the hall's corridors on the backs of the menus. He had grown into his face. He had not grown out of his fingers.

"[soft Korean accent, measured, warm]It's been a long time," Naiser said. "Hayato Amagiri."

[narrator] Hayato stopped. For a moment his narrow face did something complicated. Then he bowed his head, low, and held it.

"[light Japanese accent, quick, wry, humble]You honor me by remembering, Lord Naiser," he said. "It has been a very long time." He straightened. "[sincere]I'm only sorry it's under such circumstances as these that we meet again."

"[soft Korean accent, measured, dry]So am I," said Naiser. "But we meet. That's more than most."

[narrator] Tetsugen Akagane had stopped beside the table. He stood there a moment, looking down at the two Zettari seated in the places of honor on the east side, the Sovereign of Kushara and the Crown Prince. Something went across his heavy face that might have been a great many things.

Then the Lord of the Akagane bent his bald head toward them. It was a deep bow. Deeper, Naiser thought, than a lord of the copper line had bent to a Zettari in forty years. He held it a long time. He did not say anything at all.

Kiyome Byakuya came to stand beside him.

She looked at the Sovereign, and at the Prince, steadily, one after the other. Then she inclined her head to them, less deeply than Tetsugen and with great care, so that the glass beads at her temples hung perfectly still.

"[light Japanese accent, calm, warm, formal]Sovereign," she said. "Prince Dougou."

[narrator] Her voice was clear and calm and carried down the long room without effort.

"[light Japanese accent, calm, warm, clear]I'm glad you're still here," she said. "I want you to know that. I want it to be the first thing I say in this house, so that nobody mistakes anything I say after it. When the crows told us the Zettari were sitting at the Yukari's table, Lord Hayato thought we ought to turn back. I didn't." She drew a breath. "[earnest]We haven't come here to continue the bloodshed between our houses. And we haven't come to stop you allying with the Yukari, or to bargain against you, or to ask Lord Naiser to choose between us. We haven't come to ask you for anything at all."

[narrator, pause] She paused.

"[light Japanese accent, calm, warm, weary]We're tired," said Kiyome Byakuya. "All three of our lines. We're tired of the fighting. Our people are dying. Not on any field. In their beds. Of famine, because Kagenori takes the grain for the house-guard. Of sickness, because the springs in the south have gone bad since the war and nobody will say why, and our physicians don't know what they're treating. Of cold, because the roads are closed and there's no oil." Her hands lay still at her sides, near the white knot. "[pained]There are villages under the Byakuya that have buried a third of their children since the spring. I've walked through them. I've sat in the houses." She held the Sovereign's eyes. "Whatever my house did to yours in forty years, Sovereign, and I know a great deal of what it did, the people who are paying for it now are the ones who never chose any of it."

[narrator] She was quiet for a moment.

"[light Japanese accent, calm, warm, resolute]That's why we're here," she said. "That's all. Because someone has to be the first to say *enough*. And I'd rather say it here, in front of you, with the Yukari for witness, than in the Hall of Verdicts, where Kagenori would have my head on the script for it before the words were cold."

[narrator] She stood beside Tetsugen Akagane, with the white knot bound tight at her hip, and waited for the Sovereign of Kushara to answer.

---

# Interlude · The Truth of Muken

[narrator] The question lay on the low table where the Sovereign had set it, between the clay cups and the single lamp, and for a long moment nobody on the western side would pick it up.

Under the floor the stream went on talking to itself against the foundation posts. Somewhere at the back of the hall a lamp-wick needed trimming and was spitting about it, a small dry tick every few breaths, and the smoke from it went straight up in the still air and flattened against the beams. The Yukari built their long halls of cedar over limestone footings, and the whole room smelled of both, clean wood and cold stone, with the steam off the tea gone thin and grassy as it cooled.

Kiyome Byakuya sat with her hands open on her knees. The white of her sleeves had picked up a gray line of road dust at the cuffs that had not been there when she came in, a long smear where she had braced her forearm on the northern gatepost dismounting, and she had not brushed it off. Her sword lay on the mat at her right side with its hilt toward her and the white knot of her house bound around the guard. Her eyes went to the knot once. Then they came up, pale gray and very steady, and settled on the Sovereign of Kushara.

[light Japanese accent, calm, warm] "You asked whether the boy is why we came," she said. "He is. You have been honest with us, Sovereign, more honest than our own council has been in years, and I won't sit in this house and answer you with half of it."

[narrator] She drew a breath and let it go.

[light Japanese accent, calm, warm, grave] "The truth of Muken is this. He is my nephew. He is the son of my sister, Kasane Byakuya, who took the name Yukari at her Telling and never gave it back." A pause, short and exact. [slowly] "And he is the direct son of Gimbzo Kōkan Moto."

[narrator] Tetsugen Akagane's head came up off his chest as though someone had pulled a cord through the back of his neck.

He was a heavy man gone heavier with age, the kind of broad that had been muscle once and was now mostly the memory of it, and he had been sitting since his bow with his chin down and his scarred hands folded on his belt over the horn handle of his meat knife, the only steel he had carried through the gate. Now the knuckles on those hands had gone white. Beside him, Hayato Amagiri had been halfway through lifting his cup, and the cup stopped where it was, an inch off the table, with the tea in it trembling in a fine ring.

Neither man said anything. Neither of them had to.

[light Japanese accent, flat, iron, deep, inner voice] *And she lays it on a Zettari table like a coin.*

[narrator] That was Tetsugen's thought, and it was on his face for anyone with eyes, and he did not trouble to hide it. Sixteen years Kagenori and his kept that name off every tongue south of the Seat, and Tetsugen had known it the way you know a draught is coming from somewhere, without once going to look. Hayato's face did something more careful. He set the cup down without a sound, turned it a quarter on the wood so the glaze crack faced away from him, and looked at Kiyome the way a man looks at a ford he has crossed a hundred times and has just watched swallow a cart.

Across the table, Naiser Yukari had gone very still.

Kiyome had given her sister two houses in a single breath, and the second was his own. He did not move. He did not look at his sister. But the corona at the edge of his pupils, violet-black even at rest, brightened by the width of a hair, and the Quiet Weight of the house came into the room the way it always did when a Yukari stopped breathing to listen: every other person present found themselves leaning a little toward him without knowing why.

Beside him Naevra had lowered her eyes to the table. She was not reading, not in any way a guest could have objected to. She did not need to. A Yukari of her line saw some of the threads at rest the way a weaver sees the warp on a loom she is not working, and she had been watching Kiyome's since the woman sat down. When a person speaks a thing they hold to be true, the thread of the claim settles. It lies down along the speaker's commitment the way a plumb line settles under its own weight, and nothing in the room tugs at it. A lie does the opposite: it has to be held taut against what the speaker knows, and it hums. Every thread off Kiyome Byakuya had lain flat and still as she spoke.

Kiyome let the silence stand a moment longer. Then she went on, and her voice was lower.

[light Japanese accent, calm, warm, quietly] "The truth is I should have been there for him." She looked at her hands. "Kasane wouldn't have it. The night she went, a runner of our house brought me four lines in her hand. I was to stay behind with our house. I was needed at home more than I'd ever be needed at her side. Yusif Stark would keep her and the boy safe." Her fingers moved on her knee, as if they were still holding the paper. "And then she was gone, and I never found her after. Not once, in all the years I looked." The corner of her mouth moved, not quite a smile. [wistful] "Maybe she knew how it would end. She could always see further down a road than the rest of us. She saw things coming that the rest of us didn't believe until they were standing in the door."

[narrator] She was quiet for a breath.

[light Japanese accent, calm, sorrowful] "She tried to make Gimbzo understand. For years, she tried. But after his mother died there was no more reasoning with him. Whatever had been between the two of them, the love, the patience, all of it, it went out like a fire nobody fed." Her hands closed on her knees. [softly] "She died the morning the news of Shimane came down to her, the Starks say, of the salt-throat that takes the cutters. I think she had been dying of him for years before anyone ever killed him. The war broke her family in half, and she was the one standing in the crack when it opened."

[narrator, pause] The lamp at the back of the hall ticked. Under the floor the stream went on.

Kiyome lifted her head.

[light Japanese accent, calm, firm] "But that is our own fault." She said it to the Sovereign, and then her eyes moved past him to the young man at his side and held there. "The Moto let our pride tell us we could dictate the future of everyone. Every people, every road, every house under the Sky. And when we met people we couldn't control, we tried to destroy them." She did not look away from Dougou. [quietly] "Yours were a people we failed to control. You know better than anyone in this hall what we did about it."

[narrator] Tetsugen made a sound in his chest like a stone settling in a riverbed.

[light Japanese accent, flat, iron, deep] "I never liked the Tenrai."

[narrator] He said it to the table at first. Then he lifted his eyes and said it again, to the Sovereign, in the flat iron voice of a man who had given orders on fields where nobody could hear anything gentler.

[light Japanese accent, flat, iron, deep, contemptuous] "I never liked them. Not when Gimbzo's father was alive, and not after. They were the ones in his ear every night once his mother was in the ground. Filled his head with the road to ruin and called it destiny, and he drank it because grief makes a man thirsty for anything that tastes like a reason." His jaw worked. [bitter] "And they did well out of it. The Tenrai were one of the families that grew fat on this war. Fat on the suffering of their own people as much as yours. Every year the levies went up and every year the Tenrai granaries got deeper. Kagenori sits on a larder and calls it a seat."

[narrator] He turned his whole body on the cushion, slowly, until he faced the Sovereign square.

[light Japanese accent, flat, iron, deep, heavy] "You asked me what changed my mind. I'll give you the truth, since you said you'd rather have that from a man who's made terrible decisions than a polished apology." The scarred hands came off the knife and lay flat on his thighs. [bitter] "Nothing honorable changed it. I'd like to sit here and tell you I woke one morning with the Sennen road on my conscience. I didn't. I supported that campaign. I sat in council and argued for it. My granaries fed the columns that went down that road, and I signed the provisioning orders myself, in my own hand, with my own seal, and I knew what those columns would do to every settlement standing between them and the river. [slowly] I knew, and I signed."

[narrator] Hayato shut his eyes for half a breath.

[light Japanese accent, flat, iron, deep, grim] "What changed me was that the Tenrai learned the lesson I helped teach them." Tetsugen's voice did not rise. "Three winters ago they withheld seed grain from the Akagane tenancies along the Kurogane valley because my eldest spoke against a levy in council. One winter. Just one. [slowly] I buried forty-one of my own tenants that spring, most of them under twelve, and I stood at the edge of the pit and thought of nothing but the Sennen road. That's what turned me. The same bill I helped write for your people came to my own door with my own name on it."

[narrator] He looked past the Sovereign, then, to the son.

[light Japanese accent, flat, iron, deep] "Prince. You said saying you've had enough is easy in somebody else's house." He inclined his heavy head, an inch, no more. [gruff] "You're right. It is. So I'll say it again in mine. There's a proverb in the north: wet wood burns eventually, and a liar never dries. I've been wet wood for forty years. It took me that long to catch. [quietly] I don't ask you to think well of the fire."

[narrator] His hands went inside his coat and came out with a packet wrapped in oiled cloth and tied with black cord, the size of a small ledger. He set it on the table in front of him and did not push it across.

[light Japanese accent, flat, iron, deep] "The Akagane muster rolls and provisioning books for the Sennen campaign. Names of every commander who took grain from my stores. Names of the settlements on the route, in our own clerks' hands, with the dates we marked them cleared." His thumb rested on the cord. "Kushara can have them for its records. You'll know who did what, and you won't have to take my word on any of it." He paused. "If Kagenori ever gets his hands on a copy, it's enough to hang me in the Seat's square, so I'd ask that your clerks keep it closer than mine did. That's the first thing I'm prepared to lose. The second is simpler. The Akagane won't answer Kagenori's next muster. When the call comes, my levies stay on their land. Which means when he wants an example, he'll come to the Kurogane valley first." He sat back. [grim, dry] "He's welcome to try."

[narrator] [sighs] Hayato let out a breath through his nose.

[light Japanese accent, quick, wry, quietly] "This is true." He said it quietly, nodding at the packet, and then lifted his eyes to the Sovereign. [earnest] "All of it. And yet everything the Sovereign has said is also true. Every word."

[narrator] He was the youngest of the three, a few years under Kiyome and a whole generation under Tetsugen, lean in the way of men who spent their lives in the saddle, with a weathered, narrow face and a horseman's squint that never quite left the corners of his eyes even indoors. The rain-cape he had not taken off was still dark across the shoulders where the snowmelt had soaked through on the northern approach, and a sprig of blue-black juniper had caught in the toggle at his throat and stayed there.

[light Japanese accent, quick, wry] "We can't afford to pull our families out from under the Tenrai so easily," he said. "That's the knot of it, Byakuya-dono, and you know it as well as I do. There are men in all three of our houses who eat from Kagenori's stores now. Their children are fed from Tenrai grain because ours ran out. Tell them their lords have walked out of council and they'll go hungry for it, and see how long their loyalty lasts. We need something to hold them that's older than a granary." He turned his cup another quarter. [firm] "We need Muken to step up."

[narrator] Then he turned to the Sovereign, and his voice took on the plain working cadence of a man discussing distances.

[light Japanese accent, quick, wry, matter-of-fact] "You asked about the roads, Sovereign. Whether they're truly closed, or closed only to people without the knowledge to go round. I'll answer that honestly, because it's the one thing I'm good for." He touched the table with two fingers, drawing a line. "The main roads out of Kharven are closed to carts, closed to commons, and closed to anyone without a Tenrai pass. The Hiekawa cut and the old salt road over the Shiogane shoulder are open, because the Tenrai's own smugglers use them to sell Moto grain to the hill clans at four times the price, and they don't want them watched. They know the cut as far as their mules walk it and not a yard to either side. The side tracks off it are mine. That's how we came: up the cut, off it at a herders' shoulder the smugglers have never had a reason to find, and down a deer track onto your own north hill. That's how I can bring grain in." He drew a second line beside the first. [measured] "A mule carries two sacks in snow. The cut takes thirty mules abreast at its widest and single file at its narrowest. It's a feeding road, too narrow for an army."

[narrator] His mouth tightened.

[light Japanese accent, quick, wry, rueful] "And no, it doesn't help the families we've described as things stand. It helps nobles leaving in a hurry. You're right about that too. The only way it helps a village in the southern valleys is if somebody who isn't Moto stands at both ends of it counting sacks."

[narrator] He looked down the table, past the Sovereign, to the two Yukari.

[light Japanese accent, quick, wry] "So here's what I'm prepared to lose. Those side roads are all my house has that the Tenrai don't. The Tenrai can have the cut and welcome; it's what runs off it that they've never found, and that's why the Amagiri still have a seat at all. I'll give them up. Every pass, every ford, every hut with a woodpile in it between the Seat and the southern border, drawn out for whoever you name as witness. The day Kushara's grain goes up the Hiekawa cut and off it onto my tracks with Yukari eyes on it, those tracks stop being mine. Kagenori will learn them inside a season, and he'll close them, and after that I'm just another lord who knows the weather." He lifted one shoulder. [dry] "Better it carries grain for one winter than smugglers for twenty."

[narrator] He hesitated, and the hesitation was honest.

[light Japanese accent, quick, wry, hesitant] "And since you asked who we speak for. I'll not pretend. Byakuya-dono speaks for her house, and Lord Tetsugen speaks for his. I speak for the Amagiri roads and the twenty riders I brought. My uncle will sit the indigo chair for the Amagiri when Kagenori calls the full council, and he'll sit it at Kagenori's left hand in everything but the furniture. [rueful] I don't speak for him, and he will not thank me for any of this."

[light Japanese accent, quick, wry, inner voice] *There. Said. Let the Zettari weigh a man who can't promise his own table.*

[narrator] Kiyome had been listening with her eyes on the white knot at her sword. When she lifted them, they went first to Hayato, and something in them was very tired.

[light Japanese accent, calm, warm, gently] "But we cannot expect a child who doesn't even know his own bloodline to fix all of our problems." She said it gently, which made it heavier. "Muken is sixteen years old. He's a mercenary. He sleeps where he's paid to and he fights whoever the coin tells him to, and nobody alive has ever sat him down and told him what he is. [pointed] You want him to step up, Hayato. Step up onto what? A crown he's never heard of, held out to him by the same families who killed his father's line and his mother's peace?" She shook her head, once. [firm] "Nor can we expect the Zettari to fix them. They didn't break us. We did that ourselves, with both hands."

[narrator] She turned back to the Sovereign.

[light Japanese accent, calm, warm] "You warned us against putting our ambitions on a boy's shoulders and calling it destiny. You said it better than I would have. I'll hold you to it, and I'll hold myself to it first." Her hand moved to the white knot and rested on it. "The Byakuya are the white line. For as long as there have been Moto kings, we have been sworn to the red: to keep the Kōkan heir, to hold his seat when he can't, and to put him on it when he can. That oath is why I'm alive. It is the whole reason my house exists." Her fingers lay flat on the knot. [slowly] "So here is what I am prepared to lose. I'll go to Muken myself. I'll tell him everything I told you, and everything I didn't, and I will not ask him for a single thing. If he says no, if he wants nothing to do with the Moto and their crown and their thousand years of blood, then the Byakuya will serve him anyway, as a mercenary, in a mercenary's tent, and the crown my house kept for him will go to nobody." She took her hand off the knot. [quietly] "That's the oath, read the only honest way. It binds me to the boy. It doesn't bind the boy to us."

[narrator] Naevra lifted her eyes from the table.

She had not spoken since the delegation came in, and when she did, she spoke to Kiyome first, softly, in the old courteous register of the house, before turning the same words toward the Sovereign.

[soft Korean accent, measured, softly] "My house was asked that same question this week, Lady Kiyome. About me." She folded her hands. "Whether what a bloodline needs is a thing the person carrying it has agreed to. I've had the word destiny said at me a great many times in the last few days, by people who love me and some who don't." A small pause. [wry] "I've come to think fate is only arrogant because nobody ever taught it manners. Yours is a good answer. I hope he hears it from you before he hears the other version from Kagenori."

[narrator] Then she turned to the Sovereign.

[soft Korean accent, measured] "You spoke of the southern springs. Of whether they can be put right, and whether the work can be done without being made into somebody's leash." She laid one long hand on the table, palm down. "They can be read. Water keeps a record of everything poured into it. Your own well-diggers know half of this already: a poison in groundwater travels with the flow underground, spreading as it goes, so that the further you are from where it went in, the fainter and wider it runs. Walk upstream against it and the taint thickens until you're standing on the place it entered. What my house adds is Anamnesis. The Wellspring of memory holds the record of what the water has carried, and a Yukari who reads it can tell you what went in, when, and whether it is still going in. We can walk the southern springs from the mouth to the source and tell you where the harm is, and whose hand is on it." She looked at Kiyome. [firm] "But we need to walk them. All of them. Without escort, without a Tenrai factor waiting at the next spring with a story ready. If anyone tells us where we may not go, we stop, and we tell everyone at this table why we stopped."

[narrator] Naiser spoke then.

He had let every word in the hall run its full length before he moved, the way his house had always done, and when he spoke the room came down to meet him.

[soft Korean accent, measured, grave] "Lady Kiyome. You said your sister took the name Yukari."

[light Japanese accent, calm, warm] "I did."

[soft Korean accent, measured, cool] "That name is ours."

[light Japanese accent, calm, warm] "She heard the Telling in this valley when she was fourteen," Kiyome said. "She sat in front of Senri's stone and listened to the whole account of what the name costs, and then she asked for it. Your house gave it to her. She never once stopped answering to it." Her eyes did not leave his. [gently] "I assumed your archive would remember. I thought you might not have read that page."

[soft Korean accent, measured, quietly] "I hadn't." Naiser was quiet a moment. "Then the boy is owed something from this house as well, and he doesn't know that either."

[narrator] He turned to the Sovereign.

[soft Korean accent, measured] "You told me to draw a line between hospitality and commitment before somebody else draws it for us. I'll draw it now, in front of everyone, so it can be quoted back to me." He set both hands flat on the table. "The Yukari are hosts. We are not allies to any side present. We do not lend soldiers, we do not recognise governments, and we do not sell what we see. What we do is witness. [slowly] A crow perches. A crow watches. A crow remembers. That is the lawful function this house was left alone to perform, and it is the only one I will put our name to."

[narrator] He lifted one hand from the table.

[soft Korean accent, measured] "So. If Kushara purchases grain, and House Vokari consents to keep the accounts, the Yukari will stand at both ends of Lord Hayato's road and count every sack that goes up it and every mouth it reaches. If the Byakuya and the Akagane open their granaries to the same count, we'll count those too. Naevra will walk the southern springs and put her finding before every house here, whatever it says. And every promise made in this hall today is going into our record, word for word, with the name of the one who made it. If any of it is broken, by anyone, it'll be the Yukari who say so out loud." He let the pause finish. [pointed] "Including the promises made by Kushara. I'd ask the Sovereign to agree to that plainly, since it binds his house the same as theirs."

[narrator] He looked at Kiyome again, and something in the Quiet Weight shifted.

[soft Korean accent, measured, quietly] "And I'll tell you now, since you have been honest with my house: your sister's name makes this harder for me. A witness is meant to stand outside the thing he's watching. It seems I have a cousin in it I didn't know about." He inclined his head to her. [firm] "I'll keep the line anyway. But you should know it's there, and that it cost something to keep."

[narrator] Kiyome bowed from the waist, low, her forehead nearly to the cedar, and held it a long breath before she came up.

When she straightened she turned back to the far side of the table, to the young man sitting beside his father.

[light Japanese accent, calm, warm, level] "Prince Dougou." Her voice was quite level. "You said your people are starving too, and that you won't pretend their suffering went away because ours got too loud to ignore. I'm not asking you to. I'd think less of you if you did." She folded her hands in her lap, over the gray line of road dust on her sleeve. [steady] "You asked what we're prepared to lose. You've heard it. Lord Tetsugen's books and his valley. Lord Hayato's roads. My house's oath, and the crown it was built to keep. None of that buys your dead back. [softly] I know it doesn't."

[narrator] She looked at him for a long moment, and the white knot lay still at her side.

[light Japanese accent, calm, warm, earnest] "So when the Yukari go up the Hiekawa cut to count our graves, I'd ask you to send someone of your own with them. Somebody who'll count the small ones. Somebody who hates us enough to count them honestly." A breath. [softly] "Better still, come yourself."

---

# Interlude · The Iron Fist of the Keld

[narrator] For a while after the Crown Prince had finished, nobody in the long hall said anything at all.

The stream went on under the boards, clear and quick and cold, with a sound like small change being counted. The tea had gone cold in its clay cups an hour ago and nobody had poured more; the charcoal in the bronze brazier had sunk to a red eye under its ash. The hall was cedar from sill to ridge-beam, old wood gone the deep red-brown of a fox's back, and it smelled of resin and river-damp and ink. Through the open screens on the south side the white gravel garden lay in the soft pearl light of a high overcast, raked into long combed lines, and the three standing stones in it threw no shadows, and beyond it the great cedars stood in their ranks, dark green and red-barked, with moss bright as verdigris at their roots. Somewhere out in the cedars the house's crows were calling to one another in their harsh, patient voices, the way they always did when the enclave held more strangers than it was used to.

[soft Korean accent, measured, inner voice] *I'll count the small ones.*

[narrator] Naiser had heard a great many things said at that table in the last week. He found that he would remember that one longest.

Tetsugen Akagane was the first of them to move. He had been sitting with his enormous copper-ringed hands on his knees and his bald head bowed, looking at the oiled packet of Sennen records on the table in front of him, all the while the prince spoke to him. Now he lifted his head, slowly, and looked across the table at the young Zettari, and his heavy face did not change.

[light Japanese accent, flat, iron, deep, slowly] "No," he said. "I'm not."

[narrator] His voice was a low rumble, like a cart going over a bridge.

[light Japanese accent, flat, iron, deep, rueful] "A good man," he said. "You're right. I'm not one. I wasn't one at Sennen and I wasn't one at the Hall of Verdicts the night we took the Stark's head, and I've not become one in the month since, whatever I've done with a soldier and a horse in the dark." He looked down at his own hands again, open on his knees. [heavy] "I don't expect to be. I'm too old and I've done too much. But I'd like to be the kind of bad man who tells the truth about it, if that's still on offer." He was quiet for a moment. [quietly] "Thank you for not lying to me, Prince. Most people have stopped bothering."

[narrator] He said nothing else. It had cost him enough.

Hayato Amagiri stirred beside him. The young lord's restless fingers had been tracing, all through the prince's answer, the lines he had drawn on the table earlier with a wet fingertip: the roads through the northern mountains, long since dried to nothing on the dark wood. He lifted his hand from the table now and let it fall into his lap.

[light Japanese accent, quick, wry, earnest] "The prince is right about the grain," Hayato said. "He's right, and I should have said it before he had to." He looked across at the two Zettari, and then at Naiser. [rueful] "A road carries whatever's put on it. I've spent a year finding roads Kagenori couldn't close, because he didn't know they were there. I never once asked myself who'd be standing at the far end of them when the carts arrived." His mouth tightened. [grim] "Kagenori's quartermasters will be standing there, if we let them. Or Ashmore's. Or any lord with enough men to put across a ford."

[soft Korean accent, measured, young woman, cool] "Then count the carts," said Naevra, "as well as the graves."

[narrator] They all looked at her.

She was sitting at her brother's left hand, very straight, in gray, a crow worked small in black at her collar, with her white braid over her shoulder and her hands folded in her lap, and her silver-white eyes were steady on Hayato.

[soft Korean accent, measured, young woman, steady] "We'll put a reader on every train that goes over your roads," she said. "One of ours. A Yukari, with ink and a ledger and a crow. Every sack counted in at the northern end, and every sack counted out at the village it's meant for. Every name of every family that receives it, written down. And if a sack goes missing between the two, the crow goes up before the cart's out of sight, and the whole of the Inner World knows by nightfall whose ford it went missing at." She paused. [firm] "The Prince wants verifiable findings. So do we. There's nothing in the world harder to steal from than a thing that's being counted by somebody who isn't afraid of you."

[narrator] Hayato weighed her across the table, and then, slowly, he nodded.

[light Japanese accent, quick, wry] "Done," he said. "If Lord Naiser will lend you the readers."

[soft Korean accent, measured] "He will," said Naiser.

---

[narrator] He set his hands on the table, either side of his cold cup, and turned to the Zettari.

[soft Korean accent, measured, formal] "Sovereign," he said. "Prince Dougou. I'll answer what you've asked of this house, and I'll answer it plainly, because you've both earned plainness twice over this morning."

[narrator] He looked at the prince first.

[soft Korean accent, measured] "You said you wouldn't be anyone's symbol," Naiser said. "And you said, rightly, that wherever you go, the world will see the Crown Prince of Kushara before it sees anything else, and the Tenrai will make of that whatever serves them." He inclined his head. [pointed] "So don't go as the Crown Prince of Kushara."

[narrator] The stream ran under the floor. A shaft of light found its way through a gap in the cloud and lay for a moment across the garden, and every grain of the white gravel glittered, and then it was gone.

[soft Korean accent, measured, deliberate] "Go as the guest of this house," Naiser said. "Under the crow. Not under any Zettari banner, and not under the white or the copper or the indigo either. Under ours. A witness of the Yukari, as my readers are witnesses, traveling with my sister on the business of this house: to count what the war has done, and write it down, and send it home to be kept." He held the prince's eyes. "The Yukari have been walking into other people's ruins since before there were Zettari to keep the stones in their places. Nobody in the Inner World mistakes a Yukari witness for an army. If Kagenori wants to tell Kharven that the man who killed Gimbzo has come to meddle in their affairs, he'll have to tell them as well that the man came under the crow, with a Yukari ledger in his hand, and touched nothing but a pen." He paused. [dry] "It won't stop him lying. Nothing stops Kagenori lying. But it will make the lie harder to tell, and easier to catch."

[narrator] He turned to the Sovereign.

[soft Korean accent, measured, formal] "And your conditions," he said, "this house accepts as its own. A witness, and not an emissary. No commitment made in Kushara's name. No hand raised in the Moto's quarrels except to keep the innocent alive. And findings that can be checked by anyone who cares to read them." He nodded, once. "The readers will keep the record. My sister will see to the springs. Everything that's found will be set down in the archive of this house beside the name of the one who found it, and a fair copy sent to Kushara under seal."

[narrator] He paused, and his voice changed, a little.

[soft Korean accent, measured, softly] "And one more thing, Prince. Since you asked it."

[narrator] He looked at Dougou.

[soft Korean accent, measured, softly] "You said your own dead must be counted as carefully as the ones you're being asked to count in the north," Naiser said. "That their names mustn't be forgotten because the world has become interested in Moto suffering." He let a breath out, slowly. [sighs] "This house remembers names. It's the oldest thing we do. Older than the wards. Older than the readings. When a Yukari dies, the house speaks a name over them that's never spoken again except at the Telling. We keep them. All of them. For as long as there's been a house." He held the prince's gaze. [slowly] "Send us your dead. The names of every Zettari who fell in the forty years. Send them to this archive, and they'll be written into the same books as ours, in the same ink, by the same hands. Not as enemies of anybody. As dead." He paused. [quietly] "So that when someone a thousand years from now comes to this valley to count what the war cost, they'll find Kushara's children on the same page as everyone else's. And nobody will ever be able to say they weren't counted."

[narrator] He sat back.

Beside him, Naevra had lowered her eyes.

[soft Korean accent, measured, young woman, quietly] "I'll walk with you, Prince," she said quietly. "In the north. Every village. I'll be the one holding the ledger when you count." She looked up. [gently] "And when we come to a place where the grief is too much to look at, you can hand it to me, and I'll count for a while. There's no shame in it. My brother's readers do it for each other all the time." She paused. [softly] "It's what the ledger's for."

---

[narrator] Kiyome Byakuya had not moved through any of it.

She sat across the table from the Zettari, in her near-white, bright as new snow against the red cedar, with the white knot bound tight at her hip and the glass beads of her veil lying still at her temples, and she had listened to the prince the whole way through with the silver rings level in her gray eyes. When he said *I killed his father,* the rings had moved, once, very slightly. Nothing else had.

Now she inclined her head to him, very low.

[light Japanese accent, calm, warm, solemn] "Witnessed," she said. "That's all. Forgiveness was never mine to ask for."

[narrator] Her voice was clear and quiet.

[light Japanese accent, calm, warm, quietly] "That's all I asked for, Prince. That's all I'd have dared to ask. If you'd told me you'd come to forgive my house, I'd not have believed you, and I'd have been right not to." She raised her head. [firm] "Count them honestly. Tell the truth about what you find, and tell it about all of us. The Tenrai and the Akagane and the Byakuya, and me. If my own stewards have been feeding their families before the villages, I want to know it as badly as you do." She paused. [gently] "And when you go home, count your own. You're right that they matter. They mattered all along. Nothing I've seen in the north makes them matter less."

[narrator] She was quiet for a breath.

[light Japanese accent, calm, warm, heavy] "As for my nephew," said Kiyome.

[narrator] The beads at her temples clicked, very softly, as she turned her head toward the open screens and the north beyond them.

[light Japanese accent, calm, warm] "You've the right of it, Prince," she said. "He should hear it from his own. From someone who knew his mother. Not from the Tenrai." Her hands had closed in her lap. "And you should know something, so that you understand what I mean when I say I'll try. The Starks would line up to die, every man of them, before they let the Tenrai be the ones to tell Muken what he is. They've known since before he could walk. They've kept it from him on purpose, all his life, and they'll go on keeping it until they judge he can carry it." She looked back at the prince. [grave] "And the one guiding him now is a man I know very well. Ulf Stark."

[narrator] Naiser saw something move in Hayato's face, and go still.

[light Japanese accent, calm, warm, proud] "A man of great honor," Kiyome said. "As great as his brother's. I sat in the hall and watched Torvald Stark die laughing on the script for telling us the truth, and I've known Ulf since I was a girl in my grandmother's house and he was already a grown man with gray in his beard, and there's not a breath of difference between them, except that Ulf has always been the more patient." Her voice did not rise. [sorrowful, quietly] "And that is why Ulf will die too."

[narrator] [crow cawing] Out in the cedars a crow called once and was answered.

[light Japanese accent, calm, grave] "Kagenori will call him back," Kiyome said. "To the hold. To the Hall of Verdicts. He'll send a summons under the old law, the one that binds every Warlord of the north to answer to the seven lines for the conduct of his house, and he'll name the Foot in it, and he'll name the boy. And Ulf will go." She drew a breath. [bitter] "He'll go knowing exactly what's waiting for him on the script, because if he doesn't, Kagenori will have the excuse he's been waiting for to burn Hollenmark to the ground. So he'll ride south and kneel in the circle where his brother knelt, and buy the north a season with his head." She looked down at her hands. [sad] "And Muken will try to stop him. And Ulf won't let him. I know that man. He'll not let a sixteen-year-old boy throw himself on the Tenrai's spears to save an old one."

[light Japanese accent, flat, iron, deep, heavily] "Old men buying time with their necks," said Tetsugen heavily. "So the young ones can spend it."

[light Japanese accent, calm, warm, sorrowful] "That's the whole of it," said Kiyome. "That's what the north's become. You've seen it yourselves, if you've read the crows. Every lord in the Stark and Greymane camps past fifty-five, and every spear under them carried by a boy." She lifted her eyes. [quietly] "It's as if the whole of the old north has decided, all together, without ever saying so aloud, that their lives are a small price. A short expense. Spent now, all at once, to buy something they'll never live to see." She was quiet for a moment. "And they've put that boy at the front of it, and they mean to spend themselves round him until he's strong enough to stand on his own."

[narrator] Naiser said nothing. He was thinking of a gate in the frost, and a man in a traveling coat with his pack on his back, putting both hands on his son's shoulders, and walking into a seam in the air.

[soft Korean accent, inner voice, slowly] *You are the Bearer of Weight.*

[narrator] He knew exactly what the old men of the north were doing. He had watched it done.

---

[light Japanese accent, quick, wry, quietly] "There's worse," said Hayato Amagiri.

[narrator] He said it quietly, and they all turned to him.

The young lord of the Amagiri was looking at the table, at the place where his wet-finger roads had dried to nothing on the dark wood. His fingers had begun moving again.

[light Japanese accent, quick, wry, grim] "I know where they'll send him next," he said. "Ulf. Or Wystan, if Ulf's already gone south. The Foot's taken. The Kessen road's open. The next thing the north needs is the Keld." He looked up. "The tundra. Past the treeline. Every road I mapped through the northern mountains runs through Keld country at the far end of it, and nothing goes over those roads without the Keld letting it. Whoever holds the Keld holds the back door of the whole north." He drew a breath. [urgent] "So they'll send Muken to take it. To bring the Keld in, or put them down. Even if it means northerners killing northerners."

[soft Korean accent, measured, young woman, slowly] "The Keld swear to a man," said Naevra slowly. "Not to a letter, nor to a house. They swear to whoever comes out onto the tundra in person and sits at their fires and eats their food and stays worth following." She frowned. [thoughtful] "Old Harald leads the fires. Or did. He's too old now to sit a horse."

[light Japanese accent, quick, wry] "Not any more," said Hayato.

[narrator] He looked at Kiyome. She nodded, very slightly, for him to go on.

[light Japanese accent, quick, wry, grave] "Harald's dying," Hayato said. "Or dead. The crows aren't sure which. His grandson leads the fires now. One of the Seven, as of this winter, by the old count." He paused. [slowly] "They're calling him the Iron Fist of the Keld."

[narrator] Tetsugen grunted.

[light Japanese accent, quick, wry, uneasy] "He's young," Hayato said. "As young as Muken, near enough. Seventeen. Eighteen. Nobody's sure; the Keld don't count years the way the south does. But he's already one of the Great Warlords, and he's already got every fire on the tundra behind him, and he's never once lost a fight he chose." His fingers tapped once on the table. [grim] "And Kagenori's reached him first."

[light Japanese accent, flat, iron, deep, darkly] "Karogane," said Tetsugen.

[narrator, pause] The name went into the hall and lay there like a stone dropped into still water.

Naiser did not know it. He saw from Naevra's face that she did not either. But he saw the other two Moto lords' faces, and Kiyome's, and that was enough.

[light Japanese accent, quick, wry, bitter] "Kagenori's man," said Hayato. "His hand in the north. He's been out on the tundra since the week the Foot fell, with Tenrai silver and Tenrai seed grain and every promise the Hall of Verdicts could think to put in a man's mouth. And from what my riders heard at the treeline, he's not trying to bring the Keld into the war on our side." His mouth thinned. "He's trying to make the Iron Fist fight it for him. Make the young Keld meet Muken on the tundra and settle the north between them, northerner against northerner, while the Tenrai stand back and keep their own hands clean. And in return..." He spread his hands. [contemptuous] "In return, everything. The tundra, for ever. No tithe, no tax, no Moto boot on Keld snow while the world lasts. A seat at the Hall of Verdicts. Whatever the boy wants."

[narrator] He looked at Tetsugen.

[light Japanese accent, quick, wry, anxious] "You've seen Muken fight," Hayato said. "Or near enough. You've read Shigure's account as many times as I have. Do you think he has it in him? Against the Iron Fist, on the Keld's own ground, in the dead of the winter?"

[narrator] Tetsugen took his time. He looked down at his great copper-ringed hands, and turned them over, as he did when a thing weighed on him.

[light Japanese accent, flat, iron, deep, slowly] "If he fights it right," he said at last, "and uses his men right, and reads the ground the way he read the wall at the Foot, then yes. Any battle that can be fought out of the Foot should be his. He holds the road now. He holds the high ground. He has the Starks and the Greymanes behind him and a sister who keeps his men alive." He lifted his head. [grave] "That's not what worries me."

[soft Korean accent, measured] "What does?" said Naiser.

[light Japanese accent, flat, iron, deep, grim] "Karogane will be there," said Tetsugen Akagane. "At the battle. On the tundra. He'll not trust the Iron Fist to do it alone, and he'll not leave it to chance. He'll be standing at the Keld's shoulder when the two of them meet." His jaw worked. [slowly] "And I know Karogane. He won't need to win the fight. He'll walk out between the lines, in front of the whole of both armies, and he'll tell the boy who he is."

[narrator, pause] The hall had gone quiet enough to hear the brazier tick.

[light Japanese accent, flat, iron, deep, low] "Gimbzo's son," Tetsugen said. "In front of the Starks. In front of the Keld. In front of every boy with a spear who followed him up the ridge at the Foot because he was one of them, a tokenless nobody, the same as they were." He let it lie. [bitter] "Karogane will tell them all that their tokenless lieutenant is the son of the man who burned the north for twenty years. And then he'll stand back and watch what they do."

[narrator, long pause] The stream filled the silence under the boards.

Naevra had gone pale. Kiyome sat perfectly still, with the rings in her gray eyes lying flat and silver as winter ice. Hayato's fingers had stopped.

Naiser looked down at the cold tea in his cup, and saw his own face in it, and the bare nape behind it, and the thread-marks at his temples beginning very faintly to burn.

[soft Korean accent, inner voice] *Something with no face.* [narrator] That was what he had told the Sovereign, at this table, a week ago. [soft Korean accent, inner voice] *When it comes, it will be large enough that every house in this world will have to decide what it is before it decides what we are.*

[narrator] And the first person who would get to decide what Muken was, in front of the whole north, would be a Tenrai agent standing on the snow at the Iron Fist's shoulder.

[soft Korean accent, measured, quietly] "How long?" Naiser said.

[light Japanese accent, quick, wry, tense] "Before the battle?" Hayato shook his head. "A month. Less. Karogane's had a fortnight already. The Keld move fast once they've decided."

[soft Korean accent, measured, young woman, anxious] "Then the Starks will tell him first," said Naevra. "Surely. Ulf, or Wystan. If they know Karogane's out there..."

[light Japanese accent, calm, warm, flat] "They won't," said Kiyome.

[narrator] They all looked at her.

[light Japanese accent, calm, warm, quietly] "They won't tell him," she said. "They've kept it sixteen years. They'll keep it till the day it's torn out of their hands, because they believe, as Yusif believed, that a boy who learns it too soon will break on it." Her voice was very quiet. "And they may be right. But they don't know Karogane. And they don't know that the Tenrai mean to make the north hear it before Muken does."

[narrator] She rose.

She rose all at once, near-white linen stirring round her, with the white knot bound at her hip and the glass beads at her temples chiming very faintly in the still air of the hall, and stood looking down the long room toward the north screens and the cold pearl light beyond them, where the cedars went up the valley side rank on rank into the cloud.

[light Japanese accent, calm, warm, resolute] "Then it's settled," said Kiyome Byakuya.

[narrator] She turned back to the table.

[light Japanese accent, calm, warm, determined] "We go to Kharven's Foot," she said. "Before the battle. Before Karogane can stand on the snow and say it. Before the Starks can spend themselves trying to keep it from him." She looked at Tetsugen, and at Hayato, and both of them, one after the other, nodded. [fierce] "I'll not have my sister's son hear who his mother was from a Tenrai's mouth on a battlefield. Not if I have to ride through the whole of the north in the dead of winter to stop it."

[soft Korean accent, measured, young woman, firm] "Then you'll not ride through it alone," said Naevra.

[narrator] She had risen too. She stood at her brother's side with her hands at her sides and her chin up, the way she had stood at the north gate in the blue hour that same morning, and her silver-white eyes were on her oldest friend's face.

[soft Korean accent, measured, young woman, emotional] "Not this time," she said. "I wasn't there for you the last time it mattered. I'm going to be there for this."

[narrator] Kiyome looked at her. Something moved in her still gray eyes, and was gone, and was understood between them without a word.

Naiser sat at the low table between them, with his hands flat on the dark wood and his sister on her feet beside him and three Moto lords across from him and the Sovereign of Kushara and the Crown Prince in the places of honor, and the cold tea in the cups, and the stream running loud under the floor.

He did not rise.

He looked across the table, past the Moto, to the two Zettari, and waited.

---

# Chapter Eight: The Inheritance

[narrator] *Book One of the Kōkan · Chapter Eight: The Inheritance*

> [narrator, slowly, solemn] *Of all the questions that were asked about him in that first winter, in the cedar halls of the Yukari and the white house of the Byakuya and the Hall of Verdicts itself, only one mattered, and nobody who asked it could answer it. Was the boy anything like his father? Could he be what the seven lines would need him to be, if he lived long enough to be anything at all?*
>
> *The answer is known now. It was not known then. The chroniclers of the north who came after, who had the whole of his life laid out before them like a road seen from a hill, would name him among the greatest kings the north has ever had, and some of them would name him the greatest. But that winter he was sixteen, and tokenless, and nobody's son that anybody would admit to, and he had a cut across his ribs that would not close, and a woodpile to see to.*
>
> *From the* Annals of the North-Forged Crown

---

### I · The Woodpile

[narrator] He was chopping wood when Rob came for him.

He had been chopping wood for four days. The surgeon had told him not to. His sister had told him not to, in the voice she used for men on carts, and then in a worse voice, and then had stopped telling him and taken to standing at the edge of the yard with her arms folded watching him do it, which was worse than either. But there was a village at the bottom of the ridge under Kharven's Foot, forty houses in a fold of the hill where the Kessen road came down out of the pass, and the Tenrai had held the Foot over it for eleven years. Every winter of the eleven they had taken the village's wood for the garrison's fires. There were families in those forty houses who had spent the Thin Weeks breaking up their own furniture to burn.

So he chopped wood.

He did it one-handed, mostly. The left arm was still strapped across his chest under his shirt, where Shigure Tenrai's edge had come in under the mail and opened him from the armpit down across the ribs. It had been stitched twice. It had come open once. It hurt in a deep, dull, grinding way every time he breathed in, and in a bright, sharp way every time he swung the axe, and he had stopped noticing the difference between the two somewhere around the second day. He set the rounds on the block with his right hand, and took the axe up in his right hand, and brought it down, and the round split and fell, and he set up the next one. A full stack, against the wall of the old Tenrai barracks, as high as his shoulder and twice as long as a cart, every split face bright with pine resin that went gold where the low sun caught it. Then another. Then the boys from the vanguard who could still lift things carried it down the ridge on their backs, a load at a time, to the forty houses.

He had not planned it. He had simply looked at the village from the broken wall on the first morning after the battle, at the thin smoke from its chimneys going up straight and blue into a sky like hammered steel, and at the great stacks of seasoned pine the Tenrai had left behind in the fort's woodyard, more than the garrison could have burned in three winters, and gone down and picked up an axe.

By the third day, there were men from the village coming up the ridge to help him.

By the fourth, there were children. They could not lift the axe. They carried the split wood to the stack and laid it, carefully, the pale ends all outward, the way their grandfathers had laid it. An old woman with no teeth and a face like a winter apple had come up with them, wrapped in three shawls, madder red and moss green and a blue gone soft with washing, and had stood watching him split through half a morning, leaning on a stick. Then she had said, in the thick slow vowels of the northern hill country, [very old woman, Yorkshire accent, cracked] *How's your stack, lad?* [narrator] And when he had looked at her, not understanding, she had pointed at the long pale wall of split pine against the barracks with her stick and said it again. [very old woman, Yorkshire accent, cracked] *How's your stack?*

[Northern English accent, low and rough, puzzled] "Full," he had said.

[narrator] [laughs] And she had laughed until she had to sit down on the block, and then she had told him that it was the oldest question in the north, older than the Moto, older than the roads: what you asked a man in winter to find out if his family would live till the spring. [very old woman, Yorkshire accent, cracked, warmly] *A full stack speaks louder than a full mouth,* she had said. *Your father must have taught you that.*

[narrator] He had not known what to say to that. He had gone on chopping.

He was splitting a round of old knotted pine, with the sweat running down inside his shirt and his breath going up white and glittering where the frost took it, and his left side singing at him, when Rob Stark came across the yard. Rob still walked with the stick, but he walked faster with it now. He stopped at the edge of the woodpile and waited for the axe to come down.

[narrator] The round split. The halves fell.

[Yorkshire accent, cheerful, hoarse, subdued] "Lord Ulf wants you," Rob said. "And Lord Wystan. In the gatehouse." He paused. "Now, Sarge."

[narrator] Muken leaned the axe against the block and straightened, slowly, with his good hand pressed flat against his ribs. Rob was looking at him. Rob's face was doing what Stark faces did, which was close up like a door. But it was closing up harder than usual, and his eyes would not quite meet Muken's.

At the edge of the yard, Yukazuri had unfolded her arms.

---

### II · The Gatehouse

[narrator] The gatehouse of Kharven's Foot had been the Tenrai captain's quarters, and it still smelled of her: some sharp clean southern resin they burned in their lamps, and oiled lamellar, and ink. Shigure's table was still there, and her chair, and a rack on the wall where her long curved sword had hung. Somebody had taken the Tenrai banner down from over the hearth and put the Stark wolf up in its place, and the Greymane wolf beside it, and the two gray heads looked down from the wall at the room with their painted teeth bared and their painted eyes picked out in yellow, the brightest color in the room after the fire.

Ulf Stark sat at the table. Wystan Greymane stood at the window with his back to the room, looking out at the broken wall and the ridge and the long road going down into the south. The wolfhound lay under the table with its head on its paws, and it lifted its head when Muken came in, and thumped its tail once on the floor, and put its head down again.

There was a letter on the table in front of Ulf. Gray wax. A gold seal, broken, the Tenrai heraldry on it still sharp enough to catch the firelight.

[Yorkshire accent, gruff and warm] "Sit, lad," said Ulf.

[narrator] Muken did not sit. He stood in the doorway with his hand pressed to his ribs and looked at the letter.

[Yorkshire accent, gruff and warm, wry] "You've heard the news," Ulf said. "Or you've heard something. You've a face like a man who's heard something."

[Northern English accent, low and rough, flat] "Rob's face," said Muken. "Not mine."

[narrator] Ulf almost smiled. He put his maimed hand flat on the letter.

[Yorkshire accent, gruff and warm, heavy] "We've been called back," he said. "Wystan and I. To Kharven's Seat. To the Hall of Verdicts. To answer to the council for the Foot." He turned the letter over with one finger, so that the broken gold seal lay uppermost. "It's the old summons. The one that binds every Warlord of the north to come and give account to the seven lines when the seven lines call. My grandfather answered it once. My brother answered it a month ago." He let the letter lie. [grim] "Kagenori's hand. And his own seal."

[narrator, pause] Muken stopped breathing.

[Northern English accent, low and rough, rushed] "You can't go," he said.

[narrator] It came out of him flat and quick, before he knew he was going to say it.

[Northern English accent, low and rough, anxious] "You know what's waiting for you if you go back there. You *know*. Everybody in the north knows. They'll put you on your knees in that circle the way they did Torvald and they'll take your head off on the floor of it and send it home in a cask." His voice had begun to rise. [shouts] "You can't go. Either of you. You can't."

[Yorkshire accent, gruff and warm, gently] "Nonsense, boy," said Ulf Stark.

[narrator] He said it gently. That was the worst of it.

[Yorkshire accent, gruff and warm, patient] "Think it through," Ulf said. "Like you thought through the wall. If we don't go, what happens?" He did not wait. "Kagenori has his excuse. That's what the summons is for. It's not an invitation, lad, it's a door he's holding open. We walk through it, or he walks through ours. If the Warlords of Stark and Greymane refuse the old summons, the seven lines can call the north in open rebellion, under their own law, and come up the Kessen road with every house-guard they've got before the snow's off the passes. Before we've buried our dead. Before the Foot's walls are mended. Before the boys you led up that ridge have had time to learn which end of a spear's which." He spread his hands. [tired] "And we lose it all. Everything we bought with Torvald and the thirty at the breach. Gone by midsummer."

[soft Scottish accent, dry, calm] "Ulf's right, boy," said Wystan Greymane.

[narrator] He had turned from the window. He stood with his long gray hands clasped behind his back and the white winter glare off the ridge behind him, and his long gray face was perfectly calm.

[soft Scottish accent, dry, calm] "Our lives," he said, "can be spent for the people. That's what they're for. That's what they've always been for." He looked at Muken steadily. "We're old men. We've had our years. We've had more of them than most of the men who've followed us. And the north doesn't need us any more, the way it needed us a month ago." He paused. [quietly] "They have you now."

[narrator] Muken stared at him.

[Northern English accent, low and rough, cracking] "Why?" he said.

[narrator] His voice cracked on it. He did not care.

[Northern English accent, low and rough, desperate] "Why does anybody else have to die? We took the Foot. We *won*. Thirty of them are lying out there by the breach with their eyes closed, and the red-haired one with his spear in his hands, and we won. Isn't that enough? Isn't that *enough* for them?" He took a step into the room. [angry] "Why do they get to decide? Those six in their chairs. Why do they get to decide the fate of everybody else in the world? Who lives and who starves and who goes to the post. Why do you have to go and kneel on their floor and die like that, like... like *cattle,* on their script, because a man in a gold chair wrote you a letter?"

[narrator] Neither of the old men answered at once.

Ulf looked down at his own hands on the table, the whole one and the maimed one. When he spoke, his voice was very quiet.

[Yorkshire accent, gruff and warm, quietly] "That's just the way this life is, lad," he said. "That's all. There's no more to it than that." He was quiet for a moment. [tired] "We give. And we give. And then we give some more. And nobody promises us anything back for it, not the Sky, not the lines, not the people we give it for. We're not guaranteed a thing. Not a harvest. Not a spring. Not a good death in our beds with our grandchildren round us." He lifted his head. [firm] "We give anyway. Because the alternative is a world where nobody does. And I've seen what that world looks like, Muken. I rode in it with Gimbzo for two summers. I'll not leave it to my sons."

[narrator] Muken shook his head. He could not stop shaking it.

[Northern English accent, low and rough, despair] "It's not right," he said. "It's not right. You shouldn't have to."

[soft Scottish accent, dry, softly] "No," Wystan agreed. "We shouldn't."

[narrator] He came across the room. He was very tall, and he stopped in front of Muken and looked down at him from his great gray height, and then he put one long gray hand on Muken's good shoulder, the way he had at the foot of the ridge in the dark before the Foot.

[soft Scottish accent, dry, gently] "But we have to," he said. "And our duties aren't finished yet. Not quite."

---

[narrator] Ulf Stark stood up.

He did it slowly, as if his knees hurt him, and they probably did. The camp chair creaked under him and then was silent. The wolfhound lifted its head. Ulf stood at the table with the broken letter in front of him and the Stark wolf on the wall over his head, and he put his maimed hand flat on his own chest, over his heart, and looked at Muken.

[Yorkshire accent, gruff and warm, solemn, slowly] "As the Warlord of House Stark," he said, "of Hollenmark and the eastern March and the borders, by the right of my father and my father's father, and by the right of my brother Torvald, who held it before me and named me to it, and who died for it on the floor of their hall..."

[narrator] His voice had dropped into an old cadence, slow and heavy, like stones being set one on another.

[Yorkshire accent, gruff and warm, solemn, slowly] "I swear," said Ulf Stark, "that all the power of the house of Stark, and all its tools, its riders and its spears, its roads and its halls and its granaries, its debts and its oaths and its friends, are left to you, Muken. Muken the Tokenless. To hold for the north, until you see fit to put them down." He did not take his hand from his chest. "Before witnesses. Before the Sky."

[narrator] Muken could not speak.

Wystan Greymane stepped back, a pace, so that he stood beside Ulf. He put his own long gray hand flat over his own heart.

[soft Scottish accent, dry, solemn, slowly] "As the Warlord of House Greymane," he said, "of the Ridgewall and the western wall against Undaar-Keth, by the right of my fathers, who have held the ridge since before there was a north to hold it for..."

[narrator] He did not raise his voice. He never did.

[soft Scottish accent, dry, solemn, slowly] "I swear that all the power of the house of Greymane, and all its tools, its wall and its watch, its men and its wolves and its long memory, are left to you, Muken the Tokenless. To hold for the north. Until you see fit to put them down." He paused. "The ridge does not move. Before witnesses. Before the Sky."

[narrator] The fire settled in the hearth. Out in the yard, faintly, someone was splitting wood: a child, by the uneven rhythm of it, striking three times for every split.

Then the door behind Muken banged open.

---

### III · The Sons

[narrator] There were two of them. They came in out of the cold together, fast, with snow on their shoulders and their breath still going white, as if they had run the whole way from the stables.

The first was a Stark. Anyone would have known it from across a field. He was perhaps twenty-two, and built the way the Stark men were built, broad and heavy through the back and shoulders, but there was something less finished about him than about Rob or Ulf: as if the frame had been laid down and the weight not yet put on it. His hair was brown, cut short in the northern military style. His jaw was the Stark jaw, wide and square, and Muken saw it tighten on the left side, hard, before he said anything at all. He had deep brown eyes set under a heavy brow, and they went straight past Muken to the old man at the table.

The second was a Greymane, and he was gray already. His hair was black, long, tied back at the nape, and everything else about him was gray: his eyes, his cloak, the long narrow face, the stillness he had stopped in, just inside the door, as if he had walked into a wall. He was younger than the Stark, nineteen or twenty, and taller even than his father, and there were dark circles under his gray eyes as if he had not slept in a week.

[Yorkshire accent, gruff and warm, quietly] "Lorn," said Ulf Stark. "Godfrey."

[narrator] Lorn Stark looked at the broken letter on the table, and at his father's hand still flat on his chest, and his jaw tightened again on the left side.

Godfrey Greymane was looking only at his father.

[soft Scottish accent, measured, young man, shaking] "You can't do this," he said.

[narrator] His voice was low and rough and shaking very slightly, and he did not seem to be able to stop it.

[soft Scottish accent, measured, young man, pleading] "Father. You can't. You can't just ride down there and... and *give* yourself to them. Like a sack of salt on a scale." His hands had closed into fists at his sides. [crying] "I've already lost Mother. I buried her in the autumn, on the ridge, with the frost already in the ground. I'm not burying you in a cask. I'm not..."

[narrator] He stopped. He could not go on.

Wystan Greymane looked at his son across the room. Something moved in the long gray face that Muken had never seen there, and it was gone almost before he saw it.

[soft Scottish accent, dry, softly] "We are the keepers of the wall," Wystan said.
