[narrator] He said it quietly. He said it the way you say a thing to a child at night when they have woken from a bad dream, a thing you have said a thousand times before, the words worn smooth with saying.

[soft Scottish accent, dry, softly] "We are the shields of the innocent," he said. "We are the protectors of the realm. We stand on the ridge so that nobody behind us has to. That's what we are. That's all we've ever been. We are the Greymane." He held his son's eyes. "And it's our duty, Godfrey. It was my father's duty, and his father's. And when I'm gone, it'll be yours."

[narrator] Godfrey said nothing. His fists were shaking.

[soft Scottish accent, dry, softly] "The ridge does not move," said Wystan Greymane softly.

[narrator] His son's fists opened, slowly, a finger at a time.

Lorn Stark had not spoken. He stood just inside the door with the snow melting on his shoulders, looking at his father across the room. Muken saw him look. He saw the young Stark's jaw tighten, and work, and tighten again, and he saw him decide, somewhere in himself, that there was no argument he could make that his father had not already made to himself and answered.

[Yorkshire accent, young, rough, barely audible] "Why," said Lorn Stark.

[narrator] It was barely a question. It was barely even a word.

Ulf looked at his son.

[Yorkshire accent, gruff and warm, heavy] "You know why," he said. "You've known since Torvald." [sighs] He let out a long breath through his nose. "Our generation has to come to an end, lad. It's had its time and it's spent it. Most of it badly. We lost the war, forty years of it, and buried our sons in it, and we held on through every one of those years out of nothing but stubbornness, and stubbornness won't take the north back. Something else will." He did not look at Muken; every man in the room knew where his eyes would have gone. [firm] "So we're putting it in your hands. Yours, and Godfrey's. And his." His voice grew rougher. "We entrust him to you, Lorn. Muken. Guide him. Stand beside him. Teach him what he doesn't know, and learn from him what you don't. And see that you win the battle against the Keld."

[narrator] Lorn Stark was silent while the snow went to water on his shoulders.

Then he put his right hand flat on his chest, over his heart, the way his father had. He held it there. Then he closed it into a fist, slowly, against his breastbone, and pressed it there, hard, once. It was the Stark gesture. Muken had seen Rob do it, and Mark, and every Stark rider on the road to the Foot, before a fight or over a grave. It meant: [slowly] *here. This. I hold it.*

[Yorkshire accent, young, rough, slowly] "By my honor," said Lorn Stark.

---

[narrator] Muken stood in the middle of the room and did not understand any of it.

He did not understand why they were looking at him. Ulf and Wystan, with their hands on their hearts. Lorn with his fist against his chest. Godfrey, gray-faced, with his fists finally open. All of them looking at him as if he were something, as if he were the answer to a question he had never heard asked. He was nothing. He was a cutting woman's boy from three galleries under Halcrest, with a scrap-iron sword and a sister and a dead token in his coat. He had nothing anybody could swear to. Nothing anybody could put their trust in.

And yet.

The commonfolk had been saying it since he was small. He had never paid it any mind. The old cutters in the Orrel galleries, watching him carry a full load up four levels at nine years old without stopping: [old man, Northern English accent] *That one's going somewhere.* [narrator] The women at the stamping floor, after he broke the steward's nose at thirteen: [woman, Northern English accent] *He'll lead men, that one, you watch.* [narrator] The Lanternless, in his first season, when he had taken a column through a burning village and brought it out the far side with every man in it: [rough mercenary, Northern English accent, laughing] *Born to it, the little bastard. Born to it.* [narrator] And the old woman with no teeth on the woodpile, four days ago: [very old woman, Yorkshire accent, cracked] *Your father must have taught you that.*

[narrator] He had thought it was just a thing people said. The kind of thing you said about any boy who was big for his age and didn't die.

Now two Warlords of the north were giving him their houses.

Wystan Greymane looked at him, and then at Ulf. Ulf looked at him. For a moment the old men only looked. Then Ulf's mouth moved in his braided beard, into something that was very nearly a smile and was not.

[Yorkshire accent, gruff and warm, softly] "We're sorry, lad," he said. "Truly. We'd have liked to see it. Where you go. What you become." His voice roughened. [sad] "We'd have liked to be there when you reach the top of whatever it is you're climbing. But we won't be."

[soft Scottish accent, dry, softly] "You'll have our sons," said Wystan Greymane.

[narrator] He put his hand on Muken's shoulder one last time, and squeezed it, and let go.

Behind Muken, in the doorway, very quietly, someone drew a breath.

He did not turn round. He had known she was there since the door banged open. He had felt his own breathing slow down the moment the Stark and the Greymane came in out of the snow, the way it always did, without his ever wondering why. She had been standing in the cold of the doorway the whole time with her gray coat buttoned to the throat and her kit under her arm and her face doing nothing at all.

She had heard every word.

---

### IV · The Road South

[narrator] They rode out the next morning, before light.

Two old men on two gray horses, with no escort and no banners, down the long road from the Foot into the south, under the last of the aurora: a slow green curtain over the ridge, fringed violet at its hem, that laid a cold sheen on the snow of the road in front of them and went on burning after they had passed out of sight. Ulf Stark had left his wolfhound tied in the gatehouse. It howled for an hour after he was gone, and nobody could make it stop, and in the end Muken went in and sat on the floor beside it with his back against Shigure Tenrai's table and his hand on the great rough head until it stopped. It lay down with its head on his boot. It did not get up again for the rest of the day.

[narrator, softly] Eleven days later, a crow came up the road from the south, and Rob Stark read the letter it carried, standing in the yard by the woodpile, and then folded it very small and put it inside his coat and went away and did not speak to anybody until the next morning.

Muken never asked to read it. Nobody did.

He went down to the village at the bottom of the ridge that afternoon and chopped wood until it was too dark to see the block, until the snow round him had gone the deep blue of the hour after sunset and the windows of the forty houses showed one by one the yellow of their lamps. The old woman with no teeth came and sat on a stump nearby, wrapped in her three shawls, and watched him, and kept her own counsel. When it was full dark she got up and went home. On her way past, she put her hand on his arm, very lightly, just for a moment, and left it there, and took it away.

---

### V · The Gall of Them

[narrator] Nine days after that, a Greymane outrider came up the Kessen road at a gallop with his horse lathered to the hocks and told them what was coming up it behind him.

A party out of the south and east, riding hard, two days behind him on the road, maybe less. He gave the rest of it standing at Shigure's old table with the snow going to water on his shoulders, in the flat short way outriders gave things, as if every word had to be paid for out of his own breath.

[soft Scottish accent, terse, breathless] "A lady," the outrider said. "Moto, by the veil. Silver on her. Twenty with her, no banners, and a Yukari woman walking barefoot in the snow like it wasn't there.

[narrator] Muken heard it in the gatehouse, standing at the window where Wystan Greymane had stood, with Ulf Stark's wolfhound lying across his feet.

Something went up through him from the soles of his boots to the roots of his white hair, hot, and black, and very old, and he could not have said where it came from or what its name was.

[Northern English accent, low and rough, bitter, low] "The *gall* of them," he said.

[narrator] He said it low. Rob, and Lorn, and Godfrey Greymane, standing round the table with the outrider, all turned to look at him.

[Northern English accent, low and rough, bitter] "A Moto lord," Muken said. "Coming *here*. To the Foot. A month after they took the head of the man who took it. And a Yukari with her." [laughs] He laughed. It was not a good laugh. "Barefoot. As if the Moto couldn't come to us on their own, they've got to walk a Yukari up the road beside them, so we'll look at her bare feet in the snow and open the gate and think it's all been made right somewhere behind our backs while we were burying our dead." His hand had closed on the edge of the window frame until the knuckles went white. [angry] "What do they want? What can they possibly want from us that they haven't already *taken*?"

[narrator] The outrider looked at his boots.

He could feel it rising in him. The warmth, the reinforcement, came down into his arms and legs in a fight; this came from somewhere older than that, and heavier, and it sat behind his left eye like a coal in a grate, and every time he thought of Ulf Stark riding down that road in the dark with his hand flat on his chest, it burned a little brighter. Ulf and Wystan. Torvald before them. The thirty at the breach. The red-haired boy with his spear. The forty houses at the bottom of the ridge breaking up their furniture to burn. Sixteen years of it. Eleven years of the Tenrai on that ridge. Forty years of war.

And now the Moto were coming up the road to make *peace.*

[soft Scottish accent, measured, tired] "You can't," said Godfrey Greymane.

[narrator] Muken turned his head.

Godfrey was standing at the end of the table with his long gray hands flat on it, the way his father used to stand, and his gray eyes were on Muken's face. They had dark circles under them still. They would have them, Muken thought, for years.

[soft Scottish accent, measured, low, steady] "You can't show them that," Godfrey said. "What's in your face. Not when they come." His voice was low and steady and very tired. "They're coming to make peace. Whatever else they are. They're coming to *help*, if the outrider's right about the veil and who's wearing it. You can't meet them at the gate looking like that."

[Northern English accent, low and rough, incredulous] "Help us?"

[narrator] Muken let go of the window frame. He turned round. The wolfhound lifted its head off his feet.

[Northern English accent, low and rough, cold, controlled] "*Help* us?" he said. "They've been saying that for sixteen years, Godfrey. They've been saying it my whole life. Every Moto lord who ever put his seal to a requisition in the north said it was to help us. Every tithe. Every closed road. Every granary they took for the house-guard. *For the good of the north. For order. For unity.*" He was not shouting. He had not raised his voice at all. [bitter] "And the whole time they were saying it, we were dying. Of hunger. Of the cough. Of the cold, with no oil and no wood. The whole time." He took a step toward the table. [cold] "They executed your father, Godfrey. Not a month gone. On their floor. Do you not *feel* anything?"

[narrator, pause] The words hung in the room like breath in the cold.

Rob Stark had gone pale. Lorn Stark, at the other end of the table, had shut his eyes.

Godfrey Greymane looked at Muken. His long gray face did not change at all.

[soft Scottish accent, measured, barely audible] "Of course I feel it," he said.

[narrator] His voice had dropped almost to nothing.

[soft Scottish accent, measured, quietly, angry] "I feel angry. I feel so angry I can't sleep. I lie awake every night on that cot in the barracks and I feel it, all night, and when the sun comes up I'm still feeling it." He did not take his hands off the table. [cold] "Don't you dare ask me if I feel anything. You didn't know him. You knew him a month. I knew him my whole life."

[narrator] Muken said nothing.

[soft Scottish accent, measured, bitter] "It was us," Godfrey said. "The Greymane. The Shieldwardens of the ridge. It was us they put on the front lines against the Zettari for forty years, us and the Starks, because we were northerners and northerners were cheap. We held the passes for the Moto with our own bodies while Gimbzo's lines sat behind us in the warm. We were used like a tool, Muken. Like an axe. Used up, and then thrown away when the handle split." His jaw set. "And then they took my father's head for it. So yes. I feel something."

[narrator] He let that sit.

[soft Scottish accent, measured, firm] "But you have to remember your place," Godfrey Greymane said. "You have to. Because nobody else is going to remember it for you any more."

[narrator] He straightened up from the table.

[soft Scottish accent, measured, cold, precise] "You're a tokenless," he said. "You've no family. No house. No name. Nothing anybody in the south can see, nothing they'd sit across a table from. Even if my father named you lord before he rode out. Even with the Stark and the Greymane sworn to you on a dead man's word." He held Muken's eyes, gray on slate. "When that Moto lady comes up the road, she's going to look at you and see a sixteen-year-old sellsword with a scrap-iron sword and a bad temper. And so is the Yukari. And so is every man in the north who's watching to see what we do next." He paused. [slowly] "So if you're going to be a king, Muken. If that's what my father died for. If that's what Ulf died for." His voice did not waver. [firm] "Then at least act like one."

[narrator] The wolfhound had laid its head back down on Muken's boot.

Muken stood in the middle of the gatehouse of Kharven's Foot, under the two wolves on the wall, with the black hot thing still burning behind his left eye and a cut across his ribs that would not close, and looked at Godfrey Greymane while the light went red and long across the floor from the broken window.

He knew Godfrey was right.

He hated, more than he had ever hated anything in his life, that Godfrey was right.

[sighs] He let out a long breath through his nose, slowly. It went out of him like smoke. And the coal behind his eye, very slowly, the way a fire goes down when you stop feeding it, began to dim.

[Northern English accent, low and rough, quiet] "Two days," he said.

[narrator] Godfrey said nothing.

[Northern English accent, low and rough, steady] "Two days, the outrider said." Muken looked down at the wolfhound on his boot, and then at the broken window, and the long road going down into the south where Ulf Stark had ridden away in the dark. "Then we've two days to mend the gate."

# Chapter Nine: The Queen's Will

[narrator] *Book One of the Kōkan · Chapter Nine: The Queen's Will*

### I · The Fires of the Keld

[narrator] There was no wood on the tundra, so the Keld burned fat.

It went into the stone lamps in long white slabs, seal and reindeer and the yellow tallow of the great horned musk-beasts that came down off the high ice in the Thin Weeks, and it burned low and smoky and very hot, with a smell like a butcher's yard on a summer afternoon and a light the deep yellow of butter, which climbed the curve of the hide walls and turned them the color of honey held up to a fire. The war-lodge of Aksel Keld was lit by nine of those lamps. It was a great domed tent of reindeer hide sewn over a frame of whalebone and driftwood carried a hundred miles from the northern shore, and inside it the air was thick with smoke and fat and the sharp sour reek of the fermented milk the Keld drank from horn cups the size of a man's two fists. [wind howling] Outside, the wind came over the white flat world from the pole with nothing in a thousand miles to stop it, and leaned on the lodge, and moaned in the guy-ropes. Through the smoke-hole at the crown of the dome a strip of night showed, and across it the aurora moved, green going to violet at its hem, slow as weed in a current. Inside, it was warm enough to sweat.

Kagenori Tenrai Moto had not taken off his outer coat. He sat on a stack of white bear-pelts across the central lamp from the Keld warlord, in gold-trimmed black that the lamplight picked out thread by thread, with his narrow face composed and his hands folded in his lap. The smoke did not seem to touch him. Behind his left shoulder, a pace back, stood a man in plain gray with no house colors at all: tall, lean, hooded, with a long pale face and very pale eyes and a stillness like a heron's in shallow water. He had not spoken since the Tenrai lord came into the lodge, and every Keld captain in the tent knew his name anyway. He had been out on the tundra for six weeks, going from fire to fire with Tenrai silver in one hand and Tenrai seed grain in the other.

[narrator, pause] Karogane.

The Iron Fist of the Keld sat on no pelts at all. He sat on the bare packed snow of the lodge floor, cross-legged, the way the Keld had always sat at their own fires, with a horn cup on his knee.

[narrator] Aksel Keld was eighteen years old, and he had never lost a battle.

He had fought eleven. Some of them against the hill clans that came down off the Shiogane in the bad years to raid the reindeer herds, and some of them against Tenrai tax columns before the Tenrai learned better than to send columns onto the ice, and one of them, the one the Keld sang about, against a Thornwall warband four times the size of his own that had come out past the treeline to take the whale-oil trade by force. He had broken it on the open ice in a single afternoon, in a whiteout, by moving his riders through the snow-blind so fast and so quietly that the Thornwall men had died believing they were fighting ghosts. He had been fifteen.

He did not look like a boy. He was short, as the south counted tallness, and built the way the tundra built its people: low and wide and dense, with a barrel chest and short powerful legs and a neck like a tree-stump, all of it packed down under the weight of the cold until nothing was left on him but what the cold allowed. His face was broad and flat and wind-burned a deep red-brown across the cheekbones. His eyes were narrow and very black and set deep against the glare. Three thin blue lines were tattooed down his chin from the lower lip, the marks of a Keld who had killed a man in defense of the herds, and a fourth had been added beside them, thicker, for a chief. His hair was black and coarse and braided close to his skull in tight rows, and the braids were threaded with small rings of walrus ivory, one for each fight he had won.

[narrator, slowly] His right hand was iron.

It had been a hand, once. A Tenrai sword had taken it off at the wrist in his second battle, when he was thirteen, and the Keld smiths had made him another out of black iron and walrus tusk and sinew, a heavy articulated gauntlet that strapped to the stump and the forearm and closed by a cord running up the sleeve to his shoulder. He could not feel with it. He could not hold a cup with it, which was why the cup sat on his knee. But he could make a fist with it, a fist of solid iron the size of a man's skull, and he had killed more men with that fist than with any spear. That was where the name had come from. Most of the south believed the Iron Fist was only a name for how he ruled. The Keld knew better.

He ruled that way too.

[narrator] He looked across the lamp at the Tenrai lord with his black eyes narrowed against the smoke, and let the silence run as long as it liked.

[Icelandic accent, flat, rough, young man] "We march in two days," he said.

[narrator] His voice was low and flat and rough, the way voices got on the ice, where the wind took anything that was not said plainly.

Kagenori inclined his head. [light Japanese accent, smooth] "Yes," he said. "That is true."

[narrator] He unfolded his hands.

[light Japanese accent, smooth, pleasant] "And when we meet them on the ice," Kagenori said, "I will tear the heart out of every Stark and every Greymane who has chosen to follow Muken the Tokenless." He said it pleasantly. "With a sentence, before a single spear moves. Karogane will stand between the lines, before either army has drawn a blade, and he will tell them who their tokenless king really is." The faintest smile touched his closed mouth. [cold, amused] "Gimbzo's son. The butcher's boy. The heir of the line that burned their villages and hanged their fathers and starved their children for twenty years. Once they learn his heritage, Lord Aksel, nobody in the north will follow him one step further. Starks, Greymanes, the boys with spears, all of them. They'll put him on a post themselves."

[narrator] Aksel did not answer at once.

He turned the horn cup on his knee with his left hand, slowly, a quarter-turn, and looked down into the sour white milk in it.

[Icelandic accent, flat, rough, young man, inner voice] *It's a filthy thing,* [narrator] he thought. [Icelandic accent, flat, rough, young man, inner voice, disgusted] *To break a man with his own father's name, in front of the men who love him. Before he's even had the chance to lift a spear. It's the kind of thing the Moto do. It's exactly the kind of thing.*

[narrator] He knew it. He disliked it. He had disliked it since Karogane first sat down at his grandfather's fire and laid it out, six weeks ago, in that soft dry voice, between one cup and the next.

And it was true.

That was the trouble with it. Every word of it was true, and every Keld at every fire on the tundra would know it was true the moment they heard it. Nobody on the ice had any love for the Kōkan. Nobody anywhere did, any more: the Keld, the clans, the Banner families, the hill people, every one of them had given it up. The red line had been the Moto's crown for a thousand years, and for the last twenty of them the red line had meant Gimbzo, and Gimbzo had meant fire. The Keld had lost a third of their herds in the war to Gimbzo's requisitions. Aksel's own father had died on a Gimbzo levy, fighting the Zettari in a war that was nothing to do with the ice, on a field so far south he had never seen snow there. Most of the north, if you asked it honestly, would have told you that the Kōkan line was the worst thing that had ever happened to it. That its ending had been the one good thing to come out of the whole war.

And now there was one left. And the Starks had hidden him. And put a crown on him.

[Icelandic accent, flat, rough, young man, inner voice] *Let them find out, then,* [narrator] Aksel thought. [Icelandic accent, flat, rough, young man, inner voice, hard] *If he's worth following, he'll survive it. If he's not, better they learn now than after they've bled for him.*

[narrator] He lifted his eyes to the Tenrai lord.

[Icelandic accent, flat, rough, young man] "If you keep your promise," he said, "you've nothing to worry about."

[narrator] He let it sit.

[Icelandic accent, flat, rough, young man, hard] "No tithe on the ice. Not from the Tenrai, not from the seven lines, not from anyone, while the world lasts. In writing. Under your seal and the Shirogane's." His black eyes did not move from Kagenori's face. "The seed grain you've already sent stays sent, and more comes in the spring, enough for every fire. And no Moto boot on Keld snow without the Keld's leave. Ever." He lifted the cup, finally, with his left hand, and drank, and set it down. [proud] "Keep that, and it'll be the Keld that win this battle. Our path. Our will. The ice doesn't care about the Kōkan or the Tenrai or anybody's crown. It only cares who knows how to cross it." His voice did not change. [firm] "We'll win it for you. For the true king of the north."

[narrator] Kagenori Tenrai Moto laughed.

[chuckles] It was a soft laugh, and quite genuine, and it went round the smoky lodge and made two of the Keld captains glance at each other.

[light Japanese accent, smooth, amused] "You honor me, Lord Aksel," he said. "But you have the wrong man." He spread his narrow hands. "I am not the king. I have not become anything. Not yet." He tilted his head. [softly] "And I do not mean to."

[narrator] Aksel's black eyes narrowed a fraction.

He did not believe it. Nobody on the tundra did. Every Keld fire for six weeks had talked about nothing else: that Kagenori Tenrai wanted the red chair for himself, that he had wanted it for twenty years, that the Tenrai had been waiting beside the Kōkan in their gold for a thousand years to sit in it. Everybody knew what Kagenori wanted.

Except that everybody had it wrong.

It took Aksel a long time to see it. He saw it, in the end, the way he saw a herd turning on the ice a mile off in a whiteout, by watching what moved around it. He saw it in the way Kagenori said the word *queen.* He saw it in the way the Tenrai lord's narrow face changed, very slightly, almost not at all, whenever he spoke of the Shirogane, of the silver line, the old rival of the red. He saw it in the letters Karogane had carried, every one of them sealed with two seals, the Tenrai gold beside the Shirogane silver, and the silver always on the left, the place of precedence.

[narrator, slowly] Kagenori Tenrai wanted a crown for another head than his own.

He wanted a kingdom made for one woman, small and gray and hard as a hatchet, sitting very upright in a silver chair in the Hall of Verdicts, under worked lamps that burned white and steady on the Guild's draw while the whole north froze in the dark. He wanted to build it for her with his own hands, stone by stone, and set her on it, and stand at her right side, and see her content. For a thousand years the Shirogane had been the rival of the Kōkan, the hard silver line that had always stood one step from the red chair and never been allowed to sit in it. If the Kōkan were truly ended, and the last of them put on a post by his own people, then there was one line in the Moto house with a better claim to the north than any other.

And Shizuha Shirogane had the most to gain of anyone alive.

[light Japanese accent, smooth, softly] "All of the north will accept Queen Shizuha's rule," Kagenori said softly. "Every house of it. Every fire." He looked at Aksel across the lamp. [cold] "And those who do not accept it, Lord Aksel, will die. Quickly, I hope. But they will die." His voice did not change at all. "I hope you understand that. I'd not have you say later that you were not told."

[narrator] The wind leaned on the lodge. The fat-lamps guttered and steadied, and the yellow light swung up the hide and settled again. Behind Kagenori's shoulder, Karogane stood exactly as he had stood all evening, and his very pale eyes had come to rest on the Keld warlord's iron hand.

Aksel Keld held the Tenrai lord's eyes across the lamp while the aurora went over the smoke-hole and was gone.

Then he lowered his head, very slightly, and lifted his iron fist off his knee, and set it against his chest, over his heart, [thud] with a dull heavy sound like a hammer laid on an anvil.

[Icelandic accent, flat, rough, young man, solemn, slowly] "The queen's will," said the Iron Fist of the Keld, "is absolute."

[narrator] Behind his flat red-brown face, where nobody in the lodge could see it, a thought went past and was gone, like a fox crossing snow.

[Icelandic accent, flat, rough, young man, inner voice, whispers] *For now.*

---

### II · The Gate

[narrator] Lorn Stark had been standing at the gate of Kharven's Foot since the sun came up.

It was mended now. Two days, the outrider had said, and two days it had taken, two days and a night of every man and boy in the fort carrying stone up from the rubble of the breach and fitting it back into the gap in the curtain wall, course by course, while the Greymane masons shouted at them and the snow came down and nobody slept. The new stone was paler than the old. You could see the line of the mend from the bottom of the ridge, a long uneven seam of gray-white running down through the darker Tenrai gray, and in the early sun the frost on the new stone glittered where the old stone only darkened. Muken had looked at it, the morning it was finished, and said they should leave it that way. That every man who came up the Kessen road should see where the wall had come down.

The gate itself was new timber, raw pine, still weeping sap in the cold, and the sap froze where it ran in beads as gold as amber. Somebody had nailed the Stark wolf to it, and the Greymane wolf beside it, and above both of them, on a long pole over the gatehouse, a banner that had not existed a week ago.

It was white. Plain white wool, the cheapest the fort's stores had, unbleached and unembroidered, with nothing on it at all. The boys of the vanguard had made it the night after the crow came up from the south with the news of Ulf and Wystan, sitting in the barracks with needles borrowed from Yukazuri's kit, because nobody could agree what should go on it, and in the end they had decided that a tokenless king ought to have a banner with no token on it either. It hung over the gate of Kharven's Foot in the still morning, blank as new snow, against a sky going from rose at the ridge-line to a hard clean blue overhead.

Lorn Stark stood under it in his father's mail, which was too big for him across the shoulders, with his father's axe across his back, and watched the road.

He was twenty-two. Four weeks ago he had been his father's son and nothing more, with Hollenmark a long way off and nothing on his mind but his next hunt. Now he was the Warlord of House Stark, by his father's word on a cold road and his father's death at the Seat, and he had not yet learned what to do with his hands while he waited.

He saw the riders a long time before they reached him. Twenty-odd in plain gray, coming up the long road from the south at a steady walk, without banners. And in the middle of them, impossible to mistake even at a mile, a great black shape that moved like water over the snow and was too big for any horse.

He did not move until they were at the foot of the gate.

Then he straightened, the way his father had taught him, the way every Stark son was taught before he could walk: chin up, shoulders back, weight even on both feet, hands still. And he watched them come.

The woman in white came first. She dismounted at the foot of the gate with the ease of someone who had been riding for a long time, and her near-white robes were gray with the dust and slush of the road to the hem, and the glass beads of a veil were pinned back from her face and clicked faintly at her temples when she moved, throwing small sparks of the sun. She had a katana at her hip, bound in white cord. She looked up at the blank banner over the gate, and kept looking, before she looked at him.

Behind her came the two others. A great bald man in copper and gray, the copper gone red-bronze in the low sun, who moved like a bull and dismounted like one, heavily, with a grunt. And a slight sharp man in indigo with restless fingers, who had his eyes on the mended seam in the wall before his boots touched the snow.

Lorn bowed exactly as low as a Warlord of the north bowed to a Moto lord, which was very little.

[Yorkshire accent, young, rough, formal] "Lady Kiyome," he said.

[narrator] She inclined her head.

He turned. [Yorkshire accent, young, rough, formal] "Lord Tetsugen." The bald man grunted. "Lord Hayato." The slight man bowed, quickly, and his eyes went straight back to the wall.

[narrator] Then Lorn looked past them.

The great black cat had stopped a little way down the road and lain down in the snow like a dog, with its yellow eyes half closed and its long ivory fangs resting on its paws, and the rider had dismounted beside it. Lorn had heard the outrider's account. He had not quite believed it. He believed it now. The Zettari was the biggest man he had ever seen, half a head taller than Wystan Greymane had been, in a black-and-gold robe that hung open over a chest like a barrel of nails, and he was standing in the snow by his cat looking up at the gate of Kharven's Foot, and he was, Lorn thought, very young.

Lorn let himself look. He thought about the forty years. He thought about every Stark who had gone south on a Moto levy and come home in a cart, or not come home. He thought about the Zettari spears they had faced on those fields.

Then he thought about his father's hand flat on his chest in the gatehouse, and Muken's face, and what Godfrey had said.

[Yorkshire accent, young, rough, inner voice] *If you're going to be a king, act like one.*

[narrator] The same went for a king's men.

[Yorkshire accent, young, rough, formal] "Prince," said Lorn Stark.

[narrator] He bowed to the Zettari exactly as low as he had bowed to the Moto. Not one finger's width more or less.

Then he straightened.

[Yorkshire accent, young, rough, formal] "Our lord is waiting for you in the meeting chamber," he said.

[narrator] Kiyome Byakuya had been looking at the white banner again. Her head came round.

[light Japanese accent, calm, warm, surprised] "Lord?" she said.

[narrator] Lorn nodded.

[Yorkshire accent, young, rough, steady] "Yes," he said. "Lord. And more than lord." He glanced up, once, at the blank white wool over the gate. "The crow came up from the south three days ago with the news from the Seat. My father, and Lord Wystan." His voice did not change. He had practiced it. "The next morning every Stark and every Greymane in this fort knelt in the yard by the woodpile. Nobody told us to. Nobody gave an order. The boys of the vanguard started it, and then the old men, and then all of us." He paused. [proud] "There are no Banner Houses yet, my lady. There's no crown, and no Seat, and no chair, and no rite. But the north has a king. We've chosen him."

[narrator] He looked at her steadily.

[Yorkshire accent, young, rough, proud, slowly] "Muken is king now," said Lorn Stark. "King Muken. The Tokenless."

[narrator] Something moved in Kiyome's gray eyes. The fine silver rings in them, which had lain quiet as pond-ice the whole time she had been looking at the banner, turned once, very slowly, and were still again.

She did not say anything.

Lorn turned. [Yorkshire accent, young, rough] "This way," he said, and led them in.

---

[narrator] He took them through the gate and across the yard. It had been swept, mostly. The dead were gone from it, buried on the ridge in a long row under cairns of stone from the breach, and the burned stables had been pulled down and their timber stacked for the fires, and the slush had been shovelled into banks along the walls. But there were still dark places in the trampled snow by the well that no amount of shovelling had got out, and the barracks door still had the marks of axes on it, and the long pale wall of split wood still ran along the barracks wall as high as a man's shoulder, with children carrying more to it.

Lorn did not hurry, and did not look back at them. He walked the way his father had walked, the way the Starks walked through their own halls: heavy, even, unhurried, with his weight going straight down through his heels into the ground and his hands still at his sides. A well-trained warrior's walk. He had trained it every day since he was six years old. He had never once thought, until this morning, that it would matter more than any sword stroke he had ever learned.

He could feel the eyes of the fort on them as they crossed the yard. The Greymane men on the walls. The boys at the woodpile, with their arms full of split pine, stopped dead. The old woman with no teeth on her stump by the barracks door, in her three shawls, watching the woman in white go by with her toothless mouth set in a long hard line.

The whole of Kharven's Foot had gone silent, except for the wind, and the creak of the new gate, and a child somewhere splitting wood, three blows for every split.

---

### III · The King of the Foot

[narrator] The meeting chamber had been the Tenrai garrison's mess hall. It was the largest room in the keep, long and low and stone-floored, with a hearth at one end big enough to roast an ox in, where a fire of split pine was burning orange and loud and throwing its color halfway down the floor. The long tables had been pushed back against the walls. In their place, in the middle of the floor, someone had set one table from the barracks, rough pine with a split down the middle, and round it a scatter of mismatched chairs. Shigure Tenrai's carved chair from the gatehouse. A bench from the stables. Two camp stools. A barrel.

At the head of it, with his back to the great hearth, sat the king of the north.

Muken had not changed his coat. He still wore the dead sergeant's coat, cut down to fit him, brown wool gone the color of tea, with the darns at the cuffs in his sister's neat small stitches. But there was mail under it now, Ulf Stark's own mail, taken in at the sides by the Stark armorer until it nearly fitted. The scrap-iron sword lay across the table in front of him, unsheathed, with its black pitted blade turned edge-away. The seam on his cheek from the Vessant road had healed to a long raised pink line from his jaw to the corner of his eye. His left arm was out of its sling at last, though he still held it a little close to his side. His white hair was tied back with the same strip of leather. On his belt, hanging where every merchant in Halcrest wore his oldest contract, where anybody who looked could see it and nobody would see it hum, hung a small bronze seal-token stamped with the Stark wolf.

Ulf Stark's wolfhound lay across his feet under the table, enormous and gray, with its chin on his boot.

Godfrey Greymane sat at his right hand, on the bench from the stables, very straight, in gray, with the Greymane wolf on his breast and his long hands folded on the rough pine. There were still dark circles under his gray eyes. There would be for a long time.

Muken was looking at the door.

He had been looking at it since the outrider came in from the wall to say the riders were at the gate. He had not moved in all that time. Godfrey had told him not to. [soft Scottish accent, measured, firm] *Sit,* [narrator] Godfrey had said, an hour ago, standing in this room while the boys dragged the furniture about. [soft Scottish accent, measured, firm] *Sit, and stay sat, and let them come to you. Don't get up when they come in. Don't go to meet them. You're not a sellsword waiting to be hired any more. They've come up the Kessen road to you. Let them walk the last of it.*

[narrator] So he sat. It was the hardest thing he had done all week.

He could feel it under his breastbone, the black hot thing that had come up in him in the gatehouse when the outrider first said *Moto* and *Zettari* in one breath. It was quieter now. He had been sitting on it for two days, the way you sit on a frightened horse, and it had stopped trying to throw him. But it was there. It sat behind his left eye like a banked coal, and every time he thought of Ulf Stark riding down that road in the dark, it glowed.

He turned his head, slightly, to Godfrey.

[Northern English accent, low and rough, quiet, impatient] "Hopefully this won't take long," he said, low. "The preparations are almost done. I want the columns on the ice road the morning after next, and the Keld won't wait for us to finish being polite."

[narrator] Godfrey did not look away from the door.

[soft Scottish accent, measured] "Yes, sire," he said. "They are almost done."

[narrator, pause] There was a very small pause.

[soft Scottish accent, measured, dry] "And the Keld will wait exactly as long as it takes," said Godfrey Greymane, "for you to learn what these people came all this way to tell you." He still did not look at Muken. "My father used to say that a lord who's in a hurry to end a meeting usually leaves it knowing less than when he came in." His long gray hands did not move on the table. [gently] "Let them talk, sire. Let them say all of it. You can be in a hurry afterward."

[narrator] Muken looked at him sideways for a moment.

[Northern English accent, low and rough, quiet] "You sound like him," he said.

[soft Scottish accent, measured, wry, sad] "I've been told." Godfrey's mouth moved very slightly at the corner. "It's the only thing he left me that the Tenrai couldn't take."

[narrator] Muken did not answer. He looked back at the door.

Under the table, the wolfhound lifted its great gray head off his boot, and its ears came forward, and it looked at the door too.

Footsteps in the passage. Lorn's, heavy and even, which Muken knew. And behind them others that he didn't: a light quick tread on the stone, and a heavy dragging one, and a soft restless one that kept stopping and starting again. And under all of them, very faint, through the stone of the floor, a slow heavy weight, as if something enormous were walking very carefully in a passage built for smaller things.

The door opened.

Lorn Stark came in first and stepped aside, as Godfrey had told him to, and stood against the wall with his hands still at his sides.

And the woman in white came through the door after him.

Muken had been ready for a Moto lord. He had built one in his head for two days: someone hard and narrow and cold, in gold and black, with rings turning in their eyes the way they had turned in Shigure's on the stair. He had been ready to sit like stone and look at that face across his table and not let the black hot thing out from behind his eye.

He was not ready for her.

She stopped just inside the door and looked down the long room at him, and the pale gold morning light from the narrow windows came across her, and he saw her hair.

[narrator, long pause] It was white. Silver-white, whiter than the robes, a white so clean it looked deliberate.

[narrator, slowly] He had seen that white every day of his life.

He had seen it on the pillow beside him in the cart-sized room under Halcrest when his sister was small and frightened of the dark. He had seen it braided down her back in the room over the tea-house and pinned up off her neck at the surgeons' line under the Foot. He had seen it this morning, at dawn, when she came to change the dressing on his side.

[narrator, softly] He had seen it on his mother. Bending over him in the dark of a gallery when he was small, with a lamp behind her, so that her hair caught the light and seemed to burn. Drawing a mark on a wall with a burned stick and saying [gentle, light Japanese accent, softly] *that's ours.* [narrator, softly] Lying on the pallet in the last gallery with her face to the salt, the morning the news came down from Shimane, gone white as the wall.

[narrator] Muken did not get up.

He did not know how he did not. Every muscle in his body had gone to stand. But Godfrey's long gray hand had come down on the rough pine beside his own, flat, not touching him, only there, and he stayed in his chair.

The coal behind his left eye had flared, all at once, white-hot, and the edges of the room went gold.

The woman in white was looking at him. At his face. At his eyes. At the left one. And her own face, which had been calm and composed as still water all the way down the long room, did something very small, and broke, and was mended again so fast that only a man looking straight at her would have seen it.

He was looking straight at her.

Behind her, the bald man in copper and the slight man in indigo had stopped in the doorway. And behind them, stooping under the lintel, came the Zettari, enormous, in black and gold.

Muken did not look at any of them.

He sat at the head of his split pine table, with the scrap-iron sword across it and the dead token on his belt and Ulf Stark's wolfhound standing now beside his chair with its hackles up, and looked at the white-haired woman by the door, and did not say any of the things that were in his mouth.

What he said, in the end, was very quiet.

[Northern English accent, low and rough, quiet, steady] "You'll forgive the chairs," said King Muken. "The Tenrai took the good ones." He nodded at the scatter of benches and stools and barrels round the table. "Sit. All of you. You've come a long way up a cold road to see me, and I've a war to march to the morning after next." He paused. [cold] "So say what you came to say."

---

# Chapter Ten: The Red Line

[narrator] *Book One of the Kōkan · Chapter Ten: The Red Line*

### I · The Chair Before the King

[narrator] The fire in the great hearth had been built of Tenrai pine, split from the stable timbers, and it burned too fast and spat, orange at the heart and a clean gold along the split faces where the resin ran. Every few breaths a knot in it gave with a crack like a knuckle, and a spark went up the flue, and the shadows of the people round the split table jumped on the stone and settled again. The room smelled of pitch and wet wool and the wolfhound, and under all of it, faintly, of the seal-fat lamp somebody had left burning on the sill of the far window because it was the only lamp the Tenrai had not taken. Outside, in the yard, a child was still splitting wood. Three blows for every split. The sound came in through the narrow windows small and steady, like a clock that nobody had wound.

Muken let the prince finish.

He let him say all of it. The chair. The coat. The sword. [deep West African accent] *Younger than I expected.* [narrator] He let him walk round the room with his eyes and come back, and talk about the Keld and the Iron Fist and how entertaining it would be, and say [deep West African accent] *I don't particularly like the Moto,* [narrator] and say [deep West African accent] *not today.* [narrator] He sat through every word of it with his back to the hearth and his hands flat on the rough pine on either side of the scrap-iron sword, the way Godfrey had told him, and he did not move. Godfrey had said [soft Scottish accent, measured] *let them say all of it.* [narrator] Muken had not expected the first of them to take him at his word.

When the prince stepped aside at last, and the space in front of the table stood open, Muken looked at it for a moment. Then he looked at the man who had made it.

[Northern English accent, low and rough, quiet, steady] "You're right about the chair," he said.

[narrator] His voice was quiet. It had the flat salt-gallery evenness it always had when he was holding something down, every word laid down flat and separate, like props in a soft gallery.

[Northern English accent, low and rough, quiet] "They built the table first," Muken said. "Took the doors off the Tenrai mess and laid them on trestles so the wounded had somewhere to lie off the floor. Then they built the gate. Then they mended the wall. The chair was last. That's the right order, I think." He glanced down at the battered pine under his hands. [dry] "If they'd built me a throne before they'd mended the wall, I'd have had them burn it for the fires."

[narrator] He looked back up.

[Northern English accent, low and rough, steady] "And you're right that they chose. I'll give you that as well. Nobody handed me this." He lifted one hand off the table, an inch, and set it down again. [firm] "But you've got the wrong idea of what they chose. They didn't choose me, Prince. They chose to stop kneeling. To the Tenrai, and their tax-men, and their grain-wagons going south full while the long houses ate bark. They'd been choosing it for twenty years and never had a morning to do it on. Then they had one. I happened to be standing in the yard when it came." His mouth moved, very slightly. [quietly] "Somebody had to be. It might as easily have been Lorn. It ought to have been his father."

[narrator] Against the wall, Lorn Stark's jaw tightened on the left side and was still.

[Northern English accent, low and rough, cold] "As for curiosity and friendship," Muken said, "I wasn't going to mistake one for the other. I've sold my sword since I was fourteen. A man who looks at you the way you looked at me just now, all the way down and back up, isn't wondering whether he likes you. He's wondering how you'd go down." He held the prince's eyes. [dry] "I've had that look from sergeants and from paymasters and from men across a ditch. I know what it costs and what it doesn't. You can keep looking. It's free."

[narrator] The wolfhound, standing at his chair with its hackles still raised, [dog growling] made a sound in its chest like a door on a bad hinge. Muken put his left hand down on its great gray head without looking at it, and the sound stopped.

[Northern English accent, low and rough, steady] "And yes," he said. "The Keld."

[narrator] He did not look at Godfrey, and Godfrey Greymane spoke as if he had been waiting for his place in the line, and perhaps he had.

[soft Scottish accent, measured] "The Keld march in two days, sire," Godfrey said. "From their fires at the Grey Teeth. Our outriders have them at something under four thousand. Riders, mostly, on the short northern horses that don't founder in the drift, and every man of them can sleep in the snow in his coat and wake up and fight." His long gray hands lay flat and unmoving on the pine. "Aksel Keld leads them. The Iron Fist. You've heard he's never lost. That's true. He's eighteen, and he's fought eleven times, and he's won eleven times, and three of those were against forces greater than his by twice and more." He paused. [grave] "My father fought beside his grandfather once, on the ice, against a Shiogane raid. He said Old Harald Keld was the only man he'd ever seen who could hear a whiteout coming before the sky changed. He said the grandson would be worse."

[Northern English accent, low and rough, flat] "Worse," Muken said.

[soft Scottish accent, measured, dry] "Better, sire. For the Keld. Worse for whoever's standing in front of him."

[narrator] Lorn Stark spoke from the wall, and his voice was rough and low and did not carry quite as far as he meant it to.

[Yorkshire accent, young, rough, low] "You said entertaining, Prince."

[narrator] He had not moved from where Godfrey had put him. His hands were still at his sides. But his chin had come up, and his eyes, Stark-blue and bloodshot from two days without sleep, had settled on the big man in black and gold and stayed there.

[Yorkshire accent, young, rough, bitter] "There'll be three hundred Stark riders on that ice," said Lorn, "the morning after next. My father's men. Rob and Mark among them. Half the boys who sewed that banner on the gate." He let that sit. [pointed] "I'll grant you it may be worth watching, from where you'll be standing. I'd only ask you to remember it's my people who'll be doing the bleeding while you watch."

[narrator] He said it evenly, with his weight even on both feet, the way his father had taught him. Then he was quiet again, and looked at the floor three feet in front of him, as a Warlord of the north did when he had said what he had to say in front of a king and did not mean to say any more.

Muken let the silence after it run its length. Then he spoke again, to the prince.

[Northern English accent, low and rough, steady] "Your war or mine," he said. "You're right. It's mine. And you can put your mind at rest. I didn't ask you for an army, and I'll not ask. If the Zettari came north under arms there isn't a Moto in the Hall of Verdicts who wouldn't call it an invasion before the first horse cleared the pass, and they'd be right to, and every Stark and Greymane on that ice would be a Zettari dog in their mouths by nightfall." He tilted his head a fraction. [firm] "I'd sooner lose to the Keld on my own than win with your father's spears behind me and lose the north for it. So keep your army. And keep your hands off the Iron Fist. He's spoken for."

[narrator] [grunts] Tetsugen Akagane made a sound in his chest that might, in another man, have been a laugh.

[Northern English accent, low and rough, steady] "As for your witnessing," Muken said. "Good. I hope you do it." He looked down the table at the two men in copper and indigo, standing in the doorway where they had stopped when Kiyome stopped. "You'll want Lord Hayato for the roads. And Lorn will give you a rider who knows the long houses between here and the Kessen ford. The ones the Tenrai left standing. [grim] Go into one of them in the Thin Weeks, Prince. Count the bowls on the shelf and then count the children. That's the only sum anyone up here needs to see done."

[narrator] He sat back, very slightly, in Shigure Tenrai's carved chair.

[Northern English accent, low and rough, thoughtful] "You said you were born a prince," he said. "And that I wasn't raised one. And that this is something I have over you." He considered it. "I don't know what you think I have. [bitter] I was raised three galleries down under Halcrest, cutting salt with my mother, sleeping on a pallet with my sister so neither of us froze. Everything I own I've taken off a dead man or been given by a live one. The coat was a sergeant's who died at the Veyrath ditch. The mail was Ulf Stark's. The sword I took out of a scrap-heap at the Orrel stamping floor when I was eleven, and it's been sharpened so many times there's hardly any blade left on it." He looked at the prince. [dry] "If that's what I have over you, you can have it. I'd have traded it at any age for one winter with enough to eat."

[narrator] Then, very quietly, he added:

[Northern English accent, low and rough, quietly] "But I'll tell you one thing I do have. When those men knelt out there by the woodpile, nobody had told them to. That's the only reason I'm sitting here. If they ever stop, I'll get up." He put both hands flat on the table again. "I don't think anyone ever made you that promise. I don't think you've ever had to make it."

[narrator] The fire cracked. A spark went up.

[Northern English accent, low and rough, cold] "And you said you don't like the Moto," said Muken.

---

### II · No Silver Spoon

[narrator] He said the next part carefully, because it was the part he had been holding behind his teeth since the prince first spoke it.

[Northern English accent, low and rough, cold, slowly] "You are speaking," said Muken, "as if I claim to be a Moto."

[narrator] The room shifted. It was a small thing: a weight changing on a foot, a breath taken and held. Kiyome Byakuya stood exactly as she had stood since the door, and yet the glass beads at her temples, which had hung silent and full of small firelit sparks since she came in, touched each other once with a sound like rain starting on a slate roof.

[narrator] Muken did not notice. He was looking at the prince.

[Northern English accent, low and rough, bitter] "As if I was born with a silver spoon in my mouth," he said. "As if I'm sat here in my grandfather's chair with my grandfather's sword and you've come to look at the last of something." He lifted one hand, palm up, at the room: the split pine, the barrel, the stools, the white banner beyond the window. [cold] "Look at it. Look at me. Do I look like a lord of the seven lines to you?" His voice did not rise. "I've never set foot in the Seat. I've never seen a Wellspring with the Guild's lead on it. I've never held a token that hummed. Every Moto I ever met before this month was a Tenrai factor at the top of a gallery shaft with a ledger, telling my mother she owed more salt this season than last."

[narrator] He turned his head, then, for the first time since they had come in, and looked down the table at the three of them.

[Northern English accent, low and rough, flat] "No offense, Lady Kiyome," he said. "Lord Hayato. Lord Tetsugen."

[narrator] He gave each of them the name, exactly, one after another, as Lorn had at the gate.

[Northern English accent, low and rough, steady] "I'm sure you've come a long way, and I'm sure you've a great deal to say, and I'll hear it. Godfrey's told me I'm to hear all of it, and Godfrey's generally right." He held Kiyome's eyes. [cold] "But I'll tell you now, before you start, so nobody wastes their breath. I don't care what you people think. Of me, or of the north, or of who has a right to what. I only care what the people think. The ones out there." He jerked his chin at the window, at the yard, at the steady three-blows-to-a-split of the child at the woodpile. [firm] "And what they want. What they've wanted for twenty years. Their ambitions, if you like, though they'd never call them that. A roof that doesn't fall in. A pot with something in it. A boy who goes south on a levy and comes back. Those are the only ambitions I've got any time for."

[narrator] Tetsugen Akagane had come a step into the room. He was a heavy man, broad as a door, bald as an egg, with the copper of his house gone dark at the seams of his coat and burning red-bronze everywhere else in the firelight, and the horn handle of a meat knife at his belt that was the only steel he had brought in. He gave Muken a long weighing look from under thick gray brows, like a man looking at a horse he had been told was unbreakable.

[light Japanese accent, flat, iron, deep] "Good," Tetsugen said.

[narrator] Muken's eyes moved to him.

[light Japanese accent, flat, iron, deep, gruff] "Good," said the Akagane lord again, in his flat iron voice. "You should care what they think. None of us did. Not for forty years." He set his scarred hands on his belt. [bitter] "I'd not give you a copper for a king up here who cared what the Hall of Verdicts thought of him. We've had twenty years of one who did."

[narrator] Muken looked at him a breath longer. Then he looked away, to the window, and his face changed.

[Northern English accent, low and rough, quiet] "You came up the Kessen road," he said. "All of you. You'll have passed Harrowmere."

[narrator, pause] Nobody answered.

[Northern English accent, low and rough, cold, slowly] "There were eighty people at Harrowmere when the Tenrai took the levy in the autumn," said Muken. "I know because I took a convoy through it in the summer, and the headman tried to sell me his daughter for a sack of oats, and I gave him the oats and told him to keep her. When we came back through the week before last, there were twenty-six. The rest are in the pit behind the long house, because the ground's too hard to dig separate graves and nobody's got the strength to carry them to a death-house." He looked back down the table at the three Moto, one after another, and held each of them. [grim] "And that's one. There's Tullock. There's Brannick ford. There's Low Esk, where the Tenrai factor fenced the well and sold the water by the cup. There's every long house between here and the Marrow crossing with a woodpile you could carry off under one arm."

[narrator] His voice was very quiet now.

[Northern English accent, low and rough, quietly, bitter] "So you'll forgive me if I don't much care for the Moto. You've never once in my life given me a reason to."

[narrator] Hayato Amagiri was the one who answered him.

He had been standing half a step behind Tetsugen with his narrow face drawn and his restless fingers for once at rest, folded in the wet indigo of his rain-cape, which the fire turned the deep blue of a lake at dusk. A sprig of blue-black juniper had caught in the toggle at his throat somewhere on the northern road and stayed there. When he spoke it was in the plain working cadence of a man discussing distances, and it was all the more terrible for that.

[light Japanese accent, quick, wry, matter-of-fact] "Harrowmere was a hundred and four in my grandfather's survey," he said. "Seventy-nine when the war ended. Twenty-six, you say, and I believe you, because I passed it two days ago and counted eleven chimneys with smoke." He drew a breath through his long nose. "Tullock is nine. Brannick ford is gone; the Tenrai burned it in the summer for harboring a Stark rider. Low Esk I don't know. I'll learn." He looked straight at Muken. [grim] "I keep the roads for my house, sire. I've walked every one of them north of the Seat. And I'll tell you what no one in the Hall of Verdicts would tell you, because it's the only thing I'm good for. Every one of those numbers is in a Tenrai ledger somewhere, in a clerk's good hand, with the date it was taken down. They know. They have always known." His mouth tightened. [bitter, quietly] "They simply never thought it was a sum worth doing."

[narrator] Muken stared at the three Moto leaders.

He stared at them the way he had stared at the Tenrai factor at the top of the shaft when he was nine, with his mother's ledger-marks in the clerk's book and her cough coming up the gallery behind him. There was nothing hot in it. It was the old cold look, the gallery look, the one that came up from three hundred feet under the salt and settled behind his eyes and did not go away. The gold seam in his left iris, the hair-fine crack he had carried out of the gatehouse at the Foot, caught the firelight and held it.

[Northern English accent, low and rough, cold, quiet] "It is fine," said Muken at last.

[narrator] He said it very quietly.

[Northern English accent, low and rough, cold, quiet] "It is fine. I don't need the Tenrai's ledgers. I don't need the Hall's leave, or its chairs, or its tokens." He laid his right hand flat on the black pitted blade of the scrap-iron sword. [slowly, determined] "I'll clear it. All of it. The muck that's spread across the inner world since before I was born, the poison in the springs and in the granaries and in the Seat. I'll dig it out with my hands if I have to, the way we dug salt." His fingers closed on the blade, not hard. [firm] "And then I'll give the people the power they deserve. To keep what they grow. To eat what they keep. To choose who stands at the front of them, and to tell him to sit down when he's done."

[narrator] The fire cracked. The wolfhound, under his hand, had gone quiet, and its great ribs barely moved.

### III · The White and the Red

[narrator] Kiyome Byakuya looked at her nephew.

She had not stopped looking at him since the door. She had looked at him while the prince spoke and while the boy answered, and through Godfrey's report of the Keld and Lorn Stark's quiet hard words from the wall, and through Harrowmere and Tullock and Low Esk. She had looked at the shape of his jaw, which was Kasane's, and the set of his mouth, which was not, and the white hair tied back with a strip of leather the way her sister had tied her own hair back on the training ground at the white house when they were girls. And she had looked at his left eye, at the hair-fine crack of gold across the gray, and her own eyes had stung so hard that the silver rings in them had turned once, very slowly, against her will.

[light Japanese accent, calm, warm, inner voice] *So much hatred,* [narrator] she thought. [light Japanese accent, calm, warm, inner voice, sorrowful] *Seven lines forgive us, child. So much of it. And every word of it earned.*

[narrator] That was the worst of it. He had not said one thing that was not true. He had been raised under the salt by a woman the Moto had driven there, and fed on bark-bread and the Tenrai's arithmetic, and sent to cut salt at nine and to sell his sword at fourteen, and every Moto he had ever laid eyes on had been a man at the top of a shaft with a ledger. He had seen the worst of them. He had seen nothing else. He had never sat in the white house at the hour of the evening bell and heard the Byakuya children say the oath over the lamp. He had never seen an Akagane granary thrown open in a famine year, as Tetsugen's grandfather had thrown his open, or ridden the Amagiri roads with Hayato's people carrying medicine to villages that had no name on any map. He had never known that there were Moto who had wept, privately, for twenty years, in their own houses with the doors shut, for everything their own blood was doing.

He had only seen ten bad things and been told they were all there was.

And he was right that they were all he had been given.

[light Japanese accent, calm, warm, low, level] "You've seen the worst of us," Kiyome said.

[narrator] It was the first thing she had said since the gate. Her voice was low and level, the voice of the white line, that had kept the peace in rooms worse than this one. But Muken heard something under it, and his eyes came to her and stayed.

[light Japanese accent, calm, warm, gently] "I won't tell you there's been better," she said. "Not today. You'd be right not to believe me." She folded her hands in front of her. [hopeful] "But there has been. Once. And there could be again."

[Northern English accent, low and rough, guarded] "Maybe," said Muken.

[narrator] He held her eyes while the pine spat twice in the hearth. Then he looked away, at the white wool of the banner beyond the window, and the next words came out of him slowly, as if they had been lying a long way down.

[Northern English accent, low and rough, slowly, quiet] "I never asked to be a king," he said. "I want you to know that. All of you. I never asked for it, and I never wanted it. A month ago I wanted a full purse and a dry bed and my sister out of the Cuttings, and that was the whole of what I wanted out of the world." He turned his eyes back to the room. "I've got none of those. I've got this instead. And I don't know yet whether I'm any good at it." He lifted his chin very slightly. [steady] "So I'll not make you speeches about what kind of king I'll be. I'll let my actions speak for themselves. You can judge me by them. So can the people. They're the ones who'll have to live with them."

[narrator] Kiyome closed her eyes.

She held them closed for the space of a breath. She was thinking, Hayato knew, because he knew her, of the white knot at her hip, and the oath that went with it, and the boy's mother, and a letter of four lines in a hand she had not seen for sixteen years. She was thinking of Karogane on the tundra, going from fire to fire with Tenrai silver, and of what the Iron Fist's riders would hear shouted between the lines the morning after next if the boy did not hear it first, here, from her.

[light Japanese accent, smooth, cold] *They'll put him on a post for it,* [narrator] that was what Kagenori was counting on. [light Japanese accent, smooth, cold] *His own people will do it for us.*

[light Japanese accent, calm, warm, inner voice, fierce] *No,* [narrator] thought Kiyome Byakuya. [light Japanese accent, calm, warm, inner voice, resolute] *Not from you. From me. In his own hall, with his own men round him, while he can still choose what to do with it.*

[narrator] She opened her eyes.

[light Japanese accent, calm, warm, softly] "Muken," she said.

[narrator] It was the first time she had said his name.

She came forward then, all the way down the long room, past the barrel and the stools and the bench from the stables, with the hearth-light going rose and gold through the white of her robes, until she stood at the foot of his split pine table, close enough to touch the scrap-iron sword. The wolfhound's hackles went up again. Muken did not take his hand off its head. Kiyome did not look at it.

[light Japanese accent, calm, warm] "You said you don't claim to be a Moto," she said. "And you don't. You've never claimed anything in your life, have you? You've only ever taken what you could carry." Her gray eyes held his. [firm] "So I'll claim it for you. Because somebody has to, before somebody else does it worse."

[narrator] She put out one long white hand and laid her forefinger on the rough pine of the table, in the space between them.

[narrator, slowly] And she drew.

[narrator] It was a mark. One mark, drawn slowly, with a single finger, in the fine gray dust of the Tenrai mess where the boys had not swept. It was not a letter. It was not any letter in the Concord hand or the scribe's hand or the trader's ciphers. It had a long down-stroke and a hook, and a crossing, and a small closed loop at the foot of it like a knot in a rope, and when she lifted her finger off the end of it, it lay on the table between them in the dust, plain as a footprint.

[narrator] Muken looked at it.

His face did not change. Nothing on him moved at all. But Godfrey Greymane, sitting at his right hand, saw his knuckles go white on the edge of the table, and the gold seam in his left eye flare, once, like a struck match.

He knew it. He had always known it. His mother had drawn it on the salt of the gallery wall with the burned end of a stick, when Yukazuri was very small, and pointed at it and said [gentle, light Japanese accent, softly] *that's ours. That's our name. Don't ever show it to the clerks.* [narrator] Yukazuri could still draw it. She had drawn it on the frost of the window in the room over the tea-house at Halcrest and on the bottom of her salve tin and once, at the surgeons' line under the Foot, in the blood on her own palm, to steady her hands. Muken had tried a hundred times. His hand had never once been able to make it come out right.

[narrator, slowly] He had never seen anyone else draw it in his life.

[Northern English accent, low and rough, barely audible] "Where," he said. His voice was barely there. "Where did you learn that."

[light Japanese accent, calm, warm, softly] "From my sister," said Kiyome. "The night she was sworn."

[narrator] She did not take her eyes off his.

[light Japanese accent, calm, warm, low, even] "The truth is, Muken, you aren't a tokenless. You never have been." Her voice was very low and very even, and each word was set down exactly where it would bear the weight. "The seals in the south don't sing for you because the registers can't read you. Every token in the inner world wants a house name to sit beneath, and the name of your house was never written in any hand a register knows. It was written in that." She did not look down at the mark. [slowly] "The Kōkan wrote their names in it for a thousand years, so that no clerk and no Guild and no Measurewright could ever count them. That's why the token at your belt is dead in your hand. It's looking straight at you and can't find the column. [softly] You weren't left out of the count, child. You were the one name the whole count was built underneath."

[narrator, long pause] The fire cracked. Nobody moved.

[light Japanese accent, calm, warm, slowly] "Your mother," said Kiyome, "Kasane, is my sister."

[narrator] She stopped. She had not meant to say *is.* She did not take it back.

[light Japanese accent, calm, warm, emotional] "Kasane Byakuya. My elder, by twelve years. She heard the Telling in the Yukari valley when she was fourteen and took their name, and answered to it to the end. And when the time came for a Byakuya to stand at the Kōkan king's shoulder in the old way, the white line before the red, it was Kasane who swore." She drew a breath. [sorrowful] "And she married him. In the white house, before our grandmother's lamp, with no clerk and no witness but me, because he asked her to, and because she loved him, and because she thought she could hold him. [slowly] She married Gimbzo Kōkan Moto."

[narrator] Hayato Amagiri shut his eyes.

[light Japanese accent, calm, warm, steady, solemn] "You have never been tokenless," said Kiyome. "Not for one day of your life. You are Gimbzo's son, and Kasane's, and the last of the red line. You are the true heir to the north." Her voice did not shake. [slowly] "And to the red chair in the Hall of Verdicts, that no Kōkan has sat in for twenty years. And to everything my house was sworn, a thousand years ago, to keep for you."

---

### IV · Three Minutes

[narrator, long pause] For a while there was only the fire.

The room went quiet in the way a room only goes quiet when something has been broken in it and everyone is waiting to see how badly. The fire went on burning. The pine spat. Outside, in the yard, the child at the woodpile had stopped splitting wood, and nobody inside could have said when. The silence came in through the narrow windows instead, enormous and white, the silence of the whole snow-bound north lying glittering to the edge of the sky.

Tetsugen Akagane stood rooted in the middle of the room, with his scarred hands on his belt and the firelight red on his copper.

Hayato had opened his eyes again. He was looking at the floor.

At the wall, Lorn Stark had not moved. He stood with his weight even on both feet and his hands still at his sides, exactly as his father had taught him, and looked at the floor three feet in front of him, and his face was the color of the wall.

Godfrey Greymane did not look at the floor. He looked at Muken.

They had known. Both of them. They had known since the gatehouse, since the night before their fathers rode south down the Kessen road to the Seat and the Hall of Verdicts and the Kurenai blade. Ulf Stark had taken Lorn up onto the wall-walk in the dark and told him all of it, plainly, in twelve sentences, and then made him swear by the sky that covers us that he would carry it until it could be carried by someone with a better right. And Wystan Greymane had sat with his son by the woodpile in the cold with a cup of the skin going sour in his hand and told him the same, in rather more words, and at the end of it had said: [soft Scottish accent, dry, gently] *He'll learn it one day, Godfrey. Better from a friend's mouth than an enemy's. But not from yours. You swear it. You hold the ridge.*

[narrator] And Godfrey had held it. Every day since, at the boy's right hand, through the Foot's mending and the white banner and the kneeling by the woodpile, through *sire* and *yes, sire,* he had held the ridge. He had looked into that gray eye with its gold crack every morning and said nothing. He had found it the hardest thing he had ever done in his life.

He was not sure, now, sitting very straight on the bench from the stables, whether he was glad it was over.

[narrator, long pause] Muken was silent.

He sat in Shigure Tenrai's carved chair with his hand on the wolfhound's head and his eyes on the mark in the dust, and kept his silence so long that the shadows of the people round the table, thrown long by the fire, crept a finger's width across the floor while they watched. A minute. Then two. The seal-fat lamp on the far sill guttered and steadied. A log fell in the hearth, and nobody looked at it. Somewhere outside a horse blew on the lines. Three minutes, by a fire's measure, and perhaps more. The longest anyone in that room had ever heard a man stay silent who had something to say.

What went through him in those minutes, nobody in the room could see. He did not let them.

It went through him like water under ice: fast, and black, and a long way down.

[Northern English accent, low and rough, inner voice] *Shigure,* [narrator] he thought. [Northern English accent, low and rough, inner voice, rushed] *On the stair at the Foot, with her sword at my throat. I knew that face. I watched it on the night of the Kōkan cellars.* *Every tavern in Halcrest knew that story. The king who went down into the cellars with a lamp after his own line. Every Kōkan he'd left breathing. His own blood.*

[Northern English accent, low and rough, inner voice, slowly] *Sixteen years ago. The same year she came down into the Cuttings with me in her arms and no name the clerks would take.*

[quietly] *And Yukazuri, three years after. His too.*

[bitter] *And the factor at the top of the shaft. And her lungs. [Northern English accent, low and rough, inner voice] *Yusif,* [narrator] he thought. [narrator] He was quiet for a breath.

[Northern English accent, low and rough, quiet, sorrowful] "I keep my mother's," said Muken. "And I'll tell you what I remember, Lady Kiyome, since you've come all this way to give me the rest." [narrator] The pine spat in the hearth. Nobody so much as shifted a foot.
