# Chapter Eleven: By My Honor

[narrator] *Book One of the Kōkan · Chapter Eleven: By My Honor*

### I · The First Bowl

Lorn Stark had not expected to be spoken to.

He had said his piece from the wall and gone back to the floor three feet in front of him, as a Warlord did when he had spoken in front of a king, and he had stayed there through all of it. Through the mark in the dust and Kasane's name, and the three minutes, and Muken's mother in the last gallery with the lamp. He had stood with his weight even on both feet and his hands still at his sides and he had not let his face move, because his father had told him on the wall-walk, in the dark, that the day it came out he was to stand where the boy could see him and not look away. *[Yorkshire accent, gruff, quietly]He'll look for us, Lorn. When it lands. He'll look to see who's still standing where they were.*

[narrator] So he had stood where he was. And now the Zettari had turned to him across the long room, and said his name, and said *you were right.*

Lorn listened to all of it. The battle and the riders. *[deep West African accent, quietly]I spoke carelessly about your people.* *I hope your riders return. All three hundred of them.* [narrator] He listened with his chin up and his eyes, Stark-blue and raw at the rims, fixed on the big man's face, and when it was finished he did not answer at once. He was not a man who answered at once. His father had used to say that a quick answer was wet wood: it smoked, and it filled the room, and it gave no heat.

"[Yorkshire accent, young, rough, slowly]Thank you, Prince," he said at last.

[narrator] His voice was rough, and lower than he meant it to be.

"[Yorkshire accent, young, rough, quiet]My father had a saying. Every Kharven father has it." He did not look away. "The first bowl goes to the one who cannot fill it himself. It's about the pot, mostly. Who eats first in a lean year. But he used to say it meant the dead as well. That the first of everything you have, your grief and your anger and your good words, goes to the ones who can't ask for it anymore." He paused. "[pause]You said you've carried your own dead home. Then you know where the first bowl goes. I'll not ask more of you than that."

[narrator] He put his right hand flat on his chest, over his heart, and closed it into a fist against his breastbone, and pressed it there once. Then he let it fall to his side, and looked at the floor three feet in front of him again, and was quiet.

[softly] At the head of the table, Muken's mouth moved very slightly at the corner. He did not look at Lorn, and Lorn, who had learned that corner of a mouth on four weeks of hard road, read it well enough from the wall.

---

### II · I Killed Him

He had been listening to the prince the way he listened to a man across a ditch.

The confession about the measuring, and that he had not stopped. *Whether Gimbzo's son can become a better king than his father ever was.* *I don't know if I would either.* All of it had gone into him and down, and settled, and he had kept his hands flat on the rough pine on either side of the mark in the dust, and his face had not changed.

Then the prince said: *[deep West African accent]Something that concerns your father.*

[narrator] And then he said it.

*[long pause]I killed him.*

[narrator, tense] The wolfhound was on its feet before the words were finished. It had been lying across Muken's boots again, with its chin on his instep, and now it was up and between his chair and the room, enormous and gray and shaking, with every hair along its spine standing like a brush and its lips drawn back off teeth as long as a man's thumb, and the sound that came out of it was nothing like the door-on-a-bad-hinge sound it had made before. [dog growling] It was low and continuous and it came up through the stone floor into the soles of everyone's feet.

Muken did not put his hand on its head.

He did not move at all.

Godfrey Greymane, at his right hand, saw the gold seam in his left eye go from a hair's breadth to the width of a thread, all at once, like a crack running across ice when a man's weight comes down on it. He saw the edges of Muken's knuckles go white on the pine. He saw nothing else, because there was nothing else to see. Muken sat in Shigure Tenrai's carved chair with his face set like frozen ground and his gray eyes on the Crown Prince of Kushara, and let him say the rest of it.

*[deep West African accent, calm]I won't apologize for it. He needed to be stopped.*

*I wanted to fight him.*

*When we fought, I was happy.*

[narrator] Down the long room, Kiyome Byakuya had closed her eyes.

She had known. She had sat across from the prince in the cedar hall at the enclave when he first said it, *[deep West African accent, careless]I killed his father,*[narrator] in that deep careless voice, and the silver rings in her eyes had moved once and then held. She had carried it up four hundred miles of road beside him and not said it, because it was not hers to say. Now it was said, and she stood at the foot of her nephew's table with her hands folded in front of her and the white knot at her hip, and did not open her eyes, and her lips moved very slightly over something that might have been the oath of her house.

Tetsugen Akagane had gone still in the middle of the room with his scarred hands on his belt. His heavy face showed nothing, the way a stone shows nothing. But he had shifted his weight, very slightly, onto the balls of his feet, and his right hand had come to rest on the horn handle of his meat knife. An old soldier's hand did that on its own when a room went wrong.

Hayato had stopped turning the corner of his cape.

At the wall, Lorn Stark had lifted his eyes off the floor.

*[deep West African accent]I'm not a particularly good person, King Muken.*

*I'll fight you because I want to know how strong you are.*

*Become whatever kind of king you want to be.*

*And when the snow goes, we'll see what kind of warrior you've become as well.*

[narrator] The prince stepped back to where he had stood before.

And still Muken did not move.

It went through him, again, the way the other thing had: water under ice, black and fast and a long way down. But it was a different water this time, and it went somewhere different.

*[Northern English accent, low and rough, inner voice]Shimane,* he thought. *The crier in the galleries. The king is fallen at Shimane.*

*[inner voice, bitter]This is the man. This one. He stood in the mud at Shimane and put my father into it, and that morning, three hundred feet under Halcrest, my mother turned her face to the wall.*

*[slowly]And he was happy.*

[narrator] The black hot thing turned over under his breastbone. He felt it come up. He felt it come all the way up behind his left eye and press there, like a thumb against the back of the socket, and the edges of the room went gold, and in the gold he could see the prince's throat, and the line of his jaw, and the long open drop of the black-and-gold robe off his shoulders, and the place under the arm where the robe hung loose, and the three steps of floor between them.

He looked at all of it.

Then he made himself look at the other thing.

*[Northern English accent, low and rough, inner voice]Hiroe. The granaries, with the children still inside. Shigure on the stair. I watched it burn them.*

*The cellars. With a lamp. Every Kōkan he'd left breathing. His own blood.*

*Harrowmere. Tullock. Brannick ford. The bark in the long houses. The levies going south and the carts coming back.*

*Her lungs. Her eyes. The last year on the pallet. Kiyome said it: she'd been dying of him for years before anyone ever killed him.*

[narrator] He sat with all of that for a long breath. And then a longer one, in through the nose until the ribs ached and the cut in his side pulled under Ulf Stark's mail, out through the mouth until there was nothing left, the way Yusif had taught him in the back of a worked-out gallery with a stick of ash in his hands. *[Yorkshire accent, gruff, quietly]You're thinking. Stop thinking. Let your feet do it.*

[narrator, slowly] The thumb behind his eye eased. The gold drew back from the edges of the room, slowly, like a tide.

He lifted his left hand off the table and put it on the wolfhound's back.

It did not stop growling. But it stopped shaking.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, quiet, firm]Down," said Muken.

[narrator] The dog looked back at him over its great gray shoulder. Then, slowly, with the hackles still up along its spine, it lay down across his boots again, and put its chin on his instep, and watched the prince with its yellow eyes and did not close them.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, quiet, cold]You killed him," said Muken.

[narrator] His voice was very quiet. It was the gallery voice, flat and even. But under it, for anyone with ears, there was something that had not been there before, a kind of grain in it, like wood that had been split along a line it did not want to go.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, quiet]At Shimane," he said. "In the mud. And that morning, under Halcrest, when the crier came down the shafts, my mother turned her face to the wall and didn't turn it back." He held the prince's eyes. "I don't know if those two things were the same hour. I don't suppose you'd know either."

[narrator, pause] He let it sit.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, steady]I want you to know I thought about it," said Muken. "Just now. Sitting here. I thought about three steps of floor and the place under your arm where the robe hangs loose." He said it without any heat at all. "I've sold my sword since I was fourteen. [narrator] Godfrey's long gray hand had come to rest on the table again beside his, flat, not touching him.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, quiet]And then I thought about Hiroe," Muken said. "And the cellars. He was quiet a moment. "[pause]So no. I don't hate you for it. I'd like to. [bitter] It'd be easier. I'd have somewhere to put all this." "[steady]But you did what somebody had to do, and nobody else did it: the seven lines, the Hall, all the men who stood behind him for twenty years and watched." [narrator, fire crackling] The fire cracked. A spark went up the flue, bright as a struck coin, and was gone.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, thoughtful]As for being happy," said Muken.

[narrator] He looked down at the scrap-iron sword on the table for a moment.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, quiet]I don't know that feeling," he said. "I've been in a lot of fights. The Veyrath ditch. The Vessant road. The stair at the Foot with Shigure Tenrai's sword at my throat. I've never once been happy in any of them. I was too busy trying not to die." He lifted his eyes again. "Maybe that's the difference between us. You fight to find out how strong you are. I fight because the other man's between me and something I need." His mouth moved, very slightly. "I can't say which of us is the worse for it. But I'd not call you a bad man for being honest about yours. Every one of us in this room has done worse than enjoy a fight. Most of us did it for twenty years and called it duty."

[narrator, sighs] Tetsugen Akagane let out a breath through his nose, very slowly, and took his hand off the horn handle of his knife.

### III · Warn Him Properly

[narrator] It was Hayato who spoke into the silence after, because the prince had told him to.

He came forward a pace, into the firelight, with the blue-black juniper still caught in the toggle at his throat and his restless fingers, for once, folded quiet in the indigo of his cape.

"[light Japanese accent, quick, wry, serious]He's right," said Hayato. "The prince. We came to warn you, sire, and I've only told you half of it." He glanced, once, at Kiyome, and back. "Karogane's only the knife on that ice. The hand on it is Kagenori's, and the arm behind the hand belongs to someone else entirely."

[narrator] Muken looked at him.

"[light Japanese accent, quick, wry, low]Every letter Karogane's carried onto the tundra these six weeks," said Hayato, "has two seals on it. Tenrai gold. And Shirogane silver." He drew a line on the air with one finger, then a second beside it. "And the silver goes on the left. Always. The place of precedence. I've had three of those letters through my hands, sire, in the last month, on my own roads, opened and closed again by a man of mine who's very good at it. Kagenori doesn't sign as a lord writing for himself. He signs as a lord writing for his queen."

"[light Japanese accent, calm, warm, softly]Shizuha," said Kiyome softly.

"[light Japanese accent, quick, wry]Shizuha Shirogane." Hayato nodded. "The silver chair. She's sat one step from the red one her whole life, and her mother before her, and hers. The Shirogane have been the Kōkan's rivals for a thousand years. While there was a red line, they could never sit." He looked at Muken steadily. "Now there's one of you left. If Karogane can break you on the ice with your father's name, if your own men put you on a post for it, then there is no red line. And the next blood in the Hall with a claim to the north is silver." His fingers had started moving again, folding and unfolding the corner of his cape. "Everybody's thought for twenty years that Kagenori wants the red chair, sire. He wants to stand behind the silver one."

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, quiet]And the Keld?" said Muken.

"[light Japanese accent, flat, iron, deep]Bought." It was Tetsugen who answered, in his flat iron voice. "I'd lay my valley on it. The Keld have never in their lives fought a war for the Moto that they could get out of. They fight for the ice. Karogane will have given them the ice." He grunted. "[grunts]No tithe. Seed grain. No Moto boot on their snow. That's what I'd have offered, in his place, and the boy would have taken it, because his people are starving like everyone else's."

"[Northern English accent, low and rough]The boy," said Muken.

"[light Japanese accent, flat, iron, deep]Aksel Keld." Tetsugen's heavy face did not change. "Eighteen. I've never seen him. I fought his grandfather, once, on the ice under the Grey Teeth, in the second year of the war, when I was young enough to think I could." He was quiet a moment. "[thoughtful]Old Harald Keld moved four hundred riders through a whiteout so close to my column that I heard their horses blowing, and I never saw one of them until they were in my baggage train. I lost every cart I had and a third of my men, and I never found out how he did it." His scarred hands lay flat on his belt. "They say the grandson is better. They say he lost his right hand to a Tenrai sword at thirteen and the Keld smiths made him one of iron, and that he kills with it. I believe that too."

"[soft Scottish accent, measured, quietly]He'll come in the snow," said Godfrey quietly. "If he's anything like Old Harald. He'll wait for weather. And when it comes he'll be in among us before anyone on our side can see his banners."

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, cold]Then we'll not wait for weather," said Muken.

[narrator] Kiyome Byakuya had been listening with her eyes on the mark in the dust. She lifted them now.

"[light Japanese accent, calm, warm, low]There's one more thing," she said. "Since the prince has said we should warn you properly." Her voice was low and even. "You said tonight you'll tell the Foot. Every Stark and every Greymane, by the woodpile, before Karogane can shout it on the ice." She looked at him. "Do it. It's the right thing, and it's the clever thing, which doesn't happen often. But know what comes after it." She did not look away. "The moment the north knows what you are, so does the Hall. And on the day the Hall knows that the red line lives, and that it sits at Kharven's Foot under a white banner and calls itself king without leave of the seven, Kagenori will not need the Keld any more. "[grave]And then every line that wants to keep its chair will march north to prove it."

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, quiet]Every line," said Muken.

"[light Japanese accent, calm, warm, softly]Not every line," said Kiyome Byakuya.

[narrator, long pause] It was very quiet in the long room.

### IV · Under My Rule

[narrator] Muken looked at her, and let what she had said settle in him. Then he looked down the table, past the barrel and the stools and the bench from the stables, to where the Crown Prince of Kushara had stepped back to stand by the stone wall in the shadow beyond the firelight, with his great arms in his sleeves.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, steady]You said become whatever kind of king I want to be," said Muken. "And you said you don't know if you would have told your people the truth. And that mine chose me after seeing what kind of man I was." He paused. "I'll answer you, Prince. All of it. And then I'll answer my aunt, since she's waiting."

[narrator] He set both hands flat on the pine.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, slowly]Under my rule," said Muken.

[narrator] He stopped, as though the words were heavier than he had expected, and weighed them, and found that he could carry them.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, slowly, resolute]Under my rule," he said again, "no Moto will ever march on Zettari lands again."

[narrator] Tetsugen's head came up. Hayato's fingers stopped on the corner of his cape. Kiyome did not move at all.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, steady]No levy," said Muken. "No column, and not a single tax-cart, while I'm breathing." His voice did not rise. "Under my rule, we will control the north, the way we did for hundreds of years before the war. Our own snow. Our own salt. Our own ice. And we'll keep it well, which is more than we've done in my lifetime." His gray eyes moved, slowly, from one Moto face to the next. "And we'll reach for nothing else. We let ourselves believe we could control the whole of the inner world." He was quiet a moment. "[bitter]We couldn't even control ourselves. My father went down into the cellars with a lamp after his own blood, and the seven lines watched him do it and wrote it down. That's what we were. That's what we made of the world when we had it."

[narrator] He paused.

He found, to his own surprise, that his hands had begun, very slightly, to shake. He pressed them flatter on the wood until they stopped.

[narrator] Then he looked back at the prince.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, plainly]And I'm sure you'll make a great king," said Muken.

[narrator] He said it plainly, the way he said everything, and it was so plainly said that for a moment nobody in the room was sure they had heard it.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, steady]You said you don't know if you'd have the courage to stand in front of your people and tell them the truth." Muken held his eyes. "You just stood in front of me and told me you killed my father, and that you were happy doing it, and that you're not a good man. In my own hall. With my own dog showing you its teeth. Nobody made you. You could have let me find out from some soldier's story six months from now and never said a word." He let that sit. "I've known a great many men who'd sooner die than say a true thing that made them look worse. You're not one of them. I think that's most of what a king is."

[narrator] He was quiet a moment.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, quiet]It isn't right," he said, more quietly, "for them to force the whole of your country onto you because of whose son you are. I know a little about that, now. More than I did this morning." His mouth moved very slightly at the corner. "But I hope for the prosperity of the Zettari, Prince. I mean that. The same as I hope for the Yukari's. And not for the sake of the families. Not your house or theirs or mine. For the people." He touched the edge of the table. "The ones who fill the bowls. The ones who carry the dead home."

[narrator] Muken knew how it sounded.

He could hear it himself, in the long low stone room, with the Tenrai pine spitting in the hearth and the three Moto lords standing at the foot of his table in their copper and indigo and white: a Kōkan, Gimbzo's son, telling a Zettari prince that he wished his country well. He could hear how it sounded to them, and how it must sound to the prince, and to the old woman with no teeth by the barracks door if she had been in the room, and to every Zettari mother who had ever buried a son on a Moto field.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, steady]It may sound ironic," said Muken. "I know it does. I'm a Moto. Mine spent forty years killing yours, and twenty of them were my father's." He held the prince's eyes. "But I will not continue it. That idea. That the Moto are owed the world, and anyone who won't give it to us is owed a burning. It ends here. It ends at this table."

[narrator] He looked down, then, at the mark in the dust: his mother's mark, his name, lying where Kiyome had drawn it, with the firelight running gold along its strokes.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, slowly]I was given this life for a reason," he said slowly. "I didn't think so, this morning. This morning I thought it was just the life I had, the way a man has the weather. But a woman cut salt with her lungs going for sixteen years so I'd live to sit in this chair. A Stark gave up his name and his place among the Seven to come down four galleries every season and teach me to hold a stick." He breathed. "Two Warlords of the north rode south to the Seat knowing what was waiting for them, so that their sons could stand in this room today and not look away." He did not look at Lorn or Godfrey. "Weather doesn't do that. Somebody meant it."

[narrator] He lifted his eyes.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, resolute]So this is my fate," said Muken. "And I'll claim it. I'll claim my destiny, since everyone keeps telling me I have one." The corner of his mouth moved again. "But I'll claim it my own way. I will not force my powers onto the weak, or the innocent, the way he did. Not with a sword, and not with whatever this is." He touched the corner of his left eye, once, with two fingers, where the gold seam lay. "I'll stand in front of them. And I'll defend them with honor, or I'll not stand there at all."

[narrator, softly] At the foot of the table, Kiyome Byakuya's eyes had filled again.

She did not wipe them. She unfolded her hands. And then, slowly, as though the movement had been waiting in her body for sixteen years, she untied the white knot from the guard of her sword, one turn and then another, and drew the cord out from the hilt, and let it hang from her fingers.

"[light Japanese accent, calm, warm, barely audible, slowly]Purity before power," said Kiyome Byakuya.

[narrator] Her voice was very low. It was not meant for the room. It was meant for one person in it.

"[light Japanese accent, calm, warm, slowly, emotional]Peace before the blade." She went down on one knee on the stone floor of the Tenrai mess, in her near-white robes gray with the road, and laid the katana flat across her knee with its hilt toward him. "And the red line before the white. Until the end of the red line." She bowed her head over the sword. "[softly]That's the oath of my house, nephew. My grandmother taught it to me over the lamp, and to Kasane before me. It binds me to you. It doesn't bind you to anything at all."

[narrator, long pause] Nobody moved.

"[light Japanese accent, calm, warm, softly]I told the Sovereign of Kushara," said Kiyome, without lifting her head, "that if you said no, the Byakuya would serve you anyway, in a mercenary's tent. You haven't said no." She breathed. "So the white house will serve you in a king's hall. However mean the hall. However few the chairs."

[narrator] Muken looked at her bowed white head, and at the cord hanging from her fingers, and could not speak at once.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, very quietly, emotional]Get up, Aunt," he said at last, very quietly. "Please. You've ridden four hundred miles. The floor's cold."

[narrator] She did not get up at once. When she did, she rose the way she had risen at the enclave, all at once, with the glass beads at her temples chiming faintly, and the white cord still hanging from her fingers, and she did not tie it back onto her sword.

[narrator] Tetsugen Akagane had been watching from under his heavy gray brows. Now he grunted, low in his chest.

"[light Japanese accent, flat, iron, deep, gruff]I've no oath to give you," he said. "The Akagane never swore to the red. We only ever obeyed it." He looked at Muken with his flat stone face. "But I'll tell you this, boy. Sire. If you meant that, about the Zettari lands, then you'll want my books. The Sennen muster rolls. Every commander's name who drew grain from my stores to march south." He hooked his thumbs in his belt. "A king who means to stop his own people marching on their neighbors had better know which of his own people did the marching."

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, steady]I'll take them," said Muken.

[narrator] Hayato Amagiri had stood through all of it without fidgeting once, which for him was a feat of arms. [chuckles] Now he laughed, once, under his breath, and it had no humor in it at all.

"[light Japanese accent, quick, wry]My uncle," he said, "is going to have an apoplexy." He shook his head slowly. "[dry]*No Moto will march on Zettari lands again.* He'll call that treason in open council before you've finished saying it." He looked at Muken. "[eager]I'd say it louder, sire. Say it tonight, by the woodpile, after the other thing. Say it where every Stark rider can hear it, and every one of them will carry it home to a long house that buried a son on a southern field." He drew a line in the air with one finger. "And I'll carry it down my roads. Every hut with a woodpile in it between here and the border. Before the Hall can say you didn't mean it."

---

### V · The Only Thing That Can Be Held

[narrator] Muken looked down at his own right hand on the table.

There was a callus across the base of the fingers that had been there since he was eight years old. A thick ridge of hard skin where a length of ash had rubbed it raw in the back of a worked-out gallery, season after season, while a big quiet gray-bearded man stooped under the low roof with his own hands closed over the boy's on the end of the stick.

*[Yorkshire accent, gruff, warm, quietly]Again. Again. You're thinking. Stop thinking.*

[narrator, softly] And once, at the very end of a lesson, with the lamp going down and the stick put away against the gallery wall, the big man had crouched in front of him so that their faces were level, and put one great rough hand on the side of his head, and said the other thing. He had only ever said it once. He had said it as if it were the most important thing he had ever taught him, more than the stick, more than the feet, and Muken at eight years old had not understood a word of it and remembered every one.

[slowly] He said it now, aloud, to the room, in Yusif Stark's own words.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, slowly, solemn]*Always swear by your honor,*" said Muken. "*Because it's the only thing you have that can be held to account.*"

[narrator] At the wall, Lorn Stark made a sound in his throat, very small, and looked at the floor.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, quiet]Yusif said that to me," said Muken. "When I was eight. In the dark, under the salt, with nobody but me to hear it. He said a man's gold can be taken, and his land, and his name. They took his. He gave up his place among the Seven and his house's name to come down those galleries, and nobody ever let him have them back." He breathed. "But nobody can take a man's honor off him. He can only put it down himself. So it's the one thing anybody can ever hold him to." He looked at Lorn. "I never knew what he meant. I think I do now."

[narrator] Lorn did not lift his eyes. But his right hand came up off his side, slowly, and lay flat on his chest, and closed into a fist against his breastbone, and pressed there once, hard. And stayed.

[narrator] Muken pushed back Shigure Tenrai's carved chair.

The legs scraped on the stone. It was the loudest sound in the room. The wolfhound lifted its head off his boot and got up with him, unhurried, and stood at his left knee. Godfrey Greymane did not try to stop him. Godfrey had told him an hour ago to sit, and stay sat, and let them come to him, and he had, through all of it. He had let them come all the way. And now there was no one left to come.

[slowly] So the king of the north stood up.

He was not tall. He had never been tall. He stood at the head of his split pine table in a dead sergeant's coat gone the color of tea, with Ulf Stark's mail under it and his left arm still held a little close to his side, and his white hair tied back with a strip of leather, and the dead bronze token on his belt, and the gold seam lying quiet now in his left eye like a vein of ore in gray stone. He came round the end of the table, past the barrel, past the stools, past his aunt with the white cord in her fingers and the two lords of the seven lines, and stopped three steps from the Crown Prince of Kushara.

[narrator] The same three steps of floor he had looked at, ten minutes ago, through the gold.

[narrator] He held out his right hand. The callused one. Open, palm sideways, the way the Starks offered a hand, not the way the Moto did.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, slowly, resolute]By my honor," said Muken, "we shall fight."

[narrator] He did not lower his hand. He did not look away.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, steady]When the snow goes. No armies. No politics. No ancient quarrel. You and me, as you said." He held the prince's pale eyes. "And I hope our fight can be something all of our people remember. Yours and mine. Something the long houses can tell their children about, and the Zettari can tell theirs, and nobody has to bury anybody's son over it." The corner of his mouth moved. "[wry]I'll try to make you happy, Prince. I'll not promise. I've never done it on purpose."

[narrator, softly] He stood with his hand out, in the firelight, in the long low hall at Kharven's Foot, and waited.

---

# Chapter Twelve: The Woodpile

[narrator] *Book One of the Kōkan · Chapter Twelve: The Woodpile*

### I · The Yard After Dark

[narrator] They lit the yard with everything the Foot had left to burn.

Torches of split pine soaked in seal fat, jammed into the cracks of the curtain wall every ten paces. Two iron braziers dragged out of the Tenrai guardroom and filled with the last of the stable timber, so that the charred ends of the old stalls stood up out of the flames like the ribs of something that had died there. The blubber lamps from the barracks, every one, set in a long row along the top of the woodpile, spitting and stinking of fish-oil and hot tallow, their light coming up yellow off the pale ends of the split wood. And over all of it, very high, very faint, the green of the northern sky moving behind the cloud like something turning over in its sleep, with a hem of violet showing where the cloud thinned.

It was cold enough that the snow squeaked underfoot. The breath of six hundred people stood over the yard in a single low white cloud, and the torchlight went into it and stayed there, so that the whole of Kharven's Foot seemed to be lit from inside a lamp of frost.

They had all come. Nobody had been ordered.

Lorn had sent the word round the barracks at dusk, plainly, in four words, *[Yorkshire accent, young, rough]the king will speak,*[narrator] and then he had stood at the door of the keep and watched them come out of every door in the fort. The Stark riders first, in their hide-coats and mail, with their axes across their backs out of habit, because a Stark did not go to hear a thing said without something to hold. The Greymane foot behind them, gray-cloaked and quiet, in their long patient file. The vanguard boys, who had sewn the white banner, in a knot at the front with their elbows in each other's ribs. Then the others. The farriers and the cooks and the carters, the two Greymane masons with stone-dust still in their beards, the wounded from the surgeons' line on their crutches and their stretchers, carried out and set down along the barracks wall in a row. The women from the long houses down the Kessen road who had walked up after the Foot fell to cook and carry and nurse. Their children, wrapped to the eyes. And the old woman with no teeth, on her stump by the barracks door, in her three shawls, who had not moved from that stump in four days and did not move from it now, because the yard had come to her.

[quietly] They stood in the snow in the torchlight and the breath-cloud, six hundred of them, and waited, and the only sound was the braziers and the spitting of the lamps and, somewhere at the back, a child asking a question and being hushed.

Muken stood at the foot of the woodpile.

He had chosen it. Godfrey had wanted the gatehouse steps, where he could be seen from the whole yard, and Lorn had wanted the wall-walk over the gate, under the banner. Muken had said no to both, quietly, and walked out of the keep in the dusk and stood where he stood now, with his back to the long pale wall of split wood that ran down the barracks side as high as a man's shoulder. The same place they had knelt, the morning after the crow. *[Northern English accent, low and rough, dry]If they're going to put me on a post,*[narrator] he had said, *[Northern English accent, low and rough, dry]I'd sooner they didn't have to walk far to find the wood.*

[narrator, pause] Nobody had laughed.

He stood there now in the dead sergeant's coat with Ulf Stark's mail under it and the scrap-iron sword at his hip and the dead bronze token on his belt, bareheaded, with his white hair tied back and the torchlight on it. Ulf Stark's wolfhound sat at his left knee. Lorn Stark stood at his right hand, a pace back, in his father's mail with his father's axe; Godfrey Greymane at his left, in gray, with his long hands folded in front of him. Neither of them moved.

Kiyome Byakuya, Hayato Amagiri and Tetsugen Akagane stood in the crowd.

Muken had asked that of them too. *[Northern English accent, low and rough, quiet]Not with me,*[narrator] he had said, in the long room, as the light went. *[Northern English accent, low and rough, quiet]Down there. Among them. Where they can see you.*[narrator] Kiyome had looked at him a long moment and then nodded, once, and gone out into the yard ahead of everyone and stood in the snow at the front, a little to one side, in her near-white robes with the white cord of her house's knot still hanging loose from her sword-hilt, untied. Tetsugen had grunted and gone and stood beside a Stark farrier who was nearly as broad as he was. Hayato had found a place by the braziers with his wet indigo cape steaming in the heat and the juniper still caught in its toggle. Everyone in the yard knew who they were. Everyone in the yard had seen them ride in that morning. The crowd had left a little space round each of them, the way water leaves a space round a stone, and in each of those spaces the snow was trampled flat and nobody stood.

Down at the front, between the vanguard boys and the row of stretchers, stood Yukazuri.

She had come out of the surgeons' line with her sleeves still rolled to the elbow and the salve tin still in her apron pocket. Muken had found her there an hour ago, after the long room, bent over a Greymane boy with a split scalp, and had stood in the doorway until she finished the stitch and looked up. And then he had told her. All of it. In the dark at the back of the surgeons' tent, in the smell of boiled gut and spirit and blood, with the lamp turned low and her hands still red to the wrist, kneeling in front of her so their faces were level, the way Yusif used to kneel.

[softly] She had listened to every word without moving.

When he finished she had been quiet while the lamp burned a little lower. Then she had taken her red hand out of her lap and drawn the mark on the canvas of the tent wall beside her, in the blood on her fingers, bright on the pale cloth. Perfectly, the way she always drew it. And she had looked at it, and then at him.

*[young girl, Northern English accent, sharp, quietly]So that's whose it was,* she had said.

[narrator] That was all. She had not cried. She had wiped her hand on her apron and gone back to the Greymane boy and finished closing his scalp, and Muken had stood in the doorway and watched her hands, which did not shake at all, and thought that of the two of them it had never once been him who was the strong one.

She stood at the front of the yard now with her sleeves rolled up in the cold and her breath going up white, and looked at her brother, and waited.

---

### II · The Name

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, quiet, steady]I have to tell you something," said Muken.

[narrator] He did not raise his voice. The yard was so quiet that the words went out over it like a stone over still water, and the breath-cloud moved when he spoke, a little, and settled again.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, steady]Something I learned this morning," he said. "From these three." He did not point, and six hundred heads turned anyway, very slightly, toward the three trampled places in the snow, and turned back. "I'll tell you all of it. And when I've told you, I want you to know that if it changes who I am to you, you don't owe me anything. Not one step. You knelt here because you chose to. If you want to choose different, you can do it here, tonight, by the same wood, and no man of mine will lift a hand to stop you."

[narrator, fire crackling] He paused. The braziers cracked.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, slowly]I am Muken," he said. "That's the name my mother gave me, and the only one I've answered to." He drew a breath, and the breath went up white. "[resolute]And I am Muken Moto. First of my name."

[narrator, crowd gasps] The sound that went through the yard was very small: six hundred people taking a breath at the same time and not letting it go.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, steady]Rightful King of the Clans," said Muken. He did not stop now. He said it the way he had walked the last ledge in the galleries as a boy, every foot set down and no looking back. "And of the Banner Houses. True Heir of the North." His jaw worked once. "[emotional]Son of Kasane Moto, who cut salt under Halcrest for sixteen years to keep me fed. And son of Gimbzo."

[narrator, long pause] The yard did not breathe out.

At the front, one of the vanguard boys, the youngest, the one with the red ears who had held the needle while the others argued over what to put on the banner, said "[young boy, Yorkshire accent, barely audible]*No,*"[narrator] very quietly, the way a child says it when a thing has been dropped and broken. Nobody hushed him.

[narrator] Further back, a Stark rider with a gray braided beard turned his head aside and spat into the snow. It hissed.

By the barracks door, on her stump, the old woman with no teeth had not moved. But her hands, folded in her lap in her three shawls, had closed on each other.

[narrator] Muken let it all happen. He did not look away from any of it. He stood at the foot of the woodpile with his hands at his sides and let six hundred people look at him, at his white hair and his gray eyes and the thin gold seam in the left one that the torchlight caught, and he let them see, at last, what they were looking at. The red line. The Kōkan. The last of the blood that had burned Hiroe and emptied the cellars at Halcrest and taken their fathers south on the levies and sent them home in carts.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, quiet]Yes," he said quietly. "That one."

[narrator] Then he lifted his voice, for the first time.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, loud, fierce]But I don't want you to follow me because of my name," said Muken. "Hear me. Not because I'm a Moto, not because I'm his, not because three lords of the seven lines rode four hundred miles to tell me what blood I've got in me." His eyes went over the yard, slowly, from the stretchers to the vanguard boys to the gray-bearded Stark who had spat. "That blood never did one thing for any of you except take. If that's all I am, then put me on a post, and burn me with this wood, and I'll not blame you."

[narrator] He took one step forward, off the foot of the woodpile, into the trampled snow.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, passionate]I want you to follow me because I'll fight for you," he said. "That's all. That's the only reason worth anything. I'll fight for you. Every one of you. I'll die for you, if it comes to that, and I think it may." His voice did not shake. "And I'll put everything I have on the line. Everything. This name too, if that's what it costs. I'll put it on the line first."

[narrator] The breath-cloud moved over the yard and settled.

The yard held its breath and its ground.

And then Muken turned his head, and looked at the three trampled places in the snow, and the three people standing in them, and the yard turned its head with him.

[thoughtful] He had to do this. He had known it since the long room. He had known it, perhaps, since Kiyome knelt on the cold stone with her sword across her knee and said *it doesn't bind you to anything at all.* The north would not take his word for it. The north had taken Moto words for forty years and been paid in carts. They had to see it. Here, in their own yard, by their own wood, with their own eyes.

---

### III · The Three Oaths

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, formal]Kiyome Byakuya," said Muken.

[narrator] She came forward out of her trampled space into the light of the nearest brazier. She did not hurry. The crowd drew back from her a little further, without meaning to, and then held.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, slowly, formal]Do you, Kiyome Byakuya," said Muken, "of the White Line, commit yourself to the sanctity of the people? And give up your land, and your power, to me? The true King of the Clans. And Leader of the Banner Houses."

[narrator] Kiyome looked at him in the torchlight, with the frost standing on the shoulders of her robes and glittering there like ground glass, and took her time.

Then she bowed. The white line bowed to a lord of the seven by the finger's width, small and exact; she bent from the waist until her loose white hair fell forward past her face, and held it there, in front of six hundred people in the snow.

"[light Japanese accent, calm, warm, clear]I do," said Kiyome Byakuya.

[narrator] Her voice carried. It was the voice that had kept the peace in rooms worse than this, and it went over the yard clear as a struck bowl.

"[light Japanese accent, calm, warm]Everything I control is now yours," she said, still bowed. "The white house in the Shiraki hills, and its land, and its forty riders, and the granary under its floor that the Tenrai have never found. My sword. My name." She straightened, slowly, and looked at him. "[softly, emotional]And my oath, which was yours before you were born. I've only now been able to give it to the one it belonged to."

[narrator] She stepped back into the light by the brazier and stood there, with the white cord hanging loose from her hilt.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, formal]Hayato Amagiri," said Muken.

[narrator] Hayato came forward from beside the other brazier, steaming, his narrow face drawn tight in the heat.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, slowly, formal]Do you, Hayato Amagiri," said Muken, "of the Indigo Mist Line, commit yourself to the sanctity of the people? And give up your land, and your power, to me? The true King of the Clans. And Leader of the Banner Houses."

[narrator] Hayato's restless fingers had gone still in the folds of his cape. He looked at Muken, and then, for a moment, round the yard. At the farriers and the masons and the women from the long houses. At the row of stretchers along the barracks wall.

"[light Japanese accent, quick, wry, plainly]I do," said Hayato.

[narrator] He said it plainly, without any ceremony at all, the way he said distances.

"[light Japanese accent, quick, wry]I'll tell you all what that's worth, since it's not much," he said to the yard, in the same plain voice. "I don't hold the Amagiri land. My uncle does, and he sits at Kagenori Tenrai's left hand, and he'll curse my name in the Hall of Verdicts before the week's out." A few heads turned toward him. "What I've got is twenty riders, and every road north of the Seat that the Tenrai don't know is a road. The first grain that comes up them goes to your long houses. Not his." He jerked his chin south, at nothing, at the whole of the dark. "Yours."

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, formal]Tetsugen Akagane," said Muken.

[narrator] The great bald man did not come forward. He stood where he was, beside the Stark farrier, broad as a door, with the copper of his coat gone black in the cold except where the brazier found it and lit it red-bronze, and his scarred hands on his belt, and let the yard come to him the way the boy had let the room come to him that morning.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, slowly, formal]Do you, Tetsugen Akagane," said Muken, "Leader of the Copper Line, of the Blood and Heart Arts, commit yourself to the sanctity of the people? And give up your land, and your power, to me? The true King of the Clans."

[narrator] Tetsugen looked at him from under his thick gray brows, and kept looking. Long enough that somebody in the crowd shifted their feet on the squeaking snow.

"[light Japanese accent, flat, iron, deep]I do," said Tetsugen Akagane.

[narrator] Then, because he was who he was, he did not leave it there.

He turned, slowly, on the spot, and looked at the yard. At the Starks in their hide-coats and the Greymane foot in their gray. At the gray-bearded rider who had spat. He looked at them the way a man looks at a field he once rode over at the head of a column.

"[light Japanese accent, flat, iron, deep]Some of you will know my name," he said, in his flat iron voice. "If your fathers went south in the Sennen campaign, they ate from my granaries on the way. I signed for every sack. I knew where the columns were going and what they'd do when they got there, and I signed." He let that sit in the breath-cloud. "[pause]I'll not ask you to forgive it. I've given the boy the books. Every name. Mine's at the top." He turned back to Muken. "[gruff]My valley's yours. The Kurogane granaries. My levies, every one, and they'll answer nobody's muster but yours. And my own neck, if you find a use for it."

[narrator] He did not bow. The Akagane had never sworn to the red, and he had said so that morning. But he took his right hand off his belt and laid it flat on his chest, over his heart, the Stark way, in front of three hundred Starks. And held it there.

---

### IV · What Is Ours

[narrator] Muken turned back to the yard.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, loud]Now you've seen it," he said.

[narrator] His voice had come up. It carried to the back wall and the stretchers and the old woman on her stump, and the breath-cloud moved with it.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, loud, steady]You've seen it with your own eyes," said Muken. "These are Moto. All three. The white line, and the copper, and the indigo. Lords of the seven. The same houses that sent the levies and the tax-men and the grain-carts going south." He swept one hand toward the three of them standing in the firelight. "And they've just given it up. All of it. Land and riders and granaries and roads. In front of you. By your woodpile." He let the hand fall. "They didn't have to. They could have stayed under the Tenrai, where everything was safe and warm and fed. Kagenori would have kept them in their chairs for the rest of their lives if they'd only kept their mouths shut. They came here instead."

[narrator] He looked out over them.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, firm]So they're not our enemies," he said. "Hear me. These three aren't. They've put themselves on the side of the people, the same as you did when you knelt here. They've given up their livelihoods for it. And they've done it knowing the Hall will call them traitors for it by the end of the month." His jaw worked. "That's the line, from tonight, and it always was: the ones who take and the ones who are taken from. And I know which side of it I was born on." He touched the dead token at his belt. "Three galleries down under Halcrest. Whatever my blood is."

[narrator] He took a breath.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, steady]And there's another thing you'll carry home," he said. "Every one of you. To every long house that ever buried a son on a southern field." He looked straight out at the gray-bearded rider who had spat. "Under my rule, no Moto will ever march on the Zettari again. Or anyone else's land. No levy, no column, not one of your boys sent south to die in somebody else's war. We'll hold the north, and we'll hold it well, and we'll reach for nothing past it." His voice hardened. "[hard]I swore it this morning. By my honor. You can hold me to it."

[narrator] The gray-bearded rider looked back at him. He did not spit again.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, rising]But we have to fight for what's ours," said Muken.

[narrator] And now his voice rose all the way, for the first time in his life, and the gallery had gone out of it. It came up out of somewhere deeper, out of the salt and the stair and the long room, and the torches seemed to lean toward it.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, shouts, fierce]Nobody will give it to us," he said. "The Tenrai won't. The Hall won't. They've had forty years to give it and they've given us carts. So we'll take it." He turned his head, very slightly, north and east, toward the dark beyond the wall where the Keld fires were. "The Keld march in two days. Four thousand riders, bought with Tenrai seed grain, and an Iron Fist at the front of them who's never once lost. We'll meet them on the ice. And we'll break them." He turned back. "And then the Banner Houses who still stand in our way. Ashmore. Thornwall. Whoever else has taken Tenrai silver to keep their own people on their knees. We'll bring them in. Under the white banner, or under the snow." He drew breath. "And when the north is ours again, all of it, every fire and every stack, then we'll see who sits in the Hall of Verdicts and calls himself our master."

[narrator, pause] He stopped.

He stood at the foot of the woodpile in the torchlight with the breath going up off him in a white plume, and the scrap-iron sword at his hip, and the wolfhound risen to its feet beside him, and waited.

---

### V · Hail

[narrator, long pause] For a long moment, nothing.

The braziers cracked. The lamps spat along the top of the woodpile. Six hundred people stood in the snow and looked at the boy who had just told them he was Gimbzo's son, and nobody moved, and the silence went on so long that Godfrey Greymane, at Muken's left hand, felt his own heart counting it.

[softly] Then Yukazuri stepped forward.

She was the smallest person in the front rank. She walked out of it, between the vanguard boys and the stretchers, with her sleeves rolled to the elbow in the cold and her hands still faintly red from the surgeons' line, across the trampled snow to her brother. She did not say anything. She turned round, so that she was facing the yard, and stood beside him, at his right knee, opposite the wolfhound. And she held up her right hand, palm out, so that six hundred people could see it in the torchlight.

There was a mark drawn on it. In blood that had gone dark. The long down-stroke and the hook and the crossing and the small closed loop at the foot.

[slowly] She held it there.

[narrator] At the front, the vanguard boy with the red ears made a sound in his throat. Then he went down on one knee in the snow.

The boy beside him went down after him. And the one beside him. And then the whole knot of them, the twenty-odd who had sewn the banner, all at once, the way they had done it the first time, with their elbows in each other's ribs.

Rob Stark went down. Then Mark beside him. Then the gray-bearded rider who had spat, slowly, [grunts] with a grunt, one knee and then the other, in his frozen hide-coat that creaked at the elbow like a door.

[narrator] And then it went through the yard the way fire goes through dry grass, or the way it did in the old days when there was still grass: from the front to the back, from the Starks to the Greymane foot to the farriers and the masons and the women from the long houses with their children on their hips. The men on the stretchers along the barracks wall could not kneel, and so they lifted their fists instead, and laid them on their chests.

[pause] Not all of them.

Muken saw it, because he was looking for it. Twenty men, perhaps thirty, scattered through the crowd, who stood. A Greymane spearman near the gate who stood with his arms folded and his face like a shut door. Three Stark riders together at the back who looked at each other and did not move. A carter who turned round, as the yard went down around him, and walked away through the kneeling bodies toward the postern gate without looking back, and pulled it open, and went out into the dark.

[tense] Lorn Stark's hand moved toward his axe.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, quiet]Let him go," said Muken quietly.

[narrator] Lorn looked at him.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, quiet, steady]Let him go," said Muken again. "I told them they could. Every one of them who walks out of that gate tonight saves Karogane the shouting." He did not look at the postern. "And every one who stays has heard it from me."

[narrator] Lorn's hand came off the axe.

[narrator] And then, from the side of the yard, from the old woman on her stump by the barracks door, came a sound.

She had not knelt. She could not. She had sat on that stump in her three shawls for four days with her toothless mouth set in a long hard line, watching the boy with the white hair, and she had not said a single word to anyone. Now she lifted her head and opened her mouth, and what came out of it was cracked and thin and old as the stones of the Seat, and it carried over the whole yard.

"[very old woman, Yorkshire accent, cracked]*Hail,*" said the old woman.

[narrator] Six hundred heads turned.

"[very old woman, Yorkshire accent, cracked, trembling]*Hail to the king,*" she said. She was shaking. She did not stop. "[crying, louder]*Hail. Hail to the true king of the north.*"

[narrator] The vanguard boy with the red ears said it after her. Then the boys round him. Then Rob, and Mark, and the farrier beside Tetsugen Akagane, and the women from the long houses, and the Greymane foot in their long gray file, all of them, from their knees, in the snow, in the torchlight, in the cold that took the words out of their mouths in white plumes and lifted them into the breath-cloud over the yard until the whole of it seemed to be made of them.

"[Yorkshire accent, crowd chanting, booming]*Hail to the true king of the north. Hail to the true king of the north.*"

[narrator, crowd chanting] It went on. It went up. It came back off the curtain wall and the pale seam of the mend and the raw pine of the new gate and the blank white banner hanging over it, and down again, louder, until the torches shook in their cracks and the wolfhound threw back its head beside Muken's knee and [dog howling] howled with it, long and low and enormous, and nobody laughed at that either.

[narrator] Muken stood in the middle of it.

He did not lift his hands. He did not smile. He stood at the foot of the woodpile with his sister at his right knee holding up her bloody mark, and the dog howling at his left, and Lorn Stark and Godfrey Greymane a pace behind him with their fists on their chests, and he let it come.

Down at the front, in the light of the brazier, Kiyome Byakuya had her eyes closed and her lips moving. Tetsugen Akagane had not knelt, and would not; he had his hand on his chest still and his heavy jaw set like a stone. And by the other brazier, Hayato Amagiri was looking past all of it, past the yard and the kneeling crowd and the boy at the woodpile, at the postern gate standing a little open in the wall and the black cold beyond it, where somewhere out on the snow a single carter was walking south and east toward the Keld fires with the whole of it in his mouth.

*[light Japanese accent, quick, wry, inner voice]Two days,* thought Hayato. *[inner voice]He'll be there in one.*

[narrator] He did not say it. Nobody would have heard him.

[softly] Over the yard, high up, the green of the northern sky turned over once more in its sleep, and for the space of a few breaths it broke clear of the cloud, green and violet in long slow folds over the blank white banner, so that the banner took the color of it and burned there like a lantern. And the chant went on, and on, and the woodpile stood behind the king of the north as high as a man's shoulder, every split end of it pale in the torchlight, the fullest stack in Kharven.

---

# Chapter Thirteen: The Skeldmere

[narrator] *Book One of the Kōkan · Chapter Thirteen: The Skeldmere*

> [narrator, thoughtful] *The Keld have a word for the hour before a whiteout, when the light goes flat and the horizon lifts off the ice and floats, and a man cannot tell any more whether the white in front of him is ground or sky. They call it* the lifting. *A Keld child is taught to stop walking in the lifting, and sit down where he is, and wait. A Keld rider is taught the opposite.*
>
> *From* Customs of the Polar Reach, *a Hallenfeld quartermaster's notebook*

---

### I · The Carter

[narrator] The carter came in out of the dark an hour before dawn, half dead, on foot, with frost in his beard and both his hands gone white to the wrist.

The Keld outriders found him on the Skeldmere a mile from the fires, walking in a slow wandering line that had already bent twice back on itself, the way men walked when the cold had got into their heads. They put him across a saddle and brought him in, and laid him by the fat-lamps in the war-lodge on a reindeer hide, in the deep butter-yellow of their light, and a Keld woman with a face like a walnut rubbed snow into his hands, hard, until he [screams] screamed. Then they gave him the skin, the sour fermented milk in its sealskin, and he drank it and was sick and drank it again, and when he could speak, he spoke.

Aksel Keld sat on the bare packed snow of the lodge floor and listened to him.

He listened to all of it. The torches in the yard and the six hundred people in the breath-cloud. The woodpile. The boy standing at the foot of it with his white hair in the torchlight. *[Northern English accent, low and rough, quietly]I am Muken Moto. First of my name.*[narrator] The three lords of the seven lines coming out of the crowd one after another and giving up their land, all of it, in the snow, in front of everyone. *[Northern English accent, low and rough, quietly]Son of Kasane, and son of Gimbzo.*[narrator] The girl with the blood on her hand. The old woman who started the chant.

"[Icelandic accent, flat, rough, young man]They knelt?" Aksel said.

[narrator] The carter's lips were cracked and bleeding. He nodded.

"[Icelandic accent, flat, rough, young man, quiet]Knowing."

"[Yorkshire accent, hoarse, weak]Knowing." The carter's voice was a wet rasp. "He told them. Every word of it. Then he told them they could walk out, and nobody would stop them. I walked." He [coughs] coughed, and something came up that the walnut-faced woman wiped off his chin with a fistful of snow. "[ashamed]Twenty of us did. Thirty. Out of six hundred."

[narrator, long pause] The lodge was very quiet. The fat-lamps hissed.

Behind Aksel's left shoulder, a pace back, in plain gray with his hood down, Karogane had not moved. Aksel knew what his face was doing without turning to look. He had sat across a lamp from that face for six weeks. It was a long pale face with very pale eyes, and it did nothing, ever, and Aksel had learned to read the nothing the way he read a sky.

It was doing a great deal of nothing now.

"[Icelandic accent, flat, rough, young man, slowly]So," said Aksel Keld.

[narrator] He turned the horn cup on his knee a quarter-turn with his left hand, slowly, and looked into the milk.

"[Icelandic accent, flat, rough, young man, dry]So your sentence is spent, Tenrai." He did not turn his head. "You meant to stand between the lines and tell them whose son he was, and watch them put him on a post. The boy said it first. In his own yard. By his own wood." He drank, and set the cup down. "It's a strange thing to learn about an enemy the night before you fight him. That he's not afraid of the truth."

"[light Japanese accent, soft and dry]It changes nothing," said Karogane.

[narrator] His voice was soft and dry and perfectly level. It was the first thing he had said since the carter came in.

"[Icelandic accent, flat, rough, young man]It changes the morning," said Aksel. "A man who's been told he's following Gimbzo's son and kneels anyway won't break when you shout it at him across the ice. He'll laugh at you." He looked, at last, over his shoulder, and his narrow black eyes held the Tenrai agent's pale ones. "I had six hundred riders in my count who'd have turned their horses round on the ice when they heard it. Starks with Stark grievances. Greymanes who buried brothers at Hiroe. Now I've none. Now I've four hundred of Lorn Stark's best in front of me, who know exactly whose son they're following, and chose him anyway." He turned back to the lamp. "I'd sooner fight four thousand men who don't know why they're there than four hundred who do."

"[light Japanese accent, soft and dry]You have four thousand," said Karogane.

"[Icelandic accent, flat, rough, young man]I do," said Aksel. "And I'll use them." He lifted his iron hand off his knee and let it lie on his thigh, black and heavy, with the cord running up into the sleeve. "But don't tell me nothing's changed. Something always changes. A Keld who can't see it when it does is a Keld who walks out into the lifting and never comes back."

[narrator] He got to his feet.

He did it all at once, the way the Keld rose from their fires, with no hand on the floor, his short powerful legs taking all of him straight up off the packed snow. The walrus-ivory rings in his braids clicked. Around the lodge the Keld captains rose with him, one for each of his fights, men he had broken in and men who had broken in beside him, in their reindeer coats with their spears and their short recurved bows of horn and sinew, and the old Keld war-chant they never sang aloud but only hummed, low in the chest, began somewhere at the back of [humming] the lodge.

"[Icelandic accent, flat, rough, young man, commanding]Wake the fires," said Aksel Keld. "We ride at the gray."

---

### II · We March

[narrator] The Keld did not form ranks. They had never formed ranks. A rank was a southern thing, a wall of men who stood in a line because they could not trust themselves to stand anywhere else. The Keld came out of their snow-shelters in the black hour before the gray and found their horses by smell, and mounted, and drifted together across the ice in loose knots of twenty and thirty that moved like a herd of reindeer moves, each knot with its own captain and its own reason, and the knots drifted together in the dark until they were four thousand riders spread over a mile of the Skeldmere, breathing.

Six hundred men breathing in the cold made a sound like a tide coming in under a pier. Four thousand made a sound like the sea.

They were short men on short horses, the northern kind that did not founder in the drift and could live a week on lichen and their own fat. Every rider had two coats, the inner of reindeer hair turned in and the outer of sealskin turned out, and a hood of wolverine fur that did not take frost. Every rider had his bow in a case of sealskin at his knee and thirty arrows in the quiver at his back, the heads of black glass and bone because iron cracked in the deep cold. Every rider had a lance of birch with a long leaf head, and a short axe, and a knife. And every rider had, tied at his saddle-bow, a twist of white cloth the length of his arm, which he would pull over his face and his horse's face in the lifting, so that a Keld rider in a whiteout was a white thing on a white thing in a white world, and could not be seen until he was close enough to touch.

Aksel Keld rode out in front of them as the gray came up, and with it, low in the east behind the Grey Teeth, a band of rose and pale gold no wider than a hand.

He sat his horse, a stocky dun with a black stripe down its back, at the edge of the great loose herd of them, and looked out east over the ice to where the long dark line of the Grey Teeth stood up out of the white, and then south, toward the Foot. Toward the boy.

Karogane rode up beside him on a tall gray southern horse that stood a hand higher than any Keld mount and looked absurd for it, and stopped, and said nothing.

Aksel gave him one look, and no more.

Then he turned his horse to face the Keld, all four thousand of them, spread over the ice in the early light with their breath going up in a single white mist that the low sun turned pink along its top, and he did not raise his voice. The Keld did not raise their voices. The wind took anything that was not said plainly.

"[Icelandic accent, flat, rough, young man, steady]We march," said Aksel Keld.

[narrator] The mist moved.

"[Icelandic accent, flat, rough, young man, resolute]For the safety of our people," he said. "For the herds, and the fires, and the children in the snow-houses who'll eat this spring because of Tenrai seed grain and not because of anybody's crown." He let it sit. "We march for ours. For the ice. For the Keld." His narrow black eyes went over them, slowly, from one knot to the next, and every captain he looked at lifted his chin. "And we ride over all who'd stand in the way of it. Moto or Stark or Greymane or the Sky itself. Whatever's in front of us when the lifting comes, we ride over it, and we don't look back to see what it was."

[narrator] He lifted his right hand.

The iron hand. Black iron and walrus tusk and sinew, the heavy articulated fingers closing slowly as he drew the cord at his shoulder, until it was a fist the size of a man's skull, held up black against the rose of the eastern sky over the Skeldmere where every Keld rider on the ice could see it.

"[Icelandic accent, flat, rough, young man, slowly, proud]The Iron Fist of the Keld," said Aksel.

[narrator, crowd humming] He said nothing after it. Four thousand throats took it up, low, a hum in the chest that went across the ice like a wind coming, and the horses stamped and blew, and the frozen ground rang under their hooves and came up through the horses into the riders' knees.

Beside him, on his absurd tall gray horse, Karogane watched the Keld warlord with his very pale eyes, and his long pale face did nothing at all.

*[Icelandic accent, flat, rough, young man, inner voice]For now,*[narrator] Aksel Keld thought again, as he had thought in the lodge with his fist on his heart, and lowered his hand.

---

### III · The Earful

[narrator] At the Foot, Yukazuri was waiting at the gate.

She had been up all night. Muken could see it in the set of her shoulders under her shawl and the color of the skin under her eyes. She had been up all night at the surgeons' line with the two Greymane women who could stitch and the Stark boy who could carry, laying out everything she had on clean linen in rows: the gut and the needles, the salve tins, the spirit, the long iron probes for arrowheads and the short curved knife for what the probes could not reach, the splints, the straps, the boiled cloth in its wooden tubs with the steam still coming off it. He knew because he had come to the tent door at midnight and seen her at it, kneeling between the rows with her hands moving and her lips moving, counting, and had gone away again without saying anything because there was nothing he could say that she had not already counted.

She stood at the gate now in the first light, with the frost on the new gate-timbers glittering and the white banner hanging over her head, blank as new snow, and her sleeves already rolled to the elbow in the cold.

Behind her the yard was full of horses. Four hundred of them. Lorn Stark's vanguard, the best of the Stark riders and the boys who had sewn the banner, in their hide-coats and mail with their axes across their backs and their round shields of limewood and hide on their arms, mounted and waiting, their breath going up in a single low cloud. The Greymane foot stood along the walls above them in their long gray file, spears grounded, watching. They were not riding. Godfrey Greymane had argued against it for an hour in the long room and lost, and then argued for it, quietly, to his own men, for another hour, and won. *[soft Scottish accent, measured]Somebody holds the Foot,*[narrator] he had said, finally, standing at the end of the split pine table with his long gray hands flat on the wood. *[soft Scottish accent, measured]If the Keld come round us in the snow and we've left the gate with nobody on it, we'll have no Foot to come home to. The Greymanes hold. It's what we do.*

[narrator] He stood at the gatehouse now, a little apart from his men, very straight, in gray, and did not look at the yard.

Muken stopped in front of his sister.

He looked at her. She looked back at him, with her small sharp face set like a closed fist and her gray eyes very steady, and for a moment neither of them spoke, and the four hundred horses blew and stamped behind them, and somewhere on the wall a Greymane spearman [coughs] coughed.

"[young girl, Northern English accent, sharp, quiet]Please be careful," said Yukazuri.

[narrator] That was all. Her voice was very quiet and quite level. She did not reach for him. Her hands stayed at her sides with her sleeves rolled up, red at the knuckles from the boiled cloth.

Muken looked at her a moment longer.

[softly] Then he put his hand on her head.

It was the old thing. It was what Yusif had done, once or twice, in the gallery, when he was going away and did not want to say so. It was what Muken had done on the convoy road at the Marrow crossing and in the room over the tea-house and in the dark at the back of the surgeons' tent two nights ago, when he had told her whose mark it was. The whole weight of his hand, flat and warm through her hair, and not ruffling it, and not pressing down. Just there.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, warm]I know," he said. "If I'm not, you'll give me an earful."

[narrator] The corner of her mouth moved. It was nowhere near a smile.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, wry]So I'll try my best," said Muken.

"[young girl, Northern English accent, sharp]Try harder than your best," said Yukazuri. "Your best got you a cut across the ribs I had to close twice."

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, wry]Then I'll try harder than that."

"[young girl, Northern English accent, sharp, fierce]Good." She did not move from under his hand. "Because if you come back through this gate on a door with your guts in your lap, I'll put them back in, and I'll sew you up, and then I'll kill you myself."

[narrator, laughs] Somebody in the front rank of the vanguard laughed. It was the boy with the red ears. He stopped at once, and went red to the collar, and then, because nobody told him off, he [chuckles] laughed again, quietly, and the boy beside him did too.

[narrator] Muken took his hand off her head.

He did not say goodbye. Neither of them did. He turned and went to his horse, a rangy Stark bay with one white foot that Lorn had given him out of the Hollenmark string, and mounted, and the wolfhound came out of the gatehouse at a lope and fell in beside its stirrup without being called. He looked once at the gatehouse, at Godfrey. Godfrey Greymane looked back at him, and put his right hand flat on his chest, and held it there.

Then Muken rode out of the gate of Kharven's Foot at the head of four hundred Stark riders, under the white banner, onto the Kessen road, and did not look back.

Yukazuri stood in the gate and watched them go until the last of them had passed under the arch and the road had taken them and the snow they had kicked up had settled again.

[softly] Then she turned round and walked back across the yard to the surgeons' line and knelt down between her rows of linen, and began counting again from the beginning.

---

### IV · The Ice

[narrator, thoughtful] The Skeldmere was a lake, once.

Nobody living had seen it as one. It had frozen through in the century the grass went and never thawed, and the snow had come down on it and been packed hard by the wind and come down again, year on year, until it was a plain of white so flat and so wide that a man standing in the middle of it could see the curve of the world. Under the snow, if you dug, there was ice, black and clear, and under the ice, a long way down, the old lake was still there in the dark, and sometimes on a still night the ice moved over it and [distant booming] boomed like a great drum struck very far away, and the Keld said it was the drowned grass trying to come up.

[narrator] The vanguard came out onto it at the second hour of the morning, from the Kessen road, and stopped at the shore. The low sun lay along the Skeldmere and gilded every wind-ridge on it, and the hollows between the ridges were blue as a jay's wing.

Muken sat his bay with Lorn Stark at his right hand and looked across the ice.

He had known the number. Godfrey had told him in the long room. The outriders had told him every night for a week. He had said it to the yard himself, at the woodpile. *[Northern English accent, low and rough, quietly]Four thousand riders.*[narrator] He had thought he understood what it meant.

[pause] He had not.

They covered the ice. From the dark line of the Grey Teeth in the east to the white blur of the far shore in the north, the Keld were spread over the Skeldmere in their loose drifting knots like a herd that went on to the edge of seeing, and the edge of seeing went on further than it should have, because the light was already going flat. Four thousand riders, and every one of them a short dark shape on a short dark horse, and over all of them their breath standing in a mist that the low gray sky came down to meet. They were not moving. They sat their horses on the ice the way the ice sat on the lake, as if they had always been there and always would.

"[Yorkshire accent, young, rough, quietly, awed]Four thousand," said Lorn Stark quietly.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, quiet]Four thousand," said Muken.

"[Yorkshire accent, young, rough, anxious]There's really four thousand of them."

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, quiet]There really are."

[narrator] Lorn said nothing else. Behind them, four hundred Stark riders sat their horses on the shore of the Skeldmere and looked out at the same thing, and none of them said anything either, and the only sound was the horses and the wind and, very faint, very far out on the ice, a deep low boom that came up through the frozen ground into the horses' legs.

[narrator] Muken's eyes moved over the Keld.

He did not decide to do it. It happened the way it had happened on the stair at the Foot, and on the Vessant road, and in every ditch he had ever stood in: the Shingan opened behind his left eye like a hand unclosing, and the gold seam in his iris spread a hair, and the world changed.

The Keld were still there. The ice was still there. But now, over all of it, like frost on a window, there were lines.

Thin lines. Hair-fine. Gold. They ran through the Keld host the way cracks run through old glaze, between one knot of riders and the next, along the seams where one captain's men ended and another's began, branching and joining and branching again. Most of them were faint, hardly there. A few were brighter. And one, out on the left of the Keld host, toward the Grey Teeth, between a big knot of riders on dark horses and a smaller one on grays, was bright enough that it seemed to hum.

*[Northern English accent, low and rough, inner voice]There,* thought Muken. *That's where it's tired.*

[narrator] He did not know how he knew. He had never known. He only knew that it had never once been wrong.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, focused]Lorn," he said. "The grays. On their left, by the mountains."

[narrator] Lorn looked.

"[Yorkshire accent, young, rough]I see them."

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, thoughtful]They don't like the ones next to them," said Muken. "The big knot, on the dark horses. Something between them. Old." He did not take his eyes off the gold line. "If it breaks anywhere, it breaks there."

[narrator] Lorn Stark gave him a long sideways look. Then he looked back at the ice, and his jaw tightened on the left side, and he nodded once.

"[Yorkshire accent, young, rough, wary]The grays are the Sennet clan," he said. "The dark horses are the Haldorssons. Sennet and Haldorsson have been killing each other over the Teeth pastures since my grandfather's time. Aksel Keld broke them both in and made them ride together." He was quiet a moment. "[suspicious]You couldn't have known that."

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, quiet]I didn't," said Muken.

[narrator] Out on the ice, a knot of riders had detached itself from the front of the Keld host and was coming toward them across the snow at a walk.

---

### V · Between the Lines

[narrator] They met in the middle of the ice, halfway between the two hosts, where the snow lay untouched and very white.

Twelve Keld riders came out, in a loose ring, with Aksel Keld at the front of them on his dun with the black stripe, and the tall gray southern horse beside him. Muken took Lorn and six Stark riders, and the wolfhound, and nobody else. He had thought of taking Kiyome and her forty. He had thought better of it. *[Northern English accent, low and rough, quiet]If the Tenrai see Byakuya white beside me at a parley,*[narrator] he had said to her at dawn, *[Northern English accent, low and rough, quiet]they'll know what you've done before you're ready for them to know it.*[narrator] She had looked at him a long moment and then gone to her riders, and they had ridden off north along the shore before light, toward Thornhaven, and he had not seen them since.

Hayato had gone two nights ago. South and west, on his own roads, toward Ashbury, with his twenty. *[light Japanese accent, quick, wry]Give me two days,*[narrator] he had said, mounting in the dark of the yard with the juniper still in his toggle. *[light Japanese accent, quick, wry]Two days and a road. That's all I've ever needed.*

[narrator, pause] It had been two days.

Muken looked across the snow at the Iron Fist of the Keld.

He was younger than Muken had expected, and older.
