Eighteen, Godfrey had said, and he had the face of eighteen, broad and flat and wind-burned red-brown across the cheekbones, with the three thin blue lines down his chin and the fourth beside them. But he sat his horse like a man of forty, low and still and settled, as if the horse were part of the ice and he were part of the horse, and the iron hand lay on his thigh, black and heavy, and did not move. The walrus-ivory rings in his braids, one for each fight, clicked faintly when the wind took them.

Aksel Keld looked back at him with narrow black eyes and said nothing at all.

It was the man beside him who dismounted.

He swung down off the tall gray horse in one smooth movement, plain gray cloak swirling, and stood on the snow, tall and lean with his hood thrown back and a long pale face and very pale eyes. He carried no sword that Muken could see. He did not need one. He moved the way Shigure Tenrai had moved on the stair, with nothing wasted and nothing hurried, as though every step had been measured out for him in advance and he was only walking the measure.

*[Northern English accent, low and rough, inner voice]Karogane,* thought Muken. *So that's what a knife looks like.*

[narrator] The Tenrai agent ignored him. He looked past him, at the young Stark at his right hand, and his thin mouth moved very slightly at one corner.

"[light Japanese accent, soft and dry, mocking]Lorn Stark," said Karogane. [narrator] His voice was soft and dry and carried perfectly across the still air. "Son of Ulf. Warlord of Hollenmark." He let each title fall like a coin into a bowl. "[contemptuous]And you would stand beside a tokenless."

[narrator] Lorn Stark looked down at him from the saddle and let the silence have its say first.

Then he shook his head. Slowly. Once to each side.

"[Yorkshire accent, young, rough, cold]Watch your tone," said Lorn, "when you speak of the Rightful King of the Clans. And of the Banner Houses." His voice was rough and low and did not rise. "You are nothing more than a lord's man, Tenrai. You don't even have a chair. You'll address him properly, or you'll not address him."

[narrator] The Keld riders in the ring stirred. Aksel Keld did not.

Karogane's pale eyes moved, at last, to Muken.

Muken looked back at him.

He looked at the long pale face and the plain gray cloak and the hands, which were long and pale too, and motionless at his sides, and very clean. He looked at the Tenrai gold thread worked so fine into the hem of the gray that you could only see it when the light caught it. He thought about the stair at the Foot, and Shigure's sword at his throat, and the Tenrai factor at the top of the shaft with his ledger. He thought about Kagenori in the Hall of Verdicts, and the letters with two seals, and the silver always on the left.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, quietly, bitter]So this is what my family has become," said Muken.

[narrator] He said it quietly, almost to himself, and it carried anyway.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, disgusted]A muck," he said. "A muck of disgust. Clerks and knives and men who send other men's sons to die on the ice for a chair they'll never sit in." He did not look away from Karogane's face. "My father was a monster. I'll not argue it. But he stood in the mud at Shimane himself, at the end. He didn't send a man in gray to stand there for him."

[narrator] Something moved in Karogane's face.

It was the first time Muken had seen it move. It was very small, a tightening at the corners of the pale eyes, a whitening at the nostrils. But it was there. And when Karogane spoke again, his voice was no longer quite so soft.

"[light Japanese accent, soft and dry, venomous]You are not even supposed to be alive," he said.

[narrator] The words came out of him like something that had been held a long time.

"[light Japanese accent, soft and dry, angry]Do you understand that? You should have died in the cellars at Halcrest with the rest of your line, sixteen years ago. Your mother should never have walked out of that door. Every breath you've drawn since is a clerk's error." His long pale hands had closed at his sides. "And now you sit on a borrowed horse in a dead man's coat and think you can win this? With your little four hundred?" He swept one hand back at the Keld, at the four thousand on the ice behind him. "You think you can protect those people in your long houses? Your woodpile? Your old women and your boys with needles?" His voice dropped. "[low, threatening]You're dooming them. Every one. When the Keld are done with you, the Tenrai will come to the Foot, and the Hall after them, and there'll be nothing north of the Kessen road but ash and crows. And it'll be your name they curse for it."

[narrator, distant booming] The ice boomed, far off, under the snow.

[narrator] Muken looked at Aksel Keld.

He did it slowly, on purpose. He turned his head away from the Tenrai agent and looked at the Iron Fist on his dun horse, and held his narrow black eyes, unhurried, as if Karogane had stopped speaking some time ago and was no longer there. Aksel looked back at him. Neither of them said anything.

Then Muken turned his head back to Karogane.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, calm]Dooming," he said. "No."

[narrator, pause] He let it sit.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, steady]I'm not afraid," said Muken. "Of you. Of them." He moved his chin, very slightly, at the Keld host. "I've been doomed since I was born. You just said so. A clerk's error." His mouth moved at the corner. "[wry]I've lived sixteen years as a clerk's error. I can manage a morning more."

"[light Japanese accent, soft and dry, cold]Nobody," said Karogane, "will take the north except Shizuha."

[narrator] He had mastered himself again. The voice was soft and level and dry, and the hands had opened.

"[light Japanese accent, soft and dry, precise]Shizuha Shirogane," he said, "is the Queen of the Clans. The silver line was always the rightful second of the red. Now the red is ended, whatever a boy in a sergeant's coat tells his woodpile, and the silver stands first." He lifted his chin. "The Hall will confirm it before the thaw. The Keld have already sworn. Thornwall and Ashmore have taken her silver. By the spring there won't be a lord in the north who hasn't knelt to her, and those who haven't will be under the snow."

[narrator] Muken shook his head.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, cold]No," he said. "You'll submit to me, Tenrai."

[narrator] Karogane stared at him.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, steady]You," said Muken. "And Kagenori. And Shizuha Shirogane in her silver chair, and every line in the Hall that ever sent a levy south. All of you." His voice did not rise. "You'll give up your lands, the way the Byakuya and the Akagane and the Amagiri gave theirs. Into one hand. And I'll bind the seven lines back into one blood, the way they were before any of you learned to count." He held the pale eyes. "Seven lines and one blood. Isn't that what they teach you in the Seat? I'll make it true."

[narrator, long pause] For a moment the ice was silent.

[laughs] Then Karogane laughed.

It was a short, dry, genuine laugh, and it went across the untouched snow and made two of the Keld riders glance at each other.

"[light Japanese accent, soft and dry, amused]Do you understand," said Karogane, "what you are saying? Do you have the faintest idea?" He spread his long pale hands. "You're telling me you'll make war on the whole of the Moto house. Every line. Every seat. Every Wellspring under Guild lead from here to the southern sea. With four hundred riders and a dog."

[narrator] Muken understood.

He understood it very well. He had understood it in the long room, with his hands pressed flat on the pine to stop them shaking, and he had understood it at the woodpile, in the torchlight, with the breath-cloud over him. He understood it now, on the ice, with four thousand Keld at the Tenrai's back and the light going flat and the gold lines running through the host behind Aksel Keld like cracks in old glaze.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, cold, plainly]I understand," said Muken. "Everyone who stands against my claim will be destroyed."

[narrator] He said it quite plainly, without any heat at all.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, steady]Everyone," he said. "That's what it means. I'll not pretend otherwise. I'd sooner they didn't. I'd sooner every lord in the north came in under the white banner tomorrow and nobody had to bleed for it." He held Karogane's eyes. "But I swore at the woodpile that I'd fight for those people. Die for them. Put everything on the line. And anyone who stands between me and them is standing on the line." He gathered his reins. "So they'll be destroyed. You'll be destroyed. And I'll not look back to see what you were."

[narrator] He turned his horse.

There was nothing more to say, and everyone on the ice knew it. Lorn turned his horse after him, and the six Stark riders after him, and the wolfhound, and they rode back across the untouched snow toward the white banner at a walk, without hurrying, with their backs to the Keld.

[narrator] Behind them, Karogane was shouting.

He was shouting it at last, the thing he had come four hundred miles across the tundra to shout. He had climbed back into his saddle on the tall gray horse and stood in the stirrups, facing the Stark line, and his soft dry voice had found a carrying edge Muken would not have believed it had.

"[light Japanese accent, soft and dry, shouts]*Gimbzo's son!*" shouted Karogane. "*You are following Gimbzo's son! The butcher of Hiroe! The cellar king! His blood is in that boy and you are following it onto the ice!*"

[narrator, pause] The Stark line was silent for a moment.

Then, from somewhere in the middle of it, a voice called back. It was Rob Stark's. Muken knew it at once, rough and cheerful and a little hoarse from the cold.

"[Yorkshire accent, cheerful, hoarse, shouts]*We know!*"

[narrator] And then, from the front rank, the boy with the red ears, high and cracking:

"[young boy, Yorkshire accent, shouts, laughing]*He told us himself, you daft bastard! Two nights back! At the woodpile!*"

[narrator, crowd laughing] And then the whole Stark line was laughing. Four hundred riders on the shore of the Skeldmere in the flattening light, laughing at a Tenrai agent standing in his stirrups on a horse too tall for the ice, and the laughter went out across the snow and came back off the Grey Teeth, and Karogane stood in the stirrups with his mouth still open and his long pale face, for the first time in anyone's memory, going a dull and blotched red.

[narrator] Aksel Keld did not laugh.

He sat his dun horse beside the Tenrai and watched the boy with the white hair ride back to his line, and his narrow black eyes did not move, and his iron hand lay on his thigh. Then he lifted his left hand, once, and lowered it.

Behind him, four thousand Keld riders pulled the white cloth up over their faces.

---

### VI · The Lifting

[narrator, slowly] The light went flat.

It went all at once, the way the Keld said it did, the way the Hallenfeld quartermasters wrote in their notebooks and nobody south of the Kessen road believed. One moment there was a horizon, the dark line of the Grey Teeth and the white blur of the far shore and the gray sky over both. The next there was nothing. The ice and the sky became one white, and the white had no edge and no depth, and a man looking into it could not tell whether the thing in front of him was a mile off or a hand's width, or whether it was there at all. The horizon lifted off the Skeldmere and floated. The sky came down. The world went blind.

[tense] And out of the blindness came the Keld.

A southern host charged in a wall, with a sound. The Keld came out of the white in silence, in their loose drifting knots, white riders on white-hooded horses, and the first thing the Stark line knew of them was the arrows.

[arrows whistling] Black glass and bone, out of nowhere, with no sound until they arrived. They came down on the Stark line in long slanting flights from three directions at once, and the Starks raised their limewood shields and took them, and some of them did not. A rider two places down from Muken took one through the throat above the mail collar, a short black shaft standing out of the side of his neck under the jaw, and he put his hand up to it as though to brush away a fly, and then the blood came, bright and fast, the carotid on the left side opened, and it came down the front of his hide-coat in a sheet and steamed in the cold, and he looked at Muken with an expression of great surprise and slid sideways off his horse into the snow. He was dead before he reached it. He could not have lived another twenty breaths with that vessel open, and he did not get twenty.

[narrator] Another took one in the thigh, high, in the meat of the quadriceps, through the hide and the wool, and [screams] screamed and kept his saddle. Another took two in his horse, and the horse went down screaming and rolled on him, and the sound his leg made under it was like a green branch breaking.

[narrator, rushed] Then the Keld were among them.

They came out of the white close enough to touch, as the old saying went, white shapes that resolved into men only at a lance's length, and the long leaf-headed birch lances came in low and fast at the Stark horses' chests and the Stark riders' bellies. A Stark rider on Muken's left took a lance through the side under the arm where the mail gapped, and the leaf head went in between his ribs and out of his back, and the Keld who held it let go of the shaft and was gone back into the white before the Stark had finished falling. Another Keld came in from the right, low on his horse's neck, and swung a short axe into the knee of a Stark bay as he passed, and the horse folded and its rider went over its head into the snow, and two more Keld rode over him before he could rise.

[narrator] They did not stay. That was the terror of it. They did not stand and fight. They came out of the white and struck and were gone, and came out of it again somewhere else, and struck, and were gone, and the Stark line could not close with them because there was nothing to close with, only white, and arrows coming out of the white, and lances, and the sound of men screaming in a blindness that had no edges.

"[Yorkshire accent, young, rough, shouts]*Close up!*" Lorn Stark was roaring. "*Close up! Shields! Shields together!*"

[narrator] They closed up. They did it well. Lorn Stark's vanguard were the best riders in Hollenmark and they had been drilled since they were boys, and they pulled in round the white banner in a tight ring with their shields locked and their axes out and their horses' heads turned outward, and the Keld arrows rattled off the limewood like hail on a roof. But the ring was a target, and the Keld knew it. They circled it in the white, unseen, and shot into it, and every time a Stark rider went down, the ring had to close again, smaller.

[narrator] Muken sat his bay at the heart of it with the scrap-iron sword in his hand and the wolfhound pressed against his stirrup, snarling at nothing, and looked into the white.

[pause] He could see the lines.

That was the thing. The Shingan did not care about the lifting. The Keld were invisible to his right eye and his left eye both, and still the gold lines ran through the host, and the host was there whether he could see it or not. He could see the gold running through the white like veins through marble. He could see where the knots were, by the seams between them. He could see them moving, circling the Stark ring in the blindness, drifting in and striking and drifting out. And he could see the bright line out on the left, the humming one, between the Sennet grays and the Haldorsson dark horses, still there, still bright, still tired.

But he could not reach it. It was half a mile off across the ice, behind a thousand Keld in a white he could not ride through without losing his way inside forty paces. And between him and it, the Stark ring was getting smaller.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, urgent]*Lorn,*" he said. "How many?"

[narrator] Lorn Stark's face was white and his helmet was gone and there was blood on his cheek from a cut that had nearly taken his ear. He did not look round.

"[Yorkshire accent, young, rough, breathless]Forty down," he said. "Fifty. I don't know. They're picking us apart."

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, quiet]I know."

"[Yorkshire accent, young, rough, anxious]We can't stay here."

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, quiet]I know."

"[Yorkshire accent, young, rough, desperate, shouts]Then what in the Sky's name do we do?"

[narrator] Muken did not answer.

Out in the white, on the right, the gold lines had begun to move in a way he had not seen before. They were drawing together. Knot after knot of Keld, drifting in from their wide circle round the Stark ring, closing on one place, east of it, toward the Grey Teeth, gathering. The lines between them were going thick and bright where they had been thin, the seams knitting shut, the whole host on that side pulling into a single dense weave like a fist closing.

*[Northern English accent, low and rough, inner voice, tense]He's done picking,* thought Muken. *He's going to hit us.*

[narrator] He could see where. He could see the shape of it in the gold, the way you could see the shape of a hand inside a mitten. A wedge. A great dense wedge of a thousand riders, more, forming in the blindness on the right, with its point aimed at the eastern face of the Stark ring, and at the point of it, at the very tip of the gold, one knot brighter and denser than all the others, with a single line running through the heart of it so thick and so bright that it did not look like a crack at all. It looked like a bar of iron.

*[Northern English accent, low and rough, inner voice]Aksel.*

[narrator] The wedge began to move.

---

### VII · Thornhaven and Ashbury

[narrator, horn blowing] The horns came from the north.

Three long notes, low and harsh, from somewhere out on the white beyond the far shore of the Skeldmere, where there should have been nothing but ice and the empty tundra. The Keld did not use horns, and these were no Stark horns either. Lorn Stark's head came round and his bloody face went blank with astonishment.

"[Yorkshire accent, young, rough, astonished]That's Thornwall," he said. "That's the thorn-horn. That's *Thornwall.*"

[narrator] Muken was not listening to him. He was looking at the gold.

Out on the north side of the Keld host, at its very edge, something had come into the weave that had not been there before. A new pattern. Dense and square and rigid, utterly unlike the loose drifting knots of the Keld, with lines in it so straight they might have been ruled with a rod. It was coming out of the white from the north at a steady walk. And at its front, small and bright, there was something else, a short line of pale gold he had seen once before, in the cedar hall, in the long room, in the yard. Kiyome's line. The white line.

*[Northern English accent, low and rough, inner voice]She went to Thornhaven,* he thought. *She said she had cousins there. She didn't say she'd bring them.*

[narrator] They came out of the lifting like a wall walking.

Thornwall pikes. Twelve hundred of them, Lorn said afterward, and he had counted the banners, each with its dark red thorn-sprig standing out of the white like blood on a sleeve: the men of Thornhaven, in their heavy gray felt and their iron caps, in a solid block forty men wide and thirty deep, with their eighteen-foot ash pikes leveled before them in a hedge so thick that a horse could not have pushed its nose through it. They did not hurry. They came down out of the north onto the Keld flank at a walk, in step, to the slow beat of a drum, with the thorn-horn sounding behind them every hundred paces, and at their head, on a white horse, in near-white robes with her loose white hair blown back and the glass beads at her temples catching what light there was and throwing it back in small sparks of every color, rode Kiyome Byakuya, with forty Byakuya riders at her back and a woman beside her in Thornwall gray whom Muken had never seen.

[narrator] The Keld on the north side of the host did not know what had hit them.

They had been circling the Stark ring, drifting in and out of the white, picking at it. They had their backs to the north. They had been riding in the lifting all morning and the lifting had been theirs, their blindness, their advantage, and now something had come out of it at their backs that they could not ride round or through or past, a moving hedge of iron points with twelve hundred men behind it, and their short horses ran straight into it in the white before their riders saw it.

The sound of horses on pikes was a sound Muken never forgot.

[narrator] And then, before the Keld on the north side had understood what was happening to them, the second thing came.

It came from the south-west, out of the Kessen road, behind the Stark ring, along the shore. Riders. Not many, five or six hundred, in the plain brown and russet of the Ashmore marches, the russet bright as autumn bracken against the snow, on big southern horses, with lances and long swords and the Ashmore boar on their pennants, at the gallop, in a long ragged line. And at the front of them, on a horse too small for the company it was keeping, with an indigo rain-cape streaming out behind him and a sprig of juniper still caught in the toggle at his throat, rode Hayato Amagiri.

He had his sword out. Muken had never seen him with his sword out. He held it badly, like a man who carried it for show, and he rode badly too, too far forward, all elbows. But he was at the front.

"[light Japanese accent, quick, wry, shouts, exhilarated]*Two days!*" Hayato was yelling, as he came past the Stark ring at the gallop with the Ashmore riders streaming after him. "*Two days and a road, sire! Two days and a road!*"

[narrator] And the Ashmore horse went into the Keld on the south side of the host at full tilt.

---

[narrator, thoughtful] They told it afterward round the fires, the ones who lived, and the telling got bigger every time, the way tellings do. But this much was true, and Lorn Stark swore to it, and so did Hayato.

Kiyome Byakuya had not gone to Thornhaven on the night of the woodpile. She had sent a crow before it. She had sent it the night she arrived at the Foot, before she had even told Muken who he was, because she had known that whatever he chose, the Keld were coming, and four hundred riders could not hold the Skeldmere against four thousand. The crow had gone to the Lady of Thornwall at Thornhaven. And the Lady of Thornwall was a Byakuya. She had been born in the white house in the Shiraki hills, a cousin of Kiyome's mother, and married north forty years ago into the Thornwall line to seal a peace nobody now remembered, and she had sat in Thornhaven ever since under Tenrai taxes and Tenrai factors and Tenrai silver, and smiled, and paid, and kept the oath of her house over the lamp every night with the doors shut. *[light Japanese accent, calm, warm, softly]Purity before power. Peace before the blade. And the red line before the white.*

[narrator] Kiyome's crow had said four words. *[softly]The red line lives.*

[narrator] The Lady of Thornwall had called out her pikes the next morning. The Tenrai factor at Thornhaven had objected. She had had him put in the cellar. The pikes had marched two days without stopping across the frozen tundra north of the Skeldmere, through country nobody marched through in winter, and come down on the Keld out of the lifting because a Byakuya rider who had grown up on that ice had led them through the whiteout by the sound of the lake booming under it.

Ashmore was simpler, and Hayato told it himself, plainly, the way he told distances. Ashmore had taken Tenrai silver because Ashmore had no choice. The Ashmore marches lay on the junction of three roads, the Kessen and the Marrow and the salt road from the south, and whoever held the roads held Ashmore's grain, and for twenty years the Tenrai had held them. Hayato had ridden into Ashbury two nights ago with twenty riders and a map. He had laid the map on Lord Edric Ashmore's table. Every road into the Ashmore marches that the Tenrai knew, he had drawn in black. Every road they did not know, his roads, the Amagiri tracks, the herders' shoulders and the deer paths and the smugglers' fords, he had drawn in indigo. There had been more indigo than black.

*[light Japanese accent, quick, wry]Your grain comes up the black roads,*[narrator] Hayato had said, *[light Japanese accent, quick, wry]at the Tenrai's price. Swear to the king of the north, and from this spring it comes up the indigo, at no price at all, and no Tenrai factor will ever find the way.*

[narrator] Edric Ashmore had looked at the map under the low hum of the one worked lamp in his hall, the only licensed draw in the marches, for as long as it took his steward to bring wine and carry it away again untouched. Then he had sworn, at the junction stone at the crossroads below Ashbury, with his hand on the stone, to Hayato Amagiri in the name of the king. And he had called out his horse.

---

[narrator] On the ice, the Keld host began to come apart.

Muken saw it in the gold before he saw it with his eyes. The north side of the host, where the Thornwall pikes had come in, had gone into a chaos of broken lines and knots spinning off into the white. The south side, where the Ashmore horse had struck, was buckling, the knots pulling back on each other, the seams between them glowing hot. And out on the left, by the Grey Teeth, the bright humming line between the Sennet grays and the Haldorsson dark horses had begun, at last, to run.

He watched it go. It went like a crack runs across ice when the weight comes down: all at once, faster than the eye, along its whole length. The Sennet grays turned their horses, and turned them on the Haldorssons beside them, in the white, with their lances down, because whatever old thing lay between those two clans over the Teeth pastures had been waiting for exactly this, for the Iron Fist to be busy somewhere else, and the Iron Fist was busy.

*[Northern English accent, low and rough, inner voice]There,* thought Muken. *I told you. That's where it was tired.*

[narrator, tense] But the wedge was still coming.

The great dense wedge on the east side, the thousand riders gathered behind the bar of iron. The Thornwall pikes had not reached it. The Ashmore horse had not reached it. It was coming out of the white straight at the eastern face of the Stark ring at a gallop, the frozen ground ringing under it like a drum, the white cloths over the riders' faces streaming back, and at its very point, on a dun horse with a black stripe down its back, with his right arm drawn back and his iron fist closed, rode Aksel Keld.

He had seen the pikes. He had seen the Ashmore horse. He had seen the Sennet turn on the Haldorssons. He had understood, in the time it took the thorn-horn to sound twice, that the battle on the Skeldmere was lost, and that there was only one thing left on the ice that could win it back.

[slowly] The boy under the white banner.

*[Icelandic accent, quietly]Take the head,*[narrator] every Keld captain was taught, *[Icelandic accent, quietly]and the herd scatters.*

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, firm]*Lorn,*" said Muken. "*Hold the ring.*"

[narrator] And he put his heels to the Stark bay and rode out of it, alone, with the wolfhound running at his stirrup, straight at the point of the wedge.

---

### VIII · The Iron Fist

[narrator] They met on the open ice, on the eastern face, a hundred paces out from the Stark ring, in the white.

Aksel saw him coming. Muken saw him see it. The narrow black eyes above the white cloth widened a fraction and then narrowed again, and the Iron Fist of the Keld did something no southern rider would have done: he pulled out of the point of his own wedge, swerving his dun hard to the right, and let the thousand riders behind him go on past toward the Stark ring without him. They went past like a river round a stone. And then it was the two of them, on the open ice, in a pocket of the lifting that the passing wedge had churned clear, a ring of trampled snow perhaps forty paces across with the white standing round it on every side like a wall.

Aksel Keld turned his horse to face him.

Muken pulled up the bay.

They looked at each other across the snow.

The wolfhound had stopped at the bay's shoulder with its hackles up and its head low, and it did not make a sound.

[narrator] Aksel pulled the white cloth down off his face with his left hand. His breath went up in a plume. His broad flat face was red with cold and wet with sweat at the temples under the wolverine hood, and there was blood on the side of his neck, not his own, and his black eyes were very calm.

"[Icelandic accent, flat, rough, young man, breathing heavily]So," said Aksel Keld. "The boy who isn't afraid."

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, steady]So," said Muken. "The Iron Fist."

"[Icelandic accent, flat, rough, young man]Your Byakuya brought Thornwall."

"[Northern English accent, low and rough]She did."

"[Icelandic accent, flat, rough, young man]And your Amagiri brought Ashmore."

"[Northern English accent, low and rough]He did."

"[Icelandic accent, flat, rough, young man, faintly amused]I'd have liked to know that," said Aksel, "two days ago." He sounded faintly interested, the way a man sounds when he has lost a game of stones to a move he did not see. "The Tenrai told me Thornwall and Ashmore were bought. He told me they'd taken the silver."

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, dry]They did take it," said Muken. "Then somebody offered them something they wanted more."

[narrator] Aksel looked at him a long moment.

"[Icelandic accent, flat, rough, young man, thoughtful]The Keld were bought too," he said. "You know that. No tithe on the ice. Seed grain. No Moto boot on Keld snow." He shifted his weight in the saddle. "What would you have offered, Moto, if you'd come to my fire first?"

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, steady]The same," said Muken. "And one thing more."

"[Icelandic accent, flat, rough, young man]What?"

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, quiet]That I'd still be offering it after I'd won."

[narrator] Something went across Aksel Keld's face, very fast, like a fox crossing snow. Then it was gone.

"[Icelandic accent, flat, rough, young man, hard]Get down," said Aksel.

[narrator] He swung off the dun himself, in one movement, and stood on the trampled snow with his short powerful legs planted and his iron fist hanging at his side, and drew the short axe from his belt with his left hand.

"[Icelandic accent, flat, rough, young man]I'll not fight a man on a horse that isn't his," he said. "Get down, and we'll settle it on the ice, the way the Keld settle things. If I win, the Starks go home and you go into the death-house, and the Tenrai can do what they like with the rest. If you win." He shrugged, one heavy shoulder. "The herd scatters."

[narrator] Muken got down.

He gave the bay's reins to nothing, let them drop, and the bay stood. He stepped forward onto the trampled snow with the scrap-iron sword in his right hand, black and pitted and worn thin from a thousand sharpenings, and the wolfhound came with him a pace and then stopped, because he put his left hand down flat in front of its nose without looking at it, and it understood.

[narrator, tense] They circled.

Muken's eyes were on the iron fist. The Shingan was wide open now, wider than it had ever been, and the gold seam in his left iris had spread to a thread, and through it Aksel Keld was a man made of lines. Most of them were faint. The Iron Fist was not tired anywhere. His stance was low and wide and balanced, his weight going straight down through his short legs into the ice, and the lines through his body ran clean and even and strong. There was no crack in him that Muken could see.

Except the hand.

The iron hand was full of lines. Black iron and walrus tusk and sinew, the articulated fingers riveted at every joint, the gauntlet strapped to the stump and the forearm, the cord running up the sleeve. And through all of it, through every rivet and every strap and every finger-joint, fine gold lines running, branching, the way the lines ran through old glaze. It had been hit a thousand times. It had hit a thousand things. Every one of them had left something in it.

*[Northern English accent, low and rough, inner voice]There,* thought Muken. *If anywhere. There.*

[narrator, rushed] Aksel came in.

He was fast. Faster than Muken had believed a man built like that could be. He came off his back foot low and hard with the axe in his left hand leading, a short chopping stroke at Muken's sword-arm, and Muken took it on the flat of the scrap-iron blade and felt it go all the way up his arm into his shoulder, and stepped back, and the iron fist came round behind the axe in a short flat hook at his ribs.

The left side. The cut side. The one Yukazuri had closed twice.

He saw it coming. He saw it in the gold before it moved, the line through Aksel's hips and shoulder lighting up an instant before the fist did. He saw it, and he moved, and he was not fast enough.

[narrator] The iron fist hit him under the left arm, on the ribs, through Ulf Stark's mail and the dead sergeant's coat.

It was like being kicked by a horse. It was like the gallery roof coming down. The mail held and the rings did not part, and the force went through them anyway, through the wool and the skin and the muscle, into the bone, and he heard the ribs go. Two of them. The seventh and the eighth, at the side, where the old cut was, with a sound like green sticks breaking inside a sack. The breath went out of him all at once, and his left lung, on that side, under the broken ribs, did not want to take it back in. Something hot and wet opened under the mail, the old cut tearing, and he felt the blood start, warm, down his side under the wool.

[gasps] He went down on one knee on the ice.

The scrap-iron sword was still in his hand. He did not know how. The wolfhound was [dog snarling] roaring somewhere behind him. Aksel Keld was standing over him with the iron fist drawn back again, black against the white, and his narrow black eyes were quite calm, and Muken looked up at the fist and saw the gold in it, all the fine lines running through the iron and the tusk and the sinew, and one of them, at the base of the second knuckle, where the rivet went through, very slightly brighter than the rest.

[narrator, slowly] He could not breathe.

The white closed in round the edges of his eyes.

[barely audible] And the world went away.

---

### IX · The Bound Flame

[narrator, softly, awed] He was standing in a place made of threads.

He knew it was a place because he was standing in it, and he knew he was standing because his feet were on something, though when he looked down there was nothing there but more threads, running away from under his boots in every direction, crossing and crossing again, fine as hair, gold and silver and black, rose and sea-green and the violet of the northern sky, and colors he had no names for. They ran up into the dark above him and down into the dark below and out to every side, so many that the dark between them was only a kind of absence, and every one of them was humming. Very faintly. Each at its own note. The sound of it was like the sound of six hundred people breathing in the cold, or four thousand, or every person who had ever lived, all at once, very far away.

[narrator] His ribs did not hurt.

He put his hand to his side, under the coat. There was no blood. There was no coat. He was wearing nothing he could name, and he was cold and not cold, and he could breathe again, all the way down, and the breath went out of him and did not go up in a plume.

*[Northern English accent, low and rough, inner voice]I'm dead,* he thought. *Aksel Keld's hit me again and I'm dead.*

"[resonant, otherworldly, slow, barely audible]No," said a voice. "Not yet."

[narrator] It came from in front of him. Far away, and near. It was the quietest thing he had ever heard. But every thread in that place stopped humming for the length of a breath when it spoke, and then started again, a little lower, as though they were listening.

He looked up.

[slowly] There was a figure among the threads.

It was kneeling. That was the first thing he understood about it, and for some while it was the only thing. It knelt the way a man kneels under a load too heavy to carry standing, with one knee down and the other drawn up and its head bowed and its shoulders set, and the load was the threads. They ran into it and out of it. They came down on its bowed back and its shoulders from the whole of the dark above, more of them than Muken could count, more than there were stars, and they ran down through it and out of it into the dark below, and it held them. It held all of them. It knelt there in the middle of the place made of threads with the whole weight of them on its back, and did not move, and did not fall.

It was burning. A heavy warm light that came off it the way warmth comes off a banked fire, steady and low, gold and white, and as Muken looked at it he found that he wanted, more than he had ever wanted anything, to tell it the truth. Every truth. Every lie he had ever told, to a sergeant or a paymaster or a factor at the top of a shaft or his sister, rose up in his throat like water in a well, and he had to close his teeth on them.

It had wings.

Two great curved shapes of the same warm light rose from its shoulders and folded down around it to the ground, held close and low the way a man holds his arms in against a load, and every long feather of them was bent, very slightly, under something Muken could not see. And the figure itself, now that he looked, was bowed under that same weight from the crown of its head to the knee on the ground, and steady, and not moving at all.

*It's holding something up,* thought Muken. *Something enormous. And it has been holding it a long time.*

"[resonant, otherworldly, slow]Six," said the voice. "Into six. This is one."

[narrator] The bowed head lifted.

It did not have a face. It had where a face would be, and in that place there was only the warm light, steady and low, and two places in it that were brighter, where eyes would be. They looked at him. He felt them look at him. He felt them go into him the way the Shingan went into other men, and find every line, and every crack, and every place where he was tired.

"[resonant, otherworldly, slow]You are the boy with the eye," it said. "The crack-reader. The red line's last."

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, quiet, awed]Who are you?"

"I am called Faisal," said the kneeling figure. "The Grand Angel of Servitude. The Bound Flame. He who kneels at the edge of creation." The light moved very slightly, as if something under it had smiled. "I kneel here, in the place where the threads run, under the weight the Bell can no longer hold, and I do not get up. It is a small kingdom. It is the one I chose."

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, quiet]Why am I here?"

"[resonant, otherworldly, slow, gentle]Because you were falling," said Faisal. "And I do not let things fall. It is the only thing I was ever made to do." The bright places where eyes would be did not move from him. "And because you swore an oath, two nights ago, on your honor, by a woodpile, in front of six hundred people. I heard it. I hear every oath that is sworn on honor, even here, even under this. I heard it, and it was true. Every word of it was true." The light warmed. "Do you know how rarely I hear that? A king, swearing, and every word of it true?"

[narrator] Muken did not answer.

"[resonant, otherworldly, slow]I will teach you one thing," said Faisal. "Only one. There is not time for more, and you could not carry more. Listen."

[narrator] The threads hummed.

"[resonant, otherworldly, slow]You read cracks," said the kneeling figure. "You have always read them. You think it is a gift of your blood. Every bearer learns it first, because a crack is where the weight shows. Everything that carries a load carries it imperfectly, and the imperfection is where the load collects. In stone. In iron. In bone. In a host of riders on the ice. In a man." One great wing moved slightly under the weight. "You see where the weight has gathered, and you have learned to strike there. That is a mercenary's use of it. It is a good use. It has kept you alive."

"[Northern English accent, low and rough]But," said Muken.

"[resonant, otherworldly, slow]But you have only ever looked," said Faisal. "Listen, now. Every made thing has a note. Every thread in this place is humming at its own. So is every stone and every blade and every bone in your body and his. You have heard it all your life, and never known you were hearing it. When you strike a thing, you are only knocking on its door. If you hold its note instead. If you sing it back to it, through the steel, the way a voice holding the note of a glass will break the glass, then the weight that has gathered in its crack will carry the crack forward for you, faster than any eye, and the thing will open along its own flaw. It will break itself, because you have shown it where it was already broken."

[narrator] The light dimmed and steadied.

"[resonant, otherworldly, slow, solemn]Hear me," said Faisal. "Your father thought it was strength. Your father struck everything he met as hard as he could, and he broke a great deal, and in the end he broke himself, and your mother with him. This is listening. You find the note of a thing, and you hold it, and you wait, and the thing tells you where it will open." The bowed head inclined, slowly. "The word for it in your house's old tongue is *shake*. *Fure*. Your grandfathers' grandfathers knew it. It was taken from your line in the cellars at Halcrest with everything else. I am giving it back."

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, blurts out]Why?"

[narrator] The question came out of him before he could stop it. In that warm light, under those eyes, he could not have stopped it.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, bitter]Why give it to me? You don't know me. I'm Gimbzo's son."

[narrator, long pause] The kneeling figure did not answer at once. The threads hummed. Far away, very faint, Muken thought he heard something that might have been ice booming, or a dog roaring, or a girl's voice saying *[young girl, Northern English accent, barely audible]please be careful.*

"[resonant, otherworldly, slow, tender]I kneel," said Faisal at last, "because I must never fall. That is what I am. Kneeling, under the weight, so that the weight does not come down on what is beneath it." The light pulsed, once, very softly. "You knelt your whole life, child. Under the salt. Under the factor's ledger. Under your mother's dying and your sister's hunger. You carried it, and you did not fall, and you never once asked anybody to carry it for you." The bright places where its eyes would be held him. "Gimbzo never did that. That is yours."

[narrator] The threads began to tremble.

"[resonant, otherworldly, slow, urgent]Go back," said Faisal. "He is about to strike again. Find the note. Hold it. And when you swear, swear by your honor, as the Stark taught you." The light began to dim, and the place made of threads began to dim with it, going gray at the edges, going white. "I will be listening."

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, quiet]Will I see you again?"

"[resonant, otherworldly, slow, fading]If you live," said the Bound Flame, from very far away, "and if you keep your word. I kneel a very long way from anywhere. But I do not forget."

[narrator] And the threads went white.

---

[narrator, rushed] He was on the ice.

He was on one knee in the trampled snow with the scrap-iron sword in his hand and blood running warm down his left side under Ulf Stark's mail, and his broken ribs grinding when he tried to breathe, and the white of the lifting standing round the little cleared ring like a wall. Aksel Keld was standing over him. The iron fist was drawn back, black against the white, and coming down.

[narrator] Hardly any time had passed. The fist was still at the top of its arc. He saw it, and through the Shingan he saw the gold in it, every fine line through the iron and the tusk and the sinew, and the one at the base of the second knuckle, where the rivet went through, brighter than the rest.

[slowly] And now, for the first time in his life, he could hear it.

A note. Very high, very thin. The note of that black iron hand, of all the thousand blows it had struck and taken, humming in it like a wire in the wind. It had always been there. He had always heard it. He had never once known that he was hearing.

[narrator, slowly] Muken drew in breath. In through the nose, until the broken ribs screamed. Out through the mouth, long and slow, until there was nothing left. The Kharven breath. Yusif's breath, in the back of a worked-out gallery with a stick of ash in his hands.

[sword ringing] The scrap-iron sword, in his hand, began to ring.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, slowly, quiet]*Fure,*" said Muken.

---

# Chapter Fourteen: The Seat Road

[narrator] *Book One of the Kōkan · Chapter Fourteen: The Seat Road*

### I · The Note of Everything

[slowly, awed] It was as if the world came apart into pieces, and every piece was singing.

The ice sang. He heard it under his knee, through the trampled snow, a note so low he felt it as a weight, the whole frozen Skeldmere humming over the drowned lake beneath it like the string of a great bow drawn and held. The white of the lifting sang, high and thin and wavering, a thousand points of frost hanging in the air each at its own pitch, each one catching the hidden sun and throwing it back in sparks of blue and rose. His own body sang, the broken ribs a ragged discord on his left side, his heart under them a drum, the scrap-iron sword in his hand a long clear ringing note that went up his arm into his teeth and did not stop.

And Aksel Keld sang.

All of him. The short dense body in its two coats and its horn lamellar sewn between them, every plate of it at its own pitch. The leather straps. The bone in his forearm. The birch handle of the axe in his left hand. And the iron fist, drawn back at the top of its arc against the white, black and heavy, singing a high thin wiry note of a thousand blows struck and taken, and in the middle of that note, at the base of the second knuckle where the rivet went through, a flaw in the song. A small sour place. A crack, humming.

It was a swirl. It was a weave. It was a harmony so vast that his mind could not hold the shape of it, only the parts, the way a man in a storm can hold a single raindrop and not the rain. And for one breath, kneeling on the ice with his blood running warm under his mail, Muken understood that it had always been there. Every fight. Every ditch. Every gallery under Halcrest. The world had always been singing, and he had always been reading the cracks in the song, and he had never once known it was a song.

[Northern English accent, low and rough, inner voice] *Find the note. Hold it.*

[narrator] The fist came down.

Muken stepped off its line.

He did not decide to. His feet did it, the way Yusif had always told him they would if he stopped thinking. He came up off his knee and turned his body a quarter, left shoulder back, and the iron fist went past his face so close he felt the wind of it on his cheek and heard it, singing, a hand's width from his ear. And as it went past, as the black iron hand dropped through the place where his head had been, he laid the flat of the ringing scrap-iron blade against it.

He only touched it, the way you lay a finger on a bell.

The note of the blade went into the iron hand.

It found the sour place at the base of the second knuckle as if it had been looking for it all its life. For half a breath nothing happened. The hand went on past, Aksel's whole weight behind it, carrying him forward and down. And then the iron sang back. It sang the note of the scrap-iron blade, high and clear, louder and louder, the sound of it climbing out of the black metal into the white air like a kettle coming to the boil, and the crack at the rivet heard it.

The crack ran.

[rushed] It ran faster than an eye could follow. It went out from the second knuckle along the line it had been waiting a thousand blows to follow, through the riveted plate across the back of the hand and down into the finger-joints and up into the wrist-cuff, branching, branching again, and every place it went the iron opened along its own grain as if it had been sawn. The rivets sheared. The walrus-tusk inlay split with a sound like a dry log in a fire. The sinew cords that worked the fingers, drawn tight to the shoulder, parted one after another, snapping back up the sleeve, and the great black articulated fist that had killed more men than any spear on the tundra came apart in the air in seven pieces and fell around Aksel Keld's feet in the snow.

[long pause] Aksel stared at the stump of his arm.

[narrator] The leather cuff was still strapped to it. The stump inside it, the old wrist-end a Tenrai sword had made when he was thirteen, was bare and pale and ridged with old scar in the cold air, and the cuff's end was ragged where the iron had been. Nothing else was left. He stared at it with his narrow black eyes gone wide, and for one breath the Iron Fist of the Keld looked exactly as young as he was.

Muken swept the scrap-iron sword forward, low, at his belly.

It was a long flat cut, the whole length of the blade, and Aksel saw it coming and threw himself back from it. He was fast. Even now, with his iron hand lying in pieces in the snow, he was fast. The edge went past his gut by a finger's width, and opened the outer sealskin coat across the belly in a long gaping lip, and the horn plates of the lamellar under it showed through the cut, pale and curved and stitched in rows like the scales of a fish. Aksel staggered back two paces on the trampled snow, his short legs going wide for balance, the axe coming up in his left hand.

Muken did not stop there.

He went after him. He came in off his back foot as hard and fast as he had ever moved in his life, his broken ribs screaming and the blood running down his side and the white of the lifting going gray at the edges, and he did not lift the sword. The axe was coming up between them, and the sword would have had to go round it, and there was no time to go round anything. So he dropped his head and drove the crown of it, forehead first, the whole weight of his body behind it, straight into Aksel Keld's face.

He felt the nose go under his forehead. The bridge of it, the thin bones between the eyes. He felt it give and spread like a crushed egg. Blood burst across his brow and into his eyes and down his face, hot, salt, and [grunts] the sound Aksel made was a thick wet grunt, as if all the air in him had been punched out through his face. The back of the Keld's head snapped back. His eyes rolled up white for an instant.

Muken's own head rang like a struck pot. The world swung. For a moment he did not know where the ice was.

Aksel fell.

He went over backward with his short legs folding under him and landed flat on his back on the trampled snow, hard, with a sound like a sack of wet grain dropped off a cart. The axe went out of his left hand and skittered away across the ice. His arms went wide. His face was a mask of blood from the brow down, the nose spread flat and the lips split, and he lay with his narrow black eyes open and blinking up at the white sky, and did not get up.

[pause] Muken stood over him.

He reversed the scrap-iron sword in his hand. He did not think about it. He took it by the blade, below the guard, with the edge biting into his palm through the old callus, and he lifted it over his head like a hammer, and he brought the pommel down on Aksel Keld's chest.

The blade was still ringing.

The pommel hit the lamellar over the breastbone, through the cut sealskin, and the ring went into the horn plates as it had gone into the iron hand, and found every old crack in every plate at once. He heard them go. A crackle like ice breaking on a puddle under a boot, spreading out from the place he had struck in a ring, plate after plate. And under the plates, through them, the blow itself went on, the weight of his whole body behind the pommel, into the bone beneath. He felt the sternum give. The ribs either side of it, at the front, where they joined the breastbone by their cords of gristle, popped away from it one after another like buttons off a coat.

He lifted the sword and brought it down again.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, shouts]*This is for the people!*"

[narrator] He did not know he was going to shout it. It came up out of him from somewhere under the broken ribs and the salt and the stair and the long room, in a voice that was not the gallery voice at all, raw and cracked and enormous.

The pommel came down. The horn plates broke.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, roaring]*For Harrowmere!*" The pommel came down. "*For Tullock! For Brannick ford!*" Down. "*For every child in every long house who ate bark because of Tenrai seed grain!*" Down. "*For the carts! For the levies! For the salt! For her lungs!*" Down. "[screaming]*For everything!*"

[narrator, quietly] Aksel Keld did not fight back.

He did not lift his arm. He did not try to roll away. He lay on his back on the ice with his ruined face turned up to the white and his stump lying in the snow at his side, and took it, every blow, and his narrow black eyes were open the whole time, looking up at the boy with the white hair and the blood on his brow standing over him with the sword reversed in his hands. There was no fear in them. Something else was there, something that might, in another face, at another time, have been recognition.

Muken roared.

It had stopped being a word. It was the sound the wolfhound had made in the long room, low and continuous, coming up out of the bottom of his chest and going on and on, and the adrenaline was going through him like fire through dry grass, so that he could not feel his ribs or his split palm or the blood in his eyes, only the hammer-weight of the sword in his hands going up and coming down, up and down, and the singing of the world all round him so loud that it drowned out everything.

[long pause] Then he stopped.

He did not know why. He did not decide to. He stood over the Iron Fist of the Keld with the sword lifted for another blow and the pommel slick with blood and fragments of horn, and the roar ran out of him like water out of a broken pot, and his arms would not bring the sword down again.

[Northern English accent, low and rough, inner voice, shaken] *He's not fighting back,* he thought. *He stopped fighting back the moment the hand came apart. He's lying there and letting me.*

[quietly, unfamiliar inner voice] *He always stopped,* said something in him, in a voice he did not know. *A beaten clan was governed. Not humiliated.*

[narrator] He did not know where it came from. He let the sword down slowly until the point touched the snow.

Aksel Keld's chest was moving. Badly. A short, shallow, catching breath, with a wet sound in it at the end, and a bubble of pink froth at the corner of his split mouth that grew and burst and grew again. The front of the lamellar over his chest had caved in like the lid of a crushed box. His eyes were still open. They were still on Muken's face.

"[Icelandic accent, flat, rough, young man, whispers]So," said Aksel Keld.

[narrator] It came out of him as a whisper, thick with blood.

"[Icelandic accent, flat, rough, young man, whispers, wet, faintly amused]That's... what you'd have offered." The split lips moved in something that was nearly a smile. [pause] "At my fire."

[narrator] Muken stood over him and breathed. In through the nose, until his own broken ribs ground together. Out through the mouth. The world was still singing round him, but more quietly now, the harmony drawing back to the edges like a tide going out, and the ringing in the sword had dropped to a hum.

He turned the sword round in his hand. He wiped the pommel on his thigh. He sheathed it.

The blade went into the scabbard with a sound he had never heard it make before, a grinding catch halfway down, as if something in the steel had shifted. He did not look at it.

All around them, out in the white, the Keld were scattering. He could hear it. The thousand riders of the wedge, the ones who had gone on past toward the Stark ring, had turned somewhere out in the lifting when the iron hand came apart, as though they had felt it go, and he could hear their hooves going away in every direction across the frozen lake, east and north and west, the drumming of the ice under them fading as they went. [Icelandic accent, quietly] *Take the head,* every Keld captain was taught, *and the herd scatters.*

[narrator] The herd was scattering.

Muken stood in the little cleared ring of trampled snow with the Iron Fist of the Keld broken at his feet, and the wolfhound came up beside him with blood on its muzzle that was not its own, and pressed its great gray shoulder against his leg, and for the space of three breaths, in the white, in the silence after the singing, he thought it was over.

[pause] Then he heard the horse.

---

### II · The Shore

It was Mark Stark.

He came out of the white from the west, from the shore, on a lathered Stark bay that was going lame in the near fore, and he was riding with one arm. The other hung at his side with a black-glass arrow standing out of the meat of the upper arm, through the hide-coat and the mail, the shaft snapped off short. There was blood all down that side of him. His face was the color of the snow.

He pulled the bay up so hard it nearly went down, and looked at Muken, and looked at the body on the ice, and did not seem to see it.

"[Yorkshire accent, desperate, breathless]*They've taken her,*" said Mark Stark.

[narrator] Muken did not move.

"[Yorkshire accent, voice cracking]Yukazuri," said Mark. His voice cracked on it. "[rushed, ragged]On the shore. The surgeons' line. They came out of the lifting, gray men, a score of them, more, on southern horses. We never saw them till they were in among the carts." He swayed in the saddle and caught himself on the pommel with his good hand. "[breathless]They killed the Greymane guard. All of them. Set fire to the carts. Took her and put her in a carriage, a closed thing on runners, Tenrai black and gold, it was waiting on the shore road the whole time, Sky knows how long, and they're going south with her. On the Seat road." He breathed. "[voice breaking]I tried. I'm sorry. I tried."

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, very quietly]Where," said Muken.

[narrator] It came out of him very quietly. Mark Stark stared at him.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, roaring]*Where is she?*"

[narrator] That was not quiet. That was the roar again, all of it, coming up out of the bottom of him and tearing his throat, and the wolfhound beside him flinched from it, and Mark Stark's lame bay threw up its head and shied. The world came back. The singing came back, all at once, everything humming and wailing and ringing in the white, and every note in it was wrong.

[Northern English accent, low and rough, inner voice, frantic] *She was at the Foot. She was at the Foot behind the Greymane wall with her rows of linen and her salve tins, counting. She was at the Foot.*

[narrator] He knew why she had not stayed at the Foot. He knew it before Mark finished. He knew it the way he knew the shape of his own hand. The arrows had come out of the white and the Starks had started falling, forty, fifty, and a man with a black-glass arrowhead in his gut or a leaf-headed lance through his chest did not live long enough to be carried two hours up the Kessen road to a surgeons' tent. So she had brought the tent to them. She had loaded her linen and her gut and her needles into the carts and come down the road with the Greymane guard Godfrey would have given her, twenty men, because Godfrey could never in his life have refused her, and she had set up her line on the shore of the Skeldmere behind the Stark ring, in the snow, close enough to hear the screaming, so that the men who fell could be carried to her in minutes and not hours.

[Northern English accent, low and rough, inner voice, anguished] *Of course she did. Of course she did. That's who she is.*

[narrator, slowly] And Karogane had known she would.

Muken understood that too, standing there on the ice with the roar still in his throat. He understood it the way he had understood the gold lines in the Keld host. The Tenrai agent had never come to the Skeldmere to break the boy with a sentence. The carter had told him in the war-lodge at dawn that the sentence was spent, and Karogane had said *it changes nothing,* and he had been right, because the sentence had never been the plan. It had only been the first plan. The second plan had been sitting on the shore road in a closed carriage on runners since before the gray came up, waiting for the lifting, waiting for the king of the north to be busy somewhere out on the ice, waiting for the one person in the world he would turn his whole army round for.

[Northern English accent, low and rough, inner voice, cold] *The red line's last. Both of us. Not one.*

*He doesn't need to kill me. He only needs her.*

[narrator, rushed] Muken was already moving.

He went past Mark Stark at a run, across the trampled snow, and caught the reins of the Hollenmark bay with the one white foot where it stood waiting, and hauled himself up into the saddle with his broken ribs grinding like millstones and a sound coming out of his mouth he did not hear. The wolfhound was running before he had turned the horse. He put his heels in. The bay went.

[slowly] Behind him, on the ice, the Iron Fist of the Keld lay on his back in the snow with his chest caved in, breathing his short pink-frothed breaths, and watched him go.

---

### III · The Grey Men

[narrator] He came out of the lifting onto the shore of the Skeldmere and the world was burning.

[fire crackling] The surgeons' carts were burning. Six of them, drawn up in a half-ring on the snow above the ice with their shafts down, the way Yukazuri had always drawn up her line, a wall against the wind, and every one of them was alight from end to end. The linen was burning, the clean linen in its rows, and the boiled cloth in its tubs had boiled dry and the tubs were burning too, and the spirit had gone up in a blue roar that had blackened the snow for ten paces round. The flames stood up orange and gold against the white, so bright after the lifting that they hurt to look at, and the snow round them had gone the color of a forge mouth. The smoke went up thick and greasy into the white and lay along the shore. The smell was burning cloth and burning wood and burning spirit and, under it, something else.

The Greymane guard lay in the snow among the carts.

Twenty men. Muken did not count them; he knew the number Godfrey would have sent. They lay where they had stood, in their gray cloaks, in a rough ring round the place where the surgeons' tent had been, most of them on their backs, most of them with their spears still in their hands. Some had been cut down from horseback, the long downward strokes of a cavalry sword that open a man from the collarbone to the breastbone. Some had been shot. One, near the tent, lay on his face with his own spear through him from behind, the point standing out between his shoulder blades, and his arms folded under him as though he had been holding them that way when he died.

Muken knew that man. He had seen him at the woodpile. A Greymane spearman near the gate, with his arms folded and his face like a shut door, who had stood when the yard knelt.

[Northern English accent, low and rough, inner voice, bitter] *He let them in,* Muken thought. *And they paid him the way the Tenrai pay.*

[narrator] The wounded from the Stark ring lay where they had been carried, in a row in the lee of the carts. Some of them were still alive. Some of them were screaming. A Stark boy with no left hand was sitting up in the snow holding the stump against his chest and rocking, and looked up as Muken came past, and his mouth opened, and nothing came out of it.

Among the burning carts, the gray men were waiting.

There were eight of them. They had been left. Muken saw it at once, the way he saw the gold lines: a rear-guard, set across the mouth of the shore road where it left the Skeldmere and went south through the low drifts toward the Seat, to hold it long enough. Long enough for what, he could see over their heads. A dark shape on the road, half a mile south and going away, a long black box on runners drawn by four horses at the gallop, with riders round it, going into the white.

[Northern English accent, low and rough, inner voice, cold] *There.*

[narrator] The gray men saw him coming. They spread out across the road. They did it well, with no word spoken, the way Karogane moved, every step measured. They were dismounted, their horses held behind them by a ninth man, and they had cavalry swords in their hands, long and slightly curved, Tenrai steel with the gold worked so fine into the guards that you could only see it in the firelight. Plain gray cloaks. Hoods up. No faces.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, roaring]*Where is she?*" Muken roared at them.

[narrator] They did not answer. They came at him.

The first two came together, from either side of the bay's head, the way a pair of hounds come at a stag. He was off the horse before they reached it. He did not remember getting off. He was on the snow with the scrap-iron sword out of its scabbard in his hand, and it came out with that grinding catch again, halfway, as if the steel had swollen. He did not care. The first gray man was coming in from the left with his sword up for a cut at the head, and Muken did not parry it. He stepped in under it, inside the arc, close enough to smell the man's breath through the hood, and brought the scrap-iron sword round flat and low and up, two-handed, with his hips behind it, into the side of the man's neck below the hood.

The edge went in at the side of the throat, through the muscle there, the long cord of it that turns the head, and on. The blade was dull and pitted and worn thin, and it should not have gone through. It went through. It was still humming, very faintly, and wherever it went the flesh and the bone seemed to part ahead of it along lines that were already there. It went through the windpipe and the spine at the neck with a short hard grinding crunch, and out the other side, and the hood fell one way and the body fell the other.

The second gray man was already swinging. Muken turned on the ball of his foot with the dead man's blood still in the air between them and took the swing on the scrap-iron blade, edge to edge, and felt it bite into his own blade, deep, a notch the width of a finger, and he did not care about that either. He shoved. The gray man's sword slid off his. And Muken cut back across, high, at the place where the hood met the shoulder, and the head came off that one too.

It came off clean. It went up and turned once in the air with the gray hood still on it and came down on the burning cart behind, and lay there.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, roaring]*You will pay!*"

[narrator] He was roaring it at the cart, at the dark shape on the road, at the white, at all of them.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, roaring, raw]*You will pay for this! Every one of you!*"

[narrator] The other six came.

He met them in the smoke among the burning carts, and afterward he did not remember most of it. He remembered pieces. A sword coming at his face out of the smoke, and the scrap-iron blade coming up under it, and the sound the two met with, a high ugly shriek, and the pain in his hands. A gray man going down with his knee opened. The wolfhound with its jaws locked on a gray man's sword-arm, shaking him like a rat while he screamed, until the arm broke and the scream changed. Heat on his left side from a burning cart. Blood in his eyes again, someone's, his own. A cut along his right forearm he did not feel until later, through the coat and the skin to the meat, a long clean opening the length of his hand.

[slowly] And the scrap-iron sword, failing.

[narrator] He felt it go. It had been singing since the ice, since *Fure*, and every time it met a Tenrai blade the song in it went more sour. He had held its note too long. It had carried the cracks in the iron fist and the horn plates and the gray men's necks, and somewhere in all of that carrying it had found its own. He could hear it. A crack in the scrap-iron, near the guard, where the old blade had been forged and reforged a hundred times by men who did not care what it was. It was humming. It was running.

A gray man came in at him from the right with a lunge at his belly. Muken knocked it aside with the scrap-iron sword, and the scrap-iron sword broke.

It came apart along its own line, the way the iron fist had, the crack running up from the guard through the black pitted blade in a long jagged branching seam, and two-thirds of it fell off into the snow, and he was holding the hilt and a hand's length of broken steel.

He threw it at the gray man's face. The man flinched.

Muken went past him.

There was a Keld lance lying in the snow beside one of the burning carts. Birch, with a long leaf-shaped head, dropped by some Keld rider who had chased a Stark wounded this far and died for it. He snatched it up on the run. He turned with it. The gray man who had lunged was coming after him, and another behind him, and Muken did not set the lance or brace it. He ran at them with it held low in both hands like a hay-fork and drove it into the first gray man's belly under the ribs, through the cloak and the coat and whatever was under the coat, with all his weight and all his rage behind it, so far that the leaf head came out of the man's back and the birch shaft went in after it to the length of a hand, and the man folded over it and went down.

He let go of the lance.

The second gray man was on him. Muken caught the sword-wrist in his left hand as it came down, and held it, and the gray man was strong and the broken ribs on Muken's left side were grinding and his arm shook. His right hand went out by itself to the gray man's belt. There was a knife there. A long thin Tenrai dagger in a sheath of black lacquer. He drew it.

He drove it into the side of the gray man's head.

It went in above the ear, through the hood and the hair and the thin flat bone of the temple, to the hilt. The gray man's eyes went wide inside the hood. His sword hand opened. He stood there a moment, held up by Muken's grip on his wrist, with the black lacquer hilt standing out of the side of his head, and then Muken let go, and he went down in the snow beside the burning cart, and did not move.

---

### IV · The Salve Tin

[narrator, quietly] Muken stood among the dead and the fire.

He stood there with his chest heaving and the breath going up off him in a great white plume into the greasy smoke, and his hands hanging at his sides, empty, and the blood all over him. In his hair. In his eyes. Down the front of the dead sergeant's coat and Ulf Stark's mail, so much of it that the brown wool had gone black and stiff with it and the rings of the mail were clotted. Some of it was his. Most of it was not. His broken ribs were a red roaring furnace on his left side and every breath went in short and came out with a catch, and his right forearm was laid open and dripping off his fingers into the snow, each drop landing bright as a rowan berry and spreading pink into the crust, and his split palm, where he had held the sword by the blade, had begun at last to hurt.

The carts burned round him. The wounded in the lee of the carts had gone quiet, or most of them. The wolfhound stood a little way off over the gray man whose arm it had broken, with its head low, and the gray man was not moving either.

He looked down.

There was something in the snow at his feet, among the trampled footprints and the blood, half under the edge of the burned-out cart nearest the road. Small and round and dull. He knew what it was before he bent for it. He would have known it anywhere.

[softly] Her salve tin.

[narrator] He picked it up. It was dented. The lid had been half pulled off, as though she had had it in her hand when they took her and held on to it until someone tore her fingers off it. The salve inside had gone hard and gray in the cold. And on the lid, scratched into the tin, freshly, with the point of something sharp, a needle, the scratches still bright against the dull metal, there was a mark.

The long down-stroke. The hook. The crossing. The small closed loop at the foot like a knot in a rope.

And under it, smaller, scratched hard and quick and crooked, an arrow. Pointing south.

[softly] She had left him a sign. She had stood on the shore of the Skeldmere with her carts burning round her and the Greymane guard dying in the snow and gray men coming for her out of the smoke, and she had taken the needle out of her apron pocket and scratched their name and the way they were taking her into the lid of her salve tin, and dropped it where he would find it. Because she had known he would come. Because she had never in her life doubted that he would come.

[narrator] Muken closed his hand on the tin until the dented edge of it cut into the split palm.

He looked up.

Half a mile south, on the shore road, the long black box on runners was still visible against the white, going away. It had slowed. The road climbed there, out of the low drifts round the Skeldmere toward the first of the hills, and the four horses were laboring on the slope. The riders round it had slowed too. And among them, at the back, on a tall gray southern horse that stood a hand higher than any of the others, one rider had turned in his saddle, and was looking back at the burning shore.

[long pause] Karogane.

Muken could not see his face at that distance. He knew him by the shape against the white, tall and lean, with the hood down, sitting the gray horse without a tremor, looking back. Looking at him.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, roaring]*You think you can run?*"

[narrator] The roar came out of him with everything he had left. It tore his throat. It went out across the burning shore and the trampled snow and the dead, out across the half-mile of white to the shore road, and he saw the tall gray rider hear it. He saw the head lift.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, roaring]*You think you can run from me?*"

[narrator] The gray rider did not move.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, roaring, furious]*I will not stand for this!*" Muken was walking now, then running, out past the burning carts onto the shore road with the salve tin clenched in his bleeding hand and no weapon at all. "[screaming]*Do you hear me, Tenrai? I will not stand for it! If you do anything to her, anything, if you touch one hair on her head, you will regret it! You will regret it for the rest of your life, and I will make it a very short one!*"

[narrator] Out on the hill, the tall gray rider turned his horse away, unhurried. He turned his back on the burning shore and the boy running on the road, and touched his heels to the tall gray horse, and rode on up the slope after the black carriage, and did not look back again.

Muken ran after him.

He ran for twenty paces on the frozen road, with his broken ribs grinding and the blood from his forearm spattering on the snow and the wolfhound bounding at his side, before Lorn Stark caught him.

---

### V · The Battle Is Won

[narrator] Lorn came up on him from behind at a gallop and threw himself off his horse before it had stopped and caught Muken round the chest with both arms, from behind, and held on.

He had to hold on hard. Muken fought him. He did not know it was Lorn. He did not know anything. He twisted in the Stark's grip and drove his elbow back into Lorn's ribs and tried to throw him, roaring, and Lorn took the elbow with a grunt and did not let go, and set his feet on the frozen road the way his father had taught him, weight straight down through both heels, and held.

"[Yorkshire accent, young, rough, shouts]*Muken!*"

[narrator] The broken ribs ground together under Lorn's arms. Muken screamed. It went through the rage and came out the other side, and for a moment he went slack in the Stark's grip, and Lorn shifted his hold, and did not let go.

"[Yorkshire accent, young, rough, urgent, close]*Muken. Stop.*" Lorn's voice was rough and very close to his ear. "*Stop. We can't. Not like this.*"

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, desperate]*She's on that road...*"

"[Yorkshire accent, young, rough, shaking]*I know she's on that road.*" Lorn's arms were shaking. There was blood on his face from the cut that had nearly taken his ear, and more of it on the arms locked round Muken's chest. "*I know. I saw the carts. I saw the Greymanes.*" He breathed. "[pleading]*But look at you. Look at yourself. You've no sword. You've no horse under you. You've two ribs stove in and you're bleeding out of your arm like a stuck pig, and they've thirty men round that carriage and an hour's start, and if you run up that road after them on your feet, you'll die in the snow on it before noon. And then she's got nobody.*"

[narrator] Muken said nothing. He was breathing in short, catching gasps. The salve tin was still in his fist.

"[Yorkshire accent, young, rough, quietly]We've won," said Lorn Stark.

[narrator] He said it more quietly. He did not loosen his arms.

"[Yorkshire accent, young, rough, quiet, insistent]We've already won the battle. Do you hear me? It's over. The Keld are running. All of them, every way there is off the ice. Thornwall's got the north side and Ashmore's got the south and the Sennet clan's turned on the Haldorssons and they're killing each other over the Teeth pastures like their grandfathers did." His breath was hot on Muken's neck. "The Iron Fist's lying on his back on the Skeldmere with his chest stove in, and his herd's scattered, and there's four thousand Keld riders who'll be telling their children for fifty years about the boy who broke the iron hand with a song." He swallowed. "[gently]You did that. We won. It's done."

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, whispers]*She's not done.*"

[narrator] It came out of Muken in a whisper. It was barely a voice at all.

"[Yorkshire accent, young, rough, softly]No," said Lorn. "She's not." He was quiet a moment. "She's my king's sister. She closed the cut on my brother Rob's leg at the Foot with her own hands while he screamed at her, and then she sat with him till he slept." His arms tightened.
