"[Northern English accent, low and rough, slowly]One day," he said. "And then all will be revealed. And all of this will come to an end." His gray eyes did not move from their faces. "The Tenrai. The Shirogane. The silver chair. All of it."

[narrator] The fire ticked in the hearth.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough]I must find myself," said Muken. "First. Before anything. I've been cutting at nothing in the snow for two days and the world's been cracking under my feet, and I don't yet know how to stop it. I have to learn. And I have to heal." He touched his strapped side, once, very lightly. "I can't take the Seat with two ribs stove in and an arm that won't close. Before the battle, I have to be whole." He was quiet a moment. "Or as whole as I'm going to be."

"[soft Scottish accent, measured, quietly]Sire," said Godfrey quietly. "The Seat's three days' march. The Tenrai have the Hall and the cliff and the conduits, and whatever's left of the Keld. They'll have had a week to wall themselves in by the time we reach them. And they'll know we're coming. They sent her down the river to make sure of it." He did not look away. "Forgive me. But how can you know we'll win?"

[narrator] Muken held his eyes.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, calm]Because the tides of fate have shown me," he said.

[narrator] He said it quite simply. He did not raise his voice. He said it the way another man might have said *because it's going to snow*.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, steady]I've seen it," said Muken. "I don't know how else to say it to you. Every crack in the Seat. Every flaw in their walls and their wards and their oaths. Every Tenrai lord who's stood behind Kagenori these twenty years and hated every minute of it. They're humming, all of them. I can hear them from here." His eyes went, very briefly, to the window, and south, toward the ridge and the white country and the road. "The Seat is already broken, Godfrey. It's been broken since the night she died. It only hasn't fallen yet." He looked back. "We will win. I'm not asking you to believe it. I'm telling you what I've seen."

[narrator, long pause] The long room was silent.

Kiyome Byakuya was staring at him.

She had heard a great many Moto speak in her life. She had heard them in the Hall of Verdicts and the white house and the councils of the seven lines, lords and ladies and heirs, arguing and threatening and lying and bargaining. She had heard her grandmother speak over the lamp at the hour of the evening bell, the oath of the white line, in the old high register of the house that the children were taught before they could walk. And once, long ago, across a field under a flag of truce, she had heard the voice of a Kōkan king.

She had never heard anything like this.

The words were simple. The weight was in the thing under them. The way every one of them seemed to settle into the air of the room and stay there, the way a stone settles into snow. It was the voice of a man who was not telling you what he wanted to happen, or what he hoped would happen, or what he would make happen if he could. He was telling you what was going to happen, because he had seen it, the way you would tell a man the river was frozen because you had walked across it.

[light Japanese accent, calm, warm, inner voice, awed] *That is a Moto,* thought Kiyome Byakuya. *That is a Moto speaking. The first I have heard in my life. Seven lines forgive us, that is what we were supposed to sound like.*

[narrator] She did not say it. She could not have said it.

She looked at Tetsugen. The old Akagane lord was staring at the boy from under his thick gray brows with his heavy face gone slack with something near wonder, and his scarred hands had come off his belt. She looked at Hayato. Hayato's lips were moving slightly, as if he were trying to work out the distance to something.

"[Yorkshire accent, young, rough, blunt]I don't understand," said Lorn Stark.

[narrator] He said it roughly, from the door, without shame.

"[Yorkshire accent, young, rough]I'll be honest with you, sire, I don't understand a word of that. Tides of fate." He shook his head slowly. "But I'll have the vanguard ready in a day. And whatever you need. Name it."

"[Northern English accent, low and rough]I need you to keep everyone out of the death-house," said Muken.

[narrator] They all looked at him.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough]The death-house," said Muken. "It's empty now. It's the coldest room in the Foot and the quietest, and nobody goes into it unless somebody's died." He breathed. "I'm going in there. And I need nobody to come in after me until I come out, whatever they hear, whatever they see under the door. Not you, Lorn, nor Godfrey, nor my aunt." He looked at Kiyome. "Not even if I stop breathing."

"[soft Scottish accent, measured, worried]Sire..." said Godfrey.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough]Not even then," said Muken. "There's someone I have to go and see. And it isn't anywhere you can follow."

[narrator] He looked at them all once more. Then he turned, and walked out of the long room, past Borin Ironheart, who was standing by his anvil with his coat off and his great arms folded and seven needles still closed in one fist, watching him go with his slate eyes narrowed. And across the yard. And into the death-house in the north wall, where the sun never reached and the stone shelf at the back was empty now, with only a faint dark stain on it where she had lain. And he shut the door behind him.

[pause] They heard the bar go down on the inside.

---

### IV · The Forge

[narrator] Borin Ironheart forged Tsugigane through that day and that night and the next, and the Foot did not sleep.

They had never seen anything like it. Nobody in the north had. The Stark armorer, who had been beating out horseshoes and mail-rings at Hollenmark for thirty years and thought himself a fair smith, stood in the door of the long room for the first hour with his mouth open, and then went away and sat on the woodpile with his head in his hands, and then came back and asked, humbly, if he could work the bellows.

Borin let him. He let six of them, in shifts: the Stark armorer, two Greymane masons, Rob Stark, Tetsugen Akagane, who took off his copper coat and worked a bellows for four hours without stopping or speaking, and the boy with the red ears, who could not be made to go away. They worked the great bellows of the Foot's smithy, carried into the long room and set either side of the hearth, until their arms gave out and the next shift took over, and the fire in the hearth went from cherry red to a roaring orange to a yellow-white so bright that nobody could look at it, and stayed there, throwing the shadows of the bellows-men huge and leaping up the old Tenrai beams and turning every face in the room the color of new brass. It did not flicker. It wanted no tending. It simply burned at the heat the Dawi wanted, as though it knew.

He began with the three pieces of the old sword.

He laid them on the anvil, side by side, the hilt and the long broken middle and the point, and put his great granular hands on them, and closed his eyes, and stood like that for an hour without moving or speaking. The men at the bellows looked at each other.

"[light Japanese accent, calm, warm, quietly]He's listening," said Kiyome Byakuya quietly, from the doorway, where she had stood since he began. "A Dawi reads iron the way we read a face. He's finding out what it was before it was a sword."

[narrator] When he opened his eyes, he took the pieces off the anvil and put them in the fire.

He did not forge them. Not at first. He took the old scrap iron apart. He heated each piece until it glowed, deep cherry and then orange and then the yellow of a buttercup, and folded it, and beat it out, and folded it again, and watched the scale come off it in black flakes and the slag run out of it in bright spitting threads that skittered across the stone floor like shooting stars and winked out, and folded it again. Over and over. The men at the bellows lost count. The iron got smaller each time, and grayer, and quieter, and the sound it made under the hammer changed, from a dull clatter like a pot lid to a clear high ring like a bell.

"[deep Welsh accent, gravelly, musing]Bad scrap," said Borin, once, to no one, as he drew a glowing bar out of the fire. "Bad scrap, from a dozen different smelts. Some of it's older than this fort." He turned the bar in the tongs, looking at it. "Some of it's very old. Some of it's had blood in it more than once." He put it back in the fire. "It's all got to come out. Everything that isn't iron. Everything that was put in by men who didn't know what they were doing." He folded it again. "Then we'll see what's left."

[narrator] By midnight of the first day, what was left was a single bar of gray steel no longer than a man's forearm and no thicker than two fingers, ringing under the hammer like a struck glass.

"[Yorkshire accent, cheerful, hoarse, astonished]That's it?" said Rob Stark, at the bellows, staring at it. "That's all that was in it?"

"[deep Welsh accent, gravelly]That's all that was worth keeping," said Borin Ironheart. "Most things are mostly not."

[narrator] He laid the bar on the anvil and let it cool, and then he took the linen out of his shirt.

---

He did not let anyone watch the folding of the needles.

He sent the bellows-men out of the long room. He sent Kiyome out. He barred the door behind them, and the Foot stood in the yard in the snow and the dark with the light of the forge coming yellow-white through the cracks round the door and the narrow windows, and listened to the hammer.

It went on for six hours. It was slower than the earlier hammering, and lighter, and very even, a single stroke and then a long pause and then another, the rhythm of a man working at the very edge of what his hands could do and not daring to hurry. Sometimes there was a sound that was not hammering, a long hiss like water thrown on hot stone, or a high thin ringing that went on and on and made the old woman with no teeth, sitting on her door in the yard, put her hands over her ears. Once, near the fourth hour, the light through the cracks went out entirely, and the yard was dark, and nobody breathed, and then it came back, brighter.

And once, toward the end, they heard the Dawi speak. It was in no tongue they knew, low and harsh and very old, and it went on and on, and it sounded like a man arguing with something that was arguing back.

At the sixth hour the hammering stopped, and the door opened, and Borin Ironheart stood in it with the forge-light behind him, and he was gray to the lips.

"[deep Welsh accent, gravelly, exhausted]Bellows," he said. "Now. I'm going to quench it."

---

[narrator] He quenched it in the snow.

The Stark armorer quenched in water and the Moto smiths of the south in oil. Borin had them pack the long trough by the anvil with clean snow from the top of the curtain wall, where nobody had walked, a cartload of it, white and dry and so cold it smoked. And when the blade came out of the fire for the last time, long and slender and curved now like a crescent moon on its side, glowing the color of a winter sunrise from the spine to the edge, he did not plunge it. He laid it down in the snow.

All along its length, all at once.

[steam hissing] The sound it made was like nothing anyone in the long room had ever heard. A scream and a roar and a singing all together, and a great white cloud of steam that went up to the beams and filled the room, and through it, very faint, high and clear and sweet, a single note that went on after everything else had stopped. On and on. Quieter and quieter. Until it was only a hum, felt in the teeth and the long bones more than heard.

[narrator] When the steam cleared, Borin Ironheart was on his knees by the trough with both his hands flat on its edge and his head bowed, breathing like a man who had run up the ridge path with a stone in his arms.

Tetsugen Akagane went to him and put a hand under his arm. The Dawi let him.

"[light Japanese accent, flat, iron, deep]Is it done?" said Tetsugen.

"[deep Welsh accent, gravelly, exhausted, wry]It's done," said Borin Ironheart. "The hard part. Now it's only polishing and the hilt and the saya, and any fool can do those, and I'm the fool who's going to." He did not get up. "Give me a moment, Akagane. I've just argued with three hundred years of bad iron and a dead girl's needles and the boy's cursed blood, all at once, and I'm not as young as I was."

[narrator] Tetsugen did not let go of his arm.

"[light Japanese accent, flat, iron, deep]What did they say?" he said. "When you argued."

[narrator, long pause] The Dawi was quiet a long moment.

"[deep Welsh accent, gravelly, quiet, tired]The iron wanted to go back to sleep," said Borin. "Iron always does. The blood wanted to break things. It's very old, that blood. Very angry. It's been angry for a thousand years and it doesn't know why any more." He breathed. "And the needles." His great hands moved slightly on the trough. "The needles wanted to close things. That's all. They didn't argue at all. They just kept closing. Every crack I opened, every place the blood wanted to break, the needles went in after it and closed it." He lifted his head and looked at the blade lying in the snow, gray and plain and curved, with a single thin bright line running along it from the guard to a hand's width up the blade, where the old break had been, like a seam of silver in gray stone. "[softly]She's in there now. In the marrow. Where it broke." He looked away. "I've made a great many blades, Akagane. Kings' swords. Relics. Things the Guild would sell its own mothers for. I've never made one with a surgeon in it before."

[narrator] Tetsugen Akagane looked at the blade in the snow, with the copper-red of the forge-light moving on his scarred face.

Then he helped the Dawi up.

---

It took the rest of the night and the morning after for the tempering and the polishing and the fitting, and he did it all himself.

He drew the temper first, laying the blade spine-down over a bed of coals raked out from the hearth and turning it slowly, a finger's breadth at a time, with his eyes a hand from the steel. The colors came up out of the gray and ran along the spine like oil on water: pale straw first, then gold, then a deep bronze, then the purple of a ripe plum, and at the very back of the spine, where he let it go furthest, a clear peacock blue. He watched each color come and stopped it where he wanted it, and the edge he never let past straw. When he plunged it into the snow again, the colors stayed, a band of gold and bronze and blue along the back of the blade that the polishing would take off and that the bellows-men, who saw it, would talk about for the rest of their lives.

The hilt he bound in black rayskin and over it a wrap of plain dark red silk cord, the red of the red line, in the old diamond pattern that Kiyome Byakuya told him, standing at his elbow, the Kōkan had used. The guard he made from the old one, the iron guard of the scrap-iron sword, which had come off it in the breaking: he cleaned it and filed it and polished it until it was dark and plain and round, a simple disk with a single small hole worked through it, off-center, in the shape of a needle's eye. The collar was iron. The pommel cap was iron. There was no gold on it anywhere, and no silver, and no gem.

The saya he carved himself from a single length of black ash out of the Foot's woodpile, the long pale wall of split wood that ran along the barracks, because Muken had once said it had held his whole life up. He lacquered it black, seven coats, and dried each in front of the forge, and bound it at the mouth and the foot with iron. He tied the sageo, the cord that bound it to the belt, in white.

Kiyome had brought him the white cord. It was the cord from her own sword. The knot of her house.

"[deep Welsh accent, gravelly, gently]Are you sure," said Borin, holding it.

"[light Japanese accent, calm, warm, softly]It's his," said Kiyome Byakuya. "It was always his. I only kept it."

[narrator] The Dawi tied it on.

At dawn on the morning of the second day, he laid Tsugigane in its saya on a length of clean linen on the split pine table in the long room, in front of Shigure Tenrai's carved chair, where the scrap-iron sword had lain. He stood looking at it for a moment with his great arms folded and his slate eyes very tired.

"[deep Welsh accent, gravelly, tired, proud]There," said Borin Ironheart. "It's a legend, or it's nothing. And I don't make nothing."

[narrator] Then he lay down on the stone floor of the long room in front of the hearth, with his head on his anvil, and was asleep before anyone could bring him a blanket, and the Foot tiptoed round him for two days, and nobody woke him.

---

[narrator, quietly] On the other side of the yard, in the cold of the death-house in the north wall, the bar was still down on the door.

It had been down for a day and a night and a day. Nobody had gone in. Lorn Stark had sat with his back against the outside of the door the whole time, in the snow, in his father's mail, with his father's axe across his knees, and let nobody near it, and slept in snatches where he sat. Twice in the night he had heard something from inside. A voice, very low, that was not Muken's. [distant bell tolling] Once, toward the second dawn, a sound like a great bell being struck very far away.

[softly] And all that time, under the door, in the crack between the wood and the stone sill, there had been light.

There was no fire in the death-house. The light was like noon through cloud, steady and warm, with a thread of gold running in it, coming out under the door onto the snow of the yard, so that the snow there had never once frozen in all that time, and lay soft and wet, and in the soft wet snow, if you looked closely, very faint, there were the marks of fine branching cracks, coming and going, coming and going, like breath on a window.

---

# Chapter Seventeen: The Seven Works

[narrator] *Book One of the Kōkan · Chapter Seventeen: The Seven Works*

> *Before measured time, before chronology, and before the later unfolding of the epochs, Bara created the Works of the World within the Plane of Fate. These Works were not made as techniques or schools of sorcery, but as primal functions: the first operative laws by which existence could act, divide, endure, unravel, and complete itself.*
>
> *From* Hataraki: The Works of the Plane of Fate

---

### I · The Place Where the Threads Run

He sat down on the stone shelf at the back of the death-house, where she had lain, and put his back against the cold wall, and closed his eyes.

He did not know how to go. Faisal had never told him. The first time, on the ice, he had been falling, and the second time, in the yard, the Bound Flame had put a hand on his chest and pulled. Now there was nobody to fall for and nobody to pull, only the cold and the dark and the bar down on the door and the faint dark stain on the stone under his hand where she had lain for two days in Godfrey's cloak.

So he did what Yusif had taught him in the back of a worked-out gallery before every fight. He breathed. [slowly] In through the nose, slowly, until the strapped ribs on his left side ground together and the pain went up white behind his eyes. Out through the mouth, long, until there was nothing left in him at all. And again. And again. The Kharven breath.

And on the seventh breath, or the seventieth, he heard it.

The singing. Faint, at first, under the silence of the death-house, the way you hear the sea under a storm. The note of the stone shelf under him, low and patient and very old. The note of the wall at his back. The note of the bar across the door. The note of the snow in the yard outside, and the woodpile, and the curtain wall, and the ridge, and the cairn on the ridge with its pale stones. And under all of them, the long low humming of the world.

He listened to it. He did not reach for it. He did not try to hold any of it. He only listened, the way she had listened to a man's chest with her ear against his ribs, until the listening was all there was.

And then the dark of the death-house was full of threads.

They came up out of the stone shelf under him and down out of the dark above, gold and silver and black, fine as hair, more of them than there were stars, running away in every direction and humming each at its own note. The gold ones burned. The silver ones shone cold and clear, and the black ones had a sheen on them like a raven's wing, green and violet where the light slid along them. Where they crossed, they flared, small and bright, and the whole dark was strung with those crossings, near and far, deeper than the sky over the tundra on a winter night, as if someone had hung every aurora the north had ever seen on a loom and set it humming. He was not sitting on the shelf any more. He was not anywhere. He was standing, or floating, in the place made of threads, and his ribs did not hurt, and his breath did not go up in a plume.

Faisal was kneeling in front of him.

He was as he had been the first time, on the ice: bowed, one knee down and the other drawn up, with the whole weight of the threads coming down on his back and shoulders and running through him and out of him into the dark below, and holding it. The great wings folded low around him under the weight. The warm heavy light, gold and white, that made Muken want to say every true thing he knew.

But the light was dimmer than it had been. And at the edge of one of the great wings, where Muken in the white space had cracked the whole row of things in his rage, there was a place where the light did not reach any more. A small place. A missing piece.

"[resonant, otherworldly, slow]You came on your own," said Faisal.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough]You said you'd come when I needed it." Muken looked at the dark place on the wing. "[quiet, determined]I didn't want to wait."

"[resonant, otherworldly, slow, wry]No." The bright places where eyes would be did not move from him. "[softly]No, you never have." The bowed head inclined, very slightly. "Sit."

[narrator] Muken sat. There was nothing to sit on. He sat anyway, cross-legged, in the place made of threads, the way the Keld had sat at their fires on the bare packed snow, in front of the kneeling Bound Flame, and the threads ran through him and past him and hummed.

"[resonant, otherworldly, slow, amused]Your Dawi has begun," said Faisal. "I can hear the iron arguing with him from here." The light shifted, as if something under it had smiled. "He will win. He usually does. You have until he finishes."

"[Northern English accent, low and rough]How long is that, here?"

"[resonant, otherworldly, slow]Here," said Faisal, "there is no how long. The threads do not run in hours. Temporality is one Work among seven, and this is the place where the Works were written, before there was time to write them in." He was quiet a moment. "You may stay as long as you need. A day will pass at the Foot. It may be a great deal longer for you."

"[Northern English accent, low and rough]How much longer?"

"[resonant, otherworldly, slow, grave]As long as it takes," said the Bound Flame, "for you to learn what I can teach you, and for your bones to knit, and for you to be ready." The light did not waver. "[pause]Or for you to break. One of those."

[narrator] Muken looked at him.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, steady]Then teach me," he said.

---

### II · The Work of Ruins

They began with what he already knew.

Faisal lifted one hand from the weight he carried, and the threads in front of them parted, and in the space between them there was the row again: the stone, the bowl, the blade, the branch, the bone, the rope, the brick, the cup, hanging in the dark at the height of a seated man's eyes, each humming at its own note, each with its cracks.

"[resonant, otherworldly, slow]Again," said Faisal.

[narrator] Muken did it. Rope first. He found the sour wavering note of the rot in it and held only that, and waited, and the rope let go of itself along its own dead core and fell in two pieces into the dark. The bowl. The bone. The brick. It went faster than it had in the white space, and cleaner, and the black hot thing under his breastbone came up only once, at the bowl, and he held it there with his weight back on the reins and did not let it go.

"[resonant, otherworldly, slow]Again," said Faisal.

[narrator] The row was longer. There were twenty things in it now. Then fifty. Then a hundred, running off into the dark further than he could see, stones and blades and bones and ropes and cups and things he had no names for, and among them, scattered without any pattern, the rotten ones. He found them one after another. He held their notes. He let them go.

"[resonant, otherworldly, slow]Again," said Faisal.

[narrator] A thousand.

He lost count of the days. There were no days. There was only the row, and the notes, and the sour wavering hum of the dead things among the steady hum of the living ones, and his own attention narrowed to a needle's point, finding them, holding them, letting them go. Over and over and over. Sometimes the black hot thing came up and he lost his hold and the crazing ran out sideways into a sound thing beside the dead one, and the sound thing cracked, and Faisal said nothing, only lifted his hand, and the row was whole again, and he began from the beginning.

He did not count how many times he began from the beginning.

[pause] And then, at some point he could not have named, the row was not things any more.

It was a wall. A wall of gray stone, rough-coursed, the kind of wall a Tenrai mason would build round a fortress, with a gatehouse and a gate. He knew it was the Seat. He did not know how he knew. And it was full of cracks, all humming: the fine steady ones of stone carrying its own weight, the way a sound wall carries it, and here and there, deep, branching, sour, the ones that had stopped carrying and were only pretending. A course where the mortar had rotted. A lintel over a postern that had been cracked by frost forty winters ago and patched and never mended. A ward-stone set into the gatehouse foundation, a worked thing tied into the Seat's draw, humming with Tenrai gold and glowing in the threads like a coal, whose binding had been laid by a man who had not meant the oath he laid it with.

"[resonant, otherworldly, slow]Again," said Faisal.

[narrator] Muken looked at the wall.

He found the ward-stone. He held its note, the sour lie at the heart of it. He waited. And the ward-stone let go, along the line of the oath its maker had not meant, and cracked through, and the gold went out of it like a lamp going out. And the gatehouse wall round it stood. Every sound stone in it stood.

"[resonant, otherworldly, slow, approving]That," said the Bound Flame, "is what you will do at the Seat."

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, uncertain]Only that?"

"[resonant, otherworldly, slow, grave]Only what is dead," said Faisal. "Only what is already broken and only pretending to stand. Everything else, the men will do. The Stark and the Greymane and the Byakuya and the Dawi's iron. You will not crack one living thing that does not need cracking." The light held him. "[slowly]That is the oath I am asking of you. Not now. When you are ready to give it."

[narrator] Muken said nothing. He looked at the wall until he could hear every stone in it.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, slowly]I'll give it," he said.

---

### III · The Work of Purity

His ribs were the first thing.

Faisal did it without warning. There was no row any more and no wall. There was only the place made of threads, and the Bound Flame kneeling, and Muken sitting cross-legged before him, and Faisal said, "[resonant, otherworldly, slow]Look at yourself," and Muken looked.

[narrator] And he could see his own body, apart from the thing he was here among the threads: the one lying on the stone shelf in the death-house at the Foot with its back against the wall and its eyes closed and its chest rising and falling, very slowly, in the cold. He could see it the way he saw everything here, as threads and notes and cracks, and it was full of cracks. The seventh rib and the eighth on the left side, broken clean through, the ends grinding against each other at every breath. The old cut along the ribs, torn open twice and sewn three times, weeping. The long slash down the right forearm, sewn badly. The split palm. The bruise on the lung under the broken ribs where Aksel Keld's iron fist had driven them in. And everywhere, under all of it, fainter, older, the cracks of sixteen years: a wrist broken at eleven in the stamping floor at Halcrest and set crooked; a hairline in a shin from the Veyrath ditch; the knuckles of both hands, each one cracked and healed and cracked again.

"[resonant, otherworldly, slow]Ruin ends what is dead," said Faisal. "You know that now. But ending is only half of a Work. Something has to remain, after." The light pulsed, softly. "*Junketsu no Gō.* The Work of Purity. It establishes. Hear me, that is a larger thing than healing. It finds what ought to stand, the proper shape of a thing, what it was made to be before the world bent it, and it holds the thing to that shape until the thing remembers."

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, quiet]My ribs," said Muken.

"[resonant, otherworldly, slow]Your ribs," said Faisal. "Find what they ought to be."

[narrator] He tried. It was harder than the rope. Harder than the wall. A crack in a rope was a crack, and it hummed sour, and you found it and let it go. But a broken rib was not a crack to be let go. It was a crack to be closed, and to close it he had to hear, under the ragged discord of the break, the note the rib had sung before it broke, and hold that, and wait for the bone to remember.

He could not hear it.

He listened until the threads round him dimmed with his tiredness, and he could not hear it. Only the break. Only the grinding discord where the ends met. The black hot thing came up and told him that he was broken, that he had always been broken, that the break was what he was, and there was no other note to find.

"[resonant, otherworldly, slow]You are listening to the wrong thing," said Faisal.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, frustrated]I'm listening to the bone."

"[resonant, otherworldly, slow, patient]You are listening to the break," said the Bound Flame. "Of course you are. You have spent your whole life listening to breaks. You hear cracks the way other men hear their own names." The light dimmed, very slightly, and steadied. "[softly]Listen to her."

[narrator] Muken went still.

"[resonant, otherworldly, slow, gentle]You know how she closed a wound," said Faisal. "You watched her do it a thousand times. Did she look at the wound?"

[narrator] Muken thought of the surgeons' line under the Foot. The lamp, and her sleeves rolled to the elbow, and a Greymane boy screaming. Her small sure hands. Her eyes.

[softly] She had never looked at the wound. Not once. She had looked at the edges of it. At the whole skin round it, on either side, the living skin that had not been cut. She had looked at where it should meet. And then she had drawn it there, one stitch after another, and let it meet.

He let go of the break.

He listened to the rib on either side of it. The whole bone, above the break and below it, the living bone that had not been broken, humming its own steady note, the note of a rib that carried a man's breath in and out. He heard it. He held it. And he waited.

And very slowly, in the cold of the death-house at the Foot, on the stone shelf, under the strapped linen, the two ends of the seventh rib on Muken's left side moved toward each other, a hair's breadth, and met, and began to remember. In the place made of threads he saw it as a seam of pale gold closing along the bone, warm as lamplight through a window.

---

He did the eighth rib after. Then the cut along his side, which was worse, because the edges had been torn and sewn so many times they no longer knew where they should meet, and he had to find, under the stitching and the swelling and the old scar of the Vessant road, the note of skin that had never been cut at all. Then his forearm. Then his palm. It took a long time. Each one took longer than the last, because each one was more tired, and the black hot thing kept coming up in the middle of it and telling him that the break was what he was.

He kept letting go of the break. He kept listening to the edges. He kept waiting.

And at the end, sitting cross-legged in the place made of threads, he could see his own body lying on the shelf at the Foot, and it was not whole, because nothing ever is. The old crooked wrist was still crooked. The hairline in his shin was still there. The knuckles of both hands were still cracked and healed and cracked. But the ribs were knit, and the cut along them had closed to a long pale seam, and the forearm and the palm were only scars. And his chest, rising and falling on the shelf, was rising all the way.

"[resonant, otherworldly, slow, quietly]Do not do that to a man," said Faisal quietly, "who has not asked you to."

[narrator] Muken looked at him.

"[resonant, otherworldly, slow, grave]Purity, through recognition, restores," said the Bound Flame. "Through obsession, it purges. It finds what ought to stand and holds everything to it, and what will not be held, it burns." The bright places where eyes would be did not move. "[darkly]There is a woman in a silver chair at the Seat who believes she knows what the north ought to be. She holds everything to it. That is why your sister is on the ridge."

[narrator] Muken thought of the silver room, and of the ridge, and was quiet.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, quiet]I won't," he said.

---

### IV · The Work of Spirit

"[resonant, otherworldly, slow, gently]There is a thing I must show you," said Faisal, "that you will not want to see."

[narrator] The threads round them shifted.

This time they turned. All of them, all at once, the whole of the place made of threads turning slowly round the two of them like the sky turns round the pole star, and as they turned, the gold in them paled toward silver and the silver toward a soft violet-blue, the color of snow in the hour after sunset, and the notes changed. Lower. Softer. The humming of the living fell away somewhere behind them, and what was left was something else. Fainter. Further off. [hushed] A sound like many people breathing very quietly in a cold room, a long way off. Like the Waiting.

"[resonant, otherworldly, slow, reverent]*Seirei no Gō,*" said Faisal. "The Work of Spirit. It governs what passes between. Between life and death. Between the body and what comes after. It keeps the unseen world from becoming disorderly." He was quiet a moment. "Everyone who dies passes through it. Everyone. It is the road out."

[narrator] Muken did not move.

"[resonant, otherworldly, slow, gentle]You heard her," said the Bound Flame. "On the Seat road. Among all the notes of the world, you heard her note, small and clear, far to the south. Alive." The light dimmed, very slightly. "You have been listening for it ever since. In the death-house. In the yard. On the ridge, when you drew the mark. You are listening for it now."

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, barely audible]Yes," said Muken.

"[resonant, otherworldly, slow]It is still here," said Faisal. "Faintly. On the road out. She has not finished passing. Most do not, for some days. They linger, a little, near the ones they loved." The bright places held him. "[gently]And you are holding her."

[narrator] Muken stared at him.

"[resonant, otherworldly, slow]You do not know you are doing it," said Faisal. "But you are a Kōkan, and your blood leans toward this as much as toward Ruin. You heard her note on the Seat road, and you have held on to it since, with everything in you, the way you held her hand on the stair at Halcrest when she was small. And every moment you hold it, she cannot go." The light did not waver. "[grave]Spirit, through recognition, lets the dead pass, and keeps them in the memory of the living, and is at peace. Spirit, through obsession, will not let go. It hoards. It holds the dead by the wrist on the road out, and they cannot go forward and they cannot come back, and they wait there, in the cold, forever." A pause. "[softly]Listen."

[narrator] Muken listened.

[softly] And there it was. Among all the faint far breathing of the road out, one note he would have known anywhere. Small and clear and fierce. Quite unbroken. But no longer coming from the south. Coming from very near. From his own hand. From the place where his own fingers were closed, here, in the place made of threads, round something he had not known he was holding.

He looked down at his hand.

There was a thread in it. A single thread, fine as hair, pale, almost white. Humming. He was holding it so tightly that his knuckles had gone white, and the thread ran out of his fist and away into the faint far dark of the road out, taut, taut as a bowstring, and at the end of it, very far away, something was pulling gently against his grip. Not hard. Just pulling. The way a child pulls on your hand when she wants to go and look at something and you are not letting her.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, whispers]Yukazuri," said Muken.

[narrator] It came out of him in a whisper.

"[resonant, otherworldly, slow, gentle]Let her go," said Faisal.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, voice breaking]I can't."

"[resonant, otherworldly, slow, tender]You can," said the Bound Flame. "You must. For her sake, more than yours." He was quiet. "She is not finished passing. She cannot finish while you hold her. She will wait on the road out in the cold for as long as you hold on, because she loves you, and because she would never in her life let go of your hand first." The light warmed, very slightly. "[softly]She never did, did she."

[narrator] Muken shook his head. He could not speak.

He looked at the pale thread running out of his fist into the dark. At his own white knuckles. He thought about the stair at Halcrest. The room over the tea-house. The salve tin in the snow with the mark scratched on its lid and the crooked arrow pointing south. [Northern English accent, low and rough, inner voice] *Because she had known he would come. Because she had never in her life doubted that he would come.*

[young girl, Northern English accent, sharp, softly, remembered] *Please be careful.*

[young girl, Northern English accent, sharp, remembered] *Try harder than your best.*

[young girl, Northern English accent, sharp, fierce, remembered] *If you come back through this gate on a door with your guts in your lap, I'll put them back in, and I'll sew you up, and then I'll kill you myself.*

[narrator, slowly] He opened his hand.

The thread did not go at once. It lay across his open palm a moment longer, humming, pale, small and clear and fierce. [softly] And then it lifted. Slowly. The way a strand of hair lifts in the wind. It lifted off his palm and drew away into the faint far dark of the road out, shining as it went, frost-pale and then faintly rose, and its note went with it, further and further, softer and softer, until he could not hear it any more over the breathing of the dead.

And just before it went, at the very last, he thought he heard it change. Only a little. The way her voice used to change when she was pretending to be cross with him and could not keep it up.

[pause] Then it was gone.

[softly] Muken sat in the place made of threads with his open hand on his knee and his face turned toward the dark, and wept.

He had not wept on the ridge. He had not wept in the death-house, or at the postern, or on the shore of the Skeldmere with the salve tin in his fist. He had not wept since he was nine years old, at the top of a gallery shaft, when the factor told his mother she owed more salt. He wept now. All of it. Without any sound at all, the way the boy with the red ears had wept in the doorway of the death-house, with his shoulders shaking and his open hand on his knee and the threads of the world running through him, and humming, and the Bound Flame kneeling before him under the weight of everything, saying nothing, holding it.

[slowly] It went on for a very long time.

When it was over, Faisal said only, "[resonant, otherworldly, slow, softly]Thank you."

[narrator] Muken did not ask him what for.

---

### V · The Work of Temporality

"[resonant, otherworldly, slow]Now you will see," said Faisal, "why this happened."

[narrator] The threads turned again. Back. The faint far breathing of the road out fell away and the humming of the living came up, and with it, something new. A direction. Muken felt it before he saw it, the way you feel a current in a river before you see the water move: all the threads in that place, all of them, were not only running. They were running *from* somewhere, and *to* somewhere. They had a before and an after.

"[resonant, otherworldly, slow]*Jikan no Shigoto,*" said Faisal. "The Work of Temporality. It arranges what comes before and what comes after. Cause, and what the cause makes. Without it the world would be worse than chaos. It would be unreadable." The bright places shifted. "Follow her thread back."

[narrator] Muken did not understand. And then he did.

He looked for the place in the threads where her pale one had been. The thread was gone, and the place remained: a gap, where it had run. And he followed the gap back, the way you follow a dry streambed up a hill to where the spring was, and as he followed it, the threads round it lit up, one after another, running away from him into the past like lamps being lit down a long street.

He saw the silver room. He did not want to. He saw it: the polished floor and the caged lamps and the silver dais and a small gray woman with her hands behind her back, and he saw the thread running out of her, silver gone sour and tarnished, toward the girl on her knees. And he followed that thread back, from the woman, and it went into a room full of letters with two seals on every one, silver on the left. And back from there, into a lodge of reindeer hide on the tundra, and a Keld boy with an iron hand saying [Icelandic accent, flat, rough, young man] *the queen's will is absolute*. [narrator] And back. A council in a hall of black stone, six lords of the seven lines round a table, and a man in gold-trimmed black with a narrow face saying [light Japanese accent, smooth] *search every road, every register, every salt pan.* [narrator] And back. A cellar under Halcrest. A lamp. A man with white hair going down the stair after his own blood. And back, and back, and back, through twenty years and forty and a hundred, through wars he had never heard of and kings whose names were dust, through the silver line and the red line standing side by side in the Hall of Verdicts for a thousand years with their hands on their swords.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, slowly]It is all one thread," said Muken slowly.

"[resonant, otherworldly, slow]It is all one thread," said Faisal. "Your sister did not die because a woman in a silver chair hated her. She died because a thousand years ago the silver line was told it would always stand second, and believed it, and could not let it go. And every Shirogane since has been born into that one wound, and lived in it, and died in it, and passed it on." The light did not move. "Temporality, through recognition, lets things move on. Cause, and consequence, and then the next thing. Through obsession, it loops. The same wound. The same vow. The same moment, over and over, forever, because nobody will let it end."

[narrator] He let the threads fall still.

"[resonant, otherworldly, slow, grave]That is the reason she died," said the Bound Flame. "I told you in the yard that it was to make you your father. That is true. But under that, this. She died because a wound a thousand years old was still open, and the woman in the silver chair was living inside it." He looked at the boy. "If you go to the Seat and kill her for it, and that is all you do, you will have reopened it. The wound will pass to her sister, or her niece, or whoever sits the silver chair after. And in a thousand years, some Shirogane child will be told why it must hate the red line, and the reason will be you."

[narrator] Muken said nothing while the lit threads faded, one by one, back into the dark.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, bitter]Then what do I do?" he said at last. "Let her live?"

"[resonant, otherworldly, slow, firm]I did not say that," said Faisal. "She has killed. She will answer for it, in whatever way your law decides. That is lawful ending." The light shifted. "[gently]I said, do not let it be the only thing you do. End the woman, if you must. But end the wound with her. Close it. The way your sister would have."

---

### VI · The Work of Knowledge

The fifth Work, Faisal taught him quickly, because he already had the beginnings of it.

"[resonant, otherworldly, slow]*Chishiki no Shigoto,*" said the Bound Flame. "The Work of Knowledge. It lets the world be known instead of only suffered. It reveals what a thing is. Its true name. Its true use." The bright places where eyes would be held him. "You have done it all your life. The eye. The cracks. You looked at a host of four thousand Keld and saw where two clans hated each other over a pasture. You looked at a Greymane spearman in a crowd and saw that he would betray you." A pause. "You did not know you saw that. But you did. It is why you looked at him."

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, bitter]I didn't stop him."

"[resonant, otherworldly, slow]No," said Faisal. "Knowledge reveals. It does not act. That is the other Works' labor." The light was quiet. "But you will not miss it again. Look."

[narrator] And Muken looked, and he saw the Seat.

This time it was the people in it. Every lord of the seven lines who had stood in the Hall of Verdicts and voted for the Kurenai blade on Ulf Stark's neck. Every Tenrai factor and Shirogane steward. Every captain of the house-guard. He could see the threads running out of each of them, Tenrai gold and Shirogane silver and the many colors of the lesser houses, the whole lamplit city on its rock strung with them like a harp, and he could hear what they were humming, and most of them were humming fear. Fear of Kagenori. Fear of the silver chair. Fear of the boy in the north who had broken the Iron Fist with a song. A few were humming greed, and a few were humming nothing at all, the flat dead note of men who had stopped feeling anything years ago.

[pause] And one was humming grief.

It was very faint. A thread of gold, running out of a man in gold-trimmed black who stood alone in the silver room at the Seat at the foot of an empty dais, with his narrow face quite white, looking at the place on the polished floor where a servant in gray had wiped up a girl's blood.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, stunned]Kagenori," said Muken.

"[resonant, otherworldly, slow]Yes," said Faisal.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, disbelieving]He's grieving." Muken stared at the thread. "For her. For Yukazuri."

"[resonant, otherworldly, slow, sorrowful]For what was done in front of him," said the Bound Flame. "For what he has become, standing behind the silver chair for twenty years, building it for the woman in it, and then watching her use it to do that." The light did not waver. "He loves her. He has loved her his whole life. And he looked at what she did, and something in him broke, and it has not stopped breaking."

[narrator] Muken was silent a long moment.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, quiet]What will he do?"

"[resonant, otherworldly, slow]Knowledge does not tell you that," said Faisal. "It tells you what is. What will be is Temporality's, and Temporality does not show you a road you have not yet walked." He paused. "But you know now that he is broken. You know where. When you come to the Seat, you will not need to look for it."

---

### VII · The Work of Balance

The sixth Work was the one Faisal had been kneeling under since the world was young.

"[resonant, otherworldly, slow]*Baransu no Shigoto,*" he said. "The Work of Balance. It keeps opposites from tearing the world apart. Ruin and Purity. Life and death. Law and freedom. Motion and stillness." One great wing moved slightly under its burden. "It does not make them the same. It gives each its proper weight, so the world can carry the strain and not break."

[narrator] He was quiet a moment.

"[resonant, otherworldly, slow, gently]Come here," said the Bound Flame.

[narrator] Muken got up. How he did it in that place he never knew; he did it, and came and stood beside the kneeling figure, close, closer than he had ever been, so that the warm heavy light of it lay over him like the warmth off a banked fire, and he wanted to tell it everything.

"[resonant, otherworldly, slow]Kneel," said Faisal.

[narrator] Muken knelt.

He went down beside him, one knee down and the other drawn up, the way the Bound Flame knelt, with his head bowed. And the weight came down on him.

He had not known what it would be. He had seen the threads coming down on Faisal's back and shoulders from the whole of the dark above and running through him and out of him into the dark below, and he had thought he understood. He had not understood. A little of it came onto him now, a very little, a hair's breadth of it, and it was the heaviest thing he had ever felt. Heavier than the salt. Heavier than the gallery roof. Heavier than his mother dying and his sister dead. [strained] It came down on his back and his shoulders and pressed him toward the dark below, and every thread in it was humming, all at once, the whole song of the world in his bones, and his knee went down further, and his back bowed further, and he knew that if he let it, it would press him all the way down into the dark and through it and there would be nothing left.

"[resonant, otherworldly, slow, close and urgent]Do not fight it," said Faisal, beside him. His voice was very close. "If you fight it, it will break you. Everyone who has ever fought it, it has broken."

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, strained]Then what do I..."

"[resonant, otherworldly, slow, steady]Carry it," said the Bound Flame. "Do not push it up. Do not let it push you down. Find where it wants to go, and let it go there, through you, and out of you, and do not hold any of it longer than it needs to be held." The light was very steady. "That is Balance. It is knowing where the weight goes."

[narrator] Muken knelt under it.

He found where it wanted to go. It took him a long time. The weight came down on his back, and he did not push against it and he did not let it push him, and he felt for where it was going: down, through him, into the dark below. Into the ground. The way the weight of a roof goes down through a beam and a post and into the earth. The way the weight of a man goes down through his legs into the ice. The way Lorn Stark stood, with his weight going straight down through both heels, the way his father had taught him. He let the weight go through him. Down. Out. He held none of it. He only let it pass.

It got lighter. There was as much of it as ever; it was moving now.

"[resonant, otherworldly, slow, softly]Yes," said Faisal softly.

[narrator] They knelt together a long time, the Bound Flame and the boy, under the weight of the threads, in the place where the Works were written.

And at some point, Muken noticed that the dark place on the edge of Faisal's wing, the small missing piece where the light no longer reached, was not as dark as it had been. A little of the warm light was coming back into it, gold seeping into the gap the way dawn seeps into a valley. Very little. A hair's breadth.

[softly] He kept it to himself, and was glad.

"[resonant, otherworldly, slow, reverent]*Shigoto no Kabe,*" said Faisal, at last. "That is what your grandfathers' grandfathers called it. *The Work of Bearing Load.* When you stand, someday, in front of something that means to come down on everyone behind you, and you will not move, this is what will hold you there." The light pulsed, once. "The weight, going through you, into the ground. You will feel it in your bones for days afterward. It will crack them, a little, every time. But it will hold."

---

### VIII · The Seventh

When he got up from under the weight, at last, Muken stood in the place made of threads and looked at the Bound Flame, and asked the question he had been carrying since the white space.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, curious]There are seven," he said. "You said. Seven Works. Ruins, and Purity, and Spirit, and Temporality, and Knowledge, and Balance." He counted them on his fingers. "That's six."

[narrator] Faisal did not answer.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, insistent]The seventh," said Muken. "You said in the white space that there's a third waking under Hibiware, and you'd not speak of it. Is it that?" He looked at the kneeling figure. "What is it?"

[narrator] The Bound Flame was silent, and the threads hummed on round them, gold and silver and black.

"[long pause, resonant, otherworldly, slow, hushed]*Zentai-sei no Hataraki,*" he said at last. "The Work of Totality."

[narrator] The threads round them went very quiet.

"[resonant, otherworldly, slow, reverent]It is the crown of the others," said Faisal. "It is what makes all of them belong to one world. Ruin ends and Purity establishes and Spirit passes and Temporality orders and Knowledge reveals and Balance reconciles, and Totality is what lets all of that be one thing instead of six things tearing each other apart. It is the first permission for the world to be whole." The light gathered, and for a breath every thread in that place shone at once, white-gold, so bright and so whole that Muken could not look at it and could not look away. "It is your house's. The Kōkan's. It was the mark on your banner before your banner was blank. The crowned serpent. The raven king. *Kamigan.* Every Kōkan king who ever sat the red chair reached for it, and some of them touched it."

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, eager]Teach me," said Muken.

"[resonant, otherworldly, slow, very quiet]No."

[narrator] The word was very quiet. It went through the place made of threads like a stone dropped into a well, and every thread stopped humming for the space of a breath.

"[resonant, otherworldly, slow, firm]Not yet," said Faisal. "It is not time. It is not ready. And you are not ready for it." The bright places where his eyes would be held the boy. "[gravely]Hear me. You sat in your own yard with your hands on a table and told three lords of the seven lines that you would bind the seven lines into one blood. You stood on the Skeldmere and told a Tenrai knife that every Moto line would give up its lands into your hand." The light dimmed, very slightly. "[quietly]Do you know what Totality is, through obsession?"

[narrator] Muken did not answer.

"[resonant, otherworldly, slow, dark]It is the hunger to gather everything into one hand," said the Bound Flame. "Every distinction. Every line. Every house and every people and every difference between them, gathered up and pressed together into one shape until nothing is left that is not you." He was quiet a moment. "Enforced oneness. Dominion over everything. It is the oldest corruption there is, and the worst, and every Kōkan who ever reached for Totality before he was ready fell into it. Your father reached for it. That was what the cellars were. He wanted the red line to be one thing. Only one. Himself."

[narrator] Muken stood very still.

"[resonant, otherworldly, slow]You will unite the seven lines," said Faisal. "I believe that. I have seen the threads. But if you do it with Totality in your hands before you understand what it is, you will not unite them. You will swallow them. And in twenty years someone will go down into a cellar with a lamp." The light warmed, slightly. "When you can stand in front of seven lines and a dozen Banner Houses and a Zettari prince and want every one of them to stay exactly what they are, and still be one thing together, then I will teach you the seventh. Not before."

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, quiet]How long?"

"[resonant, otherworldly, slow]Years," said Faisal. "Many. Perhaps your whole life." A pause. "Perhaps it will be someone else's, after you."

[narrator] Muken thought about that, and about a cellar under Halcrest, and a lamp.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, slowly]All right," he said at last.

[narrator] The Bound Flame looked at him.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, steady]You're right," said Muken. "I said it on the ice. *One blood.* I meant it." He looked down at his own hands. "[rueful]And I was angry when I said it. I didn't know the difference yet." He lifted his eyes. "I'll wait."

[narrator] The light warmed.

"[resonant, otherworldly, slow, warm]Then you are readier than you think," said Faisal.

---

### IX · Renewed

He did not know how long he had been in the place where the threads run. He could not have said. When he tried, afterward, to count it for Lorn or Godfrey, it came out as a day, and as a season, and as a very long time, and none of them were wrong.

At the end of it, Faisal lifted his hand from the weight he carried one last time, and laid it, open, on Muken's chest. Over the knit ribs. Over the heart. Over the black hot thing under the breastbone, which was still there, and would always be there, but which lay quiet now, banked, like a coal under ash.

"[resonant, otherworldly, slow]You have six," said the Bound Flame. "Not mastered. You will spend your life on them. But you have them. Ruin, and Purity, and Spirit, and Temporality, and Knowledge, and Balance." The light pulsed, softly. "And Hibi, for the blade. Hibiware you have felt, and you will not touch again until you can do to a city what you did to a rope. And the seventh, waiting." He paused. "Your Dawi has nearly finished. I can hear the iron giving up."

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, quiet]Will I see you again?"

"[resonant, otherworldly, slow, warm]When you need it," said Faisal, "and when I can. What kneels at the edge does not go far from it." The light shifted, as if something under it had smiled. "But I think you will not need me so often now."

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, steady]I'll swear it," said Muken. "The oath you asked for. For the Seat."

[narrator] The Bound Flame waited.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, slowly]By my honor," said Muken, "I'll crack nothing at the Seat that is not already dead. No living thing that does not need cracking. Not wall, not ward, not man." He breathed. "And I'll close what I can. The way she would have."

[narrator] The warm heavy light went through him like heat through iron.

"[resonant, otherworldly, slow, gently]I heard it," said Faisal. "Every word of it was true."

[narrator] The hand on his chest pressed, once, very gently. And pushed.

---

He opened his eyes on the stone shelf in the death-house at the Foot.

The cold. The dark. The bar across the door. The faint dark stain on the stone under his hand. A thin blue dawn light coming under the door from the yard, and beyond the door, faint, the sound of a man breathing in his sleep, sitting against the wood: Lorn Stark, keeping the door.

He sat up.

[inhales deeply] He breathed in. All the way down, through the nose, until his lungs were full, and his ribs did not grind, and nothing went white behind his eyes. He breathed out. He looked down at himself. The strapped linen round his chest had come loose in the night and lay in a heap on the shelf beside him. Under it his side was whole, a long pale seam of new scar where the old cut had been. His right forearm was a scar. His left palm was a scar. His wrist was still crooked. His knuckles were still cracked.

He flexed his hands. They were steady.

He could hear the world singing. All of it. The stone and the snow and the wall and the woodpile and the ridge and the cairn and the long low humming of everything. He could hear it, and it did not drown him. It was only there, the way the wind is there, or the sea under a pier.

He got up off the shelf, and went to the door, and lifted the bar.

---

# Chapter Eighteen: The Samurai of Fate

[narrator] *Book One of the Kōkan · Chapter Eighteen: The Samurai of Fate*

> *My mother told me stories on the pallet in the last gallery, when the lamp was low and Yukazuri was asleep. Not the stories other mothers told. She told me about men in red armor laced with white, who knelt to nobody but one chair, and who walked out in front of the king when there was something coming that meant to come down on everyone behind him. She called them the Samurai of Fate. I asked her once where they were now. She said they were all dead, a long time ago, and turned her face to the wall.*
>
> *Attributed to Muken Moto, as set down in the* Annals of the North-Forged Crown

---

### I · The Door

Lorn Stark woke with his back against the death-house door and the bar going up behind him.

He was on his feet before he knew he was awake, with his father's axe in his hands and his heart going like a drum. The door opened inward. A gray light came out of it, the last of the noon-through-cloud light that had been coming under it for a day and a night and a day, and it was fading as he watched, draining back into the dark of the cell like water into sand.

And Muken came out.

Lorn did not know what he had expected. He had sat against that door for the better part of two days and listened to a voice inside that was not Muken's and a bell struck very far away, and watched the snow under the door crack and close, crack and close, like breath on a window. He had expected, if he was honest, to carry the king of the north out of there on a door, dead, the way they had carried his sister up the ridge.

The king of the north walked out on his own feet.

He was standing straight. That was the first thing. Not bent to the left, not holding his side, not breathing in the top of his chest the way he had been since the Skeldmere. He stood in the door of the death-house in the dead sergeant's coat, and he was standing straight, and he breathed in, all the way, and the breath went up off him in a long white plume, and nothing in his face flinched.

"[Yorkshire accent, young, rough, hoarse]Sire," said Lorn.

[narrator] His voice came out cracked. He had not used it in two days.

Muken looked at him.

The gray eyes. The ring-lines in them. The gold in the left one, the fine seam across the iris that Lorn had watched spread and spread through all the days since the long room, wider every time he looked. It was still there. But it lay quiet now. It did not burn. It sat in the gray of his eye the way a vein of ore sits in gray stone, very bright and very still.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, quiet]You kept the door," said Muken.

"[Yorkshire accent, young, rough]You told me to."

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, warm]I know." Muken looked at him a moment longer. "Thank you, Lorn."

[narrator] He did not say anything else. He did not need to. He put one hand on Lorn Stark's shoulder, over the mail, and held it there, and Lorn felt the weight of it, and something in his chest that had been clenched for two days let go all at once, so suddenly that he had to look at the snow.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough]Is it done?" said Muken. "The sword."

"[Yorkshire accent, young, rough]It's done." Lorn's voice was rough. "This morning. At dawn. The Dawi laid it on the table in the long room and lay down on the floor with his head on his anvil, and he's not moved since. Nobody's dared wake him." He swallowed. "[puzzled]And your aunt's had the Byakuya armorers at something all night in the gatehouse with the Akagane smiths and half of Hayato's riders, and none of them will tell me what. She said it was for you. She said you'd know."

[narrator] Muken was quiet a moment.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, quiet]Yes," he said. "I asked her. Before I went in." He took his hand off Lorn's shoulder. "Where's Godfrey?"

"[Yorkshire accent, young, rough, dry]In the long room. With the sword. He's been sat in front of it since dawn like a dog at a door." Lorn's mouth moved very slightly at one corner. "[amused]Says he's guarding it from the Dawi."

---

### II · Tsugigane

It lay on the split pine table in front of Shigure Tenrai's carved chair, on a length of clean linen, where the scrap-iron sword had lain.

The long room was very quiet. The fire in the great hearth had burned down to a deep red bed of coals, and it did not flicker, and it gave off a steady even heat that filled the room from wall to wall, though nobody had fed it since dawn. Borin Ironheart lay asleep on the stone floor in front of it with his head on his anvil and his great arms folded across his barrel chest and his three braided beard-trunks rising and falling, very slowly, like the bellows of a forge banked for the night. The crack he had made in the floor when he set the anvil down ran from the hearthstone to the leg of the table.

Godfrey Greymane was sitting on the bench from the stables with his long gray hands folded on his knees, in a cloak that was not his own, a Stark hide-coat somebody had found him, and when the door opened he looked up, and got to his feet, and stood very still.

Muken came across the room to the table.

He did not touch it at once. He stood and looked at it.

It was in its saya. Black lacquer, seven coats, so deep and so smooth that the red light of the coals lay in it like light in still water, bound at the mouth and the foot with plain iron. The hilt was bound in black rayskin and wrapped over in dark red silk cord, the old diamond pattern. The guard was the guard of the scrap-iron sword. He knew it at once. Cleaned and filed and polished until it was dark and plain and round, but it was the same guard, the one he had pulled out of a scrap-heap at the Orrel stamping floor when he was eleven, and there was a hole worked through it now, small, off-center, in the shape of a needle's eye.

The sageo, the cord that would bind it to his belt, was white.

He knew that cord too.

"[soft Scottish accent, measured, quietly]She gave it him," said Godfrey quietly. "Your aunt. For the saya. He asked her if she was sure." He was quiet a moment. "She said it was always yours. She'd only kept it."

[narrator] Muken did not answer. He put out his right hand, the hand with the old callus across the base of the fingers from a length of ash, and laid it on the hilt.

[softly] And the sword sang.

Not loudly. He was the only one in the room who heard it, though afterward Godfrey swore he had felt something, a stirring in the air like a breath let out. A single note. Clear and high and sweet, coming up out of the black lacquer and the iron and the steel inside it into the palm of his hand and up his arm into his chest, and settling there, in the place under his breastbone where the black hot thing lay banked.

It was not the note of the scrap-iron sword. The scrap-iron had sung harsh and clanging, a pot-lid note, a note of a hundred bad smelts and a hundred bad smiths. This was something else. It was the note of gray steel that had had everything that was not steel folded out of it. And under it, woven through it, very faint, like a thread of silver running through gray cloth, there was another note.

He knew it. He would have known it anywhere.

It was not her. He knew that. She was gone, down the road out, and he had opened his hand and let her go. But it was something she had left. The note of seven thin steel needles that had closed half the men in the north, folded into the marrow of a blade at the place where the old one had broken. A note that did not want to break anything. A note that only wanted, everywhere it went, to close.

[slowly] He drew it.

He did it slowly, an inch at a time, and the blade came out of the black ash saya without a sound, and the red light of the coals ran down it like water. It was plain. Gray steel, a long gentle curve, a single edge as fine as a hair. There was no pattern in the steel, no flame of temper-line along the edge, nothing. Only gray. And along it, from the needle-eyed guard a hand's width up the blade, in the place where the scrap-iron had broken, a single thin bright line. A seam of silver in gray stone.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, slowly]Tsugigane," said Muken.

[narrator] The name went into the blade. He felt it go. The note changed, very slightly, the way a voice changes when you call it by its name, and then settled, and hummed, and held.

On the floor in front of the hearth, without opening his eyes, Borin Ironheart [grunts] grunted once in his sleep and turned over.

---

### III · The Red Harness

Kiyome Byakuya was waiting for him in the gatehouse.

She stood in the middle of the guardroom where Shigure Tenrai had once kept her carved chair, in her near-white robes with the hem gray from the yard, and her loose white hair tied back, and her sword at her hip without its knot. The room was full of people. Byakuya armorers in white, kneeling with needle and cord. Two Akagane smiths in copper-colored aprons, still grimed to the elbow. Half a dozen of Hayato's Amagiri riders, who had been sent to strip the Tenrai armory at the Foot of every piece of lacquered iron it held. And Tetsugen Akagane himself, sitting on an upturned bucket in the corner with his scarred hands on his knees, looking as if he had not slept, which he had not.

They all stopped when he came in. They all stood.

And laid out on a long trestle down the middle of the guardroom, piece by piece, there was the armor.

Muken stopped in the door and looked at it.

"[light Japanese accent, calm, warm, quietly]I didn't know what you meant," said Kiyome Byakuya quietly. "When you asked me. Before you went into the death-house. *Make me what she told me about.* I didn't know what she'd told you." Her gray eyes were very steady. "So I sat down and thought about what my sister would have told a boy on a pallet in a salt mine, at night, if she could only tell him one thing about who she was. And I knew."

[narrator] She turned her head and looked at the trestle.

"[light Japanese accent, calm, warm]The Samurai of Fate," she said. "*Unmei no Samurai.* The guard of the red chair. They were the Kōkan king's own, from the old days, before the Recension. Every one of them sworn by name. Every one of them a Byakuya, or raised by the Byakuya, and taught the oath over the lamp. Red lacquer, for the red line. White lacing, for the white line that kept it." She breathed. "[proud]Kasane was the last of them. The last Byakuya sworn to stand at a Kōkan king's shoulder in the old way. She wore the red harness at Gimbzo's side for six years." Her voice did not shake. "She left it in the white house the night she went into the salt. It's still there. In the Shiraki hills. I couldn't bring it to you in a day." She looked at the trestle. "[softly]So we made you one."

[narrator] It was made out of Tenrai armor. Muken could see that. He could hear it, under the new lacquer, the note of the old iron, the plates that had been cut and riveted for some Tenrai captain at the Foot, whose name nobody remembered. But it had been taken apart. Every plate of it. And put back together.

The cuirass was lamellar, the small iron scales laced into rows the old way, and every scale had been stripped of its Tenrai gold and lacquered again in a single night, coat on coat, dried in front of the gatehouse braziers, in a red so deep it was nearly black. The red of old blood. Of the red line. The lacing that held the scales together was white silk cord, close-woven, in the dense diagonal pattern Kiyome's grandmother had taught her. The shoulder-guards hung from the cuirass in the same red and white. The sleeves were dark cloth sewn over with small iron plates and fine mail. The thigh-guards, the greaves, the gauntlets with their fingers of mail and their backs of red-lacquered plate.

And at the end of the trestle, the helmet. Black iron, the bowl made of many plates riveted together in a low rounded dome, and the neck-guard falling from it in curved lames of red and white. There was no crest on it. No horns, no crescent, no lion, no dragon, no crowned serpent. The front of the bowl, where a crest would have been mounted, was bare black iron.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough]No crest," said Muken.

"[light Japanese accent, calm, warm]Your banner has no token," said Kiyome. "I didn't think your helmet should either." She hesitated. "[tentative]Unless you want one."

[narrator] Muken looked at the bare black iron a long time.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, quiet]No," he said. "That's right."

[narrator] He looked at the cuirass again, at the place over the breastbone, over the heart. There was something there, worked into the red lacquer. Very small. So small he had not seen it at first. Painted in a single fine line of white, no bigger than the ball of his thumb.

A long down-stroke. A hook. A crossing. A small closed loop at the foot of it like a knot in a rope.

He did not ask who had painted it. He knew. One of the Byakuya armorers was standing by the brazier with a fine brush still in her hand and white paint on her fingers, [sniffles] and she was crying, and trying not to.

---

They dressed him in the gatehouse, piece by piece, the old way.

Kiyome did it herself, with the Byakuya armorers handing her each piece in turn. She knew the order. She had been taught it as a child, by the same grandmother who had taught her the oath over the lamp. First the under-robe, plain white, and the loose trousers bound at the ankle. Then the greaves, laced over the shins. Then the thigh-guards, tied round the waist, hanging like an apron of red scales over the front of the legs. Then the sleeves, one arm and then the other, and the gauntlets laced at the wrist. Then the cuirass itself, lifted onto him by two of the Akagane smiths because it was heavier than it looked, and closed down the right side, and tied. Then the shoulder-guards, each one hung from its cord at the shoulder and tied off behind. Then the throat-guard.

He stood still for all of it. He did not speak. He felt each piece go on, and settle, and take its weight, and he felt the weight go down through him into the stone floor of the gatehouse the way the weight of the threads had gone down through him in the place where the Works were written. He did not push against it. He let it pass.

When it was done, Kiyome stepped back.

She looked at him for a long time.

He stood in the middle of the gatehouse guardroom in the red harness of the Samurai of Fate, red as old blood and laced with white, with the tiny white mark painted over his heart and the bare black helmet in the hands of the armorer beside him. His white hair was tied back with the same strip of leather it had always been tied back with. His face, above the red throat-guard, was very still.

[softly] He was sixteen years old.

He looked, Kiyome Byakuya thought, like every story her grandmother had ever told her over the lamp. And he looked like a boy dressed up in his dead mother's clothes. And he was both, and there was no telling them apart, and she found that she could not see him properly for a moment, and had to look away.

Tetsugen Akagane got up off his bucket.

He came across the guardroom slowly, the old copper lord, broad as a door, and stopped in front of the boy in the red harness, and looked him up and down from under his thick gray brows.

"[light Japanese accent, flat, iron, deep]Your father," said Tetsugen, "never wore red." His voice was flat and iron. "Did you know that? Gimbzo. He wore black. Black lacquer, black lacing, black everything, for twenty years. He said the red line had become a thing to be ashamed of, after what the Recension made of it, and that he'd not wear its color until he'd made it worth wearing again." He was quiet a moment. "[bitter]He never did."

[narrator] Muken looked at him.

"[light Japanese accent, flat, iron, deep]You're wearing it," said Tetsugen Akagane. "Before you've made it worth anything." He hooked his thumbs in his belt. "[gruff approval]Good. Somebody had to go first."

---

Muken took Tsugigane from the table where Godfrey had laid it, and pushed the black ash saya through the white silk sash at his left hip, edge up, the old way, and tied the white sageo round the sash in the knot Kiyome showed him. And put his hand on the hilt, for the second time.

The note came up into his palm. Clear and high and sweet, with the silver thread running through it.

He walked to the narrow window of the gatehouse and looked out.

The window looked north, over the yard, and the woodpile, and the curtain wall with its long pale seam of the mend, and beyond the wall, the ridge. He could see the row of cairns on it, pale against the snow, the dead of the Foot. And at the end of the row, where the ridge looked south toward the Kessen road and the white country and the Seat, the last one. Higher than the others. With one small stone no bigger than a hen's egg set in the exact center of the top.

He stood and looked at it for a long time.

All of it. Everything since the map at Halcrest. The cellars, and his mother's lungs, and the factor at the top of the shaft. The salt. The Vessant road and the Veyrath ditch and the stair at the Foot. Ulf and Wystan riding south. The long room, and Kiyome's finger in the dust. The Skeldmere, and the iron hand coming apart in seven pieces. The shore, and the burning carts, and the salve tin in the snow. The silver room he had never seen and would see in three days. The gray sail coming up the frozen river like a leaf coming down a stream. The cairn. The road out, and the pale thread lifting off his open palm.

All of this pain. All of this suffering. He had carried it up out of the galleries on his back, and carried it across the north, and he had thought for a while, in the yard, cutting at nothing in the snow, that he would have to carry it forever, and that it would crack the world under his feet.

He did not have to carry it. He had knelt beside the Bound Flame under the weight of everything and learned that much. You did not carry the weight. You let it go through you, into the ground. You found where it wanted to go, and let it go there.

[slowly] It wanted to go into the tides of fate.

He could feel it going. Out of him, through the red harness and the stone of the gatehouse floor, into the long low humming of the world that ran under everything. Moving, and kept. The way a river moves under its ice. Into the threads, and along them, south, toward the Seat, where a thousand-year-old wound was still open and a woman in a silver chair was living inside it.

He turned from the window.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, quiet]Thank you," he said, to all of them. To Kiyome and Tetsugen and the Byakuya armorer with the white paint on her fingers and the Akagane smiths and Hayato's riders. "All of you."
