[narrator] He took the bare black helmet from the armorer's hands and put it under his left arm.

And walked out of the gatehouse.

---

### IV · Three Days

Lorn Stark was waiting at the foot of the gatehouse stair.

Of course he was. He was standing in the yard in the snow in his father's mail with his father's axe across his back and his hands still at his sides, the way he always stood, weight even on both feet, and when the gatehouse door opened and the king came down the stair in the red harness with the white lacing and the bare black helmet under his arm and the gray blade at his hip in its black saya, Lorn's face did something it had not done since he was a boy.

[softly] It went completely still.

Behind him, the yard was full. Nobody had called them. Nobody had needed to. The word had gone round the Foot from the moment the bar went up on the death-house door, the way it always went round the Foot, in the time it takes to breathe in, and they had come out of every door in the fort and stood in the yard in the snow. The Starks. The Greymanes. The Byakuya in white, and the Amagiri in indigo, and the Akagane in copper. The Thornwall pikemen who had come in off the Skeldmere and camped under the walls. The Ashmore horse. The women from the long houses and their children. The boys who had sewn the banner. The old woman with no teeth, on her door, carried out by four Stark riders and set down by the woodpile.

They looked at him.

He stopped at the foot of the stair, in front of Lorn.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, steady, commanding]Prepare," said Muken.

[narrator] His voice carried over the whole yard. It did not have to be raised. It had that weight in it still, the weight Kiyome had heard in the long room, every word set down like a stone on a cairn.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, steady, commanding]Every Stark and every Greymane," he said. "Every Byakuya and Akagane and Amagiri rider. Thornwall. Ashmore. Everyone who'll come." His gray eyes went over them, slowly, the whole yard. "We march at first light. Down the Marrow, on the ice." He paused. "[cold]The same road they sent her home on."

[narrator] Nobody spoke.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, slowly]We take Kharven-Seat," said Muken, "in three days."

---

### V · The Council of the Road

They planned it that night in the long room, round the split pine table, with Borin Ironheart still asleep on the floor in front of the hearth and everyone stepping carefully round him.

Muken sat in Shigure Tenrai's carved chair in the red harness with the helmet on the table at his elbow, and let them talk. He did not hurry them. He had learned that from Godfrey, in this room, the morning the Moto came. *Let them talk. Let them say all of it.*

[narrator] The Lady of Thornwall spoke first.

She had come in off the Skeldmere with her pikes the day after the battle and stayed. She was a small upright woman of perhaps sixty, Byakuya-born, with Kiyome's gray eyes and a Thornwall woman's weathered face and gray felt, and her name, it turned out, was Hisae. She had never once in forty years in the north been called anything but *the Lady*, and she seemed faintly surprised that anyone wanted to know it.

"[elderly woman, light Japanese accent, dry]The Seat stands on the rock above the Marrow," she said, in a voice like dry leaves. "Everyone knows that. The cliff on the west, four hundred feet to the river. The Hall of Verdicts on the crown of the rock. The Shirogane house on the western terrace under it. The Tenrai keep on the east. And the main gate on the south, where the road comes up from the river ford, and it's been the main gate for three hundred years because there's no other way up the rock that an army can use." She folded her small hands. "[knowing]No other way that the Tenrai know of."

"[light Japanese accent, calm, warm, softly]The corridors," said Kiyome softly.

"[elderly woman, light Japanese accent, dry]The corridors," said Hisae Thornwall. "Thornwall cut them. Two hundred years ago, before the Tenrai took the Seat from the Kōkan. When the Kōkan still sat the red chair and Thornwall was their forester. Long cuts through the Thornwood, the forest on the hills east of the Seat, narrow, walled in by trees so old and so close that you can't see the sky. They run from Thornhaven to the foot of the Seat's eastern face, where the rock's lowest. And at the end of them there's a stair cut into the rock behind a fall of ice that freezes every winter and hides it." She looked round the table. "The Thornwall foresters have kept the corridors cut for two hundred years. Every winter, every spring. Nobody ever asked us why. The Tenrai never knew they were there to ask about." A dry smile. "[wry]My husband's grandfather told me, the night I married into the house, that the Thornwall kept them open because one day the Kōkan would come home and need a back door. I thought he was a sentimental old fool." She inclined her head to Muken, very slightly. "I've been wrong before."

"[light Japanese accent, quick, wry]Twelve hundred pikes," said Hayato, "up a stair behind a frozen waterfall."

"[elderly woman, light Japanese accent, dry]Not all at once," said Hisae Thornwall. "Thirty at a time. But they'll be inside the eastern wall before the Tenrai know there's a hole in it."

[narrator] Muken said nothing. He was looking at the map on the table, at the pale line of the corridors Hisae had drawn on it, and the gold in his left eye had brightened a hair.

"[soft Scottish accent, measured]And the gate?" said Godfrey.

"[crisp German accent, cold]The gate," said a new voice, "is a matter of money."

[narrator] They all turned.

The woman who had spoken had come up the Kessen road that afternoon in a closed sledge with a Hallenfeld courier captain at the reins and six armed clerks riding behind. She was tall and thin and gray and wrapped in a great dark coat lined with wolf, and she had ink on her fingers. She had not taken off her gloves. She sat at the far end of the split pine table with a ledger open in front of her, and she had been writing in it, steadily, all through the Lady of Thornwall's account.

"[soft Scottish accent, measured, quietly]Ilse Hallenfeld," said Godfrey quietly, for the king's ear. "Mistress of the Hallenfeld counting-house. The junction. Every courier and quartermaster and road-toll between the Seat and the southern marches answers to her." He was quiet a moment. "She doesn't leave the junction. Ever."

"[crisp German accent, cold]I've left it," said Ilse Hallenfeld, without looking up from her ledger. "I'll want that noted." She turned a page. "The Seat has twelve hundred house-guard, give or take, and what's left of the Haldorsson Keld, who came in off the Skeldmere with Karogane's carriage because their clan had already taken Tenrai silver and had nowhere else to go. Perhaps six hundred. And it has the Tenrai lords and their practitioners, which is the problem." She looked up at last, with eyes like gray ice. "The Seat's wards are drawn. The gate-wards, the wall-wards, the wards on the Hall. All of them. They draw off the Seat's main, the Guild main that comes up from the southern Wellsprings through the conduit under the river. Brass and slate and Guild seals, the whole length of it. Without the draw, those wards are stone with some gold paint on it." She tapped the ledger with one gloved finger. "The draw-masters who keep that main are Guild. Guild draw-masters are paid by the Seat's treasury, quarterly, through the Hallenfeld counting-house, because the Seat's treasury has been running on Hallenfeld credit for eleven years and it hasn't paid us back a copper of it."

[narrator] Hayato Amagiri began, very slowly, to smile.

"[crisp German accent, cold]I've written to the draw-masters," said Ilse Hallenfeld. "I've told them the Seat's credit is closed. As of tonight. And that the Hallenfeld counting-house will pay their next quarter in full, in gold, at the junction, on one condition." She looked down the table at the boy in the red harness. "That on the morning of the third day, at the first bell, they find a fault in the main."

[narrator] The long room was very quiet.

"[crisp German accent, cold]The Seat will go silent," said Ilse Hallenfeld. "The draw-hum will stop. Every ward on the rock will go dead at the same moment. And the Tenrai will stand on their walls with their gold paint and their practitioners and find out what a Tenrai is worth without a Guild main under him." She closed the ledger. "Hallenfeld's never once in three hundred years lent money to the Kōkan, Your Grace. It's not personal. The Kōkan never asked." A thin smile. "[dry]I'm offering. The north can pay me back when it has something to pay with."

[narrator] Muken looked at her a long moment.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough]Why?" he said.

[narrator] Ilse Hallenfeld did not pretend to misunderstand him.

"[crisp German accent, cold]Because the Tenrai are bad debtors," she said. "Because the Shirogane are worse. Because a girl of thirteen came down the Marrow past my junction on a sledge with a gray sail, and my courier captains stood on the bank and took their caps off, and I asked them why." She folded her gloved hands on the ledger. "And because the counting-house has kept its books for three hundred years on one rule, which is that a debt is a debt, and it gets paid. They owe you. I'd like to see it collected."

[laughs] Edric Ashmore laughed, once, from the other side of the table.

He was a big fair man of forty in brown and russet with the Ashmore boar on his breast and a broken nose, and he had sworn to Hayato at the junction stone below Ashbury with his hand on the stone and had not stopped looking faintly amazed at himself since. "[West Country accent, hearty]And Ashmore?"

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, steady]Ashmore holds the river," said Muken.

[narrator] They looked at him.

He had not spoken since the council began. He leaned forward now, in the red harness, and put one finger on the map, on the frozen line of the Marrow where it ran under the cliff below the Seat and round the foot of the rock to the ford.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, quiet]Every Tenrai lord in the Seat will run when the wards go dead," he said quietly. "Not all of them. But most. I've seen them." The gold in his eye held steady as a lamp-flame behind glass. "They're humming fear. Every one of them. Fear of Kagenori, and fear of the silver chair, and fear of us. When the draw stops, the fear will win. And there's one way off that rock that isn't the main gate or a four-hundred-foot cliff." His finger moved. "The river postern. Under the Tenrai keep. Down a stair cut in the rock to the ice." He looked at Edric Ashmore. "[cold]Hold the river. Let them come down onto the ice. And then don't let them leave it."

[narrator] Ashmore looked at him for a long moment. Then he nodded slowly.

"[soft Scottish accent, measured]And the main gate," said Godfrey.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, quiet]The main gate," said Muken, "is mine."

---

### VI · The Seat

They came down the Marrow on the ice in three days, as he had said they would.

Three thousand and more of them. Stark riders and Greymane foot, Byakuya and Akagane and Amagiri, Thornwall pikes and Ashmore horse and the Hallenfeld clerks riding at the back of the column with their ledgers, on the frozen river road through the forest and out past the meres and down through the long white valley toward the rock, the same road the gray sail had come up. They passed the place on the bank where the Keld stragglers had stood and stared. They passed the place where the Thornwall column had taken off their caps. Nobody spoke of it. Nobody needed to.

On the third morning, in the gray before the first bell, they stood on the ice of the Marrow below Kharven-Seat.

[awed] It stood up out of the white country like a fist raised to the sky, and it was beautiful. Nobody in the column had expected that. A great dark rock rising sheer out of the frozen river on the west, four hundred feet of cliff veined with frost that glittered rose and pale gold where the first light touched it, and lit all the way up its face, in the dark before dawn, by lamps. Worked lamps, hundreds of them, in cages of brass along the cliff stair and the terraces and the long curtain walls, burning a steady amber that never flickered, so that the whole rock seemed strung with beads of honey. Halfway up it the silver house of the Shirogane sat on its terrace like a bird on a ledge, its tall windows glowing blue-white from within. Above that the long black roof of the Hall of Verdicts on the crown, steaming faintly where the snow melted off it over the heated stone. The Tenrai keep on the east, its gold leaf catching the lamplight in long bright bars. A lift-cage of black iron and brass hung on its cables from the cliff-top to the river ford, lit inside like a lantern. And down at the foot of the rock, where the poor of the Seat lived in the lower town along the river, a thousand small orange points of oil-light, crowded together, the smoke of their chimneys going straight up in the still air into the last of the aurora, which hung over the whole of it in a slow green curtain fading into the blue of the morning. The Tenrai banners hung gold and crimson from every wall. And under everything, through the rock and the walls and the ice under their feet, they could hear it. The draw-hum. The low steady sound of the Guild main coming up from the south under the river through its brass and slate, feeding the wards. It had been humming under the Seat for three hundred years. It had never once stopped.

Muken stood on the ice at the head of the Stark vanguard at the foot of the south road, in the red harness, with the bare black helmet on his head now and the gray blade at his hip, and looked up at the main gate.

He could see the ward-stone.

The one he had seen in the place where the threads run. Set into the foundation of the gatehouse, low, on the left, a block of gray granite with Tenrai gold worked into its face in a pattern of angular glyphs, humming with the draw. And under the hum of the draw, under the gold, the other note. The sour one. The note of an oath laid by a man who had not meant it. He could hear it from here, half a mile off, on the ice. He had been hearing it for three days.

He waited.

On his right, Lorn Stark sat his horse in his father's mail. On his left, Godfrey Greymane, on foot, at the head of the Greymane foot in their long gray file, with a new gray cloak at last, sewn for him in the night by the women of the long houses. Behind them, the Stark vanguard and the white banner, blank as new snow.

Far off to the east, on the hills, where the Thornwood stood dark against the white, nothing moved.

[long pause] The first bell rang in the Hall of Verdicts.

[slowly] It came down off the rock in the still cold air, deep and slow, one stroke. And as it rang, under the ice of the Marrow, under the rock, under the Seat, the draw-hum faltered.

It did not stop at once. It stuttered. It went up in pitch, high and thin and wavering, like a man's voice cracking, and then down again, lower than it had ever gone, and then, all at once, between one breath and the next, it was gone.

[hushed] The silence came down on Kharven-Seat like snow.

Every man on the ice heard it. The absence of a sound that had always been there. And then they saw it. Up the whole face of the rock, the worked lamps went out. Not all at once: in a long falling ripple, terrace by terrace, from the crown down, like a string of beads cut and sliding off the thread, amber, amber, dark. The blue-white glow in the tall windows of the silver house went dim and died. The steam stopped rising off the roof of the Hall. Halfway up its cables, the lift-cage jerked, and swung, and hung there, and its light went out. Only the orange oil-lights of the lower town along the river were left burning, small and crowded and stubborn, the way they had always burned. The Seat stood up out of the frozen river in the gray morning with its walls and its gold and its banners, dark from crown to foot, and for the first time in three hundred years it was utterly, perfectly silent, and on the walls the men in Tenrai gold stood and looked at each other, and somebody on the gatehouse began to shout.

Muken drew Tsugigane.

He did not shout. He did not raise the blade. He held it low, in his right hand, at his side, with the point a hand's width off the ice, and the note came up out of it into his palm, clear and high and sweet with the silver thread running through it. And he found the ward-stone's note, half a mile off, the sour lie at its heart, now that the draw no longer covered it. And he held it.

Only that one.

He did not strike. He waited.

[pause] The ward-stone cracked.

They heard it on the ice. A single sharp report, like a log splitting in a deep frost. The gold glyphs on its face went dark all at once, the way a lamp goes out. A crack ran across its face from corner to corner, along the line of the oath its maker had not meant, and the crack went on, into the gatehouse foundation, and found every place where the old Tenrai masons had cut a corner and the frost had been at the mortar for forty winters, and every place the lintel over the gate had been patched and never mended. Only those places. Only what was already dead.

[slowly] The gate of Kharven-Seat came down.

It came down slowly, the great gate and the gatehouse round it, along its own rot, the way the rope had come down in the place where the Works were written. It sagged, and then folded, and then fell, in a long grinding roar and a great cloud of gray dust and old snow, and when the dust cleared there was a hole in the south wall of Kharven-Seat where the gate had been, wide enough to ride twenty abreast. And on either side of it, the wall stood. Every sound stone in it. Untouched.

Muken sheathed Tsugigane.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, commanding]*Now,*" he said.

[narrator] And the north went up the road.

---

They told it afterward round every fire in the north, and they told it for a hundred years, and they never once agreed on how long it took. Some said an hour. Some said the morning. The chroniclers, who were not there, said a day. Lorn Stark, who was, said that it was over before he had finished being surprised that it had begun.

The Stark vanguard went through the broken gate at the gallop with the white banner at its head, and the Greymane foot behind them, and they met the Tenrai house-guard in the square inside the gate, and the house-guard broke. They did not break all at once. Some of them fought, and fought well, the way men fight who have been told all their lives that they are the best soldiers in the north. But their wards had gone dead under them, and the draw-hum was gone, and their practitioners on the walls were reaching for Wellsprings that would not answer through a silent main, and in the middle of it all, behind them, on the eastern wall, there were suddenly Thornwall pikes.

Twelve hundred of them. Up a stair behind a frozen waterfall that nobody in the Seat had known was there, thirty at a time, in the gray before the first bell, while every eye on the rock was on the south road and the boy in red on the ice. They came over the eastern wall in a hedge of iron points with the thorn-horn sounding, and the Tenrai keep was between them and the square, and the Tenrai keep did not hold.

And the Tenrai lords ran.

Muken had told them they would, and they did. Not all of them. But most. Down the river stair under the Tenrai keep, with their families and their gold and their fear, onto the ice of the Marrow, where Edric Ashmore and his horse were waiting. A few of them tried to fight on the ice. Most of them knelt.

The Haldorsson Keld fought to the last. They were Keld, and they had nowhere left to go, and they did not know how to kneel. They fought in the Hall square with their birch lances and their short axes and their glass-headed arrows, without the lifting, without the white cloths, without the Iron Fist, against three thousand, and Muken watched them do it from the broken gate with his hand on Tsugigane's hilt, and did not draw it. He had sworn. Nothing living that did not need cracking. These were men, and they were fighting for the only thing they had left, and the Starks would have to do it the hard way.

The Starks did it the hard way.

[softly] Rob Stark died in the Hall square at the fourth hour, under the black eaves of the Hall of Verdicts, in front of the doors.

He had been at the front all morning. He always was. Rough and cheerful and hoarse, the one who had shouted *We know!* across the Skeldmere at a Tenrai agent standing in his stirrups, the one Yukazuri had sewn up at the Foot while he cursed her and then slept. He had gone into the Haldorssons in the square with his axe and his limewood shield and the boy with the red ears at his left hand, because the boy would not stay at the back, and when a Haldorsson came at the boy low from the side with a birch lance, Rob Stark stepped across him.

The leaf-shaped head went in under his right arm, where the mail gapped at the armpit. Through the rings and the wool and between the ribs, deep. He did not fall at once. He put his axe into the Haldorsson's skull with the lance still in him, and then he sat down on the cobbles of the Hall square in the sudden quiet with his back against the black stone of the Hall's stair, the shaft standing out of his side, and looked surprised.

Lorn reached him first. Mark a breath after.

There was no saving it. Muken knew that from twenty paces, through the gold: the line of the lance through the chest, the lung on that side filling, the great vessel under it opened. Kiyome knew it when she came running across the square in her white with her hands already red from the wounded. She knelt beside him anyway, and looked, and then looked at Lorn, and did not say anything, and that was how Lorn knew.

Rob Stark looked up at his brother, and at Mark, and at the boy with the red ears, who was on his knees on the cobbles with his hands over his mouth.

"[Yorkshire accent, cheerful, hoarse, weak and wet]Tell her," said Rob. It came out wet. He was smiling. "The little surgeon. Tell her I didn't..." [coughs] He coughed, and there was a great deal of red in it, very bright on the gray stone. "[faint, smiling]Tell her I didn't scream this time."

[narrator, softly] Then he was gone.

[narrator] Lorn Stark knelt on the cobbles of the Hall square with his brother's head in his lap and did not move, while the last of the Haldorssons went down around them. When it was over, there were perhaps forty of them left alive, and Lorn got up off the cobbles, with his brother's blood to the elbows, and had them disarmed and their wounded carried to Kiyome, and did not ask anyone's leave. Muken watched him do it. He thought it was the bravest thing he had seen all day.

It was not a siege. It was not a battle. Afterward, when the chroniclers tried to write it, they found they had no word for it. The Tenrai had held Kharven-Seat for three hundred years. They had the Hall and the cliff and the walls and the wards and twelve hundred house-guard and six hundred Keld and every practitioner of the seven lines who had stood behind Kagenori. They had their word, and their name, and their power.

By noon on the third day, they had nothing. And the north had paid for the Seat with Rob Stark, and with ninety-one others, Starks and Greymanes and Thornwall pikes, whose names Ilse Hallenfeld wrote down that evening in her ledger in her fine cold hand, one by one, without being asked.

To say they were defeated would have been an understatement. The Tenrai of Kharven-Seat were not defeated. They were obliterated. Ended. The way a rope ends, that has been rotten at the core for longer than anyone remembers, the moment someone finally finds its note.

---

### VII · The Plain Blade

Karogane was waiting for him on the stair to the silver house.

It was a long stair, cut into the face of the cliff below the Hall of Verdicts, steep and narrow, with a rail of iron on the outer side and nothing beyond the rail but four hundred feet of air and the frozen river. It went down from the Hall square to the Shirogane terrace. And halfway down it, where it turned on a narrow landing, the Tenrai agent stood with his back to the rock and his plain gray cloak thrown back and his long pale hands quite empty, as though he had been standing there all morning, waiting for someone in particular to come down.

Muken came down the stair alone.

Lorn had wanted to come. Godfrey had wanted to come. He had told them both no, at the head of the stair, quietly, and they had stood there and watched him go.

He stopped three steps above the landing.

Karogane looked up at him. The long pale face. The very pale eyes. For the first time in Muken's memory, there was something in that face. Not much. A tightness at the corners of the mouth. A whiteness at the nostrils, the same as on the ice. And something in the eyes that might, on anyone else, have been called fear, and on Karogane, was only a kind of tiredness.

"[light Japanese accent, soft and dry]So," said Karogane. "The tokenless."

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, steady]Muken Moto," said Muken.

"[light Japanese accent, soft and dry]So I've heard." The soft dry voice was very level. "So the whole north has heard, apparently. Shouted from a woodpile." The pale eyes went over the red harness, the white lacing, the bare black helmet, the gray blade at his hip. "And now you've dressed up as one of her old guard. The Samurai of Fate." Something that was nearly a smile. "[mocking]Your mother wore that, you know. At Gimbzo's side. I saw her once. She looked better in it."

[narrator] Muken said nothing.

"[light Japanese accent, soft and dry]I suppose you've come to kill me," said Karogane.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough]No," said Muken.

[narrator] The pale eyes narrowed a fraction.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, steady]I've come to take you," said Muken, "to stand in the Hall of Verdicts. In front of the seven lines and the Banner Houses. And answer for her." He did not raise his voice. "Then the law will decide what happens to you. The north's law. Not mine."

[narrator] Karogane looked at him for a long moment.

Then his long pale hand went inside his gray cloak, and came out with the sword.

The short straight blade. Plain Tenrai steel with no gold on it anywhere. The one nobody had ever seen until a silver room at the foot of an empty dais. Muken knew it at once. He had never seen it. He knew it.

He could hear it.

"[light Japanese accent, soft and dry]I'd sooner not," said Karogane. "If it's all the same to you. I've stood in the Hall of Verdicts. I've watched what it does." He lifted the plain blade, easily, point toward the boy on the stair above him. "I'd sooner settle it here."

[narrator] Muken drew Tsugigane.

The Tenrai came up the stair at him.

He was fast. He was as fast as Shigure Tenrai had been on the other stair, at the Foot, and that had nearly killed Muken once. He came up the three steps between them in a single stride with the plain blade coming in low and straight at Muken's belly under the edge of the cuirass, where the lamellar met the thigh-guards, the one gap in the red harness, and Muken saw it coming before it moved. He saw it in the gold. The line through Karogane's hips and shoulder lit up an instant before the blade did, the way Aksel Keld's had on the Skeldmere.

He turned the thrust with Tsugigane's back. Steel slid on steel with a long thin hiss. Karogane's blade went past his hip by a finger's width and rang off the rock of the stair-wall behind him.

And as it went past, as the plain blade's flat lay along Tsugigane's for the length of a heartbeat, Muken heard its note.

He had heard it from three steps away. Now he heard it close. And it was not like any other note he had heard in the world. It was not sour, the way a rotten thing is sour, or wavering, the way a lie wavers. It was flat. Dead flat. The note of a thing that had never once in its life been used for anything but the one thing. In the dark. In silver rooms. On stairs. Into the backs of men who did not know it was there. A thing that had no other purpose and never had, and had long since stopped being a blade at all, and had become only that.

[Northern English accent, low and rough, inner voice] *It's dead,* thought Muken. *It's been dead for years.*

[narrator] He held its note.

He did not strike. He only held it, through Tsugigane's steel, along the length of the two blades where they lay together for that one heartbeat on the stair. And the silver thread in Tsugigane's note, the note of seven surgeon's needles folded into the marrow of the blade, went into the plain Tenrai steel and found the place where the dead thing was, and closed on it.

The plain blade broke.

Not with a crack. It simply came apart in Karogane's hand, all along its length, the way a dead branch comes apart when you step on it in the snow, into a dozen gray pieces that rang on the stone of the landing and slid off the edge between the rails of the iron balustrade and fell, turning, four hundred feet, to the frozen river below.

Karogane stood on the landing with the empty hilt in his long pale hand and stared at it.

Muken put the point of Tsugigane against his throat.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, cold]*Kneel,*" said Muken.

[narrator] Karogane did not move.

He stood there with the gray blade at his throat and the empty hilt in his hand, and looked at the boy in red. And for the first time in his life, perhaps, the long pale face did something. It broke. Not much. A crack across it, at the mouth. And what was underneath, for one instant, was the face of a tired, frightened, ordinary man who had spent his whole life being a knife in somebody else's hand, and had just watched the knife come apart.

He knelt.

Muken did not take the point from his throat.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough]Lorn," he said, without looking round. "Godfrey."

[narrator] They were already coming down the stair. They had not stayed at the top. He had known they would not.

---

### VIII · The Silver Chair

The silver house was open.

Nobody had shut the doors. There was nobody left in it to shut them. The servants in gray had fled when the draw stopped, down the river stair with the Tenrai lords, or up into the Hall square to throw themselves on Kiyome's mercy, and the long silver receiving room on the cliff side stood open to the cold, with its lamps out and its silver cages dark and the gray light coming in through the tall windows off the frozen river four hundred feet below.

Muken walked down the length of it alone.

The floor was pale gray stone polished until it held the light like still water. It was very clean. He walked across it, in the red harness and the bare black helmet, with Tsugigane in its saya at his hip, and did not look down. He could hear it. Under the polish, in the stone, near the foot of the dais, there was a note. Very faint. The note of a place where something had been spilled, and wiped up, and wiped up again, until there was nothing left to see.

He stopped at the foot of the dais.

[long pause] Kagenori Tenrai Moto was sitting on the lowest step.

He was sitting the way a tired man sits on a stair at the end of a long day, with his elbows on his knees and his narrow head bowed, in his gold-trimmed black. There was a small cup of black lacquer on the step beside him, empty, and a folded letter on the step beside the cup, sealed in gold, and on the face of the letter, in a fine hand, a single word. A name.

[softly] He had been dead for some hours.

Muken stood and looked at him for a long time.

He could see the thread. The one he had seen in the place where the threads run. A thread of gold running out of the man in gold-trimmed black at the foot of the empty dais, humming grief. It had stopped humming. But it was there, and it ran up the dais, three steps, to the silver chair.

Shizuha Shirogane was sitting in it.

She sat very upright, with her hands folded in her lap, in her silver-gray gown with the frost-work down the front. It had been cleaned. There was no mark on it anywhere. Her short gray hair was perfectly in place. Her pale coin-bright eyes were open, and fixed on the boy in red at the foot of the dais.

"[light Japanese accent, elderly woman, cold, precise]He wouldn't sit in it," said Shizuha Shirogane.

[narrator] Her voice was small and dry and perfectly clear.

"[light Japanese accent, elderly woman, cold, precise]Kagenori." She did not look down at him. "Twenty years he built this chair for me. Twenty years. And I never sat in it. Not once. I stood at the foot of it, so that everyone who came into this room would see the chair, empty, and know who it was waiting for." Her hands did not move in her lap. "He begged me, this morning, when the hum stopped. To sit in it. Once. Before you came. So that he could see it, once, before the end." A pause. "I told him I would, if he would drink from that cup first. I thought he wouldn't." She looked down, at last, at the man on the step. "He did."

[narrator] Muken said nothing.

"[light Japanese accent, elderly woman, cold, precise]So I sat," said Shizuha. "It seemed only fair." She looked back up. "[dry]It's very uncomfortable. I'd always wondered."

[narrator] The gray light came in through the tall windows. Somewhere far below, on the frozen river, a horse was neighing.

"[light Japanese accent, elderly woman, cold, precise]You came yourself," said Shizuha Shirogane. "In her colors. In the red harness. Kasane's." Something moved in her face that was not quite a smile. "I'd hoped you'd come burning. I'd hoped you'd come down that stair with the cliff cracking under your feet and the Hall falling in behind you and your eyes gone gold to the edges, the way his did, at the end. Gimbzo's." She tilted her head a degree. "I'd have been content, if you had. I'd have won."

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, quiet]I know," said Muken.

[narrator] The coin-bright eyes held his.

"[light Japanese accent, elderly woman, cold, precise]Instead you've come down my stair with your sword in its sheath," said Shizuha, "and you've broken my gate along a crack I didn't know it had, and every stone round it still standing. And you've taken my knife without killing him." Her voice did not rise. "Karogane told me once that you could be broken. That you had a crack in you, under the breastbone, and it was very old, and very hot, and all anyone had to do was find its note." A pause. "[bitter]He was right. I found it. I struck it as hard as I have ever struck anything in my life."

[narrator] She looked at him a long moment.

"[light Japanese accent, elderly woman, cold, precise, quietly]Why didn't it break?" said Shizuha Shirogane.

[narrator] Muken looked up at her, the small gray woman in the silver chair at the top of the dais that had been built for her by a dead man, with her hands folded in her lap and the cliff and the frozen river behind her through the tall windows.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, quiet]It did," he said.

[narrator] He did not say anything else. He did not explain. He stood at the foot of the dais, at the bottom of the three steps, beside the dead man in gold-trimmed black with the letter sealed in gold beside him, and he did not go up.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough]Kiyome," he said, without turning round.

[narrator] She was in the doorway. She had been there since he came in. He had heard her note, clear and steady, the white line.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, steady]Lady Shirogane is to come with us to the Hall," said Muken. "She's to be treated with every courtesy her rank deserves." He was quiet a moment. "And Lord Kagenori is to be taken up and laid out properly. In the Tenrai keep. In his own colors." He looked down at the letter on the step, at the single word on its face. "And that's to go with him. Unopened."

"[light Japanese accent, calm, warm]Sire," said Kiyome Byakuya.

[narrator] Muken turned from the dais and walked back down the long silver room toward the door, across the pale gray floor that held the light like still water. And at the place near the foot of the dais where the note was, the faint note under the polish, he did not stop. But he slowed, very slightly. And then went on.

Behind him, in the silver chair, Shizuha Shirogane sat very upright with her hands folded in her lap, and watched him go, and did not say anything at all.

---

# Chapter Nineteen: Seven Lines and One Blood

[narrator] *Book One of the Kōkan · Chapter Nineteen: Seven Lines and One Blood*

> *There was peace in the north that spring. The chroniclers who came after would count it: the first spring in forty years that no levy went south, the first in twenty that no Kōkan was hunted, the first in a thousand that the seven lines sat at one table under one name. They would call it the Spring of the Red Line, and write it in gold. The northern clans did not call it anything. They only said, when they spoke of it at all, that it was the year the boy came home from the ice with nobody.*
>
> *From the* Annals of the North-Forged Crown

---

### I · The Empty Palace

The Tenrai had kept the Kōkan's palace as a treasury.

It stood on the crown of the rock beside the Hall of Verdicts, older than the Hall and lower, a long gray house of the old Kōkan building with deep eaves and dark beams and floors of black stone worn smooth by a thousand years of feet. For three hundred years, since the Tenrai took the Seat, nobody had lived in it. They had filled its rooms with chests. Grain-tallies and tax-ledgers and the deeds to half the north, stacked to the beams in the old Kōkan bedchambers, and the iron-bound strongboxes of the Tenrai treasury in the great hall where the Kōkan kings had once sat to give judgment. They had put a lock on every door. They had never once lit a fire in it.

Muken lit one.

He did it himself, on the first night, in the small room at the back of the house that the Hallenfeld clerks, going through the chests, told him had been the Kōkan kings' own: a plain square room with a low ceiling and a single narrow window looking north, over the frozen river and the white country and the long dark line of the hills toward the Foot. There was a hearth in it, cold for three centuries. He cleaned it out with his own hands. Old ash, and a dead bird, and a nest of something that had been mice once. He carried up wood from the Hall kitchens. He built the fire the way his mother had taught him to build one in the galleries, small and close and economical, so that it would burn all night on very little, and lit it, and sat down in front of it on the black stone floor.

He was still in the red harness. He had not taken it off since the gatehouse. Nobody had dared suggest it.

The fire took. The small room filled slowly with warmth and the smell of pine and old dust burning. Somewhere outside, in the Hall square, Stark riders were singing round a brazier, low and rough and a little drunk, an old Kharven song about a woodpile. Somewhere further off, on the river, a horse was neighing. The draw-hum had come back at dusk, when Ilse Hallenfeld sent word to her draw-masters that the main could be mended, and it lay under everything again now, low and steady, under the rock and the ice and the walls, the way it had for three hundred years. Through the narrow window he could see the Seat come back to light: the worked lamps along the cliff stair and the terraces kindling again one by one in their brass cages, amber climbing the dark rock from the river to the crown like a slow tide coming in, until the whole fist of the Seat hung lit over the frozen Marrow again, and the aurora came out above it, green and violet, rippling. It was the most beautiful thing he had ever seen. He looked at it for a while and found he could not feel anything about it at all.

He sat in front of the fire and listened to all of it.

And under all of it, the singing of the world. He could still hear it. He thought he always would. The stone of the old Kōkan house, low and patient and very old. The fire. The frozen river. The ridge, far off, three days to the north, and the cairns on the ridge, and the last cairn, with the small stone on top no bigger than a hen's egg.

[softly] He did not hear her note.

He had not heard it since the road out. Since he opened his hand in the place where the threads run and let it go. He had known he would not. He listened for it anyway. He sat in front of the fire in the Kōkan kings' own room on the first night of his reign over the whole of the north, in his dead mother's armor, and listened for the note of a thirteen-year-old girl with a needle, among all the notes of the world, and it was not there.

[slowly] He had nobody.

He sat and understood it, slowly, the way a man understands that the cold has got into his feet. His mother was in the salt under Halcrest. Yusif was dead three years, somewhere on a road he had never been told the name of. His father he had never known, and was dead in the mud at Shimane, and was a monster. And his sister was on the ridge under the stones.

He had Lorn. He had Godfrey. He had Kiyome, who was his mother's sister, and who loved him, and whom he did not know. He had three thousand people who would die for him, and the whole of the north knelt at his feet, and the seven lines of the Moto waiting in the Hall of Verdicts in the morning to be told what he had decided they would become.

[softly] And he had nobody.

There was no one in all the world who had known him when he was nine years old at the top of a gallery shaft. No one who had slept on a pallet beside him in the cold so that neither of them froze. No one who had given him back half her share when he was not looking. No one who would give him an earful.

He sat in front of the fire with his hands open on his knees, and did not weep. He had done that, in the place where the threads run. It was done. He only sat, and let it go through him, into the black stone floor of the Kōkan house, into the ground, the way the Bound Flame had taught him, and held none of it longer than it needed to be held.

[slowly] It needed to be held a very long time.

[pause] Toward midnight, there was a knock on the door.

He did not answer. The door opened anyway, very quietly, and Lorn Stark came in with a blanket over one arm and a bowl in his hands, and set the bowl down on the stone floor beside him without a word. Bone broth, with the fat still floating on it. His hands were clean now. Somebody had made him wash them. His brother was lying in the Tenrai keep on a table under a Stark cloak, with Mark sitting beside him, and the Waiting would begin at dawn, and Lorn had come here first, with a bowl. Then he sat down on the floor a little way off, against the wall, with the blanket in his lap, and drew up his knees, and said nothing.

After a while, Godfrey came in too, and sat down on Muken's other side.

After a while longer, Kiyome.

They did not speak. It was not the Waiting. Nobody had died. But it was something like it. They sat on the black stone floor of the Kōkan kings' room in front of the small fire, the four of them, and nobody said anything at all, and the Stark riders went on singing in the square about the woodpile, and the fire burned down, and Lorn got up once and fed it, and sat down again.

Muken drank the broth.

It was cold by then. He drank it anyway, all of it, and set the bowl down, and sat looking at the fire.

"[Yorkshire accent, young, rough, quietly]The first bowl," said Lorn Stark quietly, after a long time, "goes to the one who can't fill it himself."

[narrator] Muken did not answer.

[softly] But some time before dawn, he fell asleep, sitting up, with his back against the wall and his head against Godfrey Greymane's shoulder, and Godfrey did not move until morning.

---

### II · The Hall of Verdicts

The Hall of Verdicts was a long black room under a long black roof on the crown of the rock, and it had been built for one purpose, and it had served that purpose for a thousand years.

The floor was black stone, polished, and it fell away from the doors toward the far end in three wide shallow steps, so that whoever stood at the back of the hall looked down on whoever stood at the front. The walls were black stone. The beams were black oak, so old they had gone hard as iron. There were no windows. The only light came from the lamps, a hundred of them, worked lamps on draw in cages of black iron hung from the beams on long chains, each one burning a clear steady gold that laid the polish of the floor with a hundred small suns, and from a single round opening in the roof directly over the place at the bottom of the hall where those who came to be judged were made to stand, so that they stood in a shaft of gray daylight and everyone else stood in the dark.

At the far end, on a low dais of black stone, there were seven chairs.

Six of them stood in a long shallow arc, each in its line's colors. Silver for the Shirogane. Gold for the Tenrai. White for the Byakuya. Copper for the Akagane. Indigo for the Amagiri. Black, with the crimson hidden in it, for the Kurenai. And in the middle of the arc, a little forward of the others, a little higher, the seventh.

The red chair.

It was not grand. It was smaller than the others, if anything. A plain high-backed chair of red lacquer over dark wood, very old, with no ornament on it at all except, worked into the top of the back in fine black lines, a mark. A long down-stroke. A hook. A crossing. A small closed loop at the foot of it like a knot in a rope.

Nobody had sat in it in twenty years. Gimbzo never had. And before Gimbzo, for three hundred years, the Kōkan kings had sat in it only on sufferance, under the Tenrai, in a hall the Tenrai held.

Muken stood in front of it for a long moment, in the morning, before the others came in.

[slowly] Then he sat down.

---

They came in.

He watched them come, from the red chair, in the lamplight. All of them. Down the three shallow steps of the black floor and into the dark of the hall, and found their places, and stood.

The Banner Houses first. Lorn Stark in his father's mail, with the Stark wolf on his breast and his father's axe across his back, who stood at the front on the left with his weight even on both feet and his jaw working on the left side. Godfrey Greymane in his new gray cloak, very straight, beside him. Hisae of Thornwall, small and upright in gray felt, with her Byakuya eyes. Edric Ashmore in brown and russet with the boar on his breast and his broken nose and his look of faint amazement. Ilse Hallenfeld in her dark coat lined with wolf, with her ledger under her arm and ink on her gloves.

And the Keld.

They had brought Aksel Keld in on a litter. Four of his own captains carried it, the ones who had come in off the tundra under a white cloth when they heard the Seat had fallen, to ask what the king of the north meant to do with their warlord. He lay on the litter under a heap of reindeer hides with his chest strapped and his ruined face swollen and purple from the brow down, the nose set crooked, the lips split and scabbed. His right arm lay on top of the hides. The stump of it was bare, the old wrist-end, ridged with scar. There was no iron hand. There would never be another. Borin Ironheart had been asked, and had said that some things were better left unmade.

His narrow black eyes were open, and on the red chair.

Then the seven lines.

Kiyome Byakuya, in her near-white, with the glass beads of her veil pinned back and her sword at her hip without its knot, went to the white chair and stood beside it. She did not sit. Tetsugen Akagane, broad as a door, in his copper coat, went to the copper chair and stood beside it. Hayato Amagiri went to the indigo chair. His uncle had not come. His uncle had been on the ice of the Marrow with the other Tenrai lords who ran, and had knelt to Edric Ashmore, and was in a room in the Tenrai keep with a Stark rider at the door. Hayato stood beside the indigo chair with his restless fingers folding and unfolding the corner of his rain-cape, and the juniper still caught in the toggle.

Sumie Kurenai came last of the Moto lords who had a chair to come to.

She was a tall dark woman of fifty in black and ash, with the crimson of her line hidden in the lining of her sleeves, and she walked down the black floor of the Hall of Verdicts the way she had walked down it a month ago to carry the Kurenai blade to a block in the square where Ulf Stark and Wystan Greymane knelt. She did not look at Lorn or Godfrey as she passed them. She went to the black chair and stood beside it. And then she turned, and looked at the boy in the red chair, and went down on one knee on the black stone.

She did not say anything. The Kurenai never did. They were the executioners' line. They had carried out the verdicts of the Hall for a thousand years, whatever the verdicts were, and asked no questions, and been hated for it, and borne it.

She knelt there with her head bowed. And then she stood, and stood beside the black chair, and waited.

Two chairs were empty. The silver. The gold.

And then the doors at the top of the hall opened again, and two more people came in, and the whole Hall turned to look.

---

### III · The Ones Who Were Chosen

The first was a young woman of perhaps twenty-five, in silver-gray.

She came down the black floor slowly, alone, with her hands clasped in front of her. She was small, like her sister. Gray-eyed, like her sister, with the same coin-bright rings in the gray. But her hair was not gray. It was black, long, braided down her back, and her face was not hard as a hatchet. It was a young face, pale, set very carefully, and it had been crying, and had stopped.

Nanami Shirogane.

Shizuha's sister. Thirty-six years her junior, the last child of a mother who had not expected another. She had been at the white house in the Shiraki hills for the last ten years, sent there by her sister to be raised by the Byakuya, because the Shirogane and the Byakuya had kept a fostering between them for three hundred years, each sending a child to the other, so that each house would always have someone inside the other's walls. She had not been at the Seat when the girl came up the stair. She had not known. Kiyome had sent for her, three days ago, before the Seat fell, and told her.

She stopped beside the silver chair, and did not sit, and looked at the boy in the red chair with her sister's eyes, and her face did not change.

The second was a man of forty in plain gray.

He was tall and lean, with a long pale face and very pale eyes, and the whole Hall stirred when it saw him, because for a moment, in the lamplight, he was Karogane. Then he came closer and he was not. He was older. Grayer. His face was lined in places Karogane's was not, round the eyes and the mouth, the lines of a man who had laughed more in his life than his brother. And he walked down the black floor of the Hall of Verdicts the way a man walks who has been told he is going to his own hanging and has decided to do it with his back straight.

Shiro Tenrai.

Karogane's elder brother. The Tenrai keep's steward of records for twenty years, who had kept Kagenori's ledgers and never once been asked to put his hand to anything else. Hayato had found him in the keep on the afternoon the Seat fell, sitting at his desk in the record-room with the door open and his hands folded on a closed ledger, waiting. [light Japanese accent, steady, composed, remembered] *I knew one of you would come,* [narrator] he had said. [light Japanese accent, steady, composed, remembered] *I've been keeping the books for this house for twenty years. I know what it owes.*

[narrator] He stopped beside the gold chair.

And beside him, holding his hand, there was a boy.

Ten, perhaps. Small and thin and pale, in gray like his father, with the long Tenrai face and the very pale Tenrai eyes, and the faint ring-lines of the seven lines' blood in them. He held his father's hand and looked round the Hall of Verdicts with those very pale eyes, at the lamps and the black beams and the seven chairs and the boy in red in the middle of them. He did not look frightened. He looked as though he were counting something.

Nobody in the Hall knew his name, then. Shiro had not given it. It would be many years before anyone thought to ask.

---

Muken looked at the two of them for a long time from the red chair.

He had chosen them. He and Kiyome, in the long room at the Foot, on the night before they marched, with the map of the Seat on the table and the Dawi asleep on the floor. *When it falls,* Kiyome had said, [light Japanese accent, calm, warm, remembered] *the silver and the gold will need someone to sit in them. You can leave them empty. Or you can burn them. Or you can fill them.* She had looked at him a long time. *If you leave them empty, someone will come for them. If you burn them, someone will come for you. If you fill them...* She had stopped.

[Northern English accent, low and rough, inner voice] *If I fill them,* he had said, *with someone who has every reason to hate me, and nothing to gain by it.*

[narrator] He looked at them now, in the lamplight, at the foot of the dais. Nanami Shirogane, with her sister's eyes and her black braid and her careful face, standing beside the silver chair. Shiro Tenrai, with his brother's face and his own lines in it, standing beside the gold, with his son's hand in his.

They looked back at him.

They did not look happy. They looked like what they were: two people who had been brought into the Hall of Verdicts on the morning their brother and their sister were to be judged in it, and told they were to sit in the chairs their brother and their sister had stood behind, and understood exactly what that meant. That the chair was not a reward. It was a sentence of its own. That they would sit in it for the rest of their lives under the eyes of a boy in red who had every reason to watch them, and that every Shirogane and every Tenrai who had ever hated the red line would come to them now, and whisper, and they would have to send them away. Over and over. Forever.

They looked disappointed. They looked afraid. And they knew. Their fates had been spelled out for them the moment they walked through the doors, and there was nothing in the world they could do about it but stand there, beside their chairs, and let it be done.

Muken understood that look. He had worn it himself, in the long room, the morning Kiyome drew the mark in the dust.

---

### IV · The Verdict

They brought in Shizuha and Karogane together.

The Kurenai brought them. That was the old law. Four Kurenai in black and ash, two to each, walking them down the black floor of the Hall from the doors to the bottom of the shallow steps, to the place under the round opening in the roof where the shaft of gray daylight came down. And left them standing in it, in the light, with everyone else in the dark.

Shizuha Shirogane stood very upright in her silver-gray gown, cleaned, with the frost-work down the front and her short gray hair perfectly in place. Her hands were bound in front of her with white cord. She did not look at the boy in the red chair. She looked at the silver chair beside him, and at her sister standing beside it, for a long time. Nanami did not look away. Neither of them said anything.

Karogane stood beside her in his plain gray, with his hands bound, and his long pale face quite still again. Whatever had cracked in it on the stair had been mended. He looked at the gold chair, and at his brother standing beside it, and at the boy holding his brother's hand. Something moved, very slightly, at the corner of his mouth. Then it was gone.

The Hall was silent.

Muken stood up from the red chair.

He came down off the dais, slowly, in the red harness, with Tsugigane at his hip, down the three low steps of black stone, and stood at the edge of the shaft of gray light, so that they were in the light and he was in the dark, and looked at them.

He did not speak for a long time.

He was listening. He could hear them both. Every note. Karogane's, flat and still and tired, the note of a knife that had just found out it was only a knife. Shizuha's, small and dry and clear and very hard, and under it, very far down, a thousand years old, the long sour note of the wound. He could hear it going out of her into the Hall, toward the silver chair, toward her sister.

[resonant, otherworldly, slow, remembered] *End the woman, if you must,* [narrator] the Bound Flame had said. [resonant, otherworldly, slow, remembered] *But end the wound with her. Close it.*

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, cold, quiet]Shizuha Shirogane," said Muken. "Karogane Tenrai."

[narrator] His voice was quiet. It filled the Hall anyway, the way a stone fills a pool when you drop it in, every word set down with that weight in it that Kiyome had heard in the long room, and the hundred lamps in their iron cages on their long chains swung very slightly, all together, as though a wind had gone through.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, cold]For your treason," said Muken, "against Kharven, and against its people."

[narrator] He let it sit.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, cold]Against the long houses, whose grain you sent south while their children ate bark. Against the Banner Houses, whose fathers you put on the block in the square outside this door. Against the seven lines, whom you set against each other for a thousand years so that one of you could sit in a silver chair." His voice did not rise. "[bitter]And against a surgeon of thirteen, with a needle in her apron, who had never in her life hurt anyone who did not need closing. Whom you had dragged into a silver room on her knees, and killed, and sent home to her brother down the river on a pike."

[narrator] Nanami Shirogane closed her eyes.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, cold, slowly]You will be executed," said Muken.

[narrator] The Hall did not move.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, cold]Tomorrow, at the first bell. In the square outside this door, where Ulf Stark and Wystan Greymane knelt." He looked from one of them to the other. "And all your possessions, your lands and your houses and your people, will come under the crown of the north. Under my hand." He paused. "Not to be kept. To be given back. To whoever sits in the chairs you stood behind, when they have shown me they can be trusted to hold them."

[narrator] He turned his head, very slightly, toward the silver chair, and the gold.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, steady]And the unification of the Moto," said Muken, "and all its territories, the seven lines and the Banner Houses and every clan and every fire under the Sky between the Polar Reach and the southern border, is to be ratified. Here. Today. In front of all of you." He turned back. "[slowly]Seven lines, and one blood. Under one crown."

[narrator] Shizuha Shirogane looked at him at last.

The coin-bright eyes, in the shaft of gray daylight, fixed on the boy in red in the dark at the edge of it. And for a long moment she said nothing at all.

"[light Japanese accent, elderly woman, cold, precise]You'll do it yourself?" she said. "Tomorrow."

"[Northern English accent, low and rough]Yes," said Muken.

"[light Japanese accent, elderly woman, cold, precise]Not the Kurenai."

"[Northern English accent, low and rough]No."

"[light Japanese accent, elderly woman, cold, precise]Why?" said Shizuha Shirogane.

[narrator] Muken looked at her.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, steady]Because I passed the verdict," he said. "And I'll not put it in someone else's hand and stand back and watch. That's what you did. In your silver room." He was quiet a moment. "[cold]I'll not be like you. Not in that. Not in anything."

[narrator] Something went across the small hard face in the shaft of light. Very fast. Gone.

"[light Japanese accent, elderly woman, cold, precise]And her," said Shizuha. She did not look round. "My sister. In my chair."

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, steady]Nanami Shirogane," said Muken, "will sit the silver chair. If she will." He did not look at Nanami. "And Shiro Tenrai the gold. If he will. And they'll sit in them under my eye for the rest of their lives, and every Shirogane and every Tenrai who comes to them in the night and whispers that the red line must be ended will be sent away. By them. In their own voices." He held the old woman's eyes. "Every one. For as long as they live. Until there is nobody left in either line who remembers why the whispering started."

[narrator] He stepped forward, one pace, into the edge of the shaft of gray light.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, quiet]That's what I'm going to do with your wound," said Muken. "The one you were born in. The one your mother was born in, and hers. The one you meant to pass on to your sister with your chair." He looked at her. "[slowly]I'm going to close it. And I'm going to make her hold the edges together for the rest of her life, until it heals."

[narrator] Shizuha Shirogane stared at him.

And then, for the first time since the silver room, since the girl on her knees, the small hard upright woman in the silver-gray gown did something nobody in that Hall had ever seen her do.

[long pause] She looked away.

---

### V · The Ratification

They did it in the afternoon, in the Hall of Verdicts, with the lamps lit and the doors open and the whole square outside full of people, Starks and Greymanes and Thornwall pikes and Ashmore riders and the women from the long houses who had walked down the Marrow behind the army, standing in the snow in the cold to hear it.

The Hallenfeld clerks wrote it down.

Ilse Hallenfeld wrote it herself, at a small table at the side of the Hall, in her ledger, in her fine cold hand, word for word as it was spoken. She did not let anyone else touch the pen. [crisp German accent, cold] *A debt is a debt,* [narrator] she said, when Hayato asked her why. [crisp German accent, cold] *This is the biggest one I've ever seen recorded. I'll not have a clerk smudge it.*

[narrator] They came forward one at a time to the foot of the dais, where the boy in red sat in the red chair, and swore.

Lorn Stark first. For the Starks of Hollenmark. He came down the shallow steps of the black floor and stood at the foot of the dais in his father's mail and put his right hand flat on his chest, and closed it into a fist against his breastbone, and pressed it there once, hard, and held it.

"[Yorkshire accent, young, rough, solemn]By the sky that covers us," said Lorn Stark. "House Stark. To the red chair, and to the man in it." His voice was rough. "[voice thick]My father would have. And Rob."

[narrator] Godfrey Greymane, for the Greymanes of Ridgewall. "[soft Scottish accent, measured, very quietly]The ridge does not move," he said, very quietly. "We'll hold it. For you, and for whoever comes after." He looked up at Muken. "[warm]My father would have, too."

[narrator] Hisae of Thornwall, small and upright, Byakuya-born. She swore for Thornhaven and the Thornwood and the corridors, and when she had finished she did something none of the Banner lords had done. She turned, and looked at Kiyome Byakuya standing beside the white chair, and the two of them, the old woman and the not-so-old one, both born in the white house in the Shiraki hills, both with the same gray eyes, said together, very softly, so that only the front of the Hall could hear it:

"[two women together, light Japanese accent, softly, reverent]*And the red line before the white. Until the end of the red line.*"

[narrator] Edric Ashmore, for the Ashmore marches. He swore with his hand on the black stone of the dais, as he had sworn with his hand on the junction stone below Ashbury, and looked faintly amazed at himself all the way through.

Ilse Hallenfeld, for the junction and the counting-house. She did not come forward. She swore from her small table at the side of the Hall, without looking up from her ledger, and wrote it down as she said it.

And then the Keld.

They carried Aksel Keld down the shallow steps on his litter, the four captains, and set him down at the foot of the dais. And the Iron Fist of the Keld lay on his back under the reindeer hides with his chest strapped and his ruined face swollen and purple and the bare stump of his right arm lying on top of the hides, and looked up at the boy in the red chair with his narrow black eyes.

He did not try to rise. He could not have.

"[Icelandic accent, flat, rough, young man, thick wet rasp]The Keld will swear," said Aksel Keld.

[narrator] His voice was a thick wet rasp through the broken nose and the split lips. It carried anyway.

"[Icelandic accent, flat, rough, young man, thick wet rasp]To you," he said. "Not to the red chair. Not to the seven lines. Not to the Seat." The narrow black eyes held Muken's. "To you. The boy on the ice. Who broke the iron hand with a song, and stood over me with his sword up, and stopped." He breathed, a short catching breath. "[strained]We'll swear to that man. On one condition."

[narrator] The Hall was very quiet.

"[Icelandic accent, flat, rough, young man]Stay worth following," said Aksel Keld.

[narrator] Muken looked down at him a long moment from the red chair.

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, steady]That's an honest condition," he said. "I accept it."

[narrator] Something moved in the ruined face on the litter. It might have been a smile. It hurt him to do it, whatever it was. He did it anyway.

"[Icelandic accent, flat, rough, young man, wry]Then the Keld are yours," said the Iron Fist. "While you're worth it."

---

[narrator] The seven lines came last.

Kiyome Byakuya, for the white line. She came to the foot of the dais and went down on both knees on the black stone, and laid her sword across them with its hilt toward him, without its knot, the way she had in the long room, and bowed her head.

"[light Japanese accent, calm, warm]Purity before power," she said. "Peace before the blade. And the red line before the white." She lifted her head. "[gently]I've said it. You know the rest."

[narrator] Tetsugen Akagane, for the copper line. He did not kneel. He had said he would not. He stood at the foot of the dais, broad as a door, and put his scarred hand flat on his chest, the Stark way, and held it there.

"[light Japanese accent, flat, iron, deep]The Akagane never swore to the red," he said, in his flat iron voice. "We only ever obeyed it. I'll not obey you, boy." His heavy face did not change. "I'll follow you. It's a different thing. You'll find out how different the first time you're wrong."

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, dry]I'm counting on it," said Muken.

[narrator] Hayato Amagiri, for the indigo line, in his uncle's place. He swore in the plain working cadence he used for distances, quickly, the words tumbling over each other, as though slowing down would lose him the end of it, and at the end of it, he looked up at the boy in the red chair and said, "[light Japanese accent, quick, wry, earnest]Two days and a road, sire. Always. Whatever you need."

[narrator] Sumie Kurenai, for the black line. She came forward and knelt, and said the oath of her house in a low dark voice that nobody in the Hall had ever heard her use, the old oath of the executioners' line, that they would carry the verdicts of the red chair wherever the red chair sent them, and ask no questions. And when she had finished, she stayed on her knees, and lifted her head, and looked at the boy in red.

"[light Japanese accent, low, dark]You'll not need us tomorrow," she said. "At the first bell."

"[Northern English accent, low and rough]No," said Muken.

[narrator] The Kurenai woman looked at him a long moment.

"[light Japanese accent, low, dark, uncertain]No one's ever said that to us," said Sumie Kurenai. "In a thousand years." She got up, slowly. "I'm not sure what we're for, now."

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, gently]You'll find out," said Muken. "I think it'll be closing things."

[narrator] She did not understand him. He could see that. But she bowed, very low, lower than she had ever bowed in her life, and went back to the black chair.

And then the silver, and the gold.

Nanami Shirogane came forward to the foot of the dais. She stood there, small and pale, with her black braid and her sister's eyes, and her hands clasped very tightly in front of her, and looked up at him. She did not kneel.

"[light Japanese accent, young woman, careful, voice shaking]I'll sit it," she said. Her voice shook, once, and steadied. "[steadier]The silver chair. If that's what you've decided."

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, quiet]It's what I've decided," said Muken. "If you'll have it."

"[light Japanese accent, young woman, careful]I don't want it."

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, quiet]I know."

"[light Japanese accent, young woman, careful, firm]She's my sister." Nanami's voice did not shake this time. "Tomorrow you're going to..." She stopped. "And I'm going to sit in her chair. Under your eye. For the rest of my life."

"[Northern English accent, low and rough]Yes."

[narrator] Nanami Shirogane looked at him for a very long time.

"[light Japanese accent, young woman, careful, slowly]I'll sit it," she said at last. "And I'll send them away. Every one who comes to me in the night. In my own voice." She lifted her chin, a degree, exactly as her sister did. "[bitter]And one day, when you're dead, I'll have done it so long that I'll have forgotten how to do anything else. That's what you want, isn't it."

"[Northern English accent, low and rough, quiet]That's what I want," said Muken.

[narrator] She swore. The oath of the silver line, in a voice like her sister's, small and dry and clear. And at the end of it, she went to the silver chair, and sat down in it, and folded her hands in her lap, and looked straight ahead, and did not look at anyone, and did not cry.

Shiro Tenrai came forward with his son's hand in his.

He swore for the gold line without being asked, steadily, the long pale face quite composed, the lines round his eyes very deep in the lamplight. At the end of it he said only, "[light Japanese accent, steady, composed]I've kept the books for twenty years, Your Grace. I know what the Tenrai owe. I'll see it paid." And he went to the gold chair and sat down in it, and the boy stood beside him with his hand still in his father's and his very pale eyes going round the Hall, counting.

[narrator] And it was done.

Ilse Hallenfeld laid down her pen.

"[crisp German accent, cold]Ratified," she said, into the silence. "Witnessed. Recorded." She blotted the page, very carefully. "Seven lines. Six Banner Houses. One crown." She looked up at the boy in the red chair with her eyes like gray ice. "It's the largest single entry the Hallenfeld counting-house has ever made, Your Grace. I'll want it noted that I made it."

---

### VI · The First Bell

[slowly] He did it himself, at the first bell, in the square outside the Hall of Verdicts, where Ulf Stark and Wystan Greymane had knelt.

He did not make a speech. Nobody made a speech. The square was full, and silent, and cold, and the breath of three thousand people went up over it in a single white cloud. The Kurenai brought them out. Shizuha first, in her silver-gray, and then Karogane, in his plain gray. Neither of them said anything. Shizuha looked once, at the end, at the doors of the Hall, where her sister stood in the doorway in silver with her hands clasped in front of her. Then she looked away.

Muken drew Tsugigane.

It sang in his hand. Clear and high and sweet, with the silver thread running through it.

He did not use Hibi. He did not need to. There was nothing dead to find. He did it with the edge and the weight and the long clean line of the cut that Yusif Stark had taught him in the back of a worked-out gallery with a stick of ash, the way a thing is done that must be done, once each, cleanly, and then is over.

[softly] He did not look away from either of them.

When it was done, he wiped the blade on a cloth Kiyome handed him, and looked at it. The gray steel. The bright seam of silver by the guard. There was no mark on it. There never would be. He sheathed it, and turned, and walked back up the steps of the Hall of Verdicts past Nanami Shirogane in the doorway, who did not look at him, and did not move, and stood there in the cold in her silver until the Kurenai had finished, and the square had emptied, and the snow had begun, very softly, to fall.

---

### VII · The Spring

[narrator] There was peace in the north.

It came the way the thaw came, slowly, and then all at once. The draw-hum came back under the Seat and stayed there, and the Hallenfeld clerks went through the Tenrai treasury chest by chest and sent the grain-tallies back to the long houses they had been taken from, and the grain after them, up the Marrow on sledges and down Hayato's indigo roads on mules, with a Yukari witness at each end counting the sacks. The levies did not go south. Not that spring, nor the next. The Stark riders went home to Hollenmark, carrying Rob Stark with them on a sledge under a cairn of his own shields, and the Greymanes to Ridgewall and the Thornwall pikes to Thornhaven, and the Ashmore horse to Ashbury, and the Keld to their fires on the tundra with their warlord on a litter, and in every long house in the north that winter, when the wood went on the fire, someone told the story of the boy who came home from the ice.

The thaw, when it came, was green. Nobody alive had seen the north green. The moss came up first along the river in the lee of the rock, a soft brilliant green like spilled paint against the last gray ice, and then, on the south face of the Seat's cliff where the sun lay longest, small white flowers nobody could name, thousands of them, in the cracks of the stone. The old woman with no teeth, who had come down the Marrow behind the army on a sledge and refused to go home, sat on a bench in the Hall square in the sun and looked at them for a whole afternoon. [very old woman, Yorkshire accent, cracked] *When the grass comes,* [narrator] she said, to nobody, and [laughs] laughed, a cracked old sound like a hinge, and wiped her eyes.

They built a woodpile in the Hall square at the Seat. Nobody ordered it. Somebody started it, the boy with the red ears, the day after the first bell, splitting pine from the Tenrai stables, and the others joined him, and by the end of the week it ran the whole length of the Hall's south wall as high as a man's shoulder, every split end of it pale in the light. Nobody burned from it. It was not for burning. It was only there.

And at the end of the row of cairns on the ridge above Kharven's Foot, someone kept a fire.

Nobody knew who. Nobody ever saw them. But every night that winter, and the next, a small fire burned at the foot of the last cairn, the one with the stone no bigger than a hen's egg on top, small and close and economical, the way a fire is built in a salt gallery to last all night on very little. And every morning it had burned down to ash, and every evening it was lit again.

The king of the north lived alone in the old Kōkan house on the crown of the rock, in the small square room at the back with the single window looking north, and slept on the floor in front of the hearth, and practiced on rope.

He was sixteen.

He had a crown he had never asked for, and a red chair, and a sword with a surgeon in it, and seven lines and six Banner Houses at his feet, and the whole of the north between the Polar Reach and the southern border under his hand.

[softly] He had nobody.

He sat in the red chair in the Hall of Verdicts and listened to the seven lines argue, and let them say all of it, and said very little, and when he did speak, the hundred lamps on their iron chains swung very slightly, all together. And sometimes, when the arguing was done and the Hall was empty, he sat on in the red chair alone in the lamplight, and listened to the singing of the world, the long low humming of everything, and the fine bright notes of the seven lines in their seven chairs, all different, all held together for the first time in a thousand years.

And among them, very faint, very far down, two notes he listened for every day.

One was silver. Small and dry and very careful. A young woman in a silver chair, sending someone away in the night in her own voice. Over and over.

The other was gold. Very pale. A boy of ten with the long Tenrai face, standing beside his father's chair with his hand in his father's, looking round the Hall of Verdicts with his very pale eyes. Counting.

Muken could not hear what the boy was counting. Knowledge revealed what a thing was. It did not tell you what it would become. That was Temporality's labor, and Temporality did not show you a road you had not yet walked.

But he listened to it. Every day.

[softly] There was peace in the north.

[slowly] He did not know how long it would last.
